Summary

In a city plagued by corruption and violent crime, Rio Vidal, a disgraced former FBI agent, is forced to partner with Agatha Harkness, a fiercely independent detective with no patience for rules—or partners.

“Listen, Vidal. I work alone. Stay out of my way, and we’ll be fine.” Rio looked up, her steady gaze locking onto Agatha’s. “That’s not how partnerships work, Harkness.”

The room seemed to hold its breath for a moment before Agatha let out a short scoff. “We’ll see.”

or Agent Rio Vidal inevitably falls for the tough Detective Agatha Harkness.

No Room for Innocence

by riovidhal

https://archiveofourown.org/works/62425783

    Chapter 1: A New Beginning

    The strong pitter-patter of rain enveloped the car, droplets drumming against the windshield held Rio in a trance. The way each drop seemed to collide with the glass mesmerized her. Her gaze locked on the rivulets racing down the glass, each drop colliding and sprawling like vines clinging to a wall. The tiny streams seemed alive, twisting and writhing before disappearing into the oblivion of glass. The steady rhythm of the rain, coupled with the muffled silence inside the car, created a cocoon of detachment.

    She did this often now, zoning out when she had things to do, like an unintentional escape from the weight of her new life.

    This unfortunate reality loomed ahead in the form of a worn, faded building. Once-orange bricks now tinged with grime stood defiantly against the grey sky, their rough surface pockmarked with years of neglect. The building stood alone at the end of the desolate road, surrounded by a sparse line of trees that swayed in the biting wind. This wasn’t even the main Chicago field office, just a low-tier satellite office reserved for minor investigations and petty crimes. The red and orange foliage of the surrounding trees tried to portray the place as a quaint, almost picturesque quality, but to Rio, it only underscored how much she hated it. The air here felt different, thick with isolation and a sense of defeat. Rio wondered if they had placed it here for safety reasons, but that quickly left her mind when she remembered that this place only held an insignificant cops and detectives that gave up and embraced mediocrity. She sighed as she held on tight to the steering wheel, her forehead meeting the cold surface, knuckles turning white and nails digging into the sticky leather.

    There she was again, starting from zero like she had seven years ago. Now, she was back to square one. Walking into that ugly building would seal the reality she’d been avoiding: her career as a Supervisory Special Agent seemed like a distant dream now, and her new position, a demotion to Special Agent handling petty cases—felt like a slap in the face. Rio even chuckled at the thought that they didn’t even let her keep the car. “Government property”, they said.

    Now, she sat behind the wheel of a battered Ford Crown Victoria. The car now reeked of stale cigarettes and cheap lavender air freshener, a cruel reminder of how far she’d fallen. The fabric seats were worn thin with some questionable stains, and the dashboard dotted with faint burns. At least they hadn’t barred her from law enforcement altogether, she thought. Small mercies.

    Rio turned her head towards her passenger seat, and sure enough, there it was. The letter of demotion, neatly folded and crumpled at the edges from her reading it over and over again. Beside it, the notice for her court proceedings lay mockingly, its embossed seal catching the faint light filtering through the rain-soaked windows. Lifting her head and ignoring the small ache at the back of her neck, she reached for the letter and leaned back in her seat.

    She unfolded it slowly, as if it were to be her first time reading it. Her fingers traced the edges of the paper, her gaze skimming over the words she already knew by heart.

    “Effective immediately… transfer… violation of protocol…” Her career, reduced to this lifeless memorandum. She chuckled bitterly to herself, all it needed was a tiny little red bow around it, with a note that said, “Fuck you Vidal, enjoy hell!”.

    “How did it come to this?” She thought.

    Once the rising star of the FBI, she’d been the agent that was praised in meetings and field reports. Her badge had been a source of pride, gleaming on her hip as she led her team through too many investigations. She’d sacrificed everything for the Bureau: birthdays, friendships, and fuck, even relationships—all for the job. And yet, when things went south, they hadn’t hesitated to throw her to the wolves. In the courtroom, her director had watched her fall with an expression as cold and detached as the government lawyers debating her fate. Each word they spoke had felt like a nail in the coffin of her career.

    The burn of tears welled in the corners of her eyes brought her back to the present, she quickly shut the letter and tossed it aside. It landed somewhere between the seat and the muddy floor mat.

    She sighed, sat up straight, and relaxed her shoulders. Closing her eyes tightly and forcing the tears away, reminding herself to be objective. If she’d climbed the ladder once, she could do it again. She had to. Once she was sure the tears were gone, she opened her eyes and took a deep breath. She angled her rearview mirror and met her own gaze. Determined brown eyes staring right back at her.

    “Get it together Vidal, it’s just a job.”

    Her voice was steadier than she expected, the words more a command than reassurance.

    Determined to make a good first impression, she adjusted her hair and the collar of her shirt. Her up do allowing a few brown strands to cascade down her face just right, softening the sharp edges of her expression. Her white button-down was unfastened just enough to suggest she wasn’t a total stiff, and her grey blazer and tailored pants completed the picture of a professional still in control—even if she barely believed it herself. Rio ran her hands down her jacket and pants as to dust it off, as if the motion could cleanse her of the past. Even if it only had been a few months since her fall from grace, this was a new beginning, and it needed treated as such.

    She adjusted the rearview mirror and grabbed the door handle. The bitter chill of rain met her skin as she pushed the door open, droplets soaking through her sleeve almost instantly.

    “Fuck,” she hissed, slamming the door shut again.

    She’d forgotten her coat. Of course.

    Leaning back, she exhaled slowly, letting the tension roll off her shoulders. Her eyes drifted back to the building looming ahead, its dimly lit windows like eyes watching her every move. There was no going back now. This was her life, for better or worse.

    “Keep your cool, Vidal,” she whispered, steeling herself for what lay ahead. Rain still pounded against the windshield, a steady rhythm that seemed to echo her own heartbeat. With one last deep breath, she opened the door again and stepped out into the grey.

    She held her breath all the way to the glass double doors, each step quick and purposeful. Rain lashed against her grey blazer, soaking through to her shirt and stung on her skin, but she didn’t stop. Walking hurriedly, she bumped into a few cops on the way there, but she didn’t care to apologize because the rain was far too cold to stop or slow down.

    Once in, she was met with chaos. A single phone rang incessantly, its shrill tone competing with the clatter of keyboards and the low murmur of voices raised in frustration. The dim lights cast a sad glow over the worn linoleum floors and mismatched furniture. The air smelled faintly of burnt coffee and damp paper, an unholy blend that immediately made her miss the sterile, polished halls back in Virginia.

    A detective brushed past her, his shoulder bumping hers. “Welcome to the jungle, new girl,” he muttered with a smirk, not bothering to slow down. Rio straightened her shoulders, refusing to let the disarray intimidate her. She offered the man a polite nod. The clamor around her was a far cry from the disciplined order she’d grown accustomed to, but she wouldn’t let it rattle her. Not today.

    Her eyes swept over the precinct, taking in the cluttered desks piled high with case files, the bulletin boards crowded with photographs and sticky notes, and the officers walking from one end of the room to the other like they had all eternity. This was her new reality, dead and unpolished. She would adapt.

    Rio adjusted the strap of her bag on her shoulder, her fingers brushing against the leather for a sense of reassurance. Her nail began scratching at it anxiously as she approached the office of the captain, a door adorned with a frosted glass panel and his name emblazoned in bold letters: Captain James Delaney.

    She cleared her throat and straightened her posture. Taking a steadying breath, she raised her hand and knocked on the glass firmly, the sound cutting through the noise of the precinct.

    “Come on in,” came the gruff response from inside.

    Rio pushed the door open and stepped into the office. It was a cramped, cluttered space dominated by an oversized mahogany desk buried under stacks of paperwork. A coffee mug with a permanent stain on the rim, and comic sans font that read “#1 Dad” sat precariously near the edge, next to a half-eaten sandwich. The room smelled faintly of old books and fresh coffee, and soft music played on an old iPod touch next to his keyboard. A stark contrast to the chaos outside.

    Behind the desk sat Captain James Delaney, a heavyset weathered man in his late fifties. His salt-and-pepper hair seemed a tad bit grown out, and his piercing blue eyes locked onto Rio the moment she entered. He leaned back in his chair, resting his hands on his belly, the leather creaking under his weight, and gestured for her to sit.

    “Vidal, right?” he asked, his gravelly voice cutting through the silence. “Yes, sir,” Rio replied.

    She smiled politely and took the seat opposite him. She kept her posture straight, her hands folded neatly in her lap, the wooden chair moving slightly under her weight. She prayed she wouldn’t collapse with it. The captain studied her for a moment, his expression unreadable. “This isn’t the FBI,” he began, his tone blunt but not unkind. “We don’t have the budget, the manpower, or the luxury to follow every protocol. You’ll figure that out soon enough.”

    Rio nodded, her jaw tightening slightly. “I’ll make it work, don’t worry Captain.” she said firmly, trying to sound hopeful.

    Delaney’s gaze softened slightly, and he leaned forward, resting his elbows on the desk. “Look, Vidal,” he said, his voice lowering. “I know what it’s like to start over. It’s not easy, and it sure as hell isn’t fair. But if you’re still here, it means someone up top thinks you’re still worth it. Don’t waste that.”

    Rio blinked, caught off guard by the unexpected note of empathy. She nodded again, this time more sincerely. “Thank you, Captain.” He leaned back again, the moment of softness gone as quickly as it had come. “Good. You’ll be partnered with Detective Harkness. Don’t expect a warm welcome. Your desk is in view of hers—I’m hoping she’ll keep you in check.”

    Rio raised an eyebrow but said nothing, deciding it was best not to ask what exactly needed “keeping in check.” Instead, she rose from her seat and extended a hand. “I appreciate the opportunity, Captain. I promise to do my best.”

    Delaney shook her hand, his grip firm. “We’ll see if you still feel that way in a week,” he said with a faint smirk. “Just remember – ” The sound of the door slamming open cut him off. Both turned to see a woman stride in with an air of confident nonchalance. She was dressed in a worn blue flannel, paired with black pants and scuffed combat boots. Her wavy, curly hair was pulled into a disheveled ponytail, loose strands framing her sharp features. In one hand, she carried a coffee cup, which she tossed toward the trash can halfway across the room. It missed, clattering to the floor without so much as a glance back.

    She scanned Rio briefly, her dark eyes assessing and unimpressed, before rolling them dramatically. “This her? Fantastic. Another suit with something to prove,” she said, her voice dripping with sarcasm.

    Rio remained composed, her tone calm but firm. “It’s Detective Vidal.” She extended her hand for the women to shake but was met with an eye roll. The woman’s lips twitched into a smirk. “Right. That’ll last about a week here.”

    “Harkness,” Delaney cut in sharply, his tone carrying a note of warning. “Show Detective Vidal around. Help her settle in.”

    Agatha raised an eyebrow, the smirk fading into a look of mild irritation. “Settle in? I’m not exactly running a concierge service here, Cap.” “You’ll make time,” Delaney replied, his voice steady but firm. “Start with the layout, then give her a rundown of current caseloads. And Harkness?” Agatha paused, annoyed. “Yeah?”

    “Try to be... professional,” he said, a hint of dry humor softening the edge of his words.

    Agatha scoffed, turning toward Rio. “Alright, suit. Follow me. Let’s get this over with.”

    Rio squared her shoulders and nodded, stepping in line with Agatha as she strode out of the office. The faint creak of Delaney’s chair and the sound of shuffling papers followed them out. Agatha led Rio briskly through the precinct, her boots echoing on the linoleum floor. She didn’t look back to see if Rio was following, her focus instead on the path ahead. As they walked, Rio’s eyes flicked over Agatha, taking in her appearance and body language.

    The woman moved with a sort of careless confidence, her boots clunking heavily against the linoleum floor. Her disheveled ponytail and worn flannel suggested someone who couldn’t care less about appearances, a stark contrast to the sharp professionalism Rio had spent years perfecting. Everything about Agatha screamed rebellion, and Rio couldn’t help but wonder if it was intentional or simply who she was.

    What’s her deal? Rio thought, watching the slight sway of Agatha’s shoulders as she led the way.

    Is she always like this, or is she trying to make a point? The dismissive wave at the precinct’s chaos and her curt tone with Delaney hinted at someone who didn’t care for authority or structure. Rio had seen agents like this before—the lone wolves who thrived on bending the rules, quick to scoff at anyone who didn’t share their approach. As they passed a cluster of desks, Rio noticed how some officers glanced up briefly at Agatha but quickly looked away, their expressions ranging from respect to unease. Agatha didn’t acknowledge them, her focus straight ahead. There was a tension about her, an energy that seemed to fill the space around her. Rio couldn’t decide if it was intimidating or just obnoxious. She’s testing me, Rio realized, her fingers brushing the strap of her bag. Everything about Agatha’s behavior so far—the sarcasm, the flippant attitude, the smirk—was calculated. She was trying to see how far she could push before Rio snapped. It was a game; one Rio had no intention of losing. Still, Rio couldn’t ignore the faint unease creeping into her chest. Agatha’s reputation must precede her if even the captain was quick to issue a warning. And that little smirk… what’s she hiding behind it? Rio made a mental note to stay on guard. Agatha seemed like the type who thrived on exploiting weakness, and Rio had already handed her enough ammunition for one day.

    Focus, she reminded herself, drawing in a steadying breath. This wasn’t Virginia. This wasn’t the Bureau. She couldn’t expect people like Agatha to fit into the molds she was used to. If she wanted to make this work, she had to adapt.

    “Here’s your kingdom,” Agatha said with a dismissive wave toward a small, cluttered desk shoved against the far wall near her own. The black chair looked ready to collapse, one wheel missing entirely. Papers were already scattered across the surface, as if someone had halfheartedly attempted to clear it.

    “Don’t expect anyone to roll out the red carpet,” Agatha added, her tone laced with sarcasm.

    Rio took a moment to assess the desk, her eyes narrowing slightly as she noticed its sorry state.

    Her gaze shifted to Agatha, who stood with an air of smug satisfaction. The sharp lines of her smirk seemed to cut through the air, daring Rio to show any hint of displeasure. Rio straightened her back and forced herself to maintain an even tone. “It’ll do. Thanks for showing me, Detective Harkness.”

    Agatha’s smirk deepened; the expression almost predatory. “Sure, princess. Let’s see how long that attitude lasts.”

    Before Rio could respond, Agatha dropped a towering stack of disorganized paperwork onto the desk with a thud that seemed intentionally loud. The forms were yellowed at the edges, some smudged with ink and coffee stains. It was a mess of bureaucratic chaos.

    “Captain wants us to stay busy,” Agatha said, her tone carrying a sharp edge. “You can handle these. Shouldn’t be too hard for someone with an FBI background, right?”

    Rio’s fingers brushed over the top sheet as she glanced at the stack, her composure faltering slightly at the sheer volume. She began flipping through the forms, quickly realizing they were an unorganized mix of outdated incident reports and procedural logs that required no critical thought. Her lips pressed into a thin line of irritation, but she masked it well. “If this is what needs to get done, I’ll take care of it.” “Good to hear,” Agatha said, walking a few feet away and leaning casually against her own desk. She took a long sip from her coffee cup, her gaze never leaving Rio. “Let’s see if you handle it as perfectly as the Bureau taught you.”

    Rio felt the jab hit its mark but refused to rise to the bait. As she settled into the precarious chair, she allowed herself a brief thought: She’s testing me. Every word, every action is calculated. She wants me to break. Rio pushed the thought aside, focusing on the task at hand, but her movements became sharper, more deliberate.

    Agatha, meanwhile, settled into her own chair, arms crossed as she watched Rio with a predatory glint in her eyes. To her, this was all part of the game —seeing how far she could push the newcomer. Bureaucrats like Rio always cracked eventually, their rigid exteriors crumbling under the weight of the chaos they thought they could fix.

    “You know,” Agatha said suddenly, her voice cutting through the monotony of rustling papers, “these forms? Total waste of time. But hey, maybe you’ll find some hidden FBI-level insight buried in there.” She leaned forward slightly, her tone dripping with mock enthusiasm. “Just don’t get too attached to that desk. We don’t do assigned seating here, princess.” Rio exhaled slowly, setting down her pen for a moment to meet Agatha’s gaze. “If you have a problem with me, Detective Harkness, maybe we should address it directly.”

    Agatha raised an eyebrow, clearly amused. “Problem? No problem. Just making sure you know where you stand.” She gestured to the messy desk and wobbly chair. “Welcome to the real world, Vidal.”

    Rio felt a flicker of irritation as she turned back to the paperwork. Agatha’s smirk wasn’t just mocking—it was a challenge.

    She thinks I’m fragile, Rio thought, flipping through another outdated form. If she expects me to fold, she’s going to be disappointed. Agatha, catching the faint crease forming between Rio’s brows, approached the other woman’s desk, her hands on either side of it, staring directly into brown eyes.

    “Listen, Vidal. I work alone. Stay out of my way, and we’ll be fine.” Rio looked up, her steady gaze locking onto Agatha’s. Despite the simmering irritation beneath her calm exterior, her voice remained measured as she met blue.

    “That’s not how partnerships work, Harkness.”

    The room seemed to hold its breath for a moment before Agatha let out a short scoff, shaking her head. “We’ll see.”

    Chapter 2: The 7th Precinct

    Chapter Notes thank you for all the positive feedback on the first chapter! enjoy some soft butchisms from detective harkness.

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The precinct was alive in a way that unsettled Rio.

    It wasn’t just the noise, though there was plenty of that—the ringing phones, the shuffling of papers, the occasional burst of laughter from a detective who had clearly lost any sense of professionalism years ago. The walls themselves seemed to hold the weight of the job, the lingering echoes of too many unsolved cases, too many victims left behind. She was used to order, control, silence.

    FBI offices had a different kind of energy. Conversations there were low, measured, spoken with purpose. Desks were organized, files pristine, and everything had its place. There was an underlying intensity, sure, but it was one that thrived on structure.

    Here? The 7th Precinct felt like a place that had been running on pure willpower for years.

    It smelled like burnt coffee and printer ink, and the vending machine in the corner flickered like it was on its last legs. Uniformed officers huddled near the break room, muttering over stale snacks, and some detectives sat half- asleep in their chairs, scrolling through case files with the kind of tired determination that spoke of too many late nights.

    And the paperwork in front of her? A disaster.

    Rio rubbed her temple, scanning the scrawled mess of reports that Agatha had dumped onto her desk without a single word of explanation. Some pages were crumpled, others stained, and the handwriting—if it could even be called that—was a chaotic mix of barely legible notes and incomplete thoughts.

    One margin had a single, vague line: "Something’s off. Guy’s lying. Dig deeper."

    That was it. No context. No elaboration. Just Agatha’s instincts, scribbled in ink.

    Rio exhaled sharply, flipping to another page. It didn’t get better. If she had submitted anything even close to this during her FBI days, her supervisor would have torn it apart. Was this what passed for case documentation here? No structure, no protocol? Just… instincts?

    She glanced across the room to where Agatha sat at her own desk—or rather, slouched. The woman had an almost infuriating ease about her, legs propped up, pen twirling lazily in her fingers as she skimmed through something on her screen. She looked wholly unconcerned about the disaster she had just handed off to Rio, as if expecting her to make sense of it like some kind of mind reader.

    And maybe that was the part that annoyed Rio most. Because Agatha did make sense of it.

    Somehow, this method—this complete lack of method—worked for her. It was like watching someone move through a case guided entirely by some invisible force, threading together details no one else could see. The problem was, Rio wasn’t wired like that. She needed facts. She needed logic. She needed something tangible to work with.

    She needed order. Instead, she got Agatha.

    Rio reached for a pen, debating whether she should start rewriting the notes herself, when— “Detective Vidal! Our newest addition to the circus!” The sudden voice cut through her focus like a blade. Before she could even react, a coffee cup landed on her desk, the steam curling into the air. Rio blinked. Her mind was still tangled in Agatha’s illegible notes, and now she had to process the fact that someone had just shouted her name like they were old friends.

    She looked up—and met William Kaplan’s wide, mischievous grin. For a second, all she could do was stare, he was… unexpected. For one, he was far too put-together for this place. Most of the detectives she had seen so far looked like they had been surviving on bad coffee and worse decisions. Wrinkled shirts, loose ties, dark circles under their eyes. But William? His navy-blue button-up was crisp and perfectly fitted. His tie was flawlessly knotted, a sharp contrast to Agatha’s perpetual “I don’t give a damn” aesthetic. And his hair? Soft brown curls, bouncing slightly when he moved, like he had a secret hair routine no one else knew about. Everything about him was precise, intentional—except for his expression, which was pure amusement.

    "Welcome gift," William announced, gesturing to the coffee cup before dropping into the chair across from her desk like they were old friends catching up.

    Rio eyed the cup, then him. "I take it you’re not just handing these out to everyone?"

    William smirked, resting his chin on his palm. "Only to the ones who look like they’re two seconds away from deciding they hate it here." She let out a small, reluctant breath, something between a sigh and a laugh. "I don’t hate it."

    "Yet," he corrected smoothly.

    She took a sip of the coffee—black, just how she liked it—and studied him, trying to place him. "And you are?"

    "Detective William Kaplan. Resident twink, precinct overachiever, and your best bet at figuring out how to survive Agatha Harkness." Rio blinked. "Did you just call yourself a twink?"

    William grinned. "What? I own it. Anyway, back to the important part— Agatha. She’s going to break you."

    "She’s not going to break me," Rio said flatly.

    William leaned back, crossing his arms, his smirk widening like he was enjoying this already.

    "Oh, you think that now. Give it time."

    Before she could respond, another voice joined the conversation, low and measured, carrying the weight of experience.

    "William, are you harassing the new detective already?"

    Rio turned and found herself face-to-face with Lilia Calderu. She knew the name before she had even arrived at the precinct. Lilia Calderu had a reputation. Not just as a detective but as the kind of officer who could make or break a career with a few well-placed words. Some in law enforcement saw her as a quiet but ruthless operator, one who ensured things ran smoothly behind the scenes while never drawing too much attention to herself. Others described her as the person you went to when things got complicated—when the usual rules weren’t enough. The woman extended a hand in greeting, her expression calm but appraising. "Detective Lilia Calderu," she introduced herself smoothly. Lilia smiled faintly. “I handle logistics, coordination, and making sure certain detectives don’t get themselves into trouble."

    She shot a pointed glance at William.

    William clutched his chest dramatically. "I am an angel, Lilia. You wound me."

    Lilia ignored him. "I read about you, you know. When you were with the FBI."

    That statement made Rio’s stomach twist.

    Her grip on the coffee cup tightened just slightly before she forced herself to relax.

    "That was a long time ago."

    Lilia studied her, then nodded. "Fair enough."

    She hesitated for a moment, then leaned in slightly, lowering her voice just enough so only Rio could hear. "Listen, I know this place can be overwhelming at first. And Agatha—she has her own way of doing things. She’s brilliant, but she’s also relentless. She’ll push you, test you, and she won’t hold your hand through any of it."

    Rio swallowed, the weight of the warning settling on her. "I can handle it." Lilia’s lips curled in something that wasn’t quite a smile. "I don’t doubt that. But don’t let her break you down. She’ll try, intentionally or not. It’s just her way. She likes to see what people are made of. Just don’t lose sight of why you’re here, and don’t be afraid to push back when you need to." For the first time since she arrived, Rio felt something other than exhaustion or frustration—something almost like reassurance. "Thanks." Lilia gave her a knowing look. "You’ll be fine. It might not feel like it now, but you will. And if you need anything, my door is always open." The intercom above them suddenly crackled to life.

    “Vidal. Harkness. Got a missing person for you. Come to my office.” Rio sighed, already anticipating the headache that would come with this. Agatha had barely looked up from her desk, simply tossing her pen down and standing without a word, moving with that deliberate kind of carelessness that made it clear she wasn’t in a hurry—but also wasn’t going to be ignored.

    As Rio grabbed her notebook and followed, she caught Lilia watching her, eyes narrowed slightly, her expression hovering somewhere between concern and curiosity.

    She wasn’t outright worried for Rio, not exactly, but there was something in her face—like she was already trying to gauge how long Rio would last here.

    William, ever the opportunist, flashed her a grin as she passed, the corners of his mouth twitching like he was holding back a joke just for the sport of it.

    "Good luck. You’ll need it."

    Rio exhaled slowly, gripping her notebook just a little tighter. Yeah. No kidding.

    The hallway leading to Captain Delaney’s office was narrower than the others, the walls lined with outdated precinct commendations and yellowed news clippings, some framed, some pinned haphazardly as if no one had taken the time to straighten them in years. The soft hum of conversation from the bullpen faded as they approached, replaced by the low, muffled strain of music filtering through the frosted glass door. Agatha didn’t slow down as they reached it. She didn’t knock. Didn’t wait. She just pushed the door open like she lived there and strode inside. Rio felt her jaw clench, but she didn’t comment, simply stepping in after her, the air shifting from the frenetic energy of the bullpen to something heavier, slower.

    Delaney didn’t look up right away, still flipping through whatever report he had been pretending to read.

    The chair beneath him groaned as he finally shifted, letting out a long, tired sigh before fixing them both with a look that held the weight of a thousand disappointments.

    "Harkness. Vidal."

    Agatha, unfazed, dropped into the chair across from the desk with the grace of someone who had done this a thousand times before. She stretched out her legs, arms draped over the armrests like she was settling in for a particularly boring movie.

    Rio, on the other hand, sat properly, flipping open her notebook, pen poised, back straight.

    Delaney grunted, rubbing his jaw before grabbing a thin case file from the mess on his desk and tossing it toward them.

    "Missing person. Lisa Moreno. Twenty-four. Went out two nights ago. Didn’t come home."

    Rio caught the file before it could slide off the desk, flipping it open with a practiced efficiency.

    The attached photo showed a young woman with dark hair, warm brown eyes, and a slightly hesitant smile, like whoever had taken the picture had called her name at the last second. It was grainy, probably pulled from social media.

    According to the preliminary notes, she was last seen leaving a bar called The Lantern, a shady dive on the west side, around 11:30 PM. She lived alone. Worked as a server at a local diner. No criminal record. No immediate red flags—no history of disappearances, arrests, or financial trouble. She had just vanished.

    Rio’s grip on her pen tightened. People didn’t just disappear, so something had to have happened.

    She flipped the page. "Who reported her missing?"

    Delaney leaned back in his chair, the wood creaking under the shift of his weight. "Her roommate. Said Lisa doesn’t do this. No history of running off. No drugs. No mental health issues. Just… gone."

    Agatha, who had been silent so far, let out a low, thoughtful hum, her fingers tapping lightly against the chair’s armrest. "Any known exes? Stalkers? Someone with a reason to want her missing?"

    Delaney nodded toward the file in Rio’s hands. "Roommate mentioned an ex-boyfriend. Nothing nasty in the breakup, but you know how that goes. Name’s in there. He’s your starting point."

    Agatha reached over and grabbed the folder from Rio, flipping through it lazily, her mouth twisting in thought.

    Delaney, meanwhile, was watching them both, his expression unreadable. Rio could feel it—that same quiet assessment she had noticed earlier. It wasn’t distrust, exactly. But there was something calculating in the way he studied them. Like he was waiting for something.

    She pushed that thought aside and focused on the case.

    "Do we have security footage?" she asked, already expecting the answer. Delaney let out a dry chuckle, shaking his head. "You think a place like The Lantern has working cameras?"

    Rio sighed, rubbing her temple. Of course not.

    Agatha finally sat up properly, flipping the folder shut with one hand. "We starting with the ex or the bar?"

    Delaney shrugged, rubbing his jaw. "Up to you. Just get it done." Rio nodded, snapping the file closed. "We’ll start at the bar. See if anyone remembers her leaving with someone."

    Agatha let out an exaggerated sigh, rolling her eyes as she stood. "Great. Can't wait to watch you take notes like an overachieving grad student while I do the actual investigating."

    Rio shot her a dry look, tucking the case file under her arm. "It's called being thorough, Harkness. You should try it sometime."

    Agatha smirked, tilting her head dramatically. "Nah. Sounds exhausting." Delaney grunted in approval, already picking up another report like this was just another Tuesday for him. Then, just as Rio reached for the door handle, his voice cut through the air again.

    "Vidal."

    She paused, looking over her shoulder. "Yeah?" Delaney still didn’t look up. Didn’t move.

    Just muttered—

    "Try to keep her from getting kicked out of the damn bar this time." Rio frowned. "I—"

    Agatha was already walking past her, smirking.

    "No promises, boss."

    Then she was out the door, leaving Rio standing there, blinking after her. She turned back to Delaney, brows furrowed. He finally looked up, meeting her gaze with a wry, knowing expression. "You’ll see."

    Rio sighed deeply, rolling her shoulders before following Agatha out. She had a feeling this day was about to get a whole lot longer. -

    The air outside the precinct was thick with exhaust fumes and the distant scent of street food, a strange mix of grime and life that clung to the city like an old coat. The sun, though still bright, had begun its slow descent toward the horizon, stretching shadows across the cracked pavement. Rio barely had time to reposition the case file under her arm before realizing Agatha was already several steps ahead of her, striding toward the parking lot with that same effortless arrogance she seemed to wear like a second skin.

    Rio quickened her pace, falling into step beside her, though it took more effort than she wanted to admit. Agatha walked like she was leading even when there was no one following.

    "Are you always in this much of a hurry, or do you just enjoy making people chase after you?" Rio muttered, adjusting the case file in her grip as she caught up to her side.

    Agatha didn’t slow down and shot her a side glance, smirking as she reached into her coat pocket and pulled out a pack of gum instead of the cigarette Rio had half-expected. She popped a piece of gum into her mouth, tucking the foil wrapper into her pocket.

    "I walk fast. Efficient. You keep up, or you don’t. Not my problem." Rio pressed her lips into a thin line, biting back the urge to roll her eyes. She exhaled, biting back a small, irritated smirk. "Yeah. Efficiency. That’s definitely the first word that comes to mind when I think about you." Agatha finally turned her head slightly, the corner of her mouth tugging into something sharp, teasing. "Flattered, Vidal. Didn’t know I was taking up so much real estate in that head of yours."

    Rio let out a slow, steady breath, keeping her expression neutral as they reached the lot. As if she’d give this small town detective the time of day. The parking lot was mostly empty aside from a few unmarked police vehicles, their paint dull with age, and a handful of personal cars that looked like they had been through at least two minor accidents each. Rio made her way toward the black sedan she’d been assigned, pulling the driver’s side door open and sliding in with precise efficiency. Agatha gravitated toward the passenger side without hesitation, yanking the door open like she owned the car.

    And, honestly? She was glad. She had seen the way Agatha carried herself, the way she leaned into recklessness like it was second nature. No way in hell was she letting her behind the wheel.

    Rio adjusted her mirrors with deliberate precision before fastening her seatbelt. "You ever drive?" she asked as she turned the key in the ignition, the engine grumbling to life.

    Agatha shrugged, resting her elbow against the door. "Not when I can avoid it. The world’s safer that way."

    Rio glanced at her, unimpressed. "And here I was thinking you liked reckless behavior."

    Agatha smirked. "I do. Just not when I’m the one doing it." -

    The first few minutes of the drive were quiet, except for the occasional chirp of a turn signal and the low hum of the engine.

    The city stretched out around them in a blur of shifting skylines and uneven streets, passing by in bursts of rusted fire escapes, neon signs, and cracked sidewalks littered with cigarette butts.

    Rio kept her eyes on the road, even as she felt Agatha’s gaze flicking toward her every few moments—not obvious, but enough that she noticed it.

    She wasn’t just looking out the window. She was watching. Rio had worked with enough seasoned agents to recognize that habit. Agatha wasn’t just sizing up the drive—she was reading the streets, cataloging faces, noting which cars had been on the road for too long. She was wired for threat detection, even when there wasn’t an obvious reason to be.

    Rio wasn’t sure if that was a good thing or a warning sign. Rio cleared her throat and flicked a glance at the GPS mounted on the dashboard to double-check their route. The Lantern wasn’t far, just past the old industrial district, wedged between a liquor store with metal bars on the windows and a pawn shop that had seen better days. The kind of place where no one asked questions if you left with a black eye. Agatha, meanwhile, was watching the city pass by through the passenger- side window, her elbow propped against the door, fingers idly drumming against her thigh.

    Rio didn’t take her eyes off the road, flicking her blinker on as she changed lanes with careful precision.

    "You always drive this slow?" Agatha muttered, her gum smacking loudly in her mouth.

    Rio didn’t take her eyes off the road. "It’s called following the speed limit." Agatha snorted, shifting slightly in her seat. "Jesus. No wonder you lasted so long in the FBI. Bet you never even jaywalked."

    Rio exhaled sharply through her nose, keeping her focus ahead. "Sorry if I don’t want to get pulled over while on duty."

    Agatha turned her head toward the window, smirking. "Sure. Duty. Nothing to do with the fact that you probably have a panic attack if you don’t use your turn signal."

    Rio felt her fingers tighten slightly on the wheel but didn’t rise to the bait. Instead, she flicked on the blinker again with deliberate emphasis. Agatha huffed a laugh. "See? You are as fun as a stack of tax forms." Rio kept her expression neutral, letting the silence stretch just long enough to make Agatha uncomfortable before finally replying, "Funny. I was just thinking you have the patience of a toddler on a sugar high." Agatha placed a hand over her heart, mock-affronted. "Wow. Two insults in one sentence. You do know how to let loose."

    Rio shook her head but didn’t fight the small smirk tugging at the corner of her lips. She hated to admit it, but she was already getting used to Agatha’s constant jabs. And the worst part? Somewhere beneath all the sarcasm and smugness, Agatha was actually paying attention—reading Rio the way she read a case file, picking apart her habits, her weaknesses, her tells. Rio wasn’t sure she liked that.

    As they neared the building, The Lantern looked exactly how Rio expected it to. It was a squat, worn-down thing, its red-brick exterior darkened with years of weather and neglect. A neon sign above the door flickered erratically, half the letters burnt out so that it read "THE LA N" instead of its full name.

    Even though it was early in the day, the place was still open, the interior dimly lit through the tinted windows. A "No Loitering" sign was taped to the glass, right next to a notice banning gang colors and weapons—not that Rio thought it stopped anyone.

    Agatha let out a low whistle, stretching her arms as she unbuckled her seatbelt. "Classy joint. Hell of a place to get murdered in." Rio shot her a look. "We’re here to prevent that,” she said, pulling the car into an empty spot along the curb.

    "Yeah, well. Seems like a losing battle." Agatha pushed the car door open and stepped out, rolling her shoulders. "C’mon, Vidal. Let’s ruin someone’s afternoon."

    The moment they stepped out, the cold air pressed against them. It smelled like old beer, cigarette smoke, and something greasy wafting from a nearby food cart. A few people near the alley beside the bar, hoods up, hands tucked deep into their pockets, their conversations hushed but wary as they eyed the unmarked car.

    As they stepped inside, Rio took a quick scan of the bar, a small dinging tune heard overhead by them walking in.

    It was small but packed with history—the walls lined with faded newspaper clippings and framed pictures of past patrons, most of them yellowed with time. The lighting was dim, with cracked leather booths, and a floor that had probably never been properly cleaned.

    A few early drinkers were scattered throughout, their eyes flicking up only briefly before returning to their conversations.

    The bartender, a stocky man in his forties with a permanent scowl, was already watching them, wiping down a glass that looked worse than the counter itself.

    Rio approached the bar first, flashing her badge as Agatha leaned beside her, looking far too entertained already.

    "Detectives. We’re looking into a missing person—Lisa Moreno. She was here two nights ago. Ring any bells?"

    The bartender grunted, glancing at the photo Rio slid across the counter. He barely looked at it before shaking his head. "Lots of girls come through here. Can’t remember all of ‘em."

    Agatha tilted her head, studying him. "That’s funny. See, most bartenders— especially in places like this—remember faces. You get a lot of repeat customers, a lot of stories. So either you’ve got an unusually bad memory..." Agatha trailed off and adjusted her jacket to the side, letting the gun on her belt peek through.

    She let the silence hang, waiting.

    Rio watched as the bartender’s jaw tightened slightly, his grip on the glass tightening. The man sighed, setting the glass down. "She was here. Came in alone, left with someone."

    Rio’s stomach tightened. "Who?"

    The bartender shrugged, tossing the rag he’d been using onto the counter. "Didn’t get a good look. Guy had a hat on, kept his head down. They talked at the bar for a bit, then left together."

    Rio noted the shift in his tone—a little too casual, too dismissive. Like he was trying to end the conversation before it even started. Agatha, however, wasn’t having it. She leaned in slightly, planting both hands flat on the sticky bar top, her voice dropping just enough to make the air feel heavier.

    "That’s convenient," she mused, her eyes narrowing as she studied the bartender. "A guy comes in, chats up a woman who disappears that same night, and you barely noticed him? Didn’t catch his face? Didn’t overhear a word?"

    The bartender’s jaw tightened, but he held his ground. "Like I said, lot of people come through here. I don’t keep track of all of ‘em." Agatha let out a low, humorless chuckle, tilting her head. "See, that’s the thing. I’ve been in enough bars like this to know that you do keep track. You remember the ones who cause trouble. The ones who stand out. The ones who leave with girls that don’t come back."

    Rio watched the exchange carefully, her hand resting lightly on her belt, right near her holstered badge. Agatha was pressing now, leaning into that intimidating, slow-burn pressure that Rio had no doubt worked plenty of times before.

    The bartender, though, wasn’t completely rattled—yet. He crossed his arms, expression tight and guarded. "Look, I got no reason to lie to you." Agatha smirked, but it didn’t reach her eyes.

    Before Rio could register the movement, Agatha had reached over the bar, grabbing the bartender’s shirt in a tight grip, dragging him forward just enough that his hands instinctively flew up in startled defense. Her knuckles went white, her hold firm but not choking, just enough to remind him that this was her conversation now.

    "No?" she said, voice low and edged with something dangerous. "That’s interesting, because right now, it kind of looks like you do." The bartender’s eyes widened just slightly, but he wasn’t a complete coward —he fought to keep his expression neutral, even as he swallowed hard. "Lady," he said, voice strained but steady, "you lay hands on me, and we both know this whole thing goes straight to IA."

    The bartender’s posture stiffened further, and Rio could feel the energy in the room start to shift. A few of the regulars at the bar had grown quieter, pretending not to listen but definitely noting what was happening. This was the part where things could go south. Rio could feel the blood drain from her face. She needed to step in—fast.

    Rio cleared her throat, stepping in slightly between them, not touching Agatha but making it clear she was taking control of the situation. "Detective Harkness," Rio said, her tone measured, professional, but firm, "step back."

    Agatha didn’t move immediately, but her eyes flicked toward Rio’s in a way that made it clear she was weighing whether or not to ignore her. "Agatha," Rio said sharply, stepping closer, voice pitched just low enough that it wasn’t an order, but a warning. "Let him go. Now." Agatha didn’t break eye contact with the bartender, but her jaw ticked, her fingers twitching slightly like she was debating whether she could squeeze just a little more out of him before letting go.

    Rio knew that hesitation—it wasn’t about power, it was about calculation. Agatha was reading the way the guy’s pulse jumped, how his shoulders stiffened—determining whether this was a man who would fold under more pressure or dig his heels in harder.

    Agatha clicked her tongue, then released him with a sharp shove, straightening like she had never touched him at all.

    "Fine," she muttered, brushing off her hands like his cheap polyester offended her.

    The bartender stumbled back half a step, adjusting his collar with a half- hearted glare, but Rio caught the way he exhaled shakily, how his hands still trembled slightly.

    Yeah. He knew something.

    And now? Now, he was just going to be more careful about who he gave it to.

    Rio caught Agatha’s sleeve and pulled her to the side, far enough from the bar so they couldn’t be heard by the patrons, or the disheveled bar tender. “Harkness. What’s your problem? Are you trying to throw this entire thing out before we even get started?” She glared.

    Agatha scoffed and raised her hands in defense. “Hey, just trying to do my job here, you know? Interrogating a witness. Ring a bell, Special Agent?” Rio held her gaze. "That sounded an awful lot like coercion. You know— intimidation of a potential witness? That thing that could get a statement thrown out in court?"

    Agatha exhaled sharply through her nose, standing up straight, but the smirk on her lips didn’t fade. "I call it motivation. Helps jog memories." Rio leveled a look at Agatha but kept her voice even. "That could’ve been an unlawful use of force. Don’t push it next time."

    Agatha raised an eyebrow. "Yeah? Well, next time, I’ll make sure we actually get an answer."

    Rio rolled her eyes and walked back to the bartender, lifting a hand in a more measured, diplomatic approach.

    "Look, we’re not here to make your life harder," she said, voice calm but deliberately even. "We just need to know what you saw. And if you remember anything later—anything at all—you call us." She slid a business card across the bar, tapping it once.

    The bartender hesitated for a moment before he nodded stiffly, but Rio could tell it wasn’t because he was feeling cooperative. He was relieved. It was because he wanted them gone.

    As they turned toward the door, Agatha muttered under her breath, "You know he’s full of shit, right?"

    "Of course I know," Rio whispered back, keeping her expression neutral. Agatha scoffed. "So what? We just sit around and wait for him to have a crisis of conscience?"

    Rio turned sternly towards the other woman. “I also know we don’t have leverage yet. We need him to be nervous enough to slip up, not scared enough to shut down completely."

    Agatha scoffed, shoving her hands in her pockets. "See, that’s your problem, Vidal. You play too nice. Guys like that? They don’t talk unless you shake ‘em first."

    Rio gave her a pointed look as they stepped outside. "And if you shake ‘em too hard, they don’t talk at all. They lawyer up." Agatha didn’t respond, but Rio could tell she wasn’t convinced.

    She exhaled, already feeling the weight of the case settling deeper in her chest.

    This wasn’t going to be easy.

    -

    Agatha was still buzzing with irritation when she slid into the passenger seat, the faint scent of cheap beer and cigarette smoke from the bar clinging to her jacket. She hated this part. The part where she had to sit back and do nothing, to play the waiting game, to pretend like she hadn’t just watched a liar slip through their fingers because rules said they needed something more than instinct and gut feeling.

    Bureaucratic bullshit.

    The guy at The Lantern knew something. She could feel it. The way his shoulders had gone rigid, the way his eyes darted toward the door like he was already thinking of his escape, the way his hand twitched slightly when she grabbed him—that little tell, that moment of pure, raw reaction. A man who had nothing to hide wouldn’t react that way.

    And yet, here they were, driving away, acting like that hadn’t been a damn near confession wrapped in denial. Her jaw tightened, fingers curling into fists against her thigh as she watched Rio slide into the driver’s seat, adjusting the mirrors with that same meticulous care she did everything with.

    God, she was methodical. Precise. Stiff.

    It was a miracle she didn’t get stress fractures just from how tightly wound she was.

    "You’re thinking too loud," Rio muttered, not looking at her as she turned the key, the engine rumbling to life.

    Agatha huffed a laugh, slouching against the seat. "Can’t help it. My brain actually works at a decent speed."

    Rio didn’t react to the jab, just put the car into gear and pulled out onto the road with calculated smoothness.

    Agatha waited a beat, then smirked. "You mad at me, Vidal?" Rio exhaled sharply through her nose, fingers tapping once against the steering wheel, but her voice was even when she finally responded. "I don’t get mad. I get concerned when my partner’s methods are gonna get us both thrown in front of IA."

    Agatha scoffed, shifting to face her more. "Oh please. He’s not gonna file a complaint."

    "You don’t know that," Rio shot back, flicking her eyes toward her briefly before returning to the road.

    Agatha grinned, enjoying the fact that she was finally getting a rise out of her.

    "Yeah, I do. He’s scared. You saw it just as much as I did." Rio let out a measured breath, tightening her grip on the wheel like she was physically restraining herself from engaging in this debate. "He was scared, sure," she admitted. "But fear fades. It turns into anger. And angry people talk to lawyers."

    Agatha tilted her head, watching her carefully. "That how it worked in the Bureau?"

    Rio’s jaw ticked, just slightly. "That’s how it works everywhere." Agatha filed that reaction away for later, shifting in her seat as she let the silence stretch between them.

    Fine. Let Rio be all by-the-book and measured about it. She’d learn soon enough.

    The ride back to the precinct was thick with unspoken tension, the air inside the car charged but not volatile—like an exposed wire just waiting for the right touch to spark.

    Rio kept one hand firm on the wheel, the other resting on the armrest, fingers tapping against the worn leather as she replayed the encounter at The Lantern in her head. Agatha’s little stunt had been a risk. One wrong move, one complaint filed, and suddenly their case was a legal mess instead of a missing person’s investigation.

    And yet—

    She couldn’t say Agatha had been entirely wrong.

    The bartender was hiding something. The problem was, now he knew they knew that. Agatha, for her part, didn’t seem particularly bothered by any of it.

    She sat with one arm propped against the door, fingers idly playing with the edge of her sleeve, looking every bit as relaxed and detached as she had on the way to the bar.

    But Rio had already figured her out.

    That wasn’t indifference—it was habit.

    The same kind of detachment Rio had seen in agents who had spent too long in the field, the ones who had learned to keep everything pushed down, locked tight, because if they let themselves feel everything, they’d never stop drowning.

    -

    The rest of the drive passed in relative quiet, aside from the distant honk of traffic and the occasional creak of leather as Agatha shifted in her seat. By the time they pulled back into the precinct parking lot, the sun had dipped further toward the horizon, casting the building in orange and shadow.

    Rio parked the car in one fluid movement, shifting into park before turning the key with precision.

    Agatha didn’t wait.

    She was already out of the car before Rio had even taken off her seatbelt, striding toward the entrance like she had somewhere better to be. Rio exhaled, rubbing a hand down her face before following. Inside, the buzz of the precinct had settled into its usual rhythm—phones ringing, the distant murmur of officers chatting, the rhythmic clack of a keyboard from Lilia’s desk.

    William was at his usual spot, perched at the edge of someone else’s desk, gesturing wildly as he told a story to an officer who looked half-amused, half-trapped.

    The moment he spotted Rio and Agatha, his face lit up.

    "Ah, the prodigal detectives return," he said, clapping his hands together. "Tell me, how was the grand adventure into the depths of fine drinking establishments?"

    Agatha shot him a deadpan look. "Horrible. No one bought me a drink." William grinned, unfazed. "Tragic. Truly. What’s the case update?" Rio sighed, tossing the case file onto her desk before rubbing her temples. "The bartender at The Lantern remembers Lisa being there," she said. "Saw her leave with a guy, but he’s claiming he didn’t get a good look." William arched a brow, glancing at Agatha. "And what do you think?" Agatha plopped into her chair, kicking her feet up onto the edge of her desk. "I think he’s lying through his teeth."

    Rio shot her a look. "Which we’ll need to prove with evidence. Not intimidation."

    William’s eyes darted between them; interest piqued. "Oh, was there an intimidation attempt? Do tell."

    Agatha just grinned, but Rio cut in before she could say something that would make her blood pressure spike.

    "The point is," Rio continued, ignoring William’s amusement, "we need to look deeper into Lisa’s connections. See if the ex-boyfriend has anything relevant. And we need to find out if The Lantern has a history of women going missing after leaving with men."

    Lilia, who had been listening from her desk, rolled her chair over smoothly. "That place has been on our radar before," she mused, tapping her nails against the arm of her chair. "It’s not high profile, but we’ve had noise complaints, bar fights, and a couple of drug busts in the past year." Rio frowned. "Any previous missing persons cases linked to it?" Lilia pursed her lips, thinking. "Not directly, but I can do a sweep. I’ll pull what we have and see if anything pops out."

    Rio nodded gratefully, but before she could speak, William leaned forward, resting his chin in his hands.

    "So, Vidal," he said, tone light but pointed, "tell me, how does working in our little chaotic circus compare to your glamorous FBI days?" Rio hesitated for half a second, and that was all it took for Agatha to catch it. She tilted her head slightly, watching her like a cat watching prey. Rio exhaled, shaking her head. "It’s different."

    William grinned. "That’s a polite way of saying ‘a disaster.’" Rio couldn’t help it—a small, tired smirk slipped through. "If I wanted a disaster, I’d have stayed at the Bureau."

    Agatha chuckled under her breath, kicking her feet off her desk. "Well, congrats. You traded one hellhole for another."

    Lilia stood, sighing. "Alright, I’ll pull the records. You two figure out your next move. And try not to kill each other in the meantime." "No promises," Agatha muttered.

    Rio sighed and rolled her eyes, bracing herself for whatever came next. She had a feeling Lisa Moreno wasn’t the first girl to disappear this way. Or, that this would be the first time Agatha Harkness would get under her skin. Chapter End Notes if you'd like to reach me, im @/riovidhal on twitter. :)

    Chapter 3: Slow and Steady

    Chapter Notes this is a sweet one.

    enjoy!

    The precinct had been a dead zone for hours; longer, really. The last few days had blended together into a mind-numbing haze of reports, interviews, and far too much time spent staring at the same damn files, hoping a fresh set of eyes would magically make something new appear. But it never did. The entire unit had been stuck in a loop, combing through paperwork, cross-referencing witness statements, double-checking alibis that weren’t going to change no matter how many times they were read. Any sense of urgency had been buried beneath stacks of case files, the momentum of the investigation slowed to a crawl by the necessary evil of procedure. It was the kind of grunt work that made detectives itch for something real. No footwork, no doors to knock on, no adrenaline, no rush of piecing something together in real time. Just endless lines of ink on paper, the occasional murmur of hushed conversation, and the dull, repetitive clicks of keyboards.

    The station’s coffee had grown stale hours ago—left to burn at the bottom of the pot, its bitter, acrid smell hanging in the air like a punishment for their collective lack of progress. No one wanted to be the one to make a fresh batch, but they all complained about the taste anyway. Tension had been settling over the bullpen in quiet waves, everyone feeling the weight of time slipping away without any breakthroughs. It had started subtly with more sighs, heavier groans, the way shoulders slumped lower as the hours dragged on. But by the time the clock crept past eight, the patience in the room had thinned to its limit.

    That was when William finally snapped.

    He groaned loudly, the sound more dramatic than necessary, and tossed his pen down onto his desk like it had personally offended him. “Alright, I can’t do this anymore,” he announced, stretching his arms over his head with an exaggerated sigh. “I say we cut our losses and grab a drink before this case actually kills me.”

    Lilia perked up immediately, slamming her laptop shut with a level of enthusiasm that suggested she’d been waiting for someone to crack first. “I’m in.”

    She barely hesitated before turning to Rio, eyebrows raised, expectant. “You coming, Vidal?”

    Rio blinked, dragging herself out of the focus she had forced onto her notes, even though her brain had checked out at least an hour ago. She hadn’t been reading, not really. More like staring at the words and hoping they’d rearrange themselves into something useful.

    She glanced between them, considering.

    A drink sounded nice, but she’d gotten comfortable in her routine; late nights at her desk, avoiding too much conversation, keeping herself occupied with work so she didn’t have to think about much else. It was easier that way. Safer.

    She shrugged, noncommittal. “Nah, I’m good.”

    William then wasted no time making his way over to Rio’s desk, his steps quick and deliberate, like he was on a mission. He leaned his hip against the edge of her desk with an easy familiarity, arms crossed over his chest, a smirk already forming.

    Lilia followed close behind, sliding into place on the other side of her desk, resting her elbows against the surface as she propped her chin in her hands. There was an air of mischief about both of them, like they had been waiting for an excuse to shake Rio out of whatever self-imposed isolation she had put herself in.

    William scoffed loudly, shaking his head as he turned toward Agatha. “You hearing this?”

    Agatha, who had been absentmindedly twirling the corner of her paperwork between her fingers, arched a brow without looking up. “Tragic,” she deadpanned. “Absolutely tragic.”

    Before she could so much as protest, Agatha then stood up from her own desk and walked over, slow and deliberate, her movements casual but calculated like a predator circling in before going for the throat. She stopped just behind William and Lilia, tilting her head slightly, lips quirking in a smirk that spelled nothing but trouble.

    William turned back to Rio, dramatically placing a hand over his heart like she had personally wounded him. “Vidal, you work too much. Come suffer with the rest of us.”

    Rio smirked but shook her head again, half-hearted. “I’d rather not wake up with a headache and the same workload.”

    William groaned, exasperated, and turned to his last line of defense. “Agatha. Talk some sense into your partner before she dies at her desk.” “Oh, come on, Vidal,” Agatha drawled, bracing a hand against the back of Rio’s chair. “Let me guess. You’re gonna stay here, finish your reports, go home, and have the exact same night you always have?” She clicked her tongue in exaggerated disappointment. “So boring.”

    It was such a simple thing to say, something Rio might’ve brushed off if it had come from anyone else. But there was something about the way Agatha said it, the easy confidence, the hint of something just a little persuasive in her tone, that made it harder to dismiss.

    Rio rolled her eyes, leaning back slightly in her chair, but they had effectively boxed her in. William and Lilia on either side, Agatha looming behind her, it was three against one. She could practically feel the walls closing in.

    Rio sighed, pretending to consider it, even though she already knew she’d lost this battle.

    Lilia grinned, bumping Rio’s arm lightly. “She’s right, you know. You’re, like, aggressively predictable.”

    “I like routine!” Rio shot back, lifting a brow.

    William scoffed. “Routine is for retirement homes.”

    Agatha leaned in just slightly, lowering her voice like she was sharing a secret. “Be honest, Vidal. When’s the last time you did something fun?” Rio huffed. “Define fun.”

    William gestured broadly. “Literally anything other than sitting here, drinking bad coffee, and pretending that staring at those files is gonna make the case magically solve itself.”

    Rio opened her mouth to argue, but Agatha tapped the back of her chair, cutting her off.

    “You’re making this way harder than it needs to be,” she said, shaking her head in mock disappointment. “One drink, Vidal. You can even pretend you’re miserable the whole time, if that makes you feel better.” Rio narrowed her eyes at her, unimpressed. “You’re really not gonna let this go, huh?”

    Agatha grinned. “Nope.”

    Lilia nudged her arm again. “Don’t fight it. It’s easier this way.” Agatha tilted her head slightly, watching her, something unreadable flickering behind her eyes. “One drink,” she added, voice dropping into something lower, something almost persuasive. “I won’t even make fun of you. Much.”

    Rio rolled her eyes.

    It wasn’t that she didn’t want to go. It was just, she had been keeping her distance, trying to navigate whatever complicated mess her partnership with Agatha was turning into without overstepping. But this? This was how it started. Drinks. Easy conversations. The kind of thing that blurred lines. Rio sighed, shaking her head, but it wasn’t real exasperation. Because the truth was, she liked this.

    She liked the way they ganged up on her, the way Agatha—especially Agatha—pushed and poked and prodded until Rio gave in. It was a familiar rhythm now, something expected, something comfortable. She hadn’t thought she’d like being bullied into socializing, but she’d come to enjoy the ritual of it. The teasing, the relentless goading, the fact that Agatha never let her take herself too seriously.

    “One drink,” Rio finally relented, pointing a finger at William like she needed to set some kind of boundary. “And if this place is a shithole, I will blame you.”

    William beamed like she had just agreed to be his best friend for life. “Oh, it’s absolutely a shithole.”

    William jumped up with a spring in his step, already moving toward the exit like he had won something monumental. Lilia followed with a smug little grin, nudging Rio’s arm once more as they walked past. Agatha, of course, lingered just a second longer, watching Rio with that same amused expression, like she was sizing her up, like she knew exactly what buttons to push.

    “Just one drink, huh?” Agatha repeated, voice light, teasing. “We’ll see about that.”

    Rio narrowed her eyes but didn’t bother responding.

    She already knew she had lost this battle the second Agatha had walked over.

    As they piled into William’s car, because of course, William insisted on driving; Rio couldn’t help but feel a slight sense of unease. Not because of the company, but because of the unknown. She had learned quickly that being roped into things with these three meant plans could change at any moment.

    William, ever the gracious instigator, tossed his jacket into the backseat and gestured for Rio to take the passenger seat. “Go on, Vidal. VIP seating.” Rio shot him a dry look as she slid into the seat, buckling in while William smirked to himself. She wasn’t sure if this was a show of good faith or if he just wanted to keep an eye on her and make sure she didn’t bolt. As she settled in, she felt a shift behind her, a presence filling the space directly behind her seat.

    Agatha.

    She stretched out like she owned the damn car, one leg crossing over the other, arms resting loosely at her sides. “Try not to be too much of a backseat driver, Vidal,” she teased, voice lazy, smooth.

    Rio sighed, adjusting her seatbelt. “I’m not that bad.”

    Agatha let out a short, amused laugh. “Yeah? I guess we’ll see.” On the other side, Lilia had taken her place behind William, kicking her feet up slightly before settling in. “Alright, let’s get this show on the road.” William, always ready to cause chaos, threw the car into reverse with a little too much confidence, backing out of the lot with the kind of reckless ease that made Rio instinctively grip the door handle.

    She heard Agatha chuckle behind her.

    As William pulled out onto the main road, the car settled into an easy rhythm with music playing low from the speakers, the hum of the city lights flickering past them. Rio barely had time to relax before Agatha, seated comfortably behind her, leaned forward just enough to be in her space. “So, be honest, Vidal,” Agatha mused, her voice lazy, teasing. “When’s the last time you actually went out?”

    Rio sighed, already regretting her decision to come along. “I go out.” William snorted loudly. “Uh-huh. When?”

    Rio opened her mouth, then immediately shut it.

    Lilia grinned, twisting slightly in her seat to glance at her. “Oh no. She’s thinking. That means it’s been a while.”

    “It has not been that long,” Rio muttered.

    Agatha hummed, unconvinced. “Right. Let me guess: your last wild night out was, what? A trip to the grocery store? Maybe an extra shot of espresso in your very exciting morning routine?”

    William grinned, playing along. “Maybe she even—gasp—sits in a different booth at that diner she always goes to.”

    Rio rolled her eyes, shaking her head. “You’re all hilarious.” Agatha smirked behind her. “I mean, I’m just saying—you give off serious never-leaves-the-house-unless-it’s-for-workenergy.”

    “Because I work a lot,” Rio shot back.

    “That’s not an excuse,” Agatha countered. “That’s a cry for help.” Lilia nudged Agatha. “We’re doing a public service.”

    William nodded sagely. “Bringing Detective Vidal out of retirement. The people will sing songs about our bravery.”

    Rio let out a slow, exasperated breath, but she couldn’t stop the tiny smirk pulling at the corner of her lips. It was annoying, but—admittedly—a little fun to be on the receiving end of their antics.

    Agatha leaned back in her seat, clearly satisfied. “You’re welcome, by the way.”

    “For what?”

    “For dragging you out of whatever sad little routine you’ve got going on,” Agatha said, tapping her fingers against the headrest. “By the end of the night, you might even admit you had fun.”

    Rio scoffed. “Doubtful.”

    William smirked. “Famous last words.”

    Lilia grinned. “Let’s make a bet. If Vidal has fun, she has to buy the next round.”

    Agatha smirked. “Oh, I like this.”

    Rio groaned, running a hand down her face. “This is entrapment.” William chuckled as he pulled up to a red light. “Welcome to having friends, Vidal.”

    Rio huffed, shaking her head, but something warm curled in her chest, something she wasn’t quite ready to name. Because for all their teasing, for all Agatha’s relentless pushing, it didn’t feel bad. It felt… nice. Rio then let out a long, slow sigh, rubbing at her temple like she could already feel the headache forming. The teasing had been relentless from the second she got in the car, and she knew they weren’t going to let up until she gave them something.

    So, with the kind of reluctant defeat that came from knowing she was absolutely about to regret this, she muttered, “Last time I went out for a drink was with my ex-girlfriend.”

    Silence. A rare, almost eerie silence settled over the car. Lilia, who had been mid-turn to poke at Rio again, froze, her mouth still slightly open as if her brain was still processing what she’d just heard. William, gripping the wheel with one hand, actually slowed down, like his whole body had glitched and he needed to reboot before continuing to drive.

    And Agatha, who had been stretching lazily in her seat, suddenly leaned forward, her arms draping over the back of Rio’s headrest. She narrowed her eyes slightly, smirk twitching at the corner of her lips. “Wait, hold on.” A short pause. Then, with clear, unmistakable delight, she asked, “You’re a dyke?”

    Rio let out another, sharper sigh, dragging a hand down her face. “Christ, Agatha—”

    “What?” Agatha cut in, grinning now. “I need a second to process. I mean, I figured something was off with you, but I wasn’t expecting this.” “Off with me?” Rio shot her a glare.

    “Yeah, you know,” Agatha said, waving a hand. “All broody, never talk about dating, act like work is the only thing that matters—” She tsked, shaking her head dramatically. “Classic sad lesbian behavior.” William, who had finally recovered from his shock, let out a loud, amused scoff. “Oh, this is rich, coming from you.”

    Agatha turned her attention to him, one brow raised. “What the hell is that supposed to mean?”

    William grinned. “I just think it’s funny that you’re acting so shocked when you literally went through the same thing.”

    Lilia’s head snapped toward Agatha. “Wait, you had a girlfriend?” Agatha leaned back, crossing her arms. “Yeah, but I did the dumping.” Rio turned slightly in her seat, giving her an unimpressed look. “Of course you did.”

    Agatha smirked. “What can I say? I have standards.”

    Lilia, still clearly fascinated by this revelation, narrowed her eyes. “Wait, why have we never heard about this?”

    “Because it’s not important,” Agatha said breezily, waving them off. “Unlike Vidal’s tragic little sob story.”

    Rio rolled her eyes. “It’s not a sob story.”

    “Oh, really?” Agatha said, tilting her head slightly. “You went out for a drink, had a nice little date night, and then—what? She just up and left you there?”

    Rio exhaled slowly. “Yes, Agatha.”

    Lilia looked personally offended. “And she just walked out? With no warning?”

    Rio gave a small shrug, but the memory still sat a little too heavy in her chest. “I mean, she gave a speech about how she thought I was ‘emotionally unavailable’ and ‘too focused on work.’” She made air quotes with her fingers. “Then she finished her drink, grabbed her coat, and walked out.” William let out a low whistle. “Damn. That’s cold.”

    Agatha, however, was still staring at her, that smirk still firmly in place. “So let me get this straight—” she stopped, then grinned wider. “Actually, poor choice of words.”

    Rio clenched her jaw. “I hate you.”

    Agatha ignored her, continuing with a dangerous level of amusement. “You, a self-proclaimed workaholic, finally decided to have a nice, normal night, and instead, you got unceremoniously dumped before you could even finish your second drink?”

    Rio pressed her lips into a tight line. “Yes.”

    Agatha let out a long, slow whistle. “God. That’s humiliating.” William snorted. “Really is.”

    Rio groaned, running a hand over her face. “Glad we’re all enjoying this.” “Oh, I’m thriving,” Agatha continued, her smirk fully forming now. “I mean, I just assumed you never went out because you were, I don’t know, married to the job. But no, it turns out you just have deep-seated emotional scars from your tragic gay past.”

    Rio shot her a glare. “Agatha.”

    Agatha beamed. “You should’ve told us sooner. We could’ve been more sensitive about it.”

    William and Lilia both nodded in mock sympathy.

    Lilia placed a dramatic hand over Rio’s shoulder. “I’m so sorry for your loss.”

    William exhaled deeply, like he was grieving for her. “Rest in peace to Rio’s one attempt at having a social life.”

    Rio groaned. “I hate all of you.”

    Agatha, still reveling in the moment, tapped her fingers against the seat. “I mean, getting dumped at a bar? That’s, like amateur lesbian heartbreak. What’s next? You gonna tell us you made a whole sad playlist about it?” Rio turned to stare out the window, lips pressing into a firm line. The silence stretched for a second too long.

    William’s eyes widened. “Oh my god.”

    Agatha barked out a laugh, full and delighted. “Holy shit.” Rio slumped in her seat, muttering under her breath. “I don’t know why I tell you people things.”

    Lilia, glowing with excitement, twisted around. “Okay, but real talk— what’s on it? Like, did you go full sad indie girl? Or is it, like, dramatic breakup anthems?”

    “I bet it’s melodramatic as fuck,” William said confidently. “Oh, absolutely,” Agatha agreed. “I’m thinking Florence + The Machine, a little Phoebe Bridgers, probably some extremely emotional Mitski.” She leaned in, her voice dripping with satisfaction. “Am I warm?” Rio refused to answer. Which was, of course, an answer.

    William gasped, gripping Rio’s arm. “I knew you had taste.” Agatha smirked. “What’s the playlist called, Vidal? Lesbian Tragedy Hours?”

    Rio shut her eyes, willing herself to survive this car ride. “I’m leaving.” “Oh no, no, no,” Agatha said, still grinning. “We are absolutely keeping this conversation going.”

    William, still grinning like a devil in disguise, drummed his fingers against the steering wheel. “Well, in that case—” he said, voice thick with amusement, “your little lesbian heart is gonna love this bar.” Rio barely had time to process what that meant before William took a sharp turn, the streetlights casting long streaks of gold across the hood of the car. The roads were quieter here, the kind that only had one or two cars parked along the curb, the sidewalks mostly empty—except for the distant hum of voices.

    Then the glow hit her. Neon pink, bold, buzzing just slightly at the edges. The Strap On.

    The words were unmistakable, mounted above the bar’s entrance in huge, blocky, unapologetic letters. A white neon outline framed the sign, making the words stand out against the darkened brick façade of the building. The entire sidewalkin front of the place was bathed in the sign’s rosy glow, giving everything an almost dreamlike, absurdly lesbian aesthetic. Rio blinked. Then blinked again. Her jaw went slack as the reality of what she was looking at fully set in.

    Slowly—very slowly—she turned her head toward William, staring. William, to his credit, looked pleased as hell with himself as he threw the car into park, barely holding back laughter.

    “William,” Rio said, voice deadpan.

    “What?” he asked, entirely too innocent.

    Rio shook her head, still stunned. “You did not just bring me to a bar called The Strap On.”

    William unbuckled his seatbelt with the air of a man who had zero regrets. “I absolutely did.”

    Rio inhaled sharply through her nose, fingers tightening on her seatbelt. “You are a fucking menace.”

    William shrugged, entirely unbothered, as he grabbed his jacket from the backseat. He gestured toward the glowing sign. “It’s fitting.” Rio groaned, tilting her head back against the headrest. “I hate you.” “Oh, come on, Vidal,” Agatha finally chimed in, voice smooth and taunting, as she leaned forward again, her arms draped over Rio’s headrest. “Don’t be a coward.”

    Rio turned her head slightly, shooting her a pointed glare. Agatha, of course, smirked wider.

    Agatha shrugged, grinning. “Every dyke has their place of reckoning.” She nodded toward the bar. “This is yours.”

    Rio squeezed her eyes shut for exactly three seconds before taking a deep, bracing breath.

    William reached over, tapped her on the back, and said, far too smugly, “You could stay in the car.”

    Rio clenched her jaw.

    She hated them. She hated all of them.

    But then she glanced up at the bar again—at the way the neon light hummed against the brick, at the faint sound of music spilling out from behind the heavy wooden doors, at the scattered groups of women and men standing outside, chatting, laughing, existing in a way that felt strangely inviting.

    She turned back to Agatha who was watching her closely, expression unreadable beneath the amusement.

    Rio could feel the challenge, the dare, the weight of expectation hanging in the air.

    And fuck her, because it worked.

    Rio exhaled sharply, then shoved her door open, stepping out onto the pavement.

    William cheered, triumphant. “That’s the spirit!”

    Agatha was the last to step out, taking her time, watching. Rio felt her watching.
