    She shoved her hands in her jacket pockets and muttered, “I swear to God, if you say one more word—”

    Agatha grinned, adjusting the collar of her coat. “Oh no, no, I wouldn’t dare.”

    Rio narrowed her eyes at her.

    Agatha held up her hands in mock innocence.

    The second they stepped inside, Rio realized The Strap On wasn’t some over-the-top, neon-drenched, chaotic lesbian nightclub like she had feared. It was, for all intents and purposes, just a bar.

    Dim lighting, old wooden floors that had seen their fair share of spilled drinks and stomping boots, booths lining the walls, and a long, well-stocked bar stretching across the back. A pool table sat near the entrance, where two women were lining up shots, laughter bubbling between them. The TVs mounted above the bar were playing a rerun of a baseball game, but nobody seemed all that interested.

    It smelled like whiskey, cheap cologne, and the faintest hint of something fried. Classic.

    There were the usual barflies hunched over their drinks, but mixed in were couples sitting close, a woman casually running a hand down her date’s arm as they talked. A guy in a well-worn leather jacket was leaning against the bar, chatting with another man in flannel, their conversation just a little too close to be purely friendly. A group at one of the high-top tables erupted into laughter, one of them slapping another on the back, eyes lingering. It wasn’t in your face, but it was comfortable like a place where people weren’t watching themselves, where no one was trying to tone anything down.

    And as much as she hated to admit it…

    Rio kind of got it.

    William, still leading the way, found them a booth in the back. The seating arrangement happened naturally—Rio slid in first, taking the seat near the wall, always instinctively defensive. William dropped in beside her, stretching out like he owned the damn place. Across from them, Agatha took the seat directly in front of Rio, her usual smirk in place, and Lilia settled beside her, already amused by the entire situation. Rio exhaled, eyeing the place again. “Well,” she muttered, “it’s… normal.” William snorted. “Yeah, what were you expecting? A giant lesbian wonderland?”

    “I don’t know,” Rio admitted. “With a name like The Strap On, I was expecting—”

    “More vibrators?” Agatha cut in smoothly.

    Rio glared.

    Lilia, shaking her head, leaned back comfortably. “Relax, Vidal. It’s just a bar.”

    Rio sighed, rolling her shoulders. “I am relaxed.”

    William, without looking up from the menu, reached over and poked her shoulder. “No, you’re rigid as hell.”

    Rio turned to glare at him, but Lilia chuckled. “I’m just glad you agreed to come.”

    William nodded. “Yeah, it was touch and go for a second there. Thought we’d have to physically drag you.” Rio sighed, rubbing a hand over her face. “I hate that you all think I’m some kind of hermit.” Agatha hummed, tapping her nails against the table. “To be fair, you give off ‘lives alone, never socializes, listens to music in the dark’ energy.” Rio frowned. “I do not.”

    William and Lilia exchanged a look.

    Agatha, unbothered, continued, “You literally just admitted that the last time you went out, your ex dumped you at the bar.”

    Rio clenched her jaw. “We’re still talking about this?”

    Agatha smirked. “Oh, we’ll be talking about this forever.” William grinned. “Oh, yeah, we’re never letting this go.” Lilia laughed, waving for the waitress. “Let’s get this over with.” The waitress arrived with a practiced ease, pen tucked behind her ear, looking effortlessly at home in the dim glow of the bar. She was lean, with a confident stance, dark brown curls pulled into a loose ponytail, and a small silver hoop through her eyebrow. She had the kind of presence that made it obvious she’d been working bars for a while—quick with a smile, slower with her attention, as if she only gave it when she really wanted to. “Alright, what are we having?” she asked, glancing at the group. William, ever the enthusiast, went first. “Whiskey sour.” Lilia hummed, tapping her nails on the table. “I’ll do a Manhattan.” Rio barely had time to glance at the menu before Agatha, without hesitation, smirked and said, “Just a beer. Surprise me.” The waitress returned the smirk, tipping her chin up slightly, and Rio caught the moment right there—the kind of unspoken exchange that didn’t require words, just a look, a flicker of mutual understanding. It was subtle, effortless, but Rio recognized it immediately.

    It wasn’t obvious, not deliberate; just a little longer than necessary, a little too knowing. The kind of glance Agatha wouldn’t give just anyone. Rio exhaled slowly, eyes flickering between them. She told herself it was just an observation, that this was the kind of thing Agatha probably did all the time, probably without even thinking. It was just flirting. Simple, careless, like tossing a coin into a fountain and forgetting about it. Still, Rio felt something cold twist in her stomach. Something unexpected. Something stupid.

    She shouldn’t care. She didn’t. Why would she? But for half a second—half a goddamn second—she wished that look had been directed at her instead. And that was the part she immediately shoved down, down, down, locking it away before it could turn into something real.

    She straightened slightly, focusing on the safe thing, the logical thing; her order. “Gin and tonic.”

    Agatha turned her head slightly at that, a slow smirk playing at the edges of her lips. “Of course you did.”

    Rio shot her a glare. “What’s that supposed to mean?”

    Agatha just chuckled under her breath, looking far too amused, but before she could say anything else, the waitress was already jotting down the order and turning away.

    And just like that, the tension shifted, the moment dissolving as conversation picked up again.

    “Alright, since we’ve got Vidal out of her cave for once,” William said, stretching his arms across the back of the booth, “I think we should talk about things other than work.”

    “Agreed,” Lilia said, sipping her water. “Something normal. Something social.”

    Rio snorted. “You realize we’re cops, right? This is already the most social we get.”

    William waved her off. “No, no, real life stuff. Like—” He turned toward Lilia. “You first. How’d you meet your husband?”

    Lilia chuckled, shaking her head. “You ask me this every time we drink.” William grinned. “And it’s still a banger of a story.”

    She sighed but smiled anyway. “Fine. I met him in a bowling alley when I was twenty-three. I was absolute shit at it, gutter-balling like crazy, and he, for some reason, found that attractive.”

    William nodded firmly. “It’s called humility. Men love that.” Agatha scoffed. “That’s bullshit. He probably just liked your ass.” Lilia didn’t even hesitate. “Oh, for sure. But he was gentlemanly about it.” As they all laughed, Rio found herself watching Agatha more than listening. It wasn’t on purpose. It wasn’t something she meant to do. But the way Agatha moved, the way she talked so effortlessly, drew attention. It wasn’t just charm, wasn’t just her usual cocky smirks and dry humor. It was something else.

    Agatha was a part of this, in a way that Rio still wasn’t. She flowed through these conversations like it was second nature, never hesitating, never second-guessing. And for the first time, Rio wondered if that ease came naturally, or if it was something learned. Because Agatha never let the conversation get too personal. She shared things, sure—stories, jokes, observations—but never anything that cut too deep.

    She had mastered the art of talking without giving anything away. Rio noticed. She also noticed the way Agatha never really looked at her when she spoke. Not directly. Not like she had looked at the waitress. Rio swallowed, shifting slightly. It was nothing, she was just overthinking things.

    Somewhere between the drinks arriving and the next round of conversation, Rio found herself growing warm.

    It wasn’t just the bar with the dim, crowded space, the subtle press of bodies moving through the room, the low hum of conversation blending into the music. It was the booth, the closeness of it, the way William’s arm rested along the back of the seat beside her, the way Agatha sat across from her with that perpetual smirk, the way Lilia leaned in every so often, tapping the side of her glass to emphasize a point.

    The fabric of her blazer felt too stiff, too much. Without really thinking about it, she shrugged it off, sliding it down her arms and draping it across the seat beside her. The cool air against her skin was a relief, though the heat lingering from the drinks made her pulse feel just a little slower, her limbs a little looser.

    Still, something about the way the sleeves of her white button-up bunched at her wrists felt wrong, like she was still too put together, still too formal for a place like this. She reached for the cuffs, rolling them up with slow, practiced movements, pushing them past her forearms, letting the fabric settle in relaxed folds around her elbows.

    The stretch felt good, the kind of easy movement that came after too many hours hunched over a desk, after too much tension in her spine. She rolled her shoulders slightly, tilting her head just enough to release the tightness at the base of her neck before she reached up, fingers combing absently through her hair. It had been down all day, strands shifting over her collar, over her shoulders, a little too messy, a little too in the way. Without thinking, she twisted it up into a loose bun, fingers deftly gathering it, securing it with a simple tie. The motion was second nature, something she had done a thousand times before, in front of a mirror, in the quiet of her own space, in the middle of long, exhausting nights at the precinct. And across the table, Agatha noticed.

    Rio wasn’t watching her, not at first. She was still focused on smoothing down the collar of her shirt, adjusting the fit of the sleeves, but something— some small shift in the air, some pause that shouldn’t have been there— made her glance up.

    Agatha’s beer was half-raised, hovering just below her lips. She wasn’t looking at anyone else, wasn’t focused on the conversation happening around them. She was watching her, eyes sharp, expression unreadable. It wasn’t like the glances she had given the waitress earlier because those had been deliberate, easy, laced with that casual charm she wore like a second skin.

    There was no smirk, no teasing remark already waiting on her tongue. Just a flicker of something that passed too quickly for Rio to catch before Agatha tilted her head back, bringing the glass to her lips in one slow, unhurried sip.

    She didn’t say anything. Didn’t comment.

    But her eyes had lingered—long enough for Rio to notice.

    Just long enough to make something in her chest pull tight before she forced herself to look away. It was stupid. A stupid moment, a stupid thing to notice, a stupid way for something like that to matter. Rio, who had spent so much of her life ignoring complicated things, keeping her emotions neatly folded into places where they couldn’t be touched—found that she didn’t like that at all.

    She focused on William instead, on his loud voice, his exaggerated gestures, on the way he launched into yet another ridiculous story that had Lilia shaking her head, half-amused, half-exasperated.

    Agatha, for her part, didn’t react.

    She just took another sip of her drink, let the conversation flow around her, let her gaze drift away like she hadn’t just seen something she wasn’t supposed to.

    Rio exhaled, shifting in her seat, forcing herself to listen, to be present, to pretend that whatever had just happened hadn’t happened at all. Because whatever that was, she wasn’t going to keep thinking about it. William slammed back the rest of his drink, setting the empty glass down with a satisfied thud before stretching with exaggerated effort. “Alright, I gotta pee.” He turned to Lilia, eyebrows raised expectantly. “C’mon, you’re coming with me.”

    Lilia barely looked up from her glass. “Absolutely not.”

    “Come on,” William groaned, dramatically pressing a hand over his heart. “You know I hate going alone. It’s weird.”

    “It’s a bathroom, not a haunted house,” Lilia replied dryly. “Debatable,” William shot back. “Men’s bathrooms are terrifying. It’s like stepping into a war zone.”

    Agatha smirked, amused. “Isn’t that just a you problem?”

    William huffed. “No. It’s an everyone problem.” Then, switching tactics, he pouted—an expression that looked absolutely ridiculous. “Lilia. My dearest friend. My guiding light. My—”

    “Jesus Christ,” Lilia muttered, rolling her eyes before finally standing. “Fine. If only to shut you up.”

    William beamed, victorious, and shot a wink at Rio and Agatha before heading toward the back of the bar, Lilia following behind him, shaking her head the entire way.

    And just like that, they were alone. For the first time that night, the booth felt smaller, the space between them shrinking in an instant. Agatha, never one for silence, never one for being unsettled, leaned back against the seat, arms crossed, gaze flickering around the room as if she was suddenly very interested in the décor.

    Rio, still nursing her drink, tilted her head slightly. “What, no snarky comment?”

    Agatha scoffed but didn’t immediately rise to the bait. Instead, she tapped her fingers lightly against the table, eyes still trained elsewhere. “Figured I’d give you a break. I’ve been so generous tonight.”

    Rio smirked, resting her elbow against the table, chin propped on her hand. “Oh? Is that what you’re calling it?”

    Agatha hummed, fingers still idly tapping, before finally gritting her teeth and sighing. “Alright,” she muttered, like she hated herself for it, “let’s make conversation.”

    Rio arched an eyebrow. “Did that physically pain you?”

    “A little,” Agatha admitted, finally meeting her gaze. She held up a hand in mock defense. “Just… don’t make it weird.”

    Rio smirked, leaning slightly forward. “No promises.”

    Agatha rolled her eyes, but the corner of her mouth ticked up, just slightly. For a second, the silence stretched, neither of them quite sure where to begin.

    Rio took the easy route. “So,” she started, swirling the last bit of her gin and tonic, “about the case—”

    Agatha immediately groaned, tipping her head back against the seat. “Oh my God,” she muttered, shaking her head. “Vidal, are you seriously trying to talk about work right now?”

    Rio blinked. “We are detectives.”

    “And we are off the clock.” Agatha gestured around them, eyes wide with mock disbelief. “This is a bar, not a goddamn precinct.”

    Rio exhaled through her nose, shaking her head. “I don’t know, you’re here. Feels pretty close.”

    Agatha let out a sharp laugh, a little unguarded, before shaking her head. “Cute.”

    Rio tilted her head. “Did you just compliment me?”

    Agatha immediately frowned. “No. Shut up.”

    Rio chuckled, taking a sip of her drink. “Relax, Harkness. I won’t tell anyone.”

    Agatha snorted, reaching for her beer. “I swear, you spend so much time wrapped up in work, you wouldn’t know how to relax if someone paid you.”

    Rio raised an eyebrow. “And you would?”

    Agatha took a slow sip, considering. “I think I’d be better at it than you.” Rio leaned forward slightly, challenging. “Prove it.”

    Agatha’s lips parted slightly, as if she was going to retort, but she hesitated. That was when she really looked at Rio. Not in the usual way, not in the casual, teasing, I’m-just-fucking-with-you way she always did. She let herself focus.

    Maybe it was the beer already softening the edges of things. Maybe it was the fact that it had been a long time—too long—since she’d sat across from a woman she actually noticed. Maybe it was the way Rio had finally shed that stupid blazer, finally let herself breathe, let her sleeves get messy, let her hair be loose and untamed before pulling it back.

    Whatever it was, it made something tighten in her chest, something slow and unwelcome.

    Because Rio had nice eyes. She always had, but Agatha had never let herself think about it.

    Brown—deep, dark, sharp, watchful. The kind of brown that caught the light in places, like polished wood, like something solid and real. The kind of eyes that held things back, the kind that didn’t just let you in—they measured you, weighed you, made you prove you were worth knowing. Agatha, who had spent her whole life never proving anything to anyone; felt herself holding her breath.

    Rio must have felt it too, because she suddenly wasn’t smirking anymore. For once, she wasn’t making a smartass remark, wasn’t poking at Agatha the way she always did.

    She was just… looking.

    Agatha felt herself hesitate.

    Just for a second, long enough to know she should stop.

    So she did.

    She blinked, scoffed lightly, and leaned back, like the moment had never happened.

    “Christ,” she muttered, shaking her head. “I need another beer.” Rio’s eyebrows lifted slightly, but she didn’t call her on it. Not yet. Instead, she just leaned back again, swirling the ice in her glass, watching as Agatha brushed it off like it hadn’t just been the first real moment of sincerity they’d had all night.

    Rio smirked, but before she could say anything else, Agatha leaned forward slightly, resting her elbow on the table, tapping her fingers against the wood as if considering her next words. Then, finally, she exhaled, shifting her jaw slightly before saying, “Alright. Normal conversation. I’ll start.” There was something different in her tone. Instead, her gaze was fixed on the condensation gathering around her beer glass, tracing lazy circles through the droplets with her fingertip. “Not gonna lie,” she muttered, voice quieter than before, “being around so many careless people—” she flicked a glance toward William, toward the other bar patrons, before shrugging, “—people like him… It still makes me nervous.”

    Rio blinked, caught a little off guard by the admission. She hadn’t expected this when Agatha offered to make normal conversation.

    Agatha let out a small, humorless laugh, shaking her head. “It’s stupid, right? I mean, I’m an adult. I should be past this shit. But…” She trailed off, finally reaching for her beer and taking a slow sip, like she was buying herself time.

When Agatha finally spoke again, she still didn’t look up. “My mother— she was…” She let out another breath, pressing her lips together. “Let’s just say she didn’t exactly send me off to Pride parades growing up.” Rio didn’t respond immediately. She knew if she spoke too soon, Agatha might shut down completely, might try to turn it into a joke and push past it before it got too real.

    Agatha was trying, and Rio wasn’t about to ruin it. Instead, she let the words settle between them, giving them space.

    Agatha’s fingers curled around her bottle, her nails tapping against it as she finally, finally lifted her eyes again. “It’s funny, though, right?” She gestured vaguely to herself with her free hand. “Like, look at me.” Rio did.

    Boots that looked worn-in but sturdy, black and scuffed at the soles. A dark flannel rolled at the sleeves, layered over a plain white T-shirt, collar just a little stretched from wear. The easy way she sat, legs spread just enough to own her space, the weight of her presence settling into every inch of the booth.

    She had always been this way.

    Rio could almost see it, a younger version of Agatha, all sharp edges and defiant posture, standing in a hallway with her arms crossed as her mother’s voice rang in her ears. A kid who hadn’t even needed to come out because it had already been written into everything she was.

    Agatha scoffed, shaking her head. “I mean, come on. The flannel? The boots? The attitude?” She smirked, but it was small, weak at the edges. “I might as well have had ‘raging dyke’ stitched onto my fucking forehead.” Rio let out a short exhale, something between a laugh and a sigh, but there was no humor in it.

    Because she understood, more than she wanted to admit.

    For a moment, neither of them spoke.

    The background noise of the bar buzzed around them, distant and unimportant, the world moving on without them while they sat suspended in something fragile—something that felt like it could tip either way. Rio’s voice was quiet when she finally said, “I understand.” Agatha looked at her then, really looked at her, as if she was searching for something beneath the words.     Rio hesitated, the words forming on her tongue before she even realized she was speaking.

    “Agatha, I—”

    But before she could say anything, the moment was shattered. William came barreling back toward the booth, Lilia following behind with a deeply unimpressed look on her face. “Okay,” William announced dramatically, sliding into the booth beside Rio, “you would not believe what just happened.”

    Agatha exhaled sharply, running a hand over her face like she could physically wipe away whatever fleeting thing had just passed between them.

    Lilia dropped down beside her, shaking her head. “It was nothing.” William ignored her completely, eyes wide, voice conspiratorial. “So I’m at the urinal, right?”

    Rio sighed.

    “No, listen.” William leaned in, lowering his voice like what he was about to say was some grand revelation. “Some guy walks in, stands way too close, and then—” he made an exaggerated gesture, “—he starts talking to me.”

    Lilia scoffed. “Yeah, because you started the conversation.” William waved her off. “That’s not the point. The point is, there is a universal agreement that you do not strike up casual conversation at the urinal. There are rules, okay?”

    Agatha chuckled under her breath, shaking her head.

    Rio barely registered what was being said. Because while William rambled on, she felt it.

    A touch—light, barely there, but impossible to ignore.

    Agatha’s foot, pressing against hers under the table.

    It was small, the kind of thing that could have easily been an accident, but it wasn’t—not when she didn’t move away, not when she left it there, the warmth of it anchoring them in place. Rio tried to keep her expression neutral, tried not to give herself away, but her grip on her glass tightened just slightly, her pulse kicking up in her throat.

    Across the table, Agatha wasn’t looking at her. But that meant nothing. Because Rio knew Agatha was very aware of what she was doing. Lilia groaned, taking a sip of her drink. “William, I swear, every time I go somewhere with you, I end up questioning why I still associate with you.” William smirked. “Because I make your life more interesting.” Agatha let out a quiet laugh, but Rio didn’t hear it. All she could focus on was the steady, unwavering pressure beneath the table.

    She realized that for the first time, Agatha wasn’t pushing her away.

    Chapter 4: Things We Carry

    Chapter Notes butch agatha harkness.

    that is all.

    They should’ve been heading home by now. It had been a long day, most of it spent staring at case files, chasing leads that dried up before they even started, and waiting on lab results that never seemed to come. They’d been at the precinct for nearly twelve hours straight, both of them running on coffee and whatever stubborn determination kept detectives from admitting when they were burned out.

    Rio had been two minutes away from calling it a night when Lilia had walked over with a fresh lead; Lisa Moreno’s ex-boyfriend. He had a history of violence, and rumor had it the last time he saw Lisa, it hadn’t been on good terms. So instead of going home, they were standing in a freezing parking lot outside a shitty motel, the night air biting against their skin.

    The wind cut through her, sharp and unforgiving, the kind of late-November cold that settled in your bones and made your fingers ache. The motel was one of those cheap, roadside places with a flickering neon sign advertising ‘Low Weekly Rates’ and doors that opened directly to the lot, paint chipping at the edges. The pavement below them was littered with crushed cigarette butts and oil stains, and a busted vending machine stood abandoned in the corner. It smelled like gas station coffee, and the kind of regret that settled into places like this.

    Now, Rio and Agatha stood in front of Room 207.

    Rio looked put-together, professional. Her grey wool coat was buttoned neatly, every part of her posture straight, composed, unreadable. Even with the cold biting at her exposed fingers, she didn’t fidget. Didn’t shiver. Didn’t let it show. She was the picture of control; calm, deliberate, every detail of her appearance carefully placed. It wasn’t vanity, it was habit. Muscle memory from years of training, from the need to be taken seriously, to command authority without having to raise her voice.

    Agatha, on the other hand, was all sharp edges and barely concealed impatience.

    She stood loose but firm, like she was always braced for a fight that hadn’t started yet. While Rio’s layers were neatly pressed, Agatha’s Carhartt jacket was worn-in, faded in spots, the sleeves just slightly frayed at the cuffs. The kind of coat that had seen more than a few winters, more than a few long nights standing in the cold, waiting for something to break. Her old, black combat boots were scuffed at the toes, laces double-knotted like she didn’t trust them to hold on their own.Her jeans were faded, creased from too many hours spent sitting in a squad car, the fabric rough with age. Nothing about her looked particularly polished—but she wore it like armor.

    Rio looked like she belonged in an office, on a task force, in a boardroom briefing.

    Agatha looked like she belonged on the street, tracking something down, pressing someone into a wall to make them talk. Two very different kinds of authority.

    And yet, they stood there together, side by side, waiting in front of a shitty motel door for a man neither of them wanted to see. Rio shifted her weight slightly, rolling her shoulders against the cold. She flicked her gaze toward Agatha, who stood perfectly still, unaffected, eyes locked on the door like she could already hear the bullshit that was about to come from the guy inside.

    “Everything okay?” Rio asked, her voice low.

    Agatha tilted her head just slightly, smirking like she already knew where this was going. “You asking ‘cause you’re worried about me? Or ‘cause you think I’m about to put this guy through a wall?”

    Rio exhaled, shaking her head. “Both seem equally likely.” Agatha let out a slow breath, rolling her shoulders, cracking her neck. It was a small tell—one Rio had started recognizing. Like she was coiling up, getting ready for a hit that hadn’t come yet. “I’ll take the lead,” Agatha said, voice firm, no room for argument.

    Rio raised an eyebrow, smirking faintly. “Didn’t know we were calling dibs now.”

    Agatha shrugged, hands slipping into her jacket pockets. “I just don’t trust this guy to not be a piece of shit. And between you and me?” She tilted her head slightly, watching Rio out of the corner of her eye. “I don’t like the idea of him running his mouth at you.”

    Agatha then exhaled sharply, like she was already regretting saying more than she meant to. She dragged a hand over her jaw, eyes flicking toward the door, then back to Rio. “Look, I don’t know. I feel a little protective over you.”

    She said it like it didn’t mean much. Like she was just stating a fact. But Rio heard the slight hesitation in her voice, the way her fingers curled in her jacket pocket like she was bracing for something.

    Before Rio could say anything, Agatha scoffed, rolling her shoulders. “Guys like this?” She nodded toward the door. “They respond to roughness. Not niceties. Not the kind of courtesy you’d give him.” Her voice was a little sharper now, like she needed to clarify, like she needed to make sure Rio didn’t read too much into it. “That’s what I mean.”

    Rio felt a small warmth creep into her chest anyway. She knew Agatha was covering, trying to backpedal in real time. But that didn’t erase what she had said.

    Rio smirked, but her voice was softer than usual. “That so?” Agatha rolled her eyes immediately. “Don’t make it a thing, Vidal.” Before either of them could sit in it too long, Agatha lifted her fist and knocked. Loud. Deliberate. No hesitation.

His eyes were bloodshot, and the smell of cheap beer and stale cigarettes drifted off him like a second skin. His gaze flicked between them before lingering on Rio. Then, that smirk widened, turning into something sleazier.

    “We know you beat her,” she continued, low and slow, like she wanted him to feel every syllable. “We know exactly what kind of fucking coward you are. The kind that hides behind locked doors and weak excuses, the kind that puts his hands on women because he’s got nothing else going for him.” Brandon’s jaw ticked, nostrils flaring.

    Agatha just smiled.

    “And I promise you, you sack of shit,” she whispered, voice cutting like a blade, “I am going to put you in a cell and watch you rot.” And then, as if nothing happened, she released him.

    Brandon stumbled back, coughing, adjusting his shirt like it suddenly fit too tight.

    Agatha didn’t move, didn’t give him the fucking space to breathe. Her voice dropped even lower, her words slow, final. “You open your mouth again, I’ll make sure you don’t walk out of here on your own two feet.”

    Rio’s pulse was in her throat, her chest tight. Because she had never seen Agatha like that before. Not this ruthless. Not this protective. Agatha finally exhaled, rolling her shoulders like she was shaking the tension off. And then, as casually as ever, she turned to Rio. “You got any more questions, Vidal?” Like she hadn’t just nearly snapped a man in half.

    Rio swallowed down the tension in her throat, forcing her pulse to steady as she looked at Agatha. The room was still thick with the charge of what had just happened—Brandon against the wall, Agatha’s hand at his throat, the unshakable promise in her voice that she was going to put him away. Rio exhaled slowly, keeping her voice steady. “No. I think we’re done here.”

    She started toward the door, ready to shake off the thick, stale air of the motel room, but Agatha didn’t move. Her boots scuffed against the cheap carpet as she lingered just a second longer, eyes locked on Brandon, expression unreadable.

    Her voice was quiet when she spoke, but there was nothing soft about it.“We’ll be back for you.”

    Brandon’s smirk twitched, just slightly. He didn’t say anything, and maybe for the first time all night, he was smart enough not to. Agatha didn’t wait for a response, she just reached for the door, grabbed the handle, and slammed it shut behind them.

    The night air was brutal. Cold enough that Rio felt it seep straight through her coat, biting at her skin, but Agatha didn’t seem to notice. She walked ahead, silent, shoulders still tight with barely restrained tension. The only sound between them was the crunch of gravel beneath their feet, the distant hum of the highway stretching out beyond the parking lot. Rio watched her from the corner of her eye, noticing the way her hands remained curled into fists, like she hadn’t quite let go of the moment in that motel room.

    The neon glow of the motel flickered weakly behind them, casting long, uneven shadows across the lot. A broken streetlamp buzzed overhead, blinking out every few seconds, throwing them in and out of darkness. Finally, Agatha exhaled sharply, her breath curling in the frigid air. “Fucking asshole.” Her voice was clipped, low, barely contained. Rio nodded. “Yeah.”

    The agreement was simple, but it hung heavy between them. Because this wasn’t just about Brandon. It wasn’t just about Lisa, either. This was about something deeper. Something that had burrowed into Agatha’s bones.

    They didn’t speak for a while. Just walked, shoes hitting pavement, moving toward the only open place nearby—a shitty roadside diner glowing in the dark like an afterthought.

    Agatha was still too tense, too silent. Her jaw worked like she wanted to say something but couldn’t, like she wasn’t ready for whatever was clawing at her ribs. Rio bumped her shoulder, just lightly, enough to shake her out of it. “Come on,” she murmured, nodding her head towards the diner attached to the motel. “Coffee first. Then you can break something.” Agatha scoffed, a short breath through her nose that was not quite a laugh, but close enough. She didn’t answer, just shook her head, rolled her shoulders, and followed.

    The diner was the kind of place that didn’t pretend to be anything more than what it was; a late-night pit stop for people who didn’t want to go home yet. It smelled like burnt coffee and fryer grease, the kind of scent that had settled into the cracked linoleum floors and wouldn’t leave no matter how many times they mopped. The booths were covered in faded red vinyl, torn in places where the stuffing poked through like old scars. The lighting was dim, a sickly yellow that made everything feel just a little off, and the neon OPEN sign buzzed faintly from the window, barely holding on. Rio and Agatha stepped inside without speaking, the warmth of the diner wrapping around them, thick and heavy compared to the biting cold outside. It should’ve been comforting, but it wasn’t. Not when Agatha still carried that tension in her shoulders, not when the weight of the interview still hung between them.

    The waitress didn’t bother greeting them. She barely looked up from behind the counter, just grabbed two ceramic mugs and filled them with coffee as if she could smell the exhaustion on them. Rio muttered a quiet thanks, but Agatha didn’t say anything, just slid into the booth without looking up. Rio followed, dropping into the seat across from her. The cushions were stiff, barely giving under her weight, and she let out a slow breath, trying to shake off the cold still clinging to her. She stretched out her legs beneath the table, and in the process, her knee brushed against Agatha’s. It was barely a touch, but neither of them moved away.

    Agatha had shed her Carhartt jacket when she sat down, the worn fabric now bunched up beside her. Her long sleeves were pushed up just slightly, revealing the sharp angles of her forearms. She reached for the steaming mug in front of her, but instead of drinking, she just stirred. The spoon clinked against the ceramic in slow, absentminded circles, over and over, like she needed something to do with her hands.

    Rio watched her, taking a quiet sip from her own cup. The coffee was too hot, burning all the way down, but she welcomed it. Anything to keep herself grounded.

    Agatha, on the other hand, hadn’t touched hers.

    Her fingers tapped against the handle of the mug, restless, like she couldn’t quite sit still. Her shoulders remained taut, her gaze distant, fixed on something that wasn’t there. She wasn’t just thinking about Brandon anymore. Rio could see it—the way something else had dug into her ribs, something raw and familiar, something she didn’t want to acknowledge. So, Rio kept her voice steady, casual. “We’ll pull his records again. See if anything else sticks.”

    Agatha nodded absently, barely reacting. “Yeah.”

    “We’ll talk to the front desk too,” Rio continued, watching her carefully. “Confirm his alibi.”

    “Yeah,” Agatha said again, her voice flat, detached.

    It wasn’t really a conversation. The words were just placeholders, like empty syllables stretching out in a conversation neither of them were invested in. Rio could hear it in Agatha’s tone, in the way she wasn’t really answering, just acknowledging noise. Another tap of her fingers against the ceramic. Another absent scrape of the spoon.

    The diner’s hum filled the space between them, the low murmur of an old radio behind the counter, the occasional clatter of plates in the back. The waitress had long since lost interest in them, moving through the motions of her shift like nothing outside of this place existed.

    Then, finally—Agatha spoke. Her voice was quiet, but sharp at the edges, like it had been chipped down to something raw.“I’m so fucking tired of this.”

    Rio set her cup down, not taking her eyes off her. “Tired of what?” Agatha exhaled slowly, but it didn’t sound like relief. It sounded like she was forcing something out, making herself say it before she changed her mind.

    “Of having to see this. Over and over again. Men like him. Women like Lisa.” She swallowed, staring into her untouched coffee, her grip tightening around the mug. “I’m tired of watching it happen. I’m tired of seeing them get away with it. I’m tired of pretending it doesn’t piss me off every goddamn time.”

    Agatha’s jaw worked, something unreadable flickering behind her expression, but she didn’t stop.

    “I’ve been doing this a long time, Vidal. I’ve seen more women beaten and discarded than I can count. Some of them make it out. Some don’t. And the ones that do?” She scoffed, shaking her head. “Half the time, they get pulled right back in. And guys like Brandon? They know it. They count on it.”

    She finally looked up, her blue eyes flashing with something sharp, something personal.

    “They count on women being too scared to run. On no one giving a shit. On cops deciding it’s just a ‘domestic dispute’ and closing the fucking file.” Her voice dipped lower, like she was biting back something worse. “And we’re supposed to sit here and act like we’re doing enough? That we’re keeping them safe?”

    Her hands shook slightly, her fingers gripping the cup too tightly. She exhaled sharply, shoving the mug aside.

    Rio studied her, taking in the way her shoulders still hadn’t relaxed, the way her fingers twitched like she was two seconds from breaking something just to let it out.

    Carefully, Rio spoke. “You knew someone like that.”

    Agatha’s gaze flicked up, sharp and quick, like a trigger had been pulled. For a second, she looked like she might deny it. Like she might shake her head and tell Rio to shut the fuck up and drop it. But then, her lips pressed into a thin line, her shoulders dropped—slightly—and finally, she exhaled through her nose, quiet but heavy, and ran a hand through her hair, her fingers digging in at the roots like she was trying to ground herself. “My dad,” she said, voice clipped, like the name itself was an old wound she hadn’t touched in years.

    Agatha let out a dry, humorless chuckle, shaking her head. “Not that he was ever much of a dad. Just some piece of shit who thought his fists were the answer to everything.” She picked at the seam of her sleeve, something distracted in the motion. “He’d drink. He’d get pissed about whatever the hell it was that day, and then he’d take it out on my mother.” Her fingers stilled against the table, her gaze fixed on something distant, something that wasn’t there. “She never fought back,” Agatha murmured, quieter now. “Never left. Just kept telling me he wasn’t really like that. That he was just stressed. That he loved us.” She scoffed, the sound sharp and bitter. “Like that was supposed to mean anything.”

    Rio’s chest tightened, and she didn’t push or press for more. She just sat with it, let Agatha speak at her own pace.

    Agatha’s knee pressed against hers under the table again, a slow shift of weight—not deliberate this time, not controlled. Like she hadn’t noticed she was doing it.

    She exhaled, rubbing a thumb against the inside of her palm, like the words left a physical ache. “I used to think she’d leave eventually,” she admitted, voice edged with something close to regret. “That one day, she’d wake up and realize she didn’t have to stay.” Her lips pressed together, the muscle in her jaw twitching before she shook her head. “But she never did.” A beat of silence. Rio swallowed. “What happened?”

    Agatha finally looked at her, her blue eyes flat, unreadable. “He drank himself to death when I was eighteen,” she said simply. “So, I guess that fixed it.”

    Rio didn’t miss the way her voice dipped at the end, like it was supposed to be a joke but landed too hard. Agatha reached for her coffee again but didn’t drink it.

    Rio studied her carefully, searching for something to say, something that wouldn’t sound empty or pointless. But what could she say? Sorry? Agatha didn’t want pity. She didn’t want comfort, didn’t want soft reassurances that the past wasn’t her fault.

    Agatha inhaled, slow and deep, pushing her hands through her hair again before dropping them onto the table. “I hate feeling like I can’t do anything,” she admitted, softer now. “Like I’m just watching it happen over and over again, knowing half these women are never gonna get out.” Rio felt that, the weight of it. The frustration of knowing but not being able to stop it. She shifted forward slightly, her hand resting close to Agatha’s on the table. Not touching. Just close.

    “You’re not just watching,” she said. “You’re doing something.” Agatha let out a breath, low and tired, like she wasn’t convinced. Her hands curled tighter around the ceramic mug, fingers pressing into the rough surface as if it was the only thing keeping her grounded. She scoffed, the sound rougher than before, shaking her head like she was trying to physically shake off whatever weight had settled onto her shoulders. “I’m fine.” It came too quick. The kind of automatic deflection that didn’t mean anything, that only existed to cut off any further conversation. Rio then watched as she let out a slow breath, shaking her head, and abruptly pushed herself up from the booth.

    “I need a smoke,” Agatha muttered, already walking away. She grabbed her Carhartt off the back of the booth, jerking it on in one fluid motion. The heavy canvas settled over her frame, stiff with use, the faded fabric creasing at the elbows as she shoved her hands into the pockets.

    The bell over the door jingled softly as she shoved it open, stepping into the cold.

    Rio sat there for a moment, fingers curling around her own coffee mug, watching the way the diner lights flickered against the windows, casting Agatha’s silhouette in sharp relief before she disappeared completely. Then, after a slow exhale, she got up and followed.

    The cold hit her again immediately. It wasn’t the same sharp bite as earlier —the heat from the diner still clung to Rio’s skin, lingering at the edges of her coat, fighting against the November chill. But the air was crisp, the kind of cold that settled in the bones, that made the neon diner lights seem warmer, softer in contrast.

    Agatha was perched on the hood of Rio’s unmarked car, legs stretched out, boots resting against the bumper. She had her jacket zipped up now, her fingers flicking a cigarette to life. The glow of the flame caught against the rough fabric of her sleeve, illuminating her face in flashes before disappearing as she took a slow, steady drag.

    She didn’t look up when Rio approached. Just exhaled smoke into the cold, watching as it curled and disappeared into the night air. Rio didn’t speak either.

    She stepped forward, the gravel shifting under her shoes, and leaned against the hood beside her.

    For a moment, there was only silence. The distant hum of the highway. The occasional rumble of a truck passing by. The faint buzz of the diner’s neon sign, flickering in and out, painting the parking lot in uneven washes of red and yellow.

    Rio shifted slightly, just enough to let their shoulders brush. A soft touch, barely there, and Agatha didn’t move away.

    Just took another drag, the ember at the tip of her cigarette flaring orange before dimming again. Rio watched her from the corner of her eye, taking in the way exhaustion settled into her face, how the tension had finally begun to unwind, if only just a little. The diner lights softened her features. Made her look less sharp and less guarded; her cheekbones casting delicate shadows, her blue eyes brighter, clearer.

    Rio exhaled; the sound barely audible over the hum of the highway. The cold pressed in around them, crisp and biting, but the warmth sitting low in her stomach had nothing to do with the November air. Her fingers toyed with the sleeve of her coat, tracing the seam with delicate, absent motions. She smoothed the fabric, then pressed her thumb into a wrinkle that wasn’t there, pretending like it needed fixing. Like she needed something to do with her hands.

    She wasn’t sure why the words felt so heavy in her mouth—why saying them out loud felt like pulling something fragile from her chest and setting it between them. But the longer the silence stretched, the more it felt like she had to.

    So, she did. Soft, barely above a whisper.

    “I feel safe with you.”

    The words settled in the space between them, small but solid, hanging in the cold air like they couldn’t be taken back.

    Rio didn’t look up. Instead, she kept her gaze fixed downward, fingers still fidgeting with the fabric, rubbing slow circles into the sleeve like it could ground her. Like it could soften the weight of what she’d just admitted. She heard it before she felt it—the soft rustle of fabric, the quiet shift of weight against the hood of the car. Agatha turned toward her. Rio could feel her looking. Could feel the weight of those blue eyes settling on her, the warmth of Agatha’s body just inches away. The space between them had never felt so small.

    Agatha didn’t scoff, didn’t roll her eyes, didn’t throw out some sarcastic remark to break the tension. Rio swallowed, her fingers still pressing into the sleeve of her coat, still pretending like something there needed smoothing out.

    Finally, soft and hesitant, quieter than usual—Agatha spoke. “Yeah?”

    The word was barely there, almost lost in the night air.

    She felt foolish for saying it now, for letting something that soft slip out between them when they were barely hanging on by thread-thin pretenses. But it was true. She did feel safe with Agatha, in a way she wasn’t used to feeling safe with anyone.

    Rio forced herself to glance for a second. And when she did, Agatha was already looking at her.

    She should’ve let the moment pass, should’ve let the words settle without expectation. But something about the way Agatha was looking at her made it impossible to break the connection.

    Not like a joke. Not like she was amused or trying to pick her apart. The neon light from the diner washed over Agatha’s face, making her eyes brighter, softer, the usual sharpness dulled by something quieter, something Rio wasn’t sure she’d ever seen before. There was something unbearably intimate about it. Like neither of them had meant to get this close, but now that they were, neither of them knew how to pull away.

    Agatha whispered. “Look at me.”

    The moment Rio lifted her gaze, Agatha moved.

    A shift—a slow, deliberate flick of her wrist. The cigarette she’d been holding, hit the pavement between them with a quiet hiss, the embers snuffed out against the damp asphalt. The soft glow of dying embers flickered against the ground for half a second before fading into nothing. Agatha never followed its descent. Never spared it a glance. It was like she had discarded it on instinct, like the only thing that mattered now was this. Rio.

    Blue eyes locked onto hers, pinning her in place, the usual sharpness dulled into something else, something warmer, heavier, quieter. It made Rio’s breath catch, her fingers tightening where they rested against the sleeve of her coat, like she could steady herself with something tangible. The realization sent a slow, warm pulse down Rio’s spine, her throat tightening in response. The wind had picked up, sharp and biting, pulling strands of hair from the loose knot at the base of Agatha’s neck, sending them across her face. But she didn’t move to fix it. Didn’t brush them away, didn’t even blink as she kept looking.

    Not through her. At her. Like she was seeing something.

    Something Rio didn’t know how to name.

    Agatha exhaled then, a slow, measured thing that wasn’t quite a sigh, wasn’t quite a chuckle but something between the two, curling around them like smoke, warm despite the cold.

    And then, softly—softer than she ever expected Agatha to sound—she asked: “You always look at people like this, Vidal?”

    Rio swallowed.

    The heat was back. Low, insistent, creeping under her skin like wildfire. And Agatha was watching her with an expression that was unreadable, yet felt like it was stripping her down to nothing.

    A muscle in Agatha’s jaw ticked, like she was considering something, like she was weighing the moment in her hands, testing its weight before deciding whether to hold onto it or let it slip through her fingers. Her gaze then flickered. Just for a second.

    Not away, but down. To Rio’s lips.

    The motion was so subtle Rio might’ve missed it if she hadn’t been watching her so closely. If she hadn’t already been so hyper-aware of the space between them, the air charged and humming like a live fire. Agatha’s thoughts flickered in time with her pulse. It was a mistake to let herself look, but she had already done it. Had already dragged her gaze down, down, down; to the place where Rio’s lips parted, soft and pink and right there.

    Her throat felt tight.

    Now, she was aware of everything. The faint hitch in Rio’s breath, barely perceptible but there. The way her fingers clenched just slightly at the hem of her coat, like she was physically restraining herself from moving, from reaching, from making whatever the hell this was worse.

    Agatha? She was no better. Because she wasn’t pulling away. And then, because she couldn’t help herself; she smirked.

    In a voice lower than before, rough around the edges, quieter, meant only for Rio, Agatha asked, “Are you gonna answer me, Vidal? Or are you just gonna keep staring?”

    She swallowed, her voice lower than before, just as soft as Agatha’s had been.

    “You’re the one staring, Harkness.”

    Agatha chuckled, like Rio had caught her off guard.

    “Yeah?” Agatha tilted her head slightly, still not breaking eye contact. “Can you blame me?”

    Rio felt it before she understood it. A shift. A pull, slow but steady. Like gravity.

    The warmth between them creeping up her spine, settling deep in her chest, making her want to—

    No.

    She clenched her jaw, tearing her eyes away, forcing herself to glance at the diner’s neon reflection on the pavement instead. This wasn’t a game she could afford to play. Not now.

    Agatha let the silence sit for a beat longer before finally stepping back and clearing her throat—not much, but enough for the thread between them to loosen, but not snap.

    She sighed, running a hand through her already-messy hair before stuffing both hands deep into her coat pockets.

    “Relax, Vidal,” she murmured, her voice still lower than usual. “Just making an observation.”

    Rio exhaled slowly, steadying herself, finding her footing again. Then—the moment was gone. The air between them shifted again, the weight lifting just slightly, the tension not entirely gone but… pushed aside. For now.

    Agatha turned first, shoving her hands deeper into her coat pockets as she stepped toward the car, her smirk still lingering. As her fingers curled around the handle she turned back, eyes flicking over to Rio with something undeniably smug.

    “You know, Vidal,” she mused, voice just a little too casual, a little too smooth, “if you keep looking at me like that, people might start to talk.” Rio exhaled sharply, shaking her head as she gripped the edge of the car door. “I thought you didn’t care what people might start to think?” Agatha hummed, lingering just outside the car, one hand resting on the roof, her other still gripping the door. She tilted her head slightly, blue eyes flickering over Rio’s face like she was memorizing something Rio wasn’t sure she wanted her to know.

    “I don’t,” Agatha said simply, voice calm, steady. “But you do.” Then she was inside, leaving Rio standing by the car, gripping the door handle a little too tightly, and her pulse betraying her.

    Chapter 5: Between the Lines

    Chapter Notes slow burn? i prefer to call it emotionally compromised officials avoiding their feelings.

    The conference room buzzed with the quiet hum of voices, the shuffle of papers, and the unbearable scrape of a chair against the scuffed tile floor. Fluorescent lights cast a dull glow over the room, their steady flicker almost hypnotic. Occasionally, the faint scratch of pens against paper would fill Agatha’s ears, and the shuffle of officers flipping through case files. Somewhere in the back, the whir of an overworked heater unit kicked in, barely making a dent in the thick, coffee-stained air.

    At the head of the table, the captain droned through updates, his voice even, deliberate, devoid of urgency.

    “—Preliminary reports should be in by the end of the week. If you haven’t already filed your statements on the Morrison case, I expect them by noon tomorrow. No exceptions.”

    More pages turned. Pens scratched against paper. Someone coughed. Agatha slouched in her chair, elbow propped on the table, her fingers idly toying with the cap of her pen. It found its way between her teeth, a habit she never quite broke, despite knowing exactly how gross it was. Agatha was bored.

    Not just a little. Painfully, mind-numbingly bored.

    The captain’s voice droned on at the front of the room, monotone and deliberate, rolling through updates like a man reading off a grocery list. Agatha tuned most of it out. She let out a slow breath as blue eyes drifting lazily across the room.

    Lilia sat a few seats down; all sharp focus and quiet efficiency. Her notepad was already filled with tight, deliberate handwriting, columns neatly organized in a way that made Agatha’s own haphazard case notes look like they were written by a deranged toddler. Every few seconds, Lilia would flip a page, pausing just long enough to underline something before moving on, unfazed by the slow drag of the meeting.

    A cup of coffee; black and untouched, sat beside her, likely forgotten the moment it cooled past drinkable. She was listening, always listening, but she never looked up unless it was absolutely necessary.

    Lilia was actually paying attention. Unlike William.

    Sitting directly beside Agatha, he had zero interest in the briefing. William was barely pretending to be engaged. While the rest of the room flipped through case files or scribbled half-legible notes, he was slouched in his chair, one arm draped lazily over the backrest, the other hand casually working his phone beneath the table. The screen’s glow reflected faintly against his fingers, his thumb lazily flicking upward, pausing only occasionally before moving on.

    Agatha didn't need to be a detective to figure out what he was doing. She caught the briefest glimpse of the interface with its unmistakable blue and yellow chat bubbles, the grid of profile thumbnails, the occasional pop- up message preview flashing across the top. Grindr.

    She scoffed, unimpressed. “Seriously?”

    William didn’t even glance up, still scrolling with the kind of relaxed focus reserved for people who had truly mastered the art of not giving a shit. “I’m multitasking,” he murmured, completely unbothered, as if balancing a homicide investigation and thirst traps was just another Tuesday for him. Agatha rolled her eyes. “If the captain catches you—”

    “He won’t.”

    His tone was confident, bordering on smug, and he punctuated it with a small, decisive tap on the screen.

    She watched as he opened a profile, his expression barely shifting as he skimmed whatever minimal effort had been put into the guy’s bio. A second later, his thumb hovered before tapping the flame icon. A match. “This is important work,” he added, voice still low enough not to draw attention.

    Agatha made a face, tilting her head to read over his shoulder. Just a single line of text, bold and to the point: Hung and bored.

    She blinked. “You’re literally looking at a guy whose bio just says, ‘hung and bored.’”

    William grinned, locking his phone and tucking it away like he hadn’t just been engaging in absolute debauchery during a murder briefing. “Yeah. He gets me.”

    Agatha shook her head, exhaling sharply through her nose, but she let it go. There was no point in arguing with William when he was in one of his “multitasking” moods. He’d tune out everything that wasn’t directly entertaining him and somehow, miraculously, still be sharp enough to catch the one detail everyone else missed when it actually mattered. She wasn’t about to encourage his nonsense, though, so instead, she turned her attention back to the room, where the captain was still droning on, his voice a steady, mind-numbing backdrop to the scattered attention of the officers in the room.

    “—the last witness statements have been logged. We’re still waiting on forensic analysis for the Keller case, but initial reports suggest—” She let the words wash over her, barely processing them.

    Agatha exhaled, pressing her elbow against the table as she shifted in her seat. She let her gaze wander, her mind already detaching from the slow drag of the meeting. The captain’s voice was still there, a steady hum at the edges of her awareness, but the words no longer registered. Her eyes moved absently over the room, skipping past the officers flipping through case files, the flickering lights overhead, the faint movement of Lilia’s pen scratching against paper. Nothing caught her attention, that is, until Rio.

    Sitting directly across from her, Rio was fully immersed in whatever was on the page in front of her. Her sleeves were pushed up to her elbows, exposing the lean lines of her forearms, her usual white button-down neatly pressed but worn in just enough to move with her. The top button was undone, and her hair which she normally left loose was pulled up into a quick, no-nonsense ponytail, a few stray strands escaping near her temples. Agatha had seen Rio focused before, but there was something different about it when she wasn’t actively speaking or moving. It was a quiet kind of intensity, like she had built an invisible wall between herself and the rest of the room. Nothing existed except the case file in front of her. Her fingers brushed against the pages as she turned them, the motion smooth and practiced. No wasted movement, no hesitation. Each page was absorbed, processed, and set aside with absolute precision. She leaned forward slightly, her forearms resting on the table, her posture perfectly straight despite the long hours they had all put in. There was a rigid control to her, like every movement was purposeful, like she had trained herself never to fidget or break concentration.

    The soft glow from the overhead lights cast a faint shadow along the curve of her jaw, accentuating the sharp lines of her face as she worked through whatever was in front of her.

    Her lips parted slightly, just enough for Agatha to notice. It took her a second to realize; Rio was mouthing the words. Not in an obvious way, not enough for anyone else to notice. It was barely there, the subtle shape of each syllable forming silently as she read. Then, as she paused on a section, her bottom lip caught between her teeth. It wasn’t intentional, a fleeting press of pressure before she released it. She had no reason to notice, no excuse for why her attention had lingered this long, but here she was, watching the way Rio’s fingers moved carefully over the text, the way she barely reacted except for that one small tell. Agatha bit down lightly on the cap of her pen, shifting her weight. She should not be looking. And yet here she was.

    Agatha caught herself before she let her stare linger too long, but the awareness of it stayed in her chest, a quiet weight pressing down as she shifted in her seat. She forced herself to glance away, willing her attention toward something, anything, else.

    She tapped the cap of her pen against the table, jaw tight, as if that would somehow shake the image of Rio's focus, the way her lips had barely moved, the way her fingers skimmed the pages like she could pull the answers straight from the ink.

    The second she exhaled, trying to shake it off, a nudge jabbed sharply into her ribs.

    Agatha twitched, barely holding back the instinct to elbow whoever had dared to interrupt her thoughts. She turned her head slightly, just enough to see William, fully leaned back in his chair, arms folded, smirk firmly in place.

    “You staring at Vidal like that on purpose, or is it just habit now?” Agatha’s jaw tensed.

    Her fingers curled around her pen, grip tightening as she snapped her gaze to him.

    William didn’t even have the decency to look guilty. If anything, he looked thrilled with himself, watching her reaction like a cat that had just cornered a particularly slow mouse. He tilted his head, voice low enough to stay between them.

    Agatha narrowed her eyes. “What the hell are you talking about?” William’s grin widened, his expression downright smug. He leaned in slightly, voice dropping into that mock conspiratorial tone he always used when he was stirring up shit just to see what would happen. “You’re chewing on that pen cap like it owes you money, and you’ve been making heart-eyes at Vidal for the past two minutes.”

    Agatha scoffed, rolling her eyes so hard it almost hurt. “Heart-eyes? You serious?”

    William gave a slow, exaggerated nod. “Yeah. Should I be worried about you swallowing that pen cap?”

    Agatha didn’t think twice, her foot struck out under the table, a precise, calculated kick to his shin.

    William yelped, jerking forward slightly, but the damn smirk never left his face. “Touched a nerve, huh?”

    Agatha huffed and shook her head; she wasn’t going to give him the satisfaction. Instead, she turned her attention firmly back to her case file. Or at least she tried, until her eyes drifted again.

    And landed back on Rio.

    Agatha let out a slow breath, shaking off the lingering amusement from William’s teasing. She wasn’t going to let him get to her. She wasn’t staring.

    But now, the idea was in her head, and that was the real problem. She shifted in her seat, adjusting her grip on her pen like it actually mattered, and cleared her throat, barely enough to be noticeable. Across the table, Rio’s eyes flicked up immediately.

    The reaction was almost automatic, like a reflex, like she had been too deep in thought and needed a second to reorient herself. She blinked once, the crease between her brows still faintly visible from whatever part of the case file had caught her attention.

    Agatha, ever the opportunist, smirked.

    She leaned forward slightly, resting her chin on her hand, and mouthed across the table: “You look tired, Vidal. Rough night?”

    Rio’s expression didn’t change at first, but Agatha didn’t miss the way her shoulders squared just a little more, her spine going from straight to rigid. Her mouth pressed into a thin line before she exhaled sharply and mouthed back.

    “I work, unlike you.”

    Agatha fought the grin pulling at her lips, tilting her head slightly as she tapped her fingers idly against her cheek.

    “Oh? Is that what we’re calling it?”

    Rio’s reaction was immediate.

    She rolled her eyes, shaking her head, but her lips twitched, betraying her. “Shut up.”

    Agatha raised a single brow, dragging her gaze over Rio’s very tense posture, the way she was gripping her pen like it was the only thing keeping her grounded.

    She could push just a little more.

    Mouth curving into a slow, deliberate smirk, she mouthed one last thing, taking her time with each word.

    “Make me.”

    Rio went completely still. Agatha had seen her pissed off before. She had seen her irritated, exasperated, and exhausted, but this was something else entirely.

    For half a second, Rio looked like she might actually combust. Her back straightened even more, her jaw tightening as if she was physically holding herself back from reacting.

    Agatha could see the way her fingers curled slightly around the edge of her case file, the way her breath hitched just enough to be noticeable. At the edge of Rio’s collar, the faintest hint of pink. Agatha’s smirk widened.

    She tilted her head, silent challenge written all over her face. The moment hung for a second too long. Agatha could still see the faintest trace of color at the base of Rio’s neck, the stiffness in her posture, the way she was gripping her pen like it was the only thing keeping her tethered to reality.

    It was hilarious.

    She was just about to press her luck and make one more teasing remark to see if she could make Rio crack completely, when the air in the room shifted. Cutting through the background noise of shuffling papers and half- listening officers, the captain’s voice landed like a weight across the table. "Harkness. Vidal. Something you’d like to share with the class?" The rustling of papers halted. The faint clicking of a pen against a notepad stopped.

    A few officers turned their heads slightly, catching just enough movement in their peripheral vision to recognize a potential train wreck in motion. Nobody said anything. Nobody wanted to be next on the receiving end. Across the table, Rio froze. Her entire body went rigid, eyes widening just slightly, face heating before she caught herself.

    Agatha saw it happen in real-time, the immediate panic, the mental reset, and then the desperate attempt at recovery.

    Rio jerked upright so fast that her coffee nearly went flying. The cup rocked dangerously close to the edge of the table before she caught it, fingers tightening around the lid like it had personally betrayed her. She swallowed hard and responded in the steadiest voice she could manage. “No, sir.”

    Agatha, on the other hand, barely reacted. She stretched lazily, completely at ease, her body language the definition of unbothered. Leaning back in her chair, she crossed her arms over her chest, tilting her head ever so slightly as a smirk pulled at the corner of her lips. “Just admiring Vidal’s work ethic, Captain. Really inspiring.”

    The captain didn’t look convinced.

    His gaze moved between them, slow and deliberate, lingering just a second too long. There was no real irritation in his expression, but there was something else like he was filing this moment away for later. After a pause that stretched just a little longer than necessary, he exhaled heavily, flipping a page in his notes. “Right. Moving on.” Relief flickered across Rio’s face for only half a second before she forced herself to focus back on her file. Agatha, however, wasn’t finished. She turned her head back toward Rio, tilting her chin slightly, still grinning like she’d just gotten away with something.

    Then, she winked.

    Rio inhaled sharply, her fingers tightening around her pen like she might snap it in half. She exhaled through her nose, slow and controlled, before finally dragging her attention back to her file.

    The captain, either oblivious or just enjoying their collective suffering, flipped to the last page of his notes with absolute slowness. The briefing had gone on long enough, at this point the air in the room felt heavier, weighed down by the sheer amount of time they had been forced to sit through updates that barely concerned half of them.

    Then, in his usual, flat monotone, he said something that immediately killed whatever hope had been lingering in the room.

    “Before we wrap, let’s go over next week’s scheduling adjustments.” A low, audible groan rippled through the room.

    William physically dropped his head against the table. Lilia pinched the bridge of her nose. Someone in the back muttered, “Jesus Christ,” under their breath.

    Agatha, barely holding back a sigh, leaned even further back in her chair, eyes flicking toward the ceiling like she was contemplating astral projection as an escape.

    The captain, completely unfazed by the mutiny brewing in the room, kept talking.

    “Rotations for next month will be finalized by the end of the week. I expect all overtime requests to be submitted no later than—”

    Agatha let her gaze drift again, catching the unmistakable look of immediate suffering on Rio’s face.

    Not even she was built for this level of bureaucratic torture. Rio had closed her case file, her fingers drummed lightly against the edge of the table, a telltale sign that her patience had officially run thin. Finally, after what felt like a lifetime, the captain closed his folder. “That’s all. Get back to work.”

    Chairs scraped against the floor as officers stood, papers shuffled back into folders, and the low murmur of conversations filled the air again. A collective sense of relief settled over the room, as if everyone had just been granted a temporary reprieve from slow bureaucratic death. Agatha stretched, rolling out her shoulders before making her way toward her desk, weaving between officers who were already making their escape. The precinct always had that strange mix of urgency and exhaustion, with people moving quickly but looking like they hadn’t slept in days. She barely made it two steps before William fell into stride beside her. She sighed before he even opened his mouth.

    “God, what now?”

    William didn’t answer right away. Instead, he walked with her, hands stuffed into his pockets, grinning like he was in on some inside joke that she wasn’t privy to.

    “You know,” he said, voice light, conversational, like he wasn’t about to be an absolute menace, “you’re not subtle.”

    Agatha stopped at her desk, dropping the file she had been holding onto the surface with a dull thud. She gave him a slow, tired look, one that very clearly said leave me alone, but William, as always, was completely immune to her exasperation.

    She pulled out her chair, ready to ignore him, but he leaned casually against the edge of her desk, effectively blocking her path.

    “The heart eyes, the longing stares,” he continued, ignoring her attempt to pretend he didn’t exist. “Really, I expected more game from you.” Agatha pinched the bridge of her nose. “I don’t have ‘heart eyes.’” William made a soft, pitying noise, shaking his head like she had just said something tragic.

    “Oh, sweetie.” He patted her shoulder, slow and condescending. “You absolutely do.”

    Agatha swatted his hand away. “I will break every single one of your fingers, you annoying twink.”

    William gasped, dramatic as always, placing a hand over his chest. “Violence? Wow. That’s how you’re handling your feelings now?” He tsked, shaking his head. “Just say you want to ravish her and move on.” Agatha opened her mouth—then closed it.

    She glared instead, shoving her chair out just enough to make him jump back half a step. “Go bother someone else.”

    William, already walking away, far too pleased with himself, turned just enough to throw one last jab over his shoulder.

    “Can’t wait for the weddingggg.”

    Agatha, still holding the stapler she had just picked up, seriously debated throwing it.

    Across the bullpen, Rio moved with purpose, her steps precise, measured, giving nothing away. She didn’t rush, didn’t weave through the crowd too quickly, but there was a sharpness to the way she walked, a quiet urgency that made it clear she had no intention of lingering. She wasn’t stopping to talk, wasn’t making eye contact, wasn’t doing anything that might give someone the impression that she was available for conversation. She just needed to get to her desk. Not because there was anything pressing to do—she didn’t even have a file in her hands—but because she needed one second. Just one goddamn second to breathe, to let the meeting fade into the background, to push away the lingering heat of Agatha’s gaze and the memory of that smirk, the way her voice had curled around the words, “Make me”.

    She reached her desk, exhaled slowly, and sat down. Her fingers skimmed over the surface, grounding herself, anchoring to something solid. She stretched her arms briefly, forcing her muscles to loosen, shoulders rolling back as she took a long sip of her now-lukewarm coffee. If she could just sit here for one quiet moment, she could shake it off, let the tension settle, and pretend none of it had gotten under her skin.

    But, of course, William had other plans.

    A chair scraped against the floor beside her, loud and obnoxious. The noise sent an immediate irritation up her spine, the kind of deliberate, grating sound that only William would make when he wanted to announce his presence.

    Without even looking she knew it was him.

    And sure enough, there he was, dropping into the seat next to her, settling in like he belonged there. His usual lazy confidence was in full force, an infuriating grin already on his lips, because somehow—somehow—he always knew exactly when someone wanted to be left alone and took that as an invitation to do the exact opposite.

    Rio did not acknowledge him. She lifted her coffee cup to her lips and took another slow sip, feigning interest in her mostly blank notepad, as if that might deter him.

    William, completely unfazed, tapped his fingers against the arm of his chair, waiting a beat before speaking.

    “So.”

    Rio closed her eyes for half a second. Bracing.

    Here we go.

    She set her coffee down, deliberate and controlled.

    William leaned in just slightly, like he was letting her in on some kind of secret, his grin widening. “Are we gonna talk about it?”

    Rio exhaled, steady and slow, then finally turned to give him the flattest, most unimpressed look humanly possible.

    “No. Don’t start.”

    William, predictably, ignored her entirely. He leaned against the edge of her desk, arms crossed, grinning like the absolute menace he was. “Oh, but I have to,” he said, shaking his head with exaggerated pity. “That was spectacular.”

    Rio didn’t react. She busied herself with the case files in front of her, flipping a page she wasn’t actually reading. Maybe if she ignored him long enough, he might go away.

    William, of course, kept going. “I mean, I knew she liked to mess with you, but you—” he let out a sharp laugh, tilting his head. “Christ, Vidal, I thought you were gonna combust in your seat.”

    Rio exhaled slowly, carefully controlled. She kept her voice flat. “I don’t know what you’re talking about.”

    William cackled, loud and entirely too pleased with himself. “Ohhh, we’re playing dumb today? Cool, cool, cool. Should I pull up the security footage? You know, from like, five minutes ago?”

    Rio’s jaw tightened. Her fingers curled just slightly around the edges of the file, flipping through pages like she might actually absorb some of the words. “Drop it, William.”

    “Come on, don’t tell me you didn’t feel that.” He waved a hand vaguely in the air. “The look she gave you? Jesus, I think I saw your soul leave your body.”

    Rio didn’t react.

    Calm. Cool. Unshakable.

    William leaned in slightly, voice dropping lower, his grin sharpening. “You’re blushing.”

    That did it.

    Rio’s head snapped up, her glare sharp and immediate. “I am not.” William just grinned wider. “Mmhm.”

    Rio closed her file a little too hard. The sharp slap of paper against wood made a few people glance their way, but she ignored them, fixing William with a look that should have set him on fire. “Don’t you have work to do?” William smirked, standing up in the slowest, most obnoxious way possible. “Sure. But watching you suffer is way more fun.”

    William clicked his tongue, shaking his head. He leaned back on his heels, sighing like this was physically painful for him to witness. “You’re really gonna sit there and pretend like I didn’t just watch Agatha fucking Harkness —the Agatha Harkness—give you literal heart eyes across the table?” Rio didn’t dignify that with an answer. She turned another page in her file, eyes scanning words she wasn’t actually reading. “You’re seeing things.” She finally looked up, scowling. “She was not.”

    William raised his brows, looking entirely too smug. “Oh, she definitely was.”

    Rio clenched her jaw. “You’re delusional.”

    William snorted, crossing his arms. “Says the woman who was mouthing things back and forth like a fucking high schooler.” He sighed dramatically. “Seriously, Vidal, it was cute. Like, grossly cute. I almost puked.” Rio glared at him. “You’re enjoying this way too much.”

    William beamed. “Oh, absolutely.” He let the moment stretch just long enough to make her shift in her seat before sighing, shaking his head. “I just never thought I’d live to see the day. Agatha Harkness, looking at someone like that—” he let out a low whistle, tapping the side of her desk for emphasis. “—like you hung the goddamn moon.”

    William stretched lazily, like this conversation had physically exhausted him, even though he was the one who started it. He smirked, walking off, hands in his pockets. “Anyway, I’ll let you sit here and pretend you’re not thinking about her.”

    And then he was gone.

    The moment William disappeared, Rio exhaled, massaging her left shoulder as if she could physically shake off the weight of the conversation. It didn’t mean anything. William ran his mouth all the time and he loved to push buttons, loved to get a reaction. That was all this was.

    She flipped another page in the case file, trying to force her attention back to the neatly typed text in front of her. Focus. That was all she needed. Just one minute of pure concentration, something to pull her back into the present. But the words swam, lines blurring together in a haze that refused to sharpen. Her eyes darted from paragraph to paragraph, but nothing stuck. It was like trying to read through fog, like her brain had simply stopped processing anything except the one thing she was trying to ignore. Her fingers tightened around the paper, pressing into the corner hard enough to crease it. She wasn’t frustrated; not exactly. It wasn’t irritation prickling at the edges of her mind. It was something softer. Something unnerving in the way it curled in her chest, refusing to be dismissed. Because the truth was that William had been right.

    Rio didn’t want to admit it, even to herself. She wanted to push past it, to treat it like a fleeting moment, a trick of the light. But the more she tried to shove it aside, the more it settled, warm and insistent, making itself at home in a place she wasn’t sure she had room for it.

    Because Agatha had been looking at her.

    Not just glancing, not just in passing. Observing her in that sharp, measured way she did when she was really paying attention to something. The kind of look that made it impossible not to feel it.

    And worse? Rio had felt anchored under it.

    There had been something deliberate in the way Agatha’s gaze had settled on her, something searching, considering.
