It had weight, like Agatha had been waiting to see if Rio would look back or if she’d react. Maybe she had. Maybe that was what had really made the warmth bloom in her chest; it was the quiet and undeniable fact that she had liked it.

    She tried to redirect her thoughts, to pull them back to something neutral, something steady. But the moment had already rooted itself inside her. She could feel it winding through her, impossible to shake, impossible to forget. Because now, it wasn’t just today’s moment at the precinct creeping in. Now, she was thinking about the other night.

    The cold had been sharp against her skin outside the diner, cutting through the layers of her coat as they lingered beside the squad car. The world around them had been quiet with the distant hum of traffic, the occasional flicker of headlights sweeping across the pavement.

    Agatha leaning against the car like she belonged there, like she had all the time in the world. The soft glow of her cigarette had cast the faintest light against her face, illuminating the sharp angles of her jaw, the curve of her lips as she exhaled. She had barely smoked it, barely paid attention to it at all, because she had been looking at Rio.

    Not in the way she looked at most people—not with amusement, not with that sharp-edged arrogance she wielded so effortlessly. This had been different like she was waiting for something.

    "You always look at people like this, Vidal?"

    Her voice had been softer than usual, quieter. Rio had felt it, all of it. She had felt it in the space between them, the air too still, the quiet too full. She had felt it in the way her chest tightened, in the way her pulse slowed instead of quickened, as if her body had settled into something inevitable. Then Agatha had tilted her head slightly, and Rio had looked. At the shape of her mouth, at the barely perceptible shift in her breathing, at the way Agatha’s eyes had lingered. Just for a second. Maybe less. But it had been enough.

    She had almost kissed her.

    Rio swallowed, blinking rapidly as she stared down at the case file she still hadn’t managed to read a word of. That night had settled into her bones, quiet and steady, refusing to leave. Not in the way a mistake lingers, but in the way something good lingers. In the way something safe stays. The realization made her chest tighten, but it wasn’t fear. It wasn’t panic. It was something far more dangerous.

    Rio suddenly felt too warm. The precinct, usually cold and sterile, felt like it was closing in around her, the weight of the moment pressing into her lungs. She needed to move, to do something, anything, before the warmth settled too deeply, before she started getting comfortable with it. She pushed back from her desk, her chair scraping loudly against the floor. The sound cut through the usual hum of conversation, but she ignored the glances sent her way. She grabbed her coffee—a poor excuse for an escape, but an escape nonetheless—and forced herself to move.

    Each step was measured, steady. She kept her gaze forward, her posture sharp, as if she could still convince herself this was nothing. She just needed a cup of water Then, she felt Agatha watching her as she walked away from her desk. The same way she had felt it in the diner parking lot. The same way she had felt it in the briefing room.

    Still, she didn’t look. Rio tried to quicken her pace as she heard Agatha’s boots begin to trail behind her.

    She made it halfway to the break room before Agatha’s voice followed her, low and teasing, just loud enough to carry.

    "You running from me, Vidal?"

    Rio’s stomach flipped.

    A slow, curling warmth spread through her chest, unwelcome but impossible to ignore. She didn’t stop walking and didn’t turn around until she fully reached the water cooler outside the breakroom. Rio turned slowly, keeping her movements deliberate as she faced Agatha. She didn’t rush. Didn’t let the moment feel like anything more than what it was—two colleagues, two detectives standing near the water cooler like this wasn’t the first real moment of privacy they’d had since that night. Agatha was already stepping closer, but there was something different about the way she moved this time. Normally, she carried herself with an air of certainty, like she always had a plan, like she already knew how a conversation was going to go before it even started.

    Now, she seemed hesitant.

    Not obvious, no, Agatha wasn’t the type to wear hesitation where people could see it. But Rio had learned to read her, had learned to pick up on the small things, the micro-expressions she tried to bury beneath her usual nonchalance.

    The tension in her shoulders, the way she shoved her hands deep into her jacket pockets like she needed to physically stop herself from doing something else. The way her jaw tensed slightly before she exhaled, like she was working up to something.

    Rio took her time, turning back toward the water cooler. She let the silence sit, dragging it out just a little longer as she filled her cup. The steady stream of water, the faint hum of the machine, whatever gave her something to focus on. Something that wasn’t Agatha.

    She took a slow sip, exhaling smoothly before murmuring, “Not running.” Her voice was even, measured. Like this was nothing. “Just thirsty.” Agatha hummed softly, but there was no conviction behind it. No real amusement.

    “Right. Total coincidence that you suddenly needed water the second I caught up to you.”

    Rio finally glanced over, arching a brow. “You caught up to me?” Agatha huffed out a quiet laugh, but it wasn’t the usual cocky, smug sound Rio had grown used to. It was softer, edged with something almost uncertain. Like she wasn’t sure whether or not to lean into the moment. She switched her weight, moving from one foot to the other, exhaling before clearing her throat. The silence between them stretched, longer than necessary. It wasn’t like Agatha to hesitate.

    Which meant whatever she was about to say wasn’t something she was used to saying.

    “So, about the other night…”

    Agatha shifted again, then lifted a hand, scratching at the back of her neck. The motion was quick, almost absentminded, like she had caught herself hesitating and was trying to cover it up with something casual. Her fingers dragged over her skin once before she stuffed her hand back into her jean pocket, clearing her throat like she was already regretting bringing it up. The words were quiet. Not exactly nervous, but not as steady as they could have been, either.

    Rio stilled.

    Her pulse kicked up slightly, but she didn’t react. Not outwardly. She kept her shoulders loose, kept her breath even, didn’t let a single thing show on her face as she lifted her cup back to her lips and took another slow sip. It was a performance in absolute control, a performance that only just barely concealed the fact that her heart was suddenly hammering against her ribs.

    Agatha exhaled again; this time lower, quieter.

    “You know,” she tried again, and now her voice was just a little rougher, like she was forcing herself to push through whatever reluctance was keeping her from saying what she wanted to say. “After the diner, when we —”

    A pause. A sharp inhale through the nose.

    “—on the squad car?”

    That.

    Slowly, smoothly, she set her cup down on the counter beside the water cooler. Then, she met Agatha’s gaze directly, studying the way she wasn’t quite looking at her, the way her fingers twitched slightly in her pockets, the way her jaw worked, like she was choosing each word carefully. Agatha never avoided eye contact. Interesting.

    Rio let the moment stretch. Let Agatha feel the silence, the weight of it. She had never seen her like this before. Not quite nervous, but not completely in control, either. Agatha was proud, always composed, always walking the line between arrogance and confidence like it was second nature.

    Right now, she was standing beside Rio like she was trying to hold herself together.

    Rio exhaled slowly, tilting her head just slightly. Then, finally, she spoke. “Yeah.” Her voice was quiet, light, but deliberate. “I remember.” And oh, did she remember.

    The moment she had spent seventy-two hours actively trying not to think about. The one she had still found herself replaying in the back of her mind anyway, on an insufferable, endless loop.

    Because yes, she very much remembered.

    The sharp bite of cold air against her skin as they stood beside the squad car, the hum of a neon sign flickering in the distance, the faint burn of cigarette smoke clinging to Agatha’s clothes.

    She remembered the way the space between them had disappeared. How their breath had mixed in the cold, how Agatha had looked at her. Not like she was teasing, not like she was playing a game, but like she was seeing something she wanted.

    Rio had barely kept herself from closing the distance.

    She had felt it in the air, in the way Agatha’s gaze had dropped just for a fraction of a second, just enough for Rio to notice.

    She had almost kissed her.

    Rio swallowed, hard. Her fingers flexed slightly against the cup, her chest warming despite the cool air circulating through the precinct. But then—Lilia.

    Fucking Lilia.

    “Hey, I need you both at my desk,” she said, stepping up beside them, completely oblivious to the suffocating tension in the air. Agatha reacted first.

    She cleared her throat, straightening her posture just slightly, hands still shoved into her jeans like she was resisting the urge to do something else with them. Rio exhaled sharply, relief and frustration tangling together in a messy knot inside her chest.

    To her absolute credit, Agatha didn’t flinch.

    Didn’t give even the faintest indication that they had just been talking about almost kissing against a squad car.

    Her entire demeanor shifted in an instant, smoothing over with that effortless, easy confidence that made it infuriatingly difficult to tell if anything ever got under her skin. She turned to Lilia, her voice light, casual, as if the moment had already slipped through her fingers like smoke. “Yeah? You finally ready to hand me that Medal of Honor?” Lilia snorted. “Yeah, for not punching out that suspect last week.” Agatha grinned. “I’m a picture of restraint.”

    Lilia rolled her eyes. “Sure, Harkness.”

    Agatha acted like nothing important had just happened and she hadn’t just cracked open something that neither of them had been ready to acknowledge. As if she hadn’t just derailed Rio’s entire nervous system with a quiet, hesitant question and a memory that neither of them had been able to shake.

    Rio stood there, gripping her cup like it might disintegrate in her hands, like if she didn’t hold onto something, she might physically lose her balance. She kept her breath even, forced herself to stay still, but every nerve in her body was frayed.

    Lilia glanced between them, one brow slightly raised, but if she noticed anything off, she didn’t comment on it. She just gave a quick wave and turned back toward her desk, disappearing around the corner, leaving nothing but silence in her wake.

    Agatha stood there, just a beat too long, deciding whether or not to say something else. Her jaw tensed slightly, fingers twitching inside her jacket pockets. Then, smoothly, she cleared her throat. “Guess we should get back to pretending to be productive.”

    Rio inhaled, steadied herself and lifted her cup to her lips like nothing had happened. “Guess so.”

    Agatha nodded, barely looking at her now, adjusting the collar of her flannel. Then, without another word, she turned on her heel and walked away.

    Rio let out a slow breath, feeling the cool of the water cup against her palm, grounding herself in the simple, steady weight of it. It was fine. Everything was fine. The moment had passed, and she’d kept herself together, kept things professional, didn’t let her expression slip.

    But as she watched Agatha walk away with her sharp, confident strides carrying her toward Lilia’s desk, something in Rio’s chest twisted. Not regret—not quite. But something close.

    She followed a few steps behind, her pace slower, more measured. The precinct was still humming with background noise like phones ringing, the murmur of conversations, the occasional scrape of a chair across the floor. It was the usual familiar chaos, but none of it seemed to fully register, because Rio’s focus was on the woman in front of her.

    Agatha walked with the same ease she always did, hands still stuffed in her jean pockets, shoulders loose, like she hadn’t just hesitated at the water cooler. Like she hadn’t almost said something she maybe shouldn’t have and she wasn’t thinking about it at all.

    But for just a second, Agatha glanced back.

    It was subtle but Rio caught it. The way Agatha’s head tilted ever so slightly to the side, and her blue eyes flicked over Rio before snapping forward again. Almost like she was checking to make sure Rio was still there. And Rio felt it that stupid warmth in her chest, the one she had been desperately trying to ignore.

    She exhaled, shifting her gaze away, letting it drift to the rest of the bullpen as they walked. William was still at his desk, hunched over his phone, probably still swiping through Grindr instead of actually doing his job. A couple of detectives were gathered near the break room, trading low voices over their coffee. Someone’s desk phone rang, shrill and insistent, cutting through the steady buzz of the room. But still Rio’s thought kept circling back.

    What would Agatha have said?

    Would she have brushed it off with another easy joke, another quip to make it seem like it didn’t mean anything? Would she have actually admitted something?

    Rio’s fingers flexed slightly around her cup, her thumb brushing absently over the rim as she walked. And what would she have done? If Agatha had actually said something, what would Rio have said back? She wasn’t sure. That uncertainty sat heavy in her chest, like something unfinished. Something she wasn’t sure she wanted to examine too closely. But then Lilia glanced over her shoulder, and Rio forced the thoughts away, smoothing out her expression. Now wasn’t the time.

    When they reached Lilia’s desk, Agatha didn’t sit right away. Instead, she grabbed two chairs from the nearby row of empty desks and dragged them over, the legs scraping lightly against the tile floor. She did it without comment, without even really looking at Rio, but there was something unspoken in the way she moved—a quiet, automatic gesture. Agatha dropped into one of the chairs, leaning back slightly, still keeping her hands stuffed in her pockets. With one foot, she nudged the other chair forward toward Rio.

    A polite invitation.

    Rio hesitated only for a second, watching Agatha for a moment longer. The sharp confidence in her stride was back. The hesitation, the uncertainty from earlier was gone. Fuck, Agatha was good at this. At moving on. At acting like things didn’t linger.

    Rio wasn’t.

    Still, she lowered herself into the chair beside her, letting her fingers tighten around her water cup before setting it down on the desk. Agatha still wouldn’t quite look at her.

    Rio wasn’t sure if she was relieved or disappointed. But, instead of lingering on it, she let out a small breath, adjusting her posture and shifting her focus entirely on Lilia.

    Lilia barely looked up as Agatha and Rio settled into their chairs, already flipping through the stack of files spread across her desk. The precinct buzzed around them, but here, at Lilia’s little workstation, the noise felt distant by the weight of whatever information she was about to drop. She smirked, tapping a file with her index finger, her tone laced with something that sounded a little too pleased. "You two are gonna love this." Rio, already accustomed by disappointment, leaned back in her chair with arms crossing loosely over her chest. "Don’t mean to be a pessimist Lilia but, I doubt it."

    Agatha, beside her flicked over the papers before looking back at Lilia with a raised brow. “Unless it’s an all-expenses-paid vacation, I’m gonna have to agree with Vidal.”

    Unfazed, Lilia slid a file toward them, the motion smooth and deliberate. "Lisa Moreno was last seen at The Lantern, right?"

    Agatha, dry as ever, didn’t bother to hide her boredom. She rested her elbow on the desk, chin propped against her hand. "We know. We were there."

    Lilia nodded, flipping a page like she hadn’t heard them, and also maybe she just didn’t care.

    “Then explain this” she tapped a section of the case notes, voice steady, her gaze sharp as she looked up at them. “That bar does have security cameras. You were right Vidal. One covers the entrance, meaning, if Lisa left, she should be on that footage.”

    Silence settled between them for a beat, heavier than before. Rio frowned, flipping through the pages herself, eyes scanning the neatly typed reports. Then her stomach dropped.

    She hadn’t seen any footage. No stills, no video timestamps, nothing. They had scoured every lead, combed through every scrap of evidence, and yet there was no footage.

    Her fingers curled slightly against the paper as she lifted her gaze. "We don’t have any footage."

    Lilia leaned back in her chair, shrugging like it was the most obvious thing in the world. "Exactly."

    She tapped the file again, voice even. "No footage was pulled. No request was even filed for it."

    Agatha tilted her head slightly, skepticism etched into the slight furrow of her brow. Her fingers tapped against the desk in an absent rhythm, the click- click-click of the pen in her hand filling the space between words. She glanced at Lilia, her expression unreadable, before finally speaking. “Let me guess, someone just forgot to check it?”

    Lilia exhaled, shifting slightly in her chair as she laced her fingers together on the desk. “I don’t know,” she admitted, her tone even, measured. “What I do know is that no one’s seen the footage.”

    Rio’s jaw tightened.

    That wasn’t just a mistake. That wasn’t just something that slipped through the cracks. The footage from The Lantern’s entrance camera should have been one of the first things requested, standard procedure. Any case involving a missing person meant pulling every available piece of surveillance from the last known location. Confirming whether Lisa had left on her own, whether someone had been with her, whether she had been in distress.

    It was textbook. It was basic police work.

    But yet—nothing.

    Her fingers curled against the edges of the case file as she scanned the paperwork again, flipping pages, looking for any mention of a request, a note from the initial investigator, anything.

    - Lisa was last seen at The Lantern.

    That was in the report, but there was no reference to a security pull, no chain of custody form, no timestamped footage review. They had all known The Lantern was the last confirmed location. It had been the foundation of their timeline, the single fixed point before Lisa Moreno had vanished. Rio’s voice, when it came, was steady and low.

    “Lisa was last seen at The Lantern. But yet, we never followed up on the one thing that could confirm how she left?”

    Lilia didn’t hesitate, didn’t shift in her seat. She simply lifted a shoulder in a slow shrug.

    “You tell me.”

    Agatha leaned back in her chair, her pen now pressed lightly between her teeth, thinking. Calculating. Her eyes flickered toward Rio, the blue of them sharp, focused.

    "So either the footage doesn’t show anything useful…” she mused, voice laced with the kind of deliberate slowness that meant she was already picking apart the angles.

    Rio’s pulse drummed against her ribs, but she forced herself to stay still. Surveillance footage was evidence. Evidence had a paper trail. If The Lantern’s security system had been accessed as part of the investigation, there would have been documentation…a retrieval log, a review report, a chain of custody form.

    The fact that it wasn’t in the case file meant only two things. One: There had been nothing useful in the footage.

    Or—

    She exhaled slowly, flipping the case file shut as she met Lilia’s gaze. “Or someone made sure it wasn’t part of the case file.”

    Rio shut the case file with a quiet thud, the realization settling like a stone in her chest. She knew what this meant. Someone had deliberately kept that footage out of the reports. Someone had seen it, had access to it, and had chosen to make sure it never became part of the investigation. “Bingo,” Lilia said in a quiet, measured tone, a spark of satisfaction in her eyes. “That’s exactly it.”

    Agatha’s gaze, which had been fixed on the empty space where the footage should have been, sharpened in response. “So,” she murmured, voice low and thoughtful, “that means someone out there was willing to bury the truth, and we’re not the only ones keeping score.”

    The room seemed to grow quieter around them as the three detectives absorbed the implications. In that silent moment, every unsaid word reverberated through Rio’s mind; questions of who might be orchestrating this cover-up, and why the chain of evidence was missing the one piece that could have put everything into perspective.

    If someone had kept that footage out of the reports, the real question was, why?

    Rio was already sitting up straighter, her fingers curling around the edge of the case file like she was ready to push away from the desk and march straight to The Lantern.

    “We need to check that footage. Now.”

    Agatha let out a slow breath through her nose, rolling her jaw slightly before shifting back in her chair. "Yeah, about that..."

    Lilia narrowed her eyes, already sensing bullshit. “What?” With a casual shrug, Agatha said, "I may have… roughed up the bartender. A little. Last time we were there."

    Lilia’s groan was immediate, her head falling back against the chair like she had been personally victimized. “Jesus, Harkness.”

    Agatha, completely unbothered, lifted one shoulder in an easy shrug. “He was being an asshole. Right Vidal?”

    Lilia sighed, shutting the case file with just a little too much force. "Well, great. That means if we try to get the footage now, they’ll probably tell us to go fuck ourselves."

    Agatha grinned. "Or, hear me out…we threaten him again."

    Lilia’s glare could have set fire to the entire desk. "Absolutely not." Rio shook her head, clearly not convinced. “We can still go down there. Ask.”

    Agatha scoffed. "Vidal, come on. I basically slammed the guy against the bar last time we were there. You really think he’s gonna hand over security footage if we just ask nicely?”

    Rio huffed, frustration tightening in her chest. “So what, we just sit on our hands?”

    Lilia cut in, already pushing back from her desk.

    “No. We get a warrant.”

    She exhaled through her nose, already reaching for her laptop, fingers moving with practiced efficiency. “I’ll get the paperwork started. If we’re lucky, we’ll have approval by tomorrow morning.”

    Rio nodded, but the tension in her shoulders didn’t ease because waiting meant time lost.

    Agatha sighed, rubbing at her temple, her lips pressing together briefly before she exhaled. “And if we’re not lucky?”

    Lilia didn’t even bother looking up, just shrugged. "Then you get to practice patience, which I hear is a valuable skill."

    Agatha scoffed, "Yeah, well, I’ll believe that when it pays my rent." Rio barely registered the exchange as fingers tapped restlessly against her arm, her thoughts spinning, flipping back through every step of the investigation, every mistake, every missed opportunity.

    Rio always trusted the facts, the hard evidence, the trail that led from one undeniable truth to the next. But this? This was a hole.

    A hole someone accidentally made or one they were supposed to fall into. She didn’t realize how long she had been sitting there, how tense her posture had become, until she felt Agatha watching her.

    “You look like you’re gonna vibrate out of your skin, Vidal. You good?” Rio blinked, snapping out of it. "I'm fine."

    Agatha studied her for a second longer, blue eyes lingering in that way that made Rio feel seen in a way she wasn’t sure she liked. Then, with a suspicious glance, she leaned back in her chair. “Hm. If you say so.” Lilia sighed with eyes still on her screen. “Go home, both of you. Get some sleep. I’ll call if anything moves."

    Agatha stretched, rolling her shoulders. "Fine by me."

    Rio exhaled slowly and didn’t move. The weight in her chest didn’t lift. It settled deep, like an off-key note lingering too long, a detail out of place. Lilia glanced up, sighing. “Seriously. Go home. You hovering over me isn’t gonna make the warrant process faster.”

    Agatha smirked, finally pushing off the desk, hands slipping into the back pockets of her jeans. But before she turned, she gave Rio’s chair leg a quick, subtle kick.

    “C’mon, Vidal. Let’s go pretend we have lives.”

    Rio let out a slow breath and pushed herself up from the chair, following Agatha’s stride out towards the front of the building.

    Agatha pushed through the precinct doors first, the cool night air hitting them as they stepped onto the sidewalk. Without missing a beat, she joked, “So, you think if I bring the bartender an edible arrangement he’ll forgive me?”

    Rio barely reacted; her thoughts still tangled in the details of the case. The missing footage. The gaps in the reports. The way everything about this felt off.

    Agatha must have noticed because after a few steps, she slowed her pace just enough to glance over her shoulder. Her smirk softened, replaced by something quieter.

    “You’re thinking too hard, Vidal.”

    Rio huffed, shaking her head. "It's my job to think hard." Agatha came to a slow stop, her boot scuffing against the pavement as she lightly kicked at a loose rock. The motion was absent, almost thoughtless, like she needed something to do with her feet. The streetlamps bathed her in a dim glow, catching the soft edges of her flannel, the sharp blue of her eyes as they flickered toward Rio.

    She didn’t smirk this time, not fully. “Yeah, but you don’t have to do it alone.”

    The words landed softer than Rio expected, settling somewhere deep in her chest as for just a second, neither of them spoke.

    Rio wanted to say something. Wanted to acknowledge the shift in the air and the way Agatha had slowed her steps, the way she was looking at her now. But before she could find the words, Agatha reached out and patted her arm, quickly and light, barely there.

    Enough to make Rio feel the warmth of it, even through her sleeve. Agatha took a step back, hands slipping into her pockets again as she turned toward her car, her voice lighter now, like she hadn’t just said something that was meant to comfort the brown eyed woman.

    “See you tomorrow, partner.”

    Rio stood there for a moment longer, her grip tightening around the case file. She let herself linger, staring at the empty space where Agatha had just been, the ghost of her words still hanging in the cold night air. You don’t have to do it alone.

    Rio exhaled slowly, pressing her lips together.

    She had a feeling that was going to be harder than it sounded.

    Chapter 6: Against All Reason

    Chapter Notes two idiots, one first aid kit, and absolutely zero self-control. They found themselves at The Lantern again, this time with confirmation that Rio had been right.

    There was security footage.

    The discovery sat between them as the last of the daylight faded behind the skyline, leaving streaks of orange and pink bleeding into the horizon. The streetlights flickered on in intervals, neon signs humming to life one by one, casting a dull glow over the damp pavement. The city had started its slow shift into night, headlights passing by in hazy streaks, the scent of car exhaust and distant rain hanging in the air.

    Outside, leaning against the brick wall just beside the entrance, Rio flipped through photos on her phone; grainy stills pulled from the security footage they’d reviewed earlier at the precinct. Following Lilia’s warrant, The Lantern was forced to send over the surveillance footage. Rio’s brows were drawn tight, the light from the screen casting a faint glow over her face as she studied each image carefully.

    They’d argued about it earlier at the precinct. Agatha had been convinced Lisa wouldn’t even be on the footage since nobody bothered to take note of it before they received the case, that they shouldn’t bother to check every angle, every frame. Rio had disagreed. Said it felt wrong and something was off about the whole damn thing.

    Lisa Moreno had walked out of The Lantern that night. That much they knew. But somewhere between that front door and the sidewalk, she’d disappeared. The problem was that security cameras covered every exit, every angle, and yet there was nothing. No sign of her getting into a car. No sign of her ducking into an alley. Just… gone.

    And now? Here they were. Going over it all again because Lisa Moreno shouldn’t have just vanished.

    Agatha sighed, shoving her hands into the pockets of her jean jacket. Her boots scuffed against the sidewalk as she shifted her weight, casting a glance at Rio. “Alright, walk me through it again,” she muttered. “We’ve got the front entrance. Cameras facing both the street and the lot across the way. No sign of Lisa on either?”

    Rio shook her head, still flipping through photos. “Nothing. Not in the parking lot, not on the sidewalk.” She swiped again, pausing at a timestamp. “Last frame we see her in? Right here. 11:43 PM. Walking toward the door. She was alone.”

    Agatha frowned, shifting her weight, casting a glance at the sidewalk in front of them. 11:43 PM. That meant she should’ve been visible on camera after she stepped outside. But she wasn’t.

    “She didn’t walk home?”

    Rio shook her head again, already knowing where Agatha was going. “No. Her apartment’s five blocks from here, and there’s cameras covering three out of the four intersections between here and there. We checked.” “And no car picked her up?”

    Rio looked up now, tucking her phone away. “If it did, it happened in a blind spot.”

    Agatha chewed on the inside of her cheek, gaze scanning the street, the dark alley just past the edge of the building. There had to be something. “Which is what we’re looking for now.”

    Agatha exhaled, glancing toward the entrance. “So, either someone tampered with the footage…”

    “….Or wait, show me exactly where our blind spot would be, Vidal.” Before Rio could respond, a voice cut through the air behind them. “You’re not the first badges sniffing around about Lisa.”

    Both women turned.

    The bouncer—a broad-shouldered guy in his late forties, arms crossed over his chest; watched them with mild disinterest. His breath curled in the cold, thick from the cigar dangling between his fingers. He took a slow drag, nodding toward them.

    “Some cop was here the day after she went missing. Asked the same shit.” Rio narrowed her eyes. “What do you mean?”

    The bouncer exhaled a cloud of smoke, tilting his head toward the entrance. “Came in like he owned the place. Asked about Lisa, wanted to see the security tapes. I let him. Not like I got anything to hide.” Agatha frowned, stepping closer. “Did he say what department he was from?”

    The guy shrugged. “Didn’t have to. He was a cop. Carried himself like one.”

    “Description?” Rio pressed.

    “White. Tall. Looked like he hadn’t slept in a week.” The bouncer scratched his jaw. “Wore a suit, but not the kind that fit right. Like he hated the damn thing. Oh, and he had a badge. Didn’t flash it, but he kept touching it. One of those nervous ticks.”

    Agatha and Rio exchanged a look. That wasn’t in the reports either. So, now they had found two discrepancies in the file for Lisa. No one bothered to check for footage, and now, someone had been here before them.

    Agatha didn’t react right away. She just let the information settle, rolling it over in her head, feeling the weight of it. Then after a second, she blinked, refocusing, and turned back to the bouncer.

    “You’re sure that’s all you remember?” Her voice was casual, but there was a firmness beneath it, a probing edge that told him she wasn’t interested in bullshit.

    The bouncer exhaled through his nose, flicking ash from his cigar onto the sidewalk. “Yeah. That’s all I got. He asked about Lisa, poked around a little, then left.”

    “Did he seem like he knew something?” Rio asked, stepping slightly closer, her black coat shifting with the movement.

    The bouncer shrugged, barely glancing at her. “I don’t know. He wasn’t exactly chatty. Just seemed… I don’t know. On edge. Kept looking around like he was expecting someone to be watching.”

    That didn’t sit right.

    Agatha let a slow breath out through her nose, nodding once. “Alright. Thanks.”

    The bouncer tipped his chin at them before turning back toward the bar entrance, exhaling smoke into the cold air.

    Agatha’s brows furrowed, her jaw tightening as she turned away from the bouncer. She stepped back toward Rio, lowering her voice just enough to keep it between them. “What the fuck?” she muttered, eyes flicking back toward the bar entrance. “Who was already here? We didn’t hear shit about that either in the reports.”

    Rio’s lips pressed into a thin line. “That’s not just an oversight,” she said quietly. “That’s intentional.”

    Agatha exhaled through her nose, crossing her arms over her chest. Her worn jacket creaked slightly with the movement, the cold air biting at her skin where the fabric gaped at the collar. Something about this wasn’t sitting right.

    They were both thinking it. Someone had been here before them. Someone who didn’t want their presence documented.

    Rio tapped the screen of her phone, flipping through the stills from the security footage, her gaze flickering over the bar’s exits, the angles of the cameras, the gaps in the coverage. Lisa had walked out of this place. But how? And who was that man the bar tender brought up during their first interrogation?

    Agatha sighed, dragging a hand through her hair. “Alright, well, I don’t—” She stopped.

    Her muscles tensed.

    Something shifted in her stance, just slightly, enough that Rio caught it immediately.

    Agatha’s voice dropped lower. “Vidal,” she murmured, not moving her head, but her eyes flicked subtly toward the right. “Five o’clock. Green hoodie. Looking at us like he’s about to bolt.”

    Rio didn’t move right away, didn’t turn her head too quickly. Instead, she exhaled like she was just thinking, fingers still idly swiping through the photos on her phone, keeping her body language loose. Then, carefully, she shifted her gaze just enough to spot him.

    The guy was thin, average height, but the tension in his posture stood out immediately. He was leaning against the side of the building, arms crossed, trying to blend in like he belonged there. But his eyes were darting. Not just toward them, but across street, an alleyway, the quickest route out. He knew he’d been made.

    Rio barely had time to register it before the man’s whole body sprung. He ran.

    “Shit—”, Agatha was already moving.

    Her boots hit the pavement hard, pushing off into a sprint as the guy took off down the sidewalk, shoving past a couple of people outside the bar. Rio was half a second behind her, heartbeat already spiking with adrenaline. “Police! Stop!” Agatha’s voice cut through the evening air, commanding, but the bastard didn’t even hesitate.

    The guy tore down the sidewalk, pushing through a small group of people lingering outside the bar. He was fast—too fast for someone who wasn’t used to running. His sneakers scraped against the pavement as he cut hard between two parked cars, heading toward the alleyway across the street. Agatha’s boots pounded against the pavement, her worn jeans flexing with every push forward as she dodged past a couple stepping out of the bar, barely missing them. Rio was right behind her, her breath steady, movements precise.

    The guy glanced back once, his hood slipping just slightly, revealing a shock of dark hair before cutting hard to the left. Straight into the alley. Big fucking mistake.

    “Bad choice, asshole,” Agatha muttered under her breath, pushing harder. She didn’t slow, didn’t hesitate. Agatha knew a dead-end when she saw one, and unless this guy had some secret passage out the other side, he was screwed.

    She followed, her shoulder brushing against the brick wall as she made the turn. The alley was narrow, dumpsters lining one side, the distant hum of the city dampened between the buildings. The guy was already halfway down, his sneakers skidding against the damp pavement as he tried to push harder.

    But he wasn’t getting away.

    Agatha pushed forward, reaching, and just as his foot lifted off the ground for another step—

    She lunged.

    Her hand caught the back of his hoodie, yanking hard. The guy let out a grunt as the force jerked him back, his balance shifting, his foot slipping against a slick patch of pavement. He twisted, trying to wrench free, but Agatha had him.

    “Fucking—” He tried to push up, but Agatha was on him in a second, her knee pressing down between his shoulder blades, her free hand already grabbing for his wrist.

    “Get the fuck off me!”

    “Not happening.” Agatha’s grip tightened as she dragged him up, shoving him against the alley wall.

    He thrashed, but she had him pinned, her grip like iron. Rio caught up just as Agatha pressed her forearm against his chest. The guy was younger than she’d expected—early twenties, but his eyes were attentive, flicking between them like he was already calculating his next move. Trying to find an exit before one even existed.

    “Why’d you run?” Rio asked, her voice slightly out of breath but still controlled.

    “I didn’t do shit,” he spat, twisting against Agatha’s hold. Agatha scoffed, shifting her weight just enough to keep him locked down. She’d taken down bigger men than him before. She wasn’t worried. “Right. Because innocent people always take off the second they see cops.” His breathing hitched, his pupils blown wide, chest rising and falling too fast. Rio could already see the cracks forming, the way panic was starting to override whatever bad decision had sent him running in the first place. Then, he cracked wide open.

    “I don’t know anything about Lisa!” The words tumbled out all at once, fast and sloppy. His voice cracked in the middle, his own panic swallowing him whole. “I swear!”

    Rio felt her stomach twist.

    Agatha’s grip tightened immediately. She hadn’t said Lisa’s name, and neither had Rio.

    The realization hit him a second too late. His mouth snapped shut, and his face twisted in horror.

    The man’s jaw then clenched, teeth grinding together, his breath shallow. Nostrils flared like a cornered animal, eyes darting between her and Rio, calculating. Agatha could feel the tension in his muscles, the way his body went tight, bracing for something.

    Rio had seen it a hundred times before, could feel it before it happened— the second a suspect made up their mind. The second fear turned into something dangerous. A split-second decision.

    Desperation.

    Agatha felt it too. She could feel the tension beneath her hands, the way his muscles coiled, tight, ready to spring. The kind that made men stupid. The kind that sent them lunging for a weapon or running straight into traffic. The kind that got people killed.

    Her fingers dug into his shoulder, pressing him harder into the pavement. “Don’t.”

    The warning was low, sharp. But he didn’t listen.

    Agatha saw it happen in real time, the moment the fight-or-flight instinct kicked in, the moment he realized he had just screwed up beyond fixing. She barely had time to react before his body shifted, before his free hand grabbed at something on the ground.

    Suddenly, the bottle came from nowhere. A blur of jagged edges, a flash of movement in the dim alley light.

    He swung. Wild, uncalculated, the arc of his arm fueled by pure panic. A sickening crunch of glass meeting flesh, a sharp, shattering pain exploding across the side of Agatha’s face.

    Her balance faltered and she stumbled back, blinking hard. Agatha’s free hand flying up to touch her eyebrow. Her fingers come away slick with red, blood already running down the side of her face.

    Rio shouted something, maybe her name, but Agatha barely heard it over the ringing in her ears. Her hand flew to the side of her head instinctively, fingers pressing against the wet heat seeping into her hair, her pulse hammering against her palm.

    But the suspect didn’t wait. He shoved past them both, knocking into Rio’s shoulder hard enough to throw her off balance. By the time she turned, he was already gone, disappearing as his footsteps echoed against the pavement.

    Agatha was still standing, but barely. Her breath came sharp through her nose, the pain pounding now, radiating from the deep gash above her eye. She could feel the blood dripping down, hot against the cold air, slipping past her temple and down the side of her cheek.

    “I’m fine,” Agatha muttered, but the words came out strained, breathless. She squeezed her eyes shut for half a second before forcing them open again, trying to steady herself, ignoring the way her balance felt just a little off.

    Agatha exhaled, shaking her head, wiping at the blood with the back of her sleeve. “That son of a bitch,” she growled.

    Rio stared at her. Then, voice clipped and controlled, “We’re going to the hospital.”

    Agatha scoffed immediately. “No, we’re not.”

    Rio’s jaw clenched. “Agatha, you’re bleeding.”

    “Yeah, no shit,” Agatha muttered, glancing down at her sleeve, now smeared with red. She grimaced but didn’t seem particularly fazed. “It’s just a cut.”

    Rio took a slow breath, clearly trying to keep herself from strangling her on the spot. “It’s not just a cut. You need stitches.”

    Agatha wiped at her forehead again, smearing more blood in the process, and sighed. “Look, I’ll clean it up. Slap a bandage on it. I’m not wasting three hours at a goddamn ER for this.”

    Rio let out a frustrated sigh through her nose, her patience fraying at the edges. “You’re bleeding all over the damn place, Agatha,” eyes locked on the deep gash cutting through her eyebrow.

    Agatha gave her a flat look. “It’s not that bad.”

    Rio’s eyes flicked to her brow, to the blood trailing down the side of her face, staining her sleeve, dripping onto the pavement. Her jaw tightened. “You’re kidding me Harkness.” Rio said, her tone dangerously even. But Agatha wasn’t listening.

    She shifted her weight, eyes already scanning the alley, the street beyond. The bastard was getting away. That little shit sucker-punched her with a damn bottle, and now he was gone, running off like he thought he could just disappear into the night.

    Not a chance.

    She needed to move. Her pulse hammered, frustration curling tight in her chest. This was what mattered. Not some stupid cut.

    “I don’t have time for this,” she snapped. “We need to get the guy, not stand here debating whether or not I can take a fucking hit.”

    She shifted forward, her boots scraping against the pavement as she tried to get up. She wasn’t wasting another second on this conversation. Rio must have sensed it because the second Agatha moved, Rio moved too. Her hand shot out, fingers gripping Agatha’s arm, anchoring her in place. “No. You’re done.”

    Agatha whipped around, her patience snapping like a live wire. “Get the hell off me, Vidal.”

    Rio didn’t flinch. Didn’t let go.

    Agatha yanked against her grip, but Rio held firm, her jaw set, her eyes cold and unwavering. “You are not running after him like this.” Agatha let out a sharp, humorless laugh. “Watch me.”

    Rio’s grip tightened. “You can barely fucking stand.”

    Agatha jerked her arm again, frustration spiking white-hot through her veins.

    “I don’t need you babysitting me, Vidal.” Her words were a deliberate strike. “You don’t get to tell me what I can and can’t do.” Something flickered across Rio’s face.

    Not anger. Something Agatha didn’t want to look at too closely. For a second, neither of them moved. Agatha’s pulse thundered in her skull, her body swaying just slightly, blood still seeping warm and steady down her temple.

    Rio stared her down, unshaken.

    Then, voice low, too calm. “You’re done.”

    Agatha’s stomach twisted. Not from the pain. Not from the blood. But from the way Rio was looking at her. She wasn’t going to let this go, nor let Agatha tear herself apart over this.

    But worse than that was the sound of his footsteps fading. The chance slipping through her fingers. She clenched her jaw.

    Then, finally, Agatha exhaled, slow and sharp, her shoulders dropping just slightly.

    Then, with an exaggerated sigh, she lifted her hands in defeat, fingers still sticky with drying blood.

    “Fine,” she muttered, voice edged with reluctant surrender. Rio didn’t loosen her grip. “Say it like you mean it.”

    Agatha scoffed, but the fight had already left her.

    Her body felt heavy, the adrenaline burning out, leaving only exhaustion in its wake.

    She let her weight shift into Rio’s hold for just a second—not enough to admit defeat, but enough to say she was done.

    “…I mean it.”

    Rio exhaled, releasing her slowly, carefully.

    Agatha didn’t argue. Instead, she blinked at Rio, the corner of her mouth twitching up just slightly, her expression slipping into something almost amused.

    She swiped at her forehead again, grimacing at the fresh smear of red, then shot Rio a sideways glance.

    “Didn’t know you had it in you to be that firm, Vidal.”

    She licked her lips, considering for half a second before tilting her head with an almost playful squint.

    “Kind of hot, actually.”

    Rio let out a sharp, exhausted breath, clearly at the end of her patience. “Get in the car, Agatha, I’m taking you to the hospital.” Agatha, as stubborn as always, just rolled her eyes. “I don’t need a hospital. Just patch me up.”

    Rio exhaled, clearly trying to reel in the frustration. “Where?” she demanded, voice clipped.

    Agatha clenched her jaw, exhaling through her nose like she was physically biting back her frustration. Her eyes flicked away, scanning the street like she was searching for another option, but there wasn’t one. She knew it. Still, she resisted.

    Her shoulders rose with another breath, tense, like she was preparing for a fight she already lost. Finally, through gritted teeth, barely above a mutter, she said, “My place is the closest.”

    Agatha sat there for a second longer than she should have, blinking hard, trying to will the dizziness away. The blood had slowed to a steady trickle, warm against her temple, but it still blurred the edge of her vision, dripping into the collar of her jacket. She wiped at it again, only managing to smear it further.

    Rio stepped closer. “Fine, come on.” she said, voice firm but not unkind, offering her hand.

    Agatha looked at it. Stubborn as ever.

    “I can stand up just fine, Vidal,” she muttered, but the words had less bite than usual.

    Rio didn’t move, didn’t drop her hand. “Humor me.”

    Agatha clenched her jaw, glancing away, the muscle in her cheek ticking like this was some kind of battle she didn’t want to lose.     No. She was not going to stand in her shower, shaking, wrecked, needing something she couldn’t have.

    Her hand shot out, gripping the hot water knob and twisting it all the way off.

    A gasp ripped from her throat as the sudden shock of cold water slammed against her skin, stealing the breath from her lungs. Her muscles locked up, her body instinctively recoiling from the chill, but she didn’t move away. She needed it.

    The sharp sting of cold, the way it bit into her shoulders, her spine, her chest—anything to rip her out of whatever haze she’d let herself slip into. Her hands were again pressed against the tile, fingers splayed wide, nails digging in like she could physically ground herself. She let the water batter her, let it steal the warmth Agatha had left behind.

    Her breath came fast and sharp, each inhale stinging against the back of her throat, but it was working. The heat under her skin, the unbearable pressure coiled low in her stomach finally dulled. Not gone, but manageable. Rio squeezed her eyes shut, letting the water run over her face, the cold burrowing deep into her bones. She stayed there, shivering, until she could finally breathe again.

    Rio reached for the knobs, shutting the water off completely. The sudden absence of the relentless downpour left only the sound of her own ragged breathing, the faint dripping of water from her skin onto the tile. She stood there for a moment, eyes closed, forcing herself to feel nothing. Then, with a sharp inhale, she stepped out onto the mat, reaching for a towel.

    The soft fabric dragged over her skin as she dried herself off, her movements precise, methodical. She started with her arms, then her shoulders, working her way down with the same rigid control she applied to everything else in her life. If she could just go through the motions, if she could just keep moving, keep busy, keep herself wrapped in routine, maybe she wouldn’t think about how her body still felt like it was buzzing. Rio swallowed hard and turned to the mirror. Her reflection was a stark contrast to the composed, sharp-edged version of herself she showed the world. Loose strands of dark hair clung to the sides of her face and her skin was flushed—not from the heat of the shower, but from something that had settled into her bones.

    She grabbed a second towel, running it through her damp hair before reaching for a brush. The repetitive motion was grounding. She combed through the tangles with practiced efficiency, parting her hair down the middle before weaving it into a loose ponytail, fingers working automatically, like muscle memory.

The loose ponytail, stray hairs, and shirt that clung just slightly. She looked... normal. Put together enough.

The early morning air in her apartment was still, quiet—aside from the faint sounds of the city beyond her window as it was waking up, the occasional honk of a horn, the distant rumble of the L-train. It was grounding in a way, a reminder that the world kept moving, even when she felt stuck.

She twisted the top back on and set it over the burner, turning the flame on with a sharp flick of her wrist.

    She waited by leaning against the counter, arms crossing loosely over her chest, her gaze drifting and not really looking at anything, but not quite closing her eyes either. She should be thinking about the case and running through crime scene information in her head, revisiting witness statements, piecing together the holes they still hadn’t filled. Instead, all she could think about was Agatha.

    The thought sat like a stone in her chest because at the end of the day, Agatha was... Agatha. Carefree, rough around the edges, always smirking like she had the upper hand in whatever conversation she was in. Nothing phased her.

She grabbed a mug from the cupboard, poured the thick, dark liquid into it, and set the pot back on the stove. She took her coffee black, no sugar, no cream. The bitterness suited her. But lately, she had found herself reaching for the sugar, just a pinch, enough to dull the edge. She wasn’t sure why. Maybe she was just getting soft.

    Rio stirred absentmindedly, watching the grains dissolve as she lifted the cup to her lips, letting the heat seep through the ceramic, warming her fingers. The first sip was strong, grounding, but it did nothing to quiet the static in her head.

    Rio set her mug down and reached for the first file.

She meticulously combed through witness statements, cross-referenced timelines, and scrutinized security footage frame by frame. She threw herself into it, into the one thing she could control, into the job that had once been her entire life.

    But even as she worked, her body betrayed her.

Her fingers occasionally drummed against the wood of her dining table, tapping out an impatient beat, only stopping when she caught herself and clenched her hands into fists.

    Her focus should’ve been sharp as she had been trained for this. The years of conditioning, discipline, and methodical thinking were for this very purpose. But of course, every so often, her mind would drift, tugging her back to someone else. Agatha.

    She shook her head as if she could physically dislodge the thought from her brain. It was stupid.

    Agatha wouldn’t—couldn’t—like someone like her.

    Rio knew what women Agatha probably went for. The kind that could match her smirk for smirk, meet her teasing head-on without missing a beat and didn’t get flustered when Agatha flirted. They probably didn’t have to force themselves to breathe when Agatha looked at them a certain way. That wasn’t Rio. She was too serious, too controlled, too boring for someone like Agatha Harkness.

    She needed to be brutally honest with herself and admit that she wasn’t the type of woman someone like Agatha would want. Agatha, being the woman she was, simply walked into a room and owned it without trying and laughed in the face of consequences; pushing boundaries just to see what would happen.

    Agatha was a fire that burned reckless and untamed, a real force of nature. And Rio? Rio followed rules, took careful steps and weighed consequences before making decisions. She had spent her entire life proving she was steady, reliable, the kind of woman who could be trusted to do things the right way. She didn’t live on the edge the way Agatha did. Even if she had let herself be something else for just that one moment, it wouldn’t have changed anything because Agatha had options. Rio wasn’t stupid. Agatha definitely wasn’t a of woman who sat around waiting for something she wanted. If she needed company, she could pick up her phone, scroll through her contacts, and find someone to entertain her for the night.

    Someone who wasn’t stiff.

    Someone who didn’t hesitate or hold back.

    Someone who matched her.

    Not an ex-FBI agent with too many walls, a woman clawing her way back from the wreckage of her career. Rio overanalyzed everything and wasn’t good at playing Agatha’s game nor built to take risks like she did. Agatha definitely wanted a woman who could keep up, made things easy and wouldn’t overthink a kiss for an entire goddamn day. Maybe the detective had been bored, and Rio just had happened to be there. That thought settled deep in her chest, heavy and hollow, suffocating her in its simplicity. Rio clenched her jaw, flipping a page in the case file trying to shove the feeling aside before it took root.

    It didn’t matter.

    Agatha probably didn’t even think about her or the kiss; and if she had any sense, she shouldn’t either.

    Chapter 8: Chasing Shadows

    Rio turned to look at the thin man—boy, in front of her, brow raised in silent impatience, his expression far too smug for her liking. “Why do you look like you’ve seen a ghost every time Harkness speaks?” Rio froze long enough for someone as perceptive as William to catch. “What are you talking about?” she replied, voice even, practiced. William tilted his head, smirking. “Oh, I don’t know. Maybe the way you’ve been sitting like a damn statue all morning. Maybe how your eyes do this thing where they snap away from her like you’re trying not to look —” He gestured vaguely at her face. “—or the way you were gripping that chair like it personally offended you.”

    Rio exhaled through her nose, loosening her posture. “You’re imagining things.”

    William snorted. “Sure, sure. But for someone who’s usually cool as hell, you’re acting real weird.”

    Rio held his gaze, unmoving. “We have a case to focus on, William.” Despite being waved off, he pushed further. “I mean, I get it. High-stress environments, adrenaline and close quarters really does things to people.” He shrugged, tone exaggeratedly casual.

    “I’ve seen this movie before. Lots of tension, lots of long stares, lots of—” He wiggled his fingers in the air like he was conducting some mystical force. “Unspoken things.”

    Rio clenched her jaw, unwilling to entertain this conversation for even a second longer. She turned without another word, her pulse still a fraction too fast.

    The ride to The Lantern was thick with unspoken words, the kind that filled the car like fog, pressing in from every angle. The rhythmic swish of the windshield wipers cut through the silence, swiping away the light drops clinging to the glass, neither of them spoke.

    Rio kept her eyes fixed on the road, hands steady on the wheel, knuckles taut but not white-knuckled. Her posture was perfectly upright, her grip exact, her expression unreadable. The only sound beyond the hum of the engine was the occasional rustle of fabric whenever Agatha shifted slightly in her seat.

    She had slouched against the passenger door, one arm propped against the window, her fingers idly tapping against the glass. Her sunglasses were perched on her nose again, even though the clouds outside barely let the sun peek through. She was doing it on purpose and pretending to be at ease, like none of this was bothering her.

    As if the woman that had permanent real estate in her mind wasn’t sitting less than two feet away, catching hints of her cologne mixed with the scent of coffee.

    The dashboard clock ticked forward, marking every second of heavy, stifling silence.

    Agatha finally broke it, her voice lazy but pointed. “You gonna talk, Vidal, or are we doing the whole brooding silence thing the whole way there?” Rio’s jaw flexed. She turned onto a side street, easing past a row of parked cars, her movements as precise as everything else she did. “Didn’t realize there was something that needed to be said.”

    Agatha huffed out a short laugh, humorless. “Yeah. Right.” Rio exhaled slowly through her nose, keeping her eyes on the road, but she could feel the weight of Agatha next to her. The way she stretched her legs out, one boot resting against the dash, the other planted firmly on the door. Everything about her was sharp edges wrapped in lazy, almost arrogant ease.

    “The bouncer,” Rio said finally, voice even. “If he really ‘went on vacation’ after mentioning another cop was there before us, that’ll be too much of a coincidence.”

    Agatha tilted her head slightly, shifting her weight, but didn’t look at her. “No shit.”

    Rio’s fingers curled just slightly against the wheel. “We need to push him this time.”

    Agatha smirked, still facing the window. “Oh, don’t worry, sweetheart. Pushing is what I do best.”

    Rio glanced at her then and caught that damn smirk. The flicker of her dangerous and reckless aura in her expression.

    Agatha shifted again, her voice lower this time, almost thoughtful. “William staying back to hunt down bottle guy might actually do us a favor. We split up, we cover more ground.”

    Rio nodded once. “That’s the idea.”

    Silence engulfed the car once more as the city moved past in a blur, buildings stretching tall and gray against the overcast sky. The wipers dragged across the windshield, clearing the heavier misting of rain that had started to settle. The heater hummed low, filling the car with artificial warmth that did nothing to ease the tension sitting thick between them. Agatha shifted; the silence was unbearable.

    “You drive like a cop,” she said suddenly, her voice cutting through the quiet.

    Rio blinked, glancing at her briefly before focusing back on the road. “I am a cop.”

    “I mean you drive like you’re on a goddamn presidential escort.” Agatha gestured vaguely toward the speedometer. “Hands at ten and two, checking every mirror like we’re about to get ambushed. Loosen up before you sprain something.”

    Rio scoffed. “Excuse me for not driving like a lunatic.”

    “Lunatic? I’ll have you know, I have a perfect driving record,” the older woman said, matter-of-factly.

    “You hit a mailbox two months ago.” Rio replied, “I saw it on your record last week.”

    Agatha rolled her eyes, “Okay, well, I wasn’t driving at the time. That was more of an accidental... parking mishap.”

    Rio shook her head, but there was something almost fond in the exhale that left her lips.

    Agatha smirked. “You smiling, Vidal?”

    “No.”

    “You are.” She pointed at Rio, grinning. “Look at you, loosening up on a Monday morning. I knew you had it in you.”

    Rio rolled her eyes, but her grip on the wheel had eased slightly. For a moment, the tension didn’t feel so suffocating.

    As they approached their target, The Lantern looked different in daylight; less of a dive bar and more of a washed-out, hollow shell of what it became at night. Without the neon glow of beer signs and the heavy bass thumping from inside, it was just another rundown joint tucked between old brick buildings, the kind of place people went to disappear for a few hours. Agatha and Rio stepped onto the curb, their feet crunching against damp gravel as they approached the entrance. The cold morning air still carried the faintest trace of last night’s liquor and cigarette smoke. It was quieter now, but there was always something lurking beneath the surface in places like this.

    Agatha was already annoyed. She didn’t like walking into situations blind, and that’s exactly what this felt like. The bouncer they had spoken to before and told them about the mystery cop who beat them to the security footage was nowhere in sight.

    Instead, standing at the door was someone new.

    The guy was built like a linebacker, arms crossed over his broad chest, a knowing smirk tugging at his lips. He looked them up and down as they approached, his gaze lingering on Rio’s blazer before flicking to Agatha’s boots, his expression unreadable but something about it crawling under her skin.

    “Morning, officers.” His voice was slick, too casual. “Or, detective and—” He gestured vaguely toward Rio. “Whatever you are.”

    Rio didn’t so much as blink. “Where’s the other guy?” she asked evenly, her tone just a little too sharp to be mistaken for casual interest. The bouncer let out a low chuckle, shaking his head like he had been waiting for the question. “Oh, him?” He clicked his tongue, rocking back slightly on his heels. “Yeah, he quit. Or went on vacation. Something like that.”

    That was bullshit. No one just quits after being asked a couple questions by cops, at least not without a damn good reason. Also, no one who just happens to leave this conveniently gets replaced by someone with that kind of smug look plastered on his face.

    Rio took a step forward, angling her body just enough to exude authority without overplaying it. “You don’t seem all that surprised we’re asking.” The bouncer just smirked. “Should I be?”

    Agatha wasn’t in the mood for games. “You tell us.”

    He lifted his hands in mock surrender. “Hey, I’m just the guy on the door. People come, people go. I don’t ask questions.”

    “Yeah?” Agatha cocked her head, crossing her arms. “Because you seem to know exactly who we’re looking for.”

    The bouncer’s smirk widened. “Lucky guess.”

    Rio’s eyes narrowed. The casual dismissal mixed with the overconfident attitude wasn’t just arrogance. It was someone who had been told they had nothing to worry about.

    Agatha took a slow step closer, lowering her voice just enough to make the words land differently. “See, the last guy was real helpful. Told us some interesting things. We were hoping to follow up.”

    “You don’t say.” His voice remained smooth, but the tension had settled in now, lurking beneath the surface. “Well, I hate to disappoint, but like I said —he’s not around anymore. And I don’t know when he’ll be back.” Agatha arched a brow. “Funny, you make it sound like we won’t find him.” The bouncer exhaled a slow breath through his nose, his patience thinning. “Lady, I don’t know what to tell you.”

    “You could start with his name.” Rio cut in, her voice quieter but laced with authority.

    The bouncer’s smirk returned, lazy, dismissive. “What does it matter?” “It matters,” Rio said, taking another deliberate step forward, “because the last time we saw him, he had some valuable information. And now he’s gone.”

    “People leave jobs all the time.”

    “They do,” Agatha said, mirroring his smug tone. “But they don’t always get told to keep their mouths shut before they go.”

    The bouncer’s expression didn’t shift, the message was clear: Back off. Agatha let out a slow exhale through her nose, cocking her head as she studied the bouncer, her expression settling into something sharp and unimpressed.

    “Oh, I get it,” she said, voice smooth but laced with something biting. “You’re the new guy, right? Brought in to look tough, flex a little, maybe intimidate us just enough to make us walk away?” She let out a low, humorless chuckle. “Cute. I bet you even practiced your little I don’t know nothin’ routine in the mirror this morning.”

    The bouncer’s smirk faltered for just a second.

    Rio shifted beside her, but she didn’t stop Agatha, she knew what she was doing. And so did the bouncer.

    “I’m just doing my job, lady,” he said, though the cockiness had an edge now.

    Agatha’s grin was all teeth. “Your job, huh? Tell me, does your job include looking the other way when women disappear?”

    The moment hung there, thick and heavy.

    “I already told you—”

    Agatha clicked her tongue, cutting him off. “Yeah, yeah, I heard you the first time. ‘People leave jobs all the time.’” She threw his own words back at him with mocking precision, stepping closer, her voice dropping lower. “But see, that’s not what happened here, is it?”

    The bouncer’s jaw tensed.

    Agatha smiled wider, sensing the crack. “What’s the matter? Cat got your tongue? Or is it your boss?”

    His nostrils flared, but he didn’t take the bait.

    “Alright, fine.” She threw her hands up, stepping back dramatically. “You wanna play it that way? Cool. We’ll just do things the old-fashioned way.” She turned toward Rio, flashing a grin that didn’t reach her eyes. “You wanna guess how many violations this place has stacked up? ‘Cause I’m thinking a lot. And I’m thinking that if we start digging, someone’s gonna have a real bad day.”

    Rio exhaled slowly, playing along, her tone calm but deliberate. “Liquor license inspections, fire code violations, unlicensed security…” She trailed off, tilting her head. “I’d say several bad days.”

    The bouncer’s hands twitched at his sides, but he kept his mouth shut. Agatha shrugged, fake sympathy dripping from her voice. “Shame, really. I’d hate to see such a fine establishment get shut down over a few minor infractions. But hey—” She raised her hands again. “I get it. You’re just doing your job, right?”

    The bouncer’s jaw clenched. His cocky grin was gone now, and his weight had shifted, like he was debating whether or not to throw them out. Agatha leaned in slightly, her voice barely above a whisper now. “But you and I both know… the last guy? He didn’t quit.”

    “You wanna rethink that answer for me, big guy?” she murmured, her voice as casual as if they were discussing the weather. “Or should I go ahead and call the city? Because I’ve got nothing but time.”

    The bouncer’s hands curled into fists at his sides, and for a second, she thought he might take a swing at her.

    Rio spoke, calm and cool. “Did he seem scared?”

    The bouncer hesitated, then gave the slightest nod. That was all they needed.

    Agatha smirked, stepping back. “See? Wasn’t so hard, was it?” The bouncer shot her a glare but didn’t say another word. Rio tugged at Agatha’s sleeve, her voice smooth. “We’re done here.” Agatha didn’t respond right away. She just stood there for an extra second, letting the silence sit heavy between them, watching the bouncer shift under its weight. Letting him feel it and wonder if she’d push further. Then, with a slow, deliberate pivot, she turned on her heel and walked away, her boots scuffing against the pavement. Stepping outside, Agatha shoved her hands deep into the pockets of her jean jacket, her fingers curling into fists. The weight of frustration sat heavy in her chest, pressing against her ribs, winding tighter with every second.

    They had been right. Someone had gotten to the first bouncer before them, but instead of feeling like progress, it felt like they’d just been played. Agatha exhaled sharply, her breath coming out in a short, irritated huff. “We’re fucking stupid,” she muttered, more to herself than to Rio. Rio turned her head slightly, her jaw tightening. “Agatha—” “No.” Agatha ripped a hand from her pocket, gesturing vaguely behind them. “We knew something was off, we knew another cop was already here, and we still waited? We let them get ahead of us?” She let out a humorless laugh, shaking her head. “We should’ve come back the next fucking day.” “Agatha,” Rio repeated, sharper now, but Agatha was too wound up to stop. “We gave them time,” she went on, her voice edged with something close to fury. “Time to clean up whatever mess they made, time to scare off the only person who could’ve told us what the hell is going on. And now he’s gone.” She turned on her heel, pacing a tight line, her body wound so tight it looked like she might throw a punch just to get the energy out. “We played this stupid,” she muttered again.

    Rio stepped in front of her, voice low and firm. “Stop.”

    Agatha’s head snapped up; Rio didn’t waver. “Stop and think.” “You’re pissed. So am I,” Rio continued, voice even, controlled, but carrying an undeniable weight. “But throwing a tantrum about it isn’t going to fix anything.”

    Agatha let out a sharp scoff. “A tantrum?”

    Rio held her ground, unwavering. “Yes. A tantrum.”

    They stared at each other, tension crackling between them. Rio exhaled slowly. “We didn’t lose,” she said, her voice calm, but edged with something unshakable. “The new guy slipped. He practically admitted the last bouncer was scared into leaving.”

    Agatha swallowed, her breathing still heavy, her fingers twitching at her sides.

    “That means we’re on the right track,” Rio continued. “And if we let this frustration take over, we start making mistakes. We can’t afford that.” Agatha clenched her jaw, swallowing back the immediate urge to snap something else, to keep pushing, to keep raging. She let out one last sharp breath, tilting her head back to glare up at the gray Chicago sky like it had personally wronged her. Then she straightened, rolling her neck back, and exhaled hard through her nose.

    “Whatever,” she muttered, raking a hand through her hair before shoving it back into her pocket. “Let’s just get back to the precinct. Twink Boy should have bottle-smash asshole waiting for us.”

    Rio blinked, her jaw tensing. “You’re talking about an active suspect, Harkness.”

    “I’m talking about a guy who cracked a bottle over my head and ran like a bitch,” Agatha shot back, her voice stern, her boots scuffing against the pavement as she turned on her heel. “And I’d like to have a little chat with him.”

    ~

    The tires hadn’t even fully settled when Agatha threw the car door open and stepped out, the sharp slam reverberating through the parking lot. She wasn’t even thinking about it, merely acting on pure frustration, on the simmering red-hot anger that had been burning in her chest ever since they left The Lantern. The sting of failure sat sour in her throat. Someone had gotten to that bouncer first, scrubbed their only lead clean before they could sink their teeth into it. Now they were back at square one. Her boots hit the pavement hard as she stalked toward the precinct, her hands still shoved deep into her jacket pockets, fingers curled into tight, bloodless fists. She needed to hit something and fix this. She wasn’t going to let this keep happening nor keep letting someone else get ahead of them. They were already playing catch-up, and Agatha was sick of it.

    “Agatha,” Rio’s voice came sharp behind her, clipped with warning. Agatha ignored it.

    “Agatha, slow down—”

    “For what?” she shot back over her shoulder, not stopping, not even hesitating as she pushed forward. “So we can sit around, twiddle our thumbs, and wait for whoever’s fucking us over to clean up more evidence?”

    Rio’s footsteps picked up, closing the distance between them. “So we can be smart about this,” she countered, voice firm. “Charging in angry isn’t going to get us answers.”

    Agatha let out a sharp, humorless laugh. “Yeah? Well, maybe I’m done playing smart.”

    The entrance doors swung open with a heavy creak, and the precinct’s familiar buzz hit her all at once—phones ringing, voices overlapping, the distant click-clack of keyboards in motion. The air smelled like defeat, and the city’s bitter chill still clinging to the walls.

    William barely had time to glance up from his desk when she blew past him.

    “Hey—” His brows shot up.

    “Not now,” Agatha snapped, already moving.

    “Jesus,” William muttered, watching her go. He turned to Rio as she followed after her, breath tight with irritation. “You wanna handle your wife before she burns the place down?”

    Rio exhaled sharply through her nose. “If I could do that, you think we’d be in this situation?” She didn’t slow, didn’t break stride. “Where’s our suspect?”

    “Holding.” William gestured vaguely toward the interrogation rooms. “Knock yourself out.”

    Agatha didn’t wait. She cut a sharp path past the cubicles, past the worn-out chairs filled with tired-looking detectives nursing their third or fourth cups of coffee. The dull, flickering overhead lights cast long shadows over the cracked linoleum, and the metallic scent of old filing cabinets hung thick in the air.

    Her target was ahead in the interrogation rooms. A row of them lined the far side of the building, dim and claustrophobic, sectioned off by reinforced glass and thick steel doors. The hallway leading to them was quieter, like stepping into a different world. The only sound was the distant hum of a heater vent rattling in the ceiling.

    She could already see the faint glow of the one-way mirror from the room ahead, the flickering bulb above the door giving off a harsh, artificial light. “Agatha, just—” Rio tried again, right behind her, but Agatha spun around just long enough to cut her off.

    “If you’re about to tell me to calm down, Vidal, I swear to god—” “I wasn’t,” Rio said coolly, holding her gaze steady. “Please just don’t get us both benched.”

    Agatha scoffed, turning back toward the interrogation room. She pushed through the door without hesitation, the handle rattling in her grip as she stepped inside.

    The suspect sat cuffed to the bolted-down metal table, his wrists resting on the scratched-up surface. The metal chair beneath him squeaked as he shifted, his gaze flicking up—uneasy. He was trying to look nonchalant, but Agatha could already see the nerves bleeding through. The way his fingers flexed, the way his jaw tightened ever so slightly.

    The moment the door clicked shut behind her, she grabbed the spare chair and dragged it across the floor, letting the godawful screeching noise ring out before turning it around in one swift motion and dropping herself onto it, arms draping over the backrest. She leaned in slightly, resting her elbows on the table, eyes sharp, unyielding.

    “Remember me, asshole?”

    The man’s jaw clenched. He exhaled through his nose, his fingers flexing against the cuffs. He didn’t say anything, but Agatha could see it in his face as his pupils flicked toward the door, the way his shoulders squared like he was trying to steel himself.

    She leaned in slightly, voice dripping with sarcasm. “Didn’t get a good enough look before you threw a bottle at my face?” She paused, tilting her chin. “Or were you too busy running like a little bitch?” The guy’s nostrils flared, his lips pressing into a thin line. “You gonna talk?” Agatha continued, her voice smooth, sharp, a blade wrapped in velvet. She gestured vaguely at the cuffs securing him to the table. “Or do I have to start getting creative?”

    Rio stood just outside the interrogation room, arms folded tightly across her chest, her jaw set as she watched through the one-way glass. The dim light cast sharp angles over her face, but her eyes were fixed on Agatha. She was pacing now, slow and deliberate, her fingers drumming idly against the back of the chair she had just straddled. Rio could read her body language like a book, Agatha seemed like she was holding back from lunging across the table.

    The man wasn’t talking. He was looking at her with that same tight-lipped, locked-jaw stubbornness that Rio had seen too many times before. She knew Agatha wasn’t exactly the type for patience in these situations. And after everything—the dead ends, the missing bouncer, the case slipping further out of their hands—she was already walking into this interrogation with a short fuse.

    That was the problem, Agatha didn’t do well with feeling powerless. She liked to push, liked to press down until something cracked. And right now, with the way she kept tapping at her bandage like it was a reminder of the bottle that hit her, Rio could tell she was balancing on the edge. Inside, Agatha tilted her head, voice low, mocking. “What’s wrong? Are we a little shy?”

    The suspect smirked.

    As if on cue, Agatha’s fingers curled against the back of the chair, knuckles going white for just a second—then, just as suddenly, she pushed herself up. The legs of the chair scraped loudly against the floor as she kicked it aside, the sharp noise making the suspect flinch.

    Rio stiffened outside the glass, jaw clenching.

    Agatha didn’t sit back down. Instead, she took her time, circling the table like a wolf sizing up its prey. Slow, deliberate steps, the worn soles of her boots barely making a sound against the tile.

    She leaned in close, right into the bastard’s ear, her voice dropping to something low and dangerous. “You think this is funny?”

    The man didn’t answer, but Rio saw the way his throat bobbed, the way his shoulders tensed just slightly. Agatha smiled, all sharp teeth and barely contained anger.

    “You hit me.” She tapped her bandage for emphasis. “What do you think is the worst thing that’s gonna happen to you today?”

    She placed a heavy hand on the top of his head, fingers pressing in just enough to make him sweat.

    “See, here’s the thing. I’m already in a bad mood.” Her voice was deceptively casual, but there was something lethal coiled underneath it. “We’re already getting fucked with in this case. Every lead? Gone. Every witness? Scared shitless. And now, here you are, sitting all smug like you don’t know anything?”
