    Her fingers flexed against his scalp.

    “That pisses me off.”

    Then, before he could react, she shoved his head to the side, not hard enough to actually hurt him—but just enough to rattle him. To make sure he felt how thin her patience was.

    The man tensed, his breath hitching as he shot a glance toward the mirror. Agatha’s gaze flicked up too, locking onto her own reflection, as if she knew Rio was watching. And she didn’t care.

    His fingers twitched against the tabletop, his knee bouncing under the table. His eyes flickered to the mirror—once, twice, then back down to his lap, shifting side to side like his skin didn’t fit right. He had started sweating, the kind that beaded at the temples and slid down the back of the neck in slow, anxious drops.

    Agatha leaned in, slow and deliberate, planting both palms on the cold steel table. She tilted her head, eyes sharp, voice cutting through the thick, oppressive silence. “You look nervous.”

    The man swallowed. Didn’t answer.

    She hummed, dragging her tongue along her teeth before she leaned in even closer, her voice dropping into something quieter, deadlier. “What’s wrong? You were all talk when you had a bottle in your hand. You were real bold when you ran like a little bitch after hitting me.” She tapped at the bandage on her brow, eyes locking onto his. “So what happened? You shy?” His jaw clenched. His fingers curled into loose fists on the table, but his shoulders curled inward like he wanted to make himself smaller. Agatha pressed forward, voice steady, calm, but with a razor-sharp edge. “I don’t have time for your little silent act. My patience?” She flattened her hands against the table, tapping her fingers once, twice. “Thin.” Still, no answer.

    Her eyes flickered toward his leg—bouncing, restless.

    She lifted her chin. “Who are you covering for?”

    The reaction was instant, a twitch of the fingers. The flicker of fear behind his eyes. Agatha smirked. There it is.

    “That’s the first real reaction I’ve gotten out of you. Lets try again, yeah?” He stared at the table, his breath coming a little faster now. Sweat dripped down the side of his temple.

    Agatha reached out and flicked the metal cuff around his wrist, making the chain rattle against the table. The sound echoed in the small room, sharp and grating. “Clock’s ticking, buddy.”

    The man still didn’t look at her. But he kept looking over her shoulder. His gaze darted towards the mirror before jerking away, like he regretted it. She turned her head slightly, tracking his line of sight. Who the hell was he expecting to be there?

    The air in the room shifted, charged with something unspoken, thick with unease.

    Agatha’s voice dropped, nothing but a sharp whisper now. “You know something.”

    His breathing picked up.

    Before he could shift again, she grabbed his face, her fingers digging into his cheeks, squeezing just enough to make it uncomfortable. His lips pushed together awkwardly under the pressure, his breath stuttering as his eyes finally snapped to hers.

    “Look at me,” she ordered, voice low, dangerous.

    His nostrils flared, his eyes flicking toward the mirror again, but Agatha didn’t let him look away.

    “You know something.”

    Her grip tightened for just a second before she shoved his face away, making his head jerk to the side. He blinked rapidly, breathing heavier now, his fingers curling against the tabletop.

    Agatha’s voice dropped, nothing but a sharp whisper now. “You know something.”

    “I don’t have the fucking patience for this.” Her voice was a low growl, a slow simmering rage bubbling just beneath the surface. “You think you’re gonna sit here and play dumb like you don’t know exactly what the fuck is going on?”

    The guy swallowed hard, but Agatha wasn’t finished.

    Agatha let out a dark chuckle, shaking her head. “Look at you. Fucking pathetic. You’re sweating like a pig and can’t even look me in the eye.” She leaned down, right beside his ear, her voice dropping to something dangerously soft. “You’re scared.”

    His breath hitched.

    She pulled back just slightly, tilting her head. “What’s the matter, huh? You worried about who’s watching? That it?”

    No response.

    Agatha grabbed his shirt collar in one quick movement, yanking him forward, his chair scraping against the floor with an ugly screech. His breath sharpened, but she didn’t stop.

    “Let me make this real fucking clear for you, asshole.” Her voice was low, razor-edged, cutting through the tension in the room like a knife. “Whoever you’re protecting? They’re not the ones sitting in this chair. You are.” Her grip tightened, knuckles going white against his shirt. “And if you don’t start talking, I’m gonna make your life a living fucking hell.”

    The suspect’s breathing came faster now, his eyes darting behind her again, searching for something.

    Agatha yanked him forward another inch, lips curled back. “You think they’re coming to save you? That the big bad fucking boogeyman you’re covering for is gonna swoop in and make this all go away?” She scoffed, shaking him slightly. “Let me tell you something—whoever the fuck they are, they don’t give a shit about you.”

    Agatha let go of his collar with a rough shove, making him stumble back into his chair.

    “Last fucking chance.” Her voice was almost a whisper now, low and lethal. “Who the fuck are you covering for?”

    His chest rose and fell sharply with hands curled into fists against the table. Finally, his lips parted. “She didn’t just disappear.”

    The words were quiet. Shaky.

    Agatha’s brows furrowed. “What?”

    The man exhaled, shuddering. His throat worked around a swallow, and then—quietly, like he already knew he shouldn’t be saying it—“You’re asking the wrong questions.”

    The words sent a chill down Agatha’s spine.

    The tension in the room cracked like a gunshot as the door swung open, Captain Delaney stepping inside with a presence that immediately sucked the air from the room.

    “Captain,” Rio trailed behind him, her voice composed, as if she had already tried to reason with him before he walked in.

    Delaney barely spared her a glance. His attention was on the guy in the chair, who wouldn’t even look at him. “You’re done here,” he said flatly. Agatha turned, her jaw tight, “Excuse me?”

    Delaney sighed, running a hand over his jaw like this was already exhausting him. “Cut him loose.”

    Agatha scoffed loudly. “The fuck we are.”

    Delaney’s gaze snapped to her, sharp. “That how you talk to your commanding officer?”

    Rio sent her a sharp look before stepping in, her voice careful. “Captain, with all due respect, he assaulted an officer. That alone is—” “We don’t have enough,” Delaney cut in smoothly. “No priors, no outstanding warrants. He gets a lawyer; we’re looking at a headache.” Agatha bristled. “So what? We just let him walk? Like hell we do.” “I don’t want IA breathing down my neck.” Delaney cut in smoothly, waving her off. “And I sure as hell don’t want to deal with the paperwork.” Agatha’s face twisted and she took a step forward, voice heated. “He knows something. We were this close to breaking him, and you want us to just let him waltz out the fucking door?”

    Delaney shook his head, eyes narrowing. “You’re lucky you’re not getting written up for excessive force.”

    Rio spoke, “Sir, she wasn’t out of line—”

    “Stand down, Vidal.”

    Agatha let out a scoff, disbelief curling deep in her stomach. “Excessive force?” she repeated, eyes going wide before she let out another humorless laugh. “Jesus, Cap, you act like I put a gun in his mouth.” Delaney’s jaw tightened. “It’s not your call.”

    “Like hell it isn’t.” Agatha scoffed, hands curling into fists at her sides; she was pushing too hard.

    “Harkness,” she warned lowly, but Agatha wasn’t hearing it. She took a step forward, voice sharp. “This guy was there that night, Captain. He knows something, and you want to let him walk—” Delaney’s nostrils flared. “Enough.” His voice boomed through the room, snapping like a whipcrack.

    Agatha’s spine went rigid. A thick silence settled, heavy with the impact of that single word. Delaney’s face was carved from stone, his voice low. “You don’t run this precinct, Harkness.” Then, deliberately, he added, “And you sure as hell don’t question my orders.”

    Agatha’s jaw clenched, her eyes dark and blazing, but for the first time, she didn’t have a comeback.

    Rio noticed the way Agatha’s whole body tensed, how the heat of her anger sputtered, and just for a second, the weight of humiliation. Delaney adjusted his cuffs, like the conversation was already over. “Let him go.”

    Agatha was still stiff and caught between anger and something she’d never admit was shame. All she could do was stand down and look at her worn boots below her.

    Rio exhaled, slow and measured, before finally speaking for the both of them. “…Understood, sir.”

    Agatha’s head snapped toward her; disbelief flashed across her face. Delaney gave a small, satisfied nod. Then, without another word, he turned and walked out. The dissipating stride of his shoes against the linoleum floor was the only sound engulfing them both.

    Agatha let out a sharp, bitter laugh, shaking her head. Her hands clenched and unclenched at her sides, her whole body practically vibrating with anger. “Motherfucker,” she muttered under her breath, exhaling sharply through her nose.

    Rio watched her carefully, stepping forward, voice softer this time. “Agatha —”

    Agatha waved her off without looking back. “No,” she snapped, already heading for the door. “Not right now. I’m going for a walk.” Agatha stormed out of the precinct doors, her boots hitting the pavement hard as she shoved her hands into the pockets of her jacket, shoulders tight with frustration. The cold air hit her face, but it barely did anything to cool the heat still burning in her chest. She dragged a hand through her hair, shoving it back roughly before exhaling, sharp and slow.

    She was pissed. At Delaney. At the case. At the way they kept getting played.

    At Rio.

    The door creaked open behind her, the metal hinges groaning against the weight of the push. Agatha didn’t turn—she didn’t have to. She felt it, the shift in the air, the quiet, controlled presence stepping outside. "Agatha."

    Rio’s voice was steady, smooth. It cut through the cold like a scalpel, measured. Like she wasn’t feeling any of this.

    Agatha’s jaw tightened. Her hands curled into fists at her sides, her pulse still drumming hot beneath her skin. She kept walking, her boots scuffing against the pavement, quick, deliberate strides, like she could outrun whatever the hell this was.

    She heard Rio exhale behind her—short, sharp, frustrated. "Will you just stop for a second?"

    Agatha exhaled sharply through her nose, the cold air burning on the way out. Her shoulders were stiff, her pulse still high, still charged with everything she wasn’t saying. But she stopped. Jaw tight as she turned to face her.

    Rio stood there in the afternoon light, backlit by the glow of the precinct entrance. Always put together, always composed. Even now, even when everything was slipping through their fingers, she had the same measured, impassive expression that she always wore when she was trying to pretend she wasn’t feeling anything at all. The one that made Agatha want to push until she cracked.

    Only once. Just for her.

    Agatha’s breath came out slow, unsteady, curling in the cold between them. “What?” she bit out, her voice razor-sharp. “Gonna tell me to be smart again?”

    Rio’s lips parted, just slightly. A flicker of something passed over her face —hesitation, restraint—but it was gone in an instant, buried beneath that maddening control of hers. Instead of taking the bait, she exhaled, slow and careful, like she was collecting herself.

    “We need to think about this logically.”

    Agatha huffed out a humorless chuckle, shaking her head. “Yeah? Cause playing it safe is really working out for us.”

    The words came out harder than she meant, her voice rough, edged with frustration that had nothing and everything to do with Rio. But she didn’t walk them back. She couldn’t. She was too wound up. Too fucking tired of hitting dead ends, of feeling like they were chasing shadows while someone else pulled the strings.

    And Rio—fucking Rio—stood there as if none of it was touching her. Like she wasn’t suffocating in the same tension, in the same goddamn helplessness. Not feeling at all.

    "We have to be careful," Rio said, her voice even. “I don’t like it any more than you do, but we can’t just barrel through this."

    Agatha let out a sharp, humorless scoff, shaking her head. "No, of course not. Better to just sit back and let them fuck us over, right? Better to do nothing."

    Something flickered in Rio’s gaze but she smothered it just as fast. Agatha saw the way she inhaled, slow and steady, like she was forcing herself to stay measured, to not react.

    "I never said do nothing."

    "Yeah?" Agatha took a step forward. A challenge. She knew she shouldn’t, knew she was pushing too hard, but she didn’t care. Not right now. "Cause that’s what it looks like from where I’m standing."

    Rio’s jaw twitched. "You think I don’t care?"

    Agatha exhaled hard through her nose, her heart hammering now. The air between them had shifted and she could feel it curling under her skin. Rio’s stare was too much, and still, she didn’t look away.

    "If you did," she murmured, voice dropping lower, softer, meaner, "you wouldn’t be acting like this."

    Rio stiffened for a second enough for Agatha to see the crack. Her breath hitched, barely audible, and Agatha heard it. Saw the way Rio’s throat worked as she swallowed, the way her fingers began to mess with the button on the cuff of her shirt, like she was holding something back. Agatha was close enough to see it all. The hesitation was written in the tight set of Rio’s shoulders, in the tension coiled through every rigid inch of her. She wasn’t just talking about the case anymore and they both knew it. Agatha’s breath caught as she almost said everything and tore herself open, let her know what had been clawing at her chest since that night in the bathroom—since the second Rio kissed her back and then ran like it never happened.

    Agatha could feel her holding back. Rio was choosing silence over saying anything real. Over admitting that Agatha was right.

    And fuck, that pissed her off more than anything.

    She took a step back, shaking her head again, letting out a dry, humorless laugh. “That’s what you do, right? You shut down, you compartmentalize, you don’t let anything actually fucking get to you.” She scoffed again, stepping back another inch. “I don’t know why I expected anything else.” “I’m being careful,” Rio said, voice low, controlled. “You think I like this? You think I like standing here, acting like—” She cut herself off, exhaling hard through her nose.

    Agatha tilted her head, daring her to just fucking say it. Rio didn’t.

    “Jesus Christ,” Agatha let out, voice sharper now, harsher. “You’re such a fucking coward.”

    Rio flinched and began to shake her head, wanting to disagree. “You want to keep pretending?” Agatha pushed, stepping forward again, invading her space now. “Keep playing it safe? Keep being the perfect little by-the-book agent while we get fucked over—while you stand there and act like you don’t feel a goddamn thing?”

    Rio’s nostrils flared. Her jaw clenched so hard it looked like she was grinding her teeth, her breath shuddering out of her like she was barely holding it together.

    “Agatha, it’s not—” Rio’s voice cracked, barely above a breath, raw at the edges, like the words cost her something. “It’s not like that.” Agatha scoffed, harsh and sharp, cutting through the space between them. “Then what the fuck is it, Vidal?”

    Rio’s breathing was off, shoulders rising and falling too quickly, hands curling into fists like she was forcing herself to stay still. Agatha caught the way her gaze flickered, barely, down to Agatha’s lips, then back up again. Rio’s arm lifted, just barely, fingers reaching for Agatha’s cheek without realizing it. The movement was slow, careful, like she was testing the weight of it, like she was fighting herself every inch of the way. For a second, Agatha thought she was going to do it.

    She felt it in the air between them pressing down on her like a weight she couldn’t shake. Rio’s fingers hovered just barely, like she was about to reach out to touch her. Agatha could see it and feel the slow, unconscious, pull drawing them closer, the hesitation laced with something timid. Then, Rio’s hand curled into a fist and dropped back to her side, her body stiffening, her jaw locking like she was sealing something inside her chest, she looked away.

    “You’re a coward,” Agatha whispered, and it was deadly.

    Rio shook her head, frustrated now, dragging a hand through her hair before fingers gripped the back of her neck, tension coiling tight through every inch of her.

    Agatha exhaled sharply, stepping back so fast it felt like a physical tear. She shook her head like she could shake Rio out of her system. “Forget it.”

    Agatha spun on her heel, each step carrying the weight of everything she refused to say. The cold wind lashed against her skin whipping at the loose strands of her hair, but she barely felt it—not when her blood was still burning hot beneath the surface.

    She stalked forward, each inhale slicing through her like glass. She could still feel the ghost of Rio’s presence behind her, the heat of her gaze boring into the back of her skull. Even without turning around, she knew Rio was still standing there, unmoving, watching.

    Rio Vidal would always choose restraint.

    Maybe in every aspect of her life. Maybe that was just who she was. The agent who always played it safe, the woman who never let herself take too much, want too much. Agatha had spent the whole weekend coming to terms with it, but now? Now, she knew for certain.

    Rio was unreachable.

    The precinct doors came into view, glowing under the dull gray sky. The sharp buzz of city life pressed in with its hum of passing cars, distant wail of sirens, and the echo of heavy footsteps on wet pavement. It should’ve grounded her.

    She shoved through the doors, not pausing, not even sparing a glance behind her. But the weight in her chest was unbearable, pressing against her ribs, a suffocating ache that had nothing to do with the cold. Somewhere deep inside, she hated that small, desperate part of her that still wished Rio would chase after her.

    Chapter 9: Everything and Nothing

    Chapter Notes ah yes, the five love languages: words of affirmation, acts of service, gift giving, physical touch, and...angsty avoidance? what are you doing here?

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The room was quiet in the way only late nights could have it be, with its hushed voices in the distance, the faint hum of computers, and occasional rustle of paperwork. The overhead fluorescents had been switched off, leaving only the glow of the desk lamps to cast the room in a dim, warm orange hue. It softened the harsh edges of the space with long, jagged shadows, like the tangled case itself. It was just past 7 PM, late enough for exhaustion to settle into their bones, but not late enough to call it quits. Outside, the city never truly slept. The muffled wail of a distant siren echoed through the streets, followed by the occasional honk of a car horn. The air smelled stale, and like freshly printed paper, warm off the machine. It was the kind of scent that wove into your clothes after too many hours spent in the same chair, staring at the same board, trying to make sense of the senseless puzzle.

    A large suspect board stood before them, covered in a mess of crime scene photos, scribbled notes, and red strings mapping out the tangled connections between their suspects. The longer they stared at it, the more it started to feel like they were circling just out of reach with answers hidden beneath millions of layers of deception.

    Agatha stood a few feet away, arms crossed with blue eyes flicking between the evidence. She hadn’t said much all night. Not to Rio, anyway. She had actually spent the last week avoiding her, deliberately and painfully.

    Taking her lunch breaks at odd hours, leaving the precinct the second their shifts were over, staying buried in paperwork at her desk when she knew Rio was nearby. If Rio was in the break room, Agatha suddenly wasn’t hungry. If she heard Rio’s voice down the hall, she’d take the long way around.

    She made sure their paths didn’t cross for longer than necessary, never giving Rio the chance to pull her aside, or allowing for another quiet moment where Rio might try to talk.

    Agatha simply didn’t know what she would do if Rio tried. She wasn’t angry really, not anymore. But the hurt sat in her chest like a stubborn bruise, deep and lingering. Every clipped word and sharp glance from that argument outside the building replayed in her head like a bad song stuck on loop.

    But what was worse and crawled under her skin more than anything, was the fact that Rio hadn’t pushed nor chased after her that day. She had let Agatha walk away; it was hesitation.

    A hesitation that meant Rio had wanted it, wanted her, but still refused to do a damn thing about it.

    Somehow, that made Agatha feel even more stupid.

    Agatha stood stiffly, forcing her gaze back to the suspect board, pretending she wasn’t still hyperaware of the woman standing just a few feet away from her. Pretending she wasn’t still waiting for Rio to say something. Across the room, Rio was quieter than usual. More subdued. She had been trying, in small ways, to reach out and fix whatever had cracked between them, but Agatha wasn’t letting her.

    It wasn’t just the avoidance in the late lunches, the purposeful distance, the way Agatha’s eyes barely flicked to her before moving away. It was deeper than that, Rio felt it every time they were in the same room. The strain of her absence.

    Agatha wasn’t just keeping her at arm’s length, she was shutting her out completely. Rio knew why. Every time Agatha so much as glanced at her, the words came rushing back.

    "You’re a coward."

    "You don’t care."

    They had cut through her like a blade. Not because they were cruel, but because they were true.

    Agatha wasn’t wrong, Rio had run. She had spent years keeping people at a careful distance, spent years perfecting the art of restraint. Then Agatha came barreling into her life, with her sharp grins and relentless push, and suddenly, holding herself back felt unbearable.

    Yet she still did it even when Agatha was standing inches away, her breath warm against her lips and she had wanted to give in.

    Rio rolled a tension knot out of her shoulder, her eyes roaming over the photos and notes. She was trying to stay focused, and pretend everything wasn’t still sitting heavy between them. But every time her gaze flickered to Agatha’s still-healing cut, everything felt raw.

    She had put that bandage there and been the one to tilt Agatha’s chin, clean the wound with careful fingers, to press the gauze against her skin with a tenderness she never let herself give.

    Now, that same cut mocked her. A quiet, unspoken reminder of how much Agatha had let her in that night—and how much Rio had failed her in return.

    They weren’t fine, and neither of them had the courage to bring it up. Lilia let out an exhausted sigh from her side of the room, leaning against the desk. “This case is a goddamn nightmare.”

    “Agreed,” William muttered, sipping from his fifth cup of coffee, which was probably half sugar by now.

    Agatha stayed silent. Rio did, too.

    It had been a long week. And it was about to get longer.

    The sharp slap of a sticky note onto the board cut through the quiet. “John Johnson,” Lilia announced, pressing the paper into place with two fingers before stepping back, scanning the board like she was putting the final piece of a puzzle together.

    Agatha stirred slightly at the name. The guy who had split her eyebrow open with a bottle. “Seriously? John Johnson?” She scoffed. Lilia flipped open her notepad. “So, turns out our guy’s got a bit of a rap sheet. Nothing major, just enough to paint a picture—bar fights, resisting arrest, trespassing. Basically, your standard asshole with anger issues.” William leaned against the table, tapping a pen against his chin. “Alright, but that doesn’t exactly scream criminal mastermind. What else?” Lilia smirked, always one for a dramatic pause. “He works at a bar.” Rio raised a brow. “And?”

    “And—” Lilia slapped another piece of paper onto the desk, flipping it around for them to see. “Not just any bar. Some dingy little joint on the south side called Miller’s.” She scoffed. “Barely even has a name. No website, no social media, and the health department has tried to shut it down at least three times. It’s one of those places that doesn’t pop up on Google Maps unless you’re actively standing outside of it.” William whistled lowly. “Sounds like a real five-star establishment. How’d you even track this down?”

    Lilia smirked. “Patience, young grasshopper.”

    Agatha tilted her head. “So, he’s a bartender?”

    “Not officially. He’s not listed as a bartender, security, or anything official, but he gets paid cash weekly from the owner, some guy named Ray Miller. And according to the very messy records I had the pleasure of digging through, that bar just happens to be a known hangout for some of our favorite local assholes.”

    Rio leaned forward just a fraction, her gaze sharpening. “Meaning?” “Meaning,” Lilia continued, “either he’s working off the books, or he’s doing something else for Miller.”

    A heavy silence settled over them as the implications sank in. Agatha finally spoke; voice steady. “Then we need to figure out what that is.”

    Lilia nodded. “If this bar is some kind of side operation for Miller, it’s worth checking out. He’s paying Johnson for a reason, and considering our guy already has a tendency to solve his problems with a broken bottle, I’d say it’s not just for his bartending skills.”

    Rio flipped through the file, absorbing the details. “Any priors on the owner, Miller?”

    “Nothing concrete,” Lilia admitted. “But he’s been in and out of trouble for years. Money laundering suspicions, gambling debts, some vague ties to organized crime, but nothing that’s ever stuck.”

    “Great,” William muttered. “A guy with shady connections and a temper working for another guy with even shadier connections and no confirmed paper trail.”

    Lilia flipped to another page in her notepad. “And it gets better.” Rio arched a brow. “Better how?”

    “Miller might not have a record, but he’s had his name show up in a few interesting places.” Lilia tapped the page. “Unregistered businesses. Shell companies. A couple of real estate ventures that make no sense for a guy running a hole-in-the-wall bar.”

    William leaned forward. “Laundering?”

    “Most likely. He’s got his hands in a few dirty pots, but never deep enough to get burned. And get this—some of the women who’ve gone missing in the last couple of years?” Lilia glanced at them all. “They’d been seen at Miller’s before they disappeared.”

    Agatha let out a slow breath. “So, our bottle-smashing asshole could be more than just muscle.”

    Lilia nodded. “Exactly. We don’t know his role yet, but Miller’s paying him for a reason. And if Lisa went missing after The Lantern, we need to find out if he was involved.”

    Rio closed the file, thinking. “We need to drop an ear in at Miller’s.” William frowned. “How?”

    Agatha smirked, adjusting her weight. “We visit and take a closer look. See if anyone’s whispering in Johnson’s ear when he spots women alone. See if he’s the one luring them out.”

    Rio nodded. “We watch, listen. Try to figure out what exactly John Johnson does for Miller.”

    Lilia tapped her pen against the edge of the board, glancing between Agatha and Rio. “Alright. Then the three of us go.”

    Agatha blinked. “The three of us?”

    Lilia shrugged. “If you two walk in alone, you’re gonna look like cops trying too hard not to look like cops. But me?” She smirked, crossing her arms. “No one suspects the sixty-year-old lady ordering a whiskey neat.” William let out a low chuckle. “Oh, this I gotta see.”

    Rio considered it, nodding slowly. “It makes sense. A third person adds a buffer. Less obvious. And if something goes south—”

    “I’ll be right there to back you up,” Lilia finished, already satisfied with her own plan. “Besides, it’s been a while since I had a decent excuse to sit in a bar and eavesdrop. And if this guy really is luring women out, I wanna see his game firsthand.”

    Agatha smirked. “You just wanna drink on the job.”

    Lilia shot her a look. “I don’t see you complaining.”

    William snorted. “I’m just picturing the poor bastard that tries to flirt with Lilia and lives to regret it.”

    “Damn right,” Lilia muttered.

    Rio sighed, rubbing her temple. “Fine. We go in, order a drink, and watch Johnson. See who he talks to, who he listens to. We don’t engage unless we have to.”

    Agatha leaned back against the desk with her arms crossed. “Great. Can’t believe I get to spend another thrilling night with my favorite shadow.” Rio sighed, already feeling the beginning of an argument. “It’s our job, Agatha.”

    “You say that like it makes it sound better.”

    “It’s not supposed to sound better. It’s supposed to get us answers.” Agatha tilted her head, eyes gleaming. “Wow. Do you practice those lines in the mirror, or does it just come naturally?”

    Rio’s grip on her pen tightened. She had known this was coming. Agatha was in a mood, had been all week, and now when they should have been focusing on the case, she was gearing up for a fight.

    Rio set her jaw. “Agatha.”

    Agatha pushed off the desk and stretched dramatically. “Well, don’t let me get in the way of your thrilling tactical monologues, Agent Vidal. Wouldn’t want to disrupt your crime-fighting process.”

    Agatha didn’t stop there.

    “Seriously, Vidal, do you ever get tired of sounding like a walking handbook?” she pressed, voice edged. “Or is that just what helps you sleep at night?”

    Rio forced herself to stay still, to keep her focus on the board. Agatha knew she should stop. Knew she was being cruel for the sake of it. But the week of silence and avoidance, of waiting for Rio to say something —anything—had worn her thin.

    So, she kept going.

    “Or is this how you handle everything? Shut up, stay quiet, pretend nothing’s happening, like you’re some untouchable, unfeeling machine.” Agatha huffed a humorless laugh, shaking her head. “You know, it’s honestly impressive at this point.”

    Rio’s fingers curled slightly around the pen. She wasn’t doing this. Not here. Not now.

    But Agatha wanted a reaction.

    Agatha crossed her arms and stepped closer, tone cutting. “You know what, Vidal? I actually admire it.” Her voice was deceptively light, but there was razor-sharp attitude under it, cutting. “The way you keep everything bottled up. The way you let people walk all over you because it’s the ‘smart’ thing to do.”

    Rio’s shoulders tensed, but she still didn’t look at her. Agatha smirked, but there was no real humor in it. “I mean, it’s honestly a skill, going through life that afraid of making the wrong move. You keep your head down, you follow orders, you never say what you actually mean. It’s like you’re terrified of yourself. Terrified of what happens when you actually feel things.”

    That did it.

    Rio’s gaze flicked to her, sharp and unreadable. She didn’t bite back. She wouldn’t give Agatha the satisfaction of losing control. Instead, she pressed her lips together before turning back to the board, like Agatha hadn’t just carved into her with words.

    The room was dead silent. Then, Lilia’s voice cut through it, firm and steady. “Harkness.”

    Agatha barely flicked her eyes toward her.

    “That’s enough.”

    Agatha scoffed. “Oh, what, she can—”

    “I said,” Lilia’s voice was sharper this time, “that’s enough.” Agatha stilled; Lilia never used that tone unless she meant it. Lilia’s stare was heavy, unwavering, the of years of experience pressing down like a warning. Then, softer, “What the hell’s gotten into you?” Agatha’s jaw locked, barely contained frustration simmering beneath the surface, she wanted to roll her eyes and throw her hands up to make some sarcastic quip about how she was always like this. But she didn’t. She looked at Rio again and saw the way her fingers were in a tight fist at her side, she still wasn’t looking at her.

    Agatha took a deep breath whilst running a hand through her hair. Then, without another word, she grabbed her coffee and walked toward the door. Lilia shook her head before turning back to Rio, who was still standing exactly as she had been, shoulders squared, expression blank, like she was standing at attention.

    For a long moment, no one spoke. Then William, quietly, because even he knew better than to crack a joke this time, muttered, “…Well, that was fucking awful.”

    Rio hadn’t moved from where she stood, her back straight, shoulders squared like she was standing at attention, like she could will herself into being unbothered. Her gaze stayed fixed on the suspect board, but she wasn’t really looking at it anymore. Her hands were curled loosely into fists at her sides, frustration running through every inch of her frame. She was too still.

    Lilia studied her carefully before stepping forward. "Hey," she said, keeping her voice even. Rio didn’t turn.

    Lilia tried again. Softer. “Rio.”

    Rio blinked slowly, an acknowledgment that she’d heard.

    Lilia took another step closer, lowering her voice. “You wanna tell me what the hell that was?”

    Rio finally moved, just a fraction, just enough to let out a slow, measured breath. “It’s fine.”

    Lilia frowned. “That didn’t look fine.”

    Rio forced the tightest smile Lilia had ever seen, her lips barely curving before they flattened again. “We all know that’s just how Agatha is.” Lilia gave her a look. “She’s sharp, yeah. Mean when she wants to be. But that?” She gestured loosely to the door Agatha had just stormed through. “That was different.”

    Rio’s throat worked as she swallowed. “She’s just mad about the case. The bouncer, the dead ends. She doesn’t like hitting walls.”

    Lilia studied her. "That wasn’t about the case." Rio’s grip on the desk tightened, knuckles going a shade lighter.

    She took a slow, deliberate breath before looking down, gaze skimming over the files, the pens, the coffee cups littering the table—anywhere but at Lilia.

    “Look,” Lilia said, more gently this time, arms crossing as she leaned a hip against the desk beside her. “I’m not gonna push, but I need you to be honest with me here, Vidal. That wasn’t just Agatha being an asshole. That was personal.”

    Rio didn’t answer, just fixed her stance and rolled another stress knot out of her shoulder, the stiffness didn’t leave.

    Lilia exhaled. “You didn’t deserve that.”

    That, at least, got a reaction.

    Rio let out a slow breath, forced another one of those small, empty smiles. "It’s fine," she said, voice even. "She’ll get over it."

    Lilia didn’t buy it, but she didn’t argue either. She pressed a firm hand to Rio’s arm in a solid, grounding anchor of reassurance.

    Rio sat there, staring down at nothing, like she was holding back trying not to feel the sting of Agatha’s words still hanging in the air. Then, slowly, she lifted her own hand, gave Lilia’s a light, brief pat, a silent thank you. Lilia gave a small nod, her expression unreadable but knowing. “Let’s go back to what we’re here to do, yeah?”

    The warmth of her hand lingered for a second longer before she pulled away.

    Rio steadied herself. Lilia was right, they had to go back to the case. The one thing she was always supposed to be good at. She could focus on that. She nodded once, quiet, before pushing herself up from the desk where she had been leaning. The room around her felt too loud, even in its near silence —the distant hum of computers, the occasional shuffle of papers, the low murmur of voices. That perpetual scent of burnt coffee cemented in the air, latched on to every fabric of the precinct like a bad memory. Rio’s reached for the case file she abandoned earlier, flipping through the pages, but her mind wasn’t absorbing the information. John Johnson. Bartender. Assault record. A man prone to violence but never the kind that got him serious time.

    Her jaw tightened, she should be focused on that, the case, but her mind kept circling back.

    Rio forced her breath slow down and stay controlled. She straightened her posture, trying to shake off the residual tension curling in her spine. The long sleeves of her black shirt felt too fitted now, like the fabric was clinging too tightly to her frame, like it was holding everything in. Her fingers toyed with the leather band of her watch pressed against her wrist, cool, grounding her.

    Focus.

    Her eyes dropped back down to the file. John Johnson’s record wasn’t clean, but it wasn’t the worst she’d seen. Small charges, nothing that ever really stuck. A history of bar fights, a few assault charges, but no major convictions. He was expendable—the kind of guy people like Miller used to do their dirty work.

    A pawn; much like Agatha had accused her of being.

    Rio’s fingers pressed against the page. No. She wasn’t going to let Agatha pull her into another fight without even being in the damn room. But God, did it sting.

    The way Agatha had looked at her. Like she was disappointed, as if Rio had let her down in some unforgivable way.

    Rio dragged a hand through her hair, inhaling steady. She wasn’t going to fight fire with fire. Agatha would push like she always did. But Rio wasn’t reckless like her, she would handle this the way she handled everything else.

    With patience.

    Her grip on the file eased as she scanned the next page, zeroing in on Miller’s name. He had a past with laundering and suspected ties to organized crime. Never enough to stick, but enough to suggest he was dangerous.

    Rio absorbed the information, taking it apart piece by piece, dissecting it. This she could control.

    Not Agatha, and definitely not whatever was happening between them. Suddenly, the door creaked open, letting in a brief gust of cold air before it slammed shut behind her. Rio didn’t have to look up to know it was Agatha. She knew the sound of her boots against the floor, the way she carried herself, like she owned the space she walked into, and the world owed her for simply existing in it.

    Out of the corner of her eye, Rio caught the way Agatha scrubbed the back of her hand across her nose as she made her way to her desk. The late-night cold had imprinted into her skin, the tip of her nose pink from the wind. She peeled off her jacket in one sharp motion, throwing it onto the back of her chair before stepping back in front of the suspect board, arms crossed, jaw tight, staring.

    As if staring hard enough would make the pieces rearrange themselves. As if the truth was buried in the spaces between the pinned photos, the scrawled notes, the red lines connecting names that meant nothing—until they did.

    Rio swallowed.

    She should be focusing on the case. On Miller. On Johnson. On everything that had led them here, to this tangled mess that wasn’t just about Lisa Moreno anymore.

    But instead, she was watching Agatha.

    The dim glow of the desk lamps hit her jawline, casting sharp shadows across her face. Her fingers flexed against her arm, tension thrumming through them, like she was holding back.

    Rio knew what everyone else saw when they looked at Agatha Harkness— reckless, stubborn, infuriatingly self-assured. The kind of cop who didn’t follow orders, who got written up for shit that would’ve gotten anyone else suspended. Who didn’t care if she burned bridges as long as she got what she wanted.

    That wasn’t what Rio saw.

    She saw the way Agatha paced when she was thinking, the way her fingers twitched when she was working through a problem in her head. She saw the way she bit her cheek when she was frustrated, the way her mouth curled in when she was trying to protect herself from feeling anything real. And she saw the way Agatha had been avoiding her.

    A whole fucking week of silence, not that Rio had done much to fix it. She had tried small things. A glance, word, or even a moment that might’ve been the start of something—if Agatha had let it be.

    But she hadn’t and now Rio didn’t know where to begin.

    She had never been good at this, at finding the words when they actually mattered. The job was different. The job had rules, steps, a process. But this —her and Agatha—was messy. This, she couldn’t control, and that scared her more than anything.

    Rio rolled her sleeves up slowly, the fabric catching against her forearms as she tried to ease the restless energy curling in her stomach. The chair creaked beneath her as she leaned back, stretching her legs out slightly, fingers drumming idly against the desk.

    Across the room, Agatha stood in front of the suspect board looking so goddamn untouchable. Her nose was still pink from the cold outside, a small contrast to the hardness of her expression. She looked tired and wound up.

    And Rio, God help her, she missed her.

    She missed the snide remarks and the way Agatha would roll her eyes at her like it was second nature. How Agatha would poke and prod until she got some kind of reaction out of her, it was endearing in its own way. She missed the way Agatha made her feel, but before that thought could go any further—

    “Alright, children,” William groaned, his voice cutting through the silence, snapping Rio out of it. “Some of us actually have lives to get back to.” Rio blinked, shaking herself free from whatever haze she’d been slipping into, eyes refocusing just as William pushed back from his desk with a dramatic sigh, stretching his arms over his head. His chair let out a soft creak as he rocked back, joints popping in protest before he finally let his arms drop.

    Lilia smirked, already slipping into her coat, buttoning it with the casual ease of someone who had seen this performance before. "Yeah, like your neon-lit video gaming thing?"

    William gasped, placing a hand over his chest in mock offense, staggering back a step like she’d just wounded him. "Excuse me, that is my passion." Lilia chuckled, shaking her head as she turned to Agatha, no eye roll, no sarcastic quip, not even the slightest flicker of acknowledgment. The older woman shook her head before turning toward Rio. "You coming?"

    For a second, Rio hesitated, it would be easier to head home and step away, putting some distance between herself and whatever… this was. "I think I’ll stay back. Get in some extra time with the case before tomorrow." Lilia studied her for a second, her gaze flickering briefly between Rio and Agatha, something unreadable shifting behind her eyes. She let it stretch for just long enough to make Rio wonder what she was thinking. Then she reached out, pressing a firm hand to Rio’s shoulder—a warm reassurance through the fabric of her sleeve. "Don’t stay too late." Rio huffed out a small, tired smile. "No promises." She patted her hand back, acknowledging the comfort, grateful for it.

    Lilia gave her one last look before stepping away, nudging William toward the door with an expectant raise of her brow. "Let’s go, kid." William let out an exaggerated groan but complied, dragging his feet like a sulking teenager as he followed her toward the exit. Just before stepping out, he cast one last glance at Rio. “Good luck,” he mouthed. Then, the door clicked shut behind them.

    The silence left in their wake was unbearable.

    Rio sighed, pressing the heel of her palm against her thigh, feeling the fabric of her trousers crease beneath her touch. The movement was small, almost absentminded, but it gave her focus as she tried to settle the restless energy curling beneath her skin.

    The precinct was empty now.

    The faint voices of Lilia and William had long since disappeared down the hall, their footsteps fading into nothing. The shuffle of paperwork and distant ringing of phones with the occasional murmur of officers finishing up reports was gone. Even the bullpen, always buzzing with some level of movement, had gone completely still.

    Agatha hadn’t moved. She still stood there with her arms crossed and shoulders squared like she was bracing for something, with an intensity that made Rio’s chest feel tight. Rio knew that look, the way Agatha tensed, the barely-there shift of her weight, the way her fingers gripped at her own arms like she was holding herself together.

    Rio felt the environment press in around her. This was the first time they had truly been alone since the fight.

    Rio pushed back from her chair, the legs scraping softly against the floor as she stood. Clearing her throat and adjusting her shirt before making her way toward the board. The soft glow of the desk lamps cast long, flickering shadows against the precinct walls, stretching out their silhouettes like ghosts in the dim light.

    Each step was deliberate, careful, the low hum of the vending machine the only sound breaking the silence.

    She stopped next to Agatha, close enough to feel the residual warmth radiating from her, catching the faint scent of whatever wood scented soap she used. Agatha didn’t turn to acknowledge her, but Rio caught the slight tightening of her shoulders, her fingers flexed against the fabric of her sleeve.

    Rio hesitated for half a second, but she wasn’t backing down this time. Not walking away, nor running from the words lodged in her throat, the ones she should have said a week ago.

    Rio lingered for a moment, debating whether to break the silence. Then, finally, she asked, voice steady, “You staying?”

    Agatha didn’t look away from the board. “Unless you’re about to tell me I can’t.”

    Rio’s faintest hint of amusement flickering through her as she shook her head. “Didn’t say that.”

    A pause.

    "I don’t want you to think I don’t care." The words quieter than usual, softer, but they cut through the stillness like a sharp edge. Agatha sighed—not sharp, not biting. Just… tired.

    Rio thought for a second that she might actually look at her. But Agatha barely tilted her head, her eyes still locked on the board as she muttered, “We need to be careful in that bar. You look like a narc, Vidal.” Not an acknowledgment. Not even close.

    The silence stretched between them, heavy and unyielding, as they both stood there, eyes locked on the case display like it might offer clarity—like if they studied the tangled web of notes and connections long enough, it might make the pull between them easier to dismiss.

    Rio crossed her arms over her chest, her fingers digging into the fabric of her sleeve. She could hear Agatha breathing beside her —slow, controlled, like she was deliberately keeping herself from speaking. Not acknowledging that they were both standing there, shoulder to shoulder, pretending like there wasn’t a storm still raging in between them. The strain was suffocating.

    Rio pressed her tongue against the inside of her cheek.

    She wasn’t good at this. At saying things that mattered when they needed to be said. Agatha had always been better at filling space with sharp words and feeling things loudly; even when she didn’t want to. Rio held things back because it was safer that way, but not tonight.

    "I don’t want things to be like this."

    Agatha didn’t move at first, her arms still folded tight over her chest. Then, finally, she let out a slow, humorless breath, turning her head just enough for Rio to see the sharp glint in her eye.

    "Like what?" she asked, tilting her head slightly. "Difficult?" Rio swallowed. "Agatha—"

    "Sorry, Vidal," Agatha cut her off, shaking her head, sarcasm biting into her voice. "I forgot who I was talking to."

    "That’s not fair."

    Agatha didn’t answer. Just stood there, the glow of the desk lamp catching on the sharp line of her cheekbone.

    Then, finally, she turned her head. Her gaze flicked toward Rio, something unreadable in her expression. “Neither is a lot of shit," she murmured. The exhaustion in Rio’s bones creeped in, winding tight around her ribs. The job was exhausting. The case was exhausting. But this—this was something else entirely.

    She watched the way Agatha’s fingers tapped absently against her arm, the way she kept her body angled just enough to not be facing her fully. “Agatha," Rio tried again, quieter this time, measured. "I don’t want things to be like this."

    Agatha’s eyes met Rio’s just enough for her to catch the flicker of uncertainty. Then, as quickly as it had come, it was gone. Smothered under the weight of neutral practice.

    Rio swallowed. "You know this isn’t fair.” she said again, softer this time. Agatha huffed a breath, shaking her head, as if the conversation wasn’t even worth having. She turned forward again, her arms tightening over her chest, her fingers flexing like she was holding everything in.

    "Vidal," she muttered, voice flatter now. "This is a job. Thought you’d know that better than anyone else."

    The words landed sharply in Rio’s stomach. She swallowed around them, forcing past the immediate sting.

    Agatha had always been good at cutting things down before they could get too close. Now the detective was the one retreating. The irony wasn’t lost on her.

    Rio’s fingers curled against her palms, her nails pressing faintly into skin. "That’s not all this is," she said, careful.

    Agatha scoffed, "Yeah?”. She tilted her head. "Well, maybe that’s the problem."

    The words landed deep, threading between Rio’s ribs like a hook. She turned away before she could show it.

    Agatha let out a breath, raking a hand through her hair before shaking her head. "We’re not doing this right now," she muttered.

    Rio clenched her jaw, feeling the last threads of patience pull dangerously thin.

    "Then when, Agatha?" The question came quiet. Too measured. "When are we doing this?"

    Silence.

    Agatha didn’t answer. Didn’t even acknowledge it.

    Without a word, the older woman walked away towards the desk, her back to Rio. “We’ve got work to do," she muttered, but it didn’t sound like an excuse. It sounded like a cop-out.

    Fine.

    Rio cleared her throat, reeling it back, swallowing it all down. "Right," she murmured. "Of course."

    After a moment, she pulled the conversation back to where it should have been all along. “Tomorrow,” she said finally, pushing past everything else lingering between them. “We should figure out the best vantage points before we go in.”

    Agatha didn’t respond right away as she stared down at a report on her desk, but Rio knew she wasn’t actually looking at it. Her hands twitched like she wanted to do something—pick up a pen, shuffle through papers, anything to make this moment less unbearable.

    Then, finally, she nodded once.

    The nod should have been enough, like a simple acknowledgment and signal to let it go. But the silence sat too thick between them, filling the space like almost tangibly.

    Rio leaned back against the side of her own desk, her hands holding on to the edges for support.

    She could leave now; they had nothing left to say, or at least nothing that would fix this. But she didn’t step away.

    Rio kept her voice even, steady. “We should be careful about how we approach Johnson,” she said, gaze trained on the tangled mess in front of them. “If he’s involved, he’ll be watching for anyone paying too much attention to him.”

    Agatha made a quiet hum in response, wanting to fill the space. She didn’t look at her.

    The silence felt heavier this time, teetering on the edge of finality. The kind that stretched too long and threatened to set in like concrete if neither of them moved.

    Rio waited for anything Agatha could give her.

    Then— Agatha pushed off the desk, reaching for her coat. The movement was smooth, unhurried, like she had already made up her mind minutes ago. She didn’t say anything at first, just pulled the fabric into her hands, fingers curling around the worn material, holding onto it like an anchor. Rio watched, uneasy settling low in her stomach. It wasn’t dramatic or some grand, heartbreaking moment—but it felt like one anyway. “I should go,” Agatha said, detached.

    The words landed between them like a line drawn in permanent ink. Rio blinked, realizing the moment was slipping through her fingers. “Oh.” The word came quieter than she meant, almost absentminded. “Yeah. You’re right.”

    The air between them stretched unbearably thin, the heaviness of everything they weren’t saying pressing in from all sides.

    Rio fidgeted as she watched the woman in front of her. She wanted to say— don’t go yet, stay a little longer, stop leaving like this—but the words sat too heavy in her throat, impossible to pull out now that she knew Agatha would dismiss her.

    Perhaps Agatha felt it too, because for the first time all night, she hesitated. Blue flickered over Rio, quick and fleeting, like she was memorizing her without meaning to.

    The way she still stood there, still waiting for something Agatha didn’t know how to give.

    A muscle feathered in Agatha’s jaw.

    She looked away fast, like she hadn’t looked at all.

    "Alright," she muttered, tone clipped, too brisk for someone who had spent the last five minutes not moving at all. "I’ll see you tomorrow." Rio didn’t respond right away, caught off guard by the sudden shift. A moment ago, Agatha had been standing there, and now she was running. Not physically, but in every other way that mattered.

    The realization weighed heavy in Rio’s chest, but she didn’t chase it. Agatha shouldered past the tension in the room like she could outrun it, moving toward the door with too much purpose.

    Rio’s fingers slid into her pockets. “Yeah,” she murmured, barely loud enough for Agatha to hear. “See you tomorrow.”

    Agatha didn’t turn, or did anything that might indicate she had heard her at all.

    The door clicked shut behind Agatha, the sound final.

    Without thinking, Rio turned back to her desk and planted a hand against the cool surface, it felt solid and real beneath her palm. The chill seeped into her palm, anchoring her, but it barely made a difference. Her eyes flicked down, tracing the scattered case files, the pen she had abandoned earlier, the coffee ring staining the corner of a report she should’ve been paying attention to. Details she normally would have noticed instantly, but now they blurred together, slipping past her like water through her fingers.

    She flexed her hand against the desk, watching her veins pop from under her skin.

    Oh, how she wished her pulse would settle.

    Dragging a hand through her hair, she tried to brush off the lingering pressure against her ribs. She reached for the case file she had been pretending to read earlier, its edges felt crisp beneath her fingertips, grounding, tangible. Work was tangible.

    Agatha was not, and she knew better than to chase ghosts. Tomorrow, she told herself.

    Tomorrow, she’d figure out how to pretend none of this had happened. Tomorrow, she’d find a way to stop feeling unsteady every time Agatha Harkness was near.

    Chapter End Notes p.s: this is sort of a double update. important gay event coming tonight or tomorrow. hold on tight.

    Chapter 10: The Art of the Con

    Chapter Notes undercover rule #3: when in doubt, kiss your coworker.

    (wait, that’s not a rule?)

    See the end of the chapter for more notes The line outside Miller’s stretched down the block, a restless coil of bodies shifting beneath the neon glow. The sign above the door hummed faintly, casting warped blue light over the cracked pavement, while another smaller sign—Miller’s Saloon—blinked in and out, missing half a letter, as if the place itself was on its last leg.

    Every time the door swung open, a wave of heat and noise spilled into the cold, thick with whiskey, cigarette smoke, and the stale musk of sweat- soaked leather. The muffled bassline of country music bled through the walls, the twang of a steel guitar tangling with drunken laughter and the occasional low murmur of something less friendly.

    Further up, a man in cowboy boots and a flannel shirt was trying to charm the bouncer, his voice carrying over the music, half-slurred, half-confident. The bouncer, unimpressed, lifted a hand, motioning him toward the back of the line. The guy groaned, muttering something under his breath as he turned away, nearly knocking into the couple behind him.

    Agatha stood near the entrance, weight braced against one hip, fingers toying with the cool band of silver around her thumb. It was an absentminded habit, twisting it against her skin, a way to channel the nervous energy that had been simmering under the surface all week. She hadn’t worn the ring in a while, but she figured it made her look more laid back, like she wasn’t just another cop lurking where she didn’t belong. That was the goal, anyway. The air out here was razor-sharp, creeping through the gaps in her leather jacket, slipping beneath the soft white fabric of her shirt to chill her skin. Inside, it would be the opposite, the heat pressing in from all sides, bodies packed too close, air thick. But that was fine, she’d take it over the silence she had been drowning in.

    Rio stood beside her, hands tucked into the pockets of her grey blazer, posture steady, unbothered, or at least pretending to be. The cold clung to the air against her exposed skin, and despite the structured fabric that hugged her frame, a slight shiver betrayed her. She should’ve worn a coat. The weight of her hair, layers of brown perfectly around her face, did little to fight off the bite of the wind. Unlike Agatha, whose fingers twisted the ring on her thumb, Rio was still, composed. A contrast—one patient, the other barely containing her impatience.

    The wait was dragging.

    The neon signs overhead flickered and buzzed, their sickly glow painting the pavement in distorted reds and yellows, making it look slick, like it had just rained. The wind howled through the narrow street, slipping past the gaps in Agatha’s collar, sending a slow chill down her spine. Almost there.

    The muffled thump of bass rattled against the walls, a promise of warmth, of cover, of distraction. Where the real work would begin. Agatha let out a breath, rocking back on her heels before muttering, “Jesus. How long does it take to get into a place like this? Feels like we’ve been standing here for a fucking hour.”

    Lilia, standing just ahead of them in line, didn’t even bother turning around before cutting in. “Quit your griping, Harkness. You’re acting like a kid in a grocery store.”

    Agatha huffed,“Yeah, well, at least in a grocery store, there’s heating.” She cast a glance toward Rio, who stood composed despite the cold, hands tucked neatly in her blazer pockets. Agatha narrowed her eyes slightly. “And at least some people had the sense to dress for the weather.” That earned her a steady look, the kind Rio had perfected, effortlessly conveying disapproval without so much as a word.

    As the line inched forward, they reached the front, now just one group away from being let in. Agatha raised her hands in relief, a restless energy coiling beneath her skin.

    “Finally,” she muttered under her breath, impatience threading through her tone.

    Lilia turned on her heel, fixing them both with a pointed look before letting out a long, exaggerated sigh. “Alright, children, listen up.” Agatha arched a brow, unimpressed.

    Lilia, ever the voice of reason (and chaos), crossed her arms. “You two have been acting like strangers for days, and now you’re about to walk into a bar run by a guy with known criminal ties. So, whatever the hell this is—” she gestured vaguely between them, “—leave it at the door.”

    Agatha’s patience was already thin. “Oh, thank you for the wisdom, Lilia. I never would’ve guessed we should focus on the job. Really, what would we do without you?”

    Agatha’s lips pressed into a thin line, the sharpness in her gaze flaring. She was wound tight, and Lilia’s casual observation was clearly a match to a powder keg.

    “There’s nothing going on,” Agatha bit out. “Everything’s fine.” But her tone was clipped, defensive.

    Rio, arms still tucked for warmth, kept her gaze fixed on the entrance, refusing to turn or acknowledge the exchange.

    Lilia didn’t miss it. “I mean it, Harkness.”

    Agatha, already teetering on the edge of irritation, let out a deep sigh. “Jesus, Lilia, I said it’s fine. Do you need me to spell it out for you?” And just to be difficult, she turned toward Rio, “Unless Vidal needs you to?”

    The implication was clear. An unnecessary jab, one meant to poke at the way Rio had been carefully avoiding her, at the silence between them like a chasm neither was willing to bridge.

    Rio tensed, but she didn’t rise to it. Didn’t so much as glance in Agatha’s direction.

    Lilia, however, did.

    “I don’t know what the hell has gotten into you two,” she said, voice firm. “But whatever this is? I don’t want to see it once we step inside.” She gestured loosely between them, exasperation heavy in her sigh. “You’re not cops tonight, and you sure as hell aren’t a couple of brooding idiots trying to out miserable each other. Act normal. Hell, act like you like each other.”

    Agatha’s scoff was immediate. “That’s asking a lot.”

    The words came out dry, but the slight way her mouth curled at the corner wasn’t lost on Rio.

    Rio finally spoke, her voice even. “We’re here to do a job.” Her words carried a weight that went beyond the obvious. Beyond the fact that they were walking into a bar run by criminals, that they had to keep their focus sharp. There was something else under the surface, lingering between them like the press of a blade against thin ice.

    “Exactly,” Lilia said, her eyes wondering between them like she was sizing them up, gauging whether they were about to completely blow this. She let out a slow, considering breath before shaking her head. “Which is why you need to sell it.”

    Agatha frowned. “Sell what?”

    Lilia smirked, tilting her head, the glint in her eye entirely too pleased, like she had been waiting to drop this on them.

    “The fact that you’re on a date.”

    Agatha let out a humorless laugh, sharp and short. “You’re kidding.” Lilia, to her credit, didn’t even blink. “You wish,” she said easily. Agatha shook her head, folding her arms tightly across her chest like she could physically hold in her irritation. “Absolutely fucking not.” Lilia arched a brow, unimpressed. “You got a better idea?” “We could just be friends getting drinks.” Agatha said, stubborn. “People do that all the time.”

    Lilia didn’t even hesitate. “Not you two.”

    The older woman smirked, watching realization creep into her face before driving the point home. “No one’s gonna believe that. You two look at each other like you either want to kill or kiss each other.”

    Rio turned her head away as if the flickering neon sign above them had suddenly become the most interesting thing in the world.

    Agatha narrowed her eyes, shaking her head again, jaw ticking like she wanted to argue, wanted to tear Lilia’s entire logic apart. But the worst part was that she couldn’t.

    Lilia wasn’t wrong.

    But Rio’s throat clearing, the way she suddenly refused to look at her, only made it worse.

    “No,” Agatha whined, but Lilia was already grinning.

    “Oh, yes,” she countered.

    Agatha groaned. “And what the hell are you gonna be doing while we’re over here playing surveillance team?”

    Lilia barely glanced at her. “Getting us intel.”

    Agatha scoffed. “Must be nice. We’re the ones stuck rubbing shoulders, and you get to sit back and enjoy the show.”

    Lilia ignored her. “I’ll be at the bar, close enough to keep an eye on Johnson and see if he’s working alone or if he’s got handlers moving pieces for him. You two will have the bigger field of vision.”

    Rio nodded once. “Watching for patterns.”

    “Exactly.” Lilia took a few steps as the line inched forward. “His usual type, if he makes contact with anyone before his shift ends, if he signals someone else to make a move. We need a full picture.”

    Agatha ran her tongue over her teeth. “Right. So, to recap—you get to sit at the bar with a clear exit, while we’re stuck in a goddamn booth playing the part.”

    “Different roles, same mission,” Lilia said plainly. “We need eyes from every angle.”

    Agatha muttered something under her breath but didn’t argue. Lilia clapped a hand against her shoulder as the line moved again. “Eyes open, ears sharp. We’re on the clock the second we step inside.” The line finally lurched forward again; the entrance just steps away now. Agatha took a deep breath and relaxed her shoulders, trying to shake the tension settling beneath her skin. Lilia’s words echoed in her head—Eyes open, ears sharp. Easy enough. The hard part wasn’t the job itself. It was everything else.

    The bouncer at the door barely looked up at first, just held out a hand, waiting.

    Agatha handed over her ID, watching as his gaze moved down, then up. He was a big guy, the kind that didn’t need to flex to make a point. The scar across his chin made him look even meaner, but his expression remained unreadable.

    Her grip tightened around the edge of her jacket. She held his stare with the same detached, uninterested air she always carried. Next to her, Rio passed over her own ID, her face cool, composed. If the bouncer noticed the way she subtly squared her shoulders, he didn’t let it show.

    He flipped the IDs back, glancing between them once more. Then at Lilia. Then back at Agatha.

    A beat of silence stretched—then he nodded, handing them back. “Go on.” His voice was rough, disinterested.

    Agatha was the first to step past, and the heat hit first. The bar was a mess of sound and movement, a wall of noise pressing in from every direction. Country music blasted from the overhead speakers, the kind of old, twangy tune that belonged on a dusty backroad more than a packed, smoky dive.

    Agatha had spent enough summers back home in Georgia to recognize the sound instantly. It was the same kind of music her mother would hum along to while cooking, scratchy vinyls that spun in the background of every too- hot afternoon. The memory twisted in her stomach, unwelcome and out of place.

    The air inside was thick. Stale cigarette smoke clung to the exposed wooden beams, mixing with the stench of spilled beer and cheap whiskey. The floor was scuffed, a little sticky in places, the kind of grime that had been pressed into the wood over years of boots dragging, drinks sloshing, and fights breaking out.

    Bull skulls were mounted along the walls, their hollow eyes catching in the dim, uneven lighting. Some had cowboy hats perched on their heads like some kind of sick joke. Others had dollar bills stuffed between their teeth, graffiti scrawled along the bone—dates, names, crude jokes left behind by past patrons.

    The bar itself stretched along the back wall, lined with battered stools, the leather cracked and worn. Neon beer signs flickered above, casting a dull glow over the bottles lined up behind the counter. The bartender—their guy —was busy pouring drinks, barely glancing up as he handed off a cheap draft to a man with a bad mustache and a worse attitude.

    Booths were packed with bodies, groups leaning in close, voices raised to be heard over the music. In the corner, an old pool table sat under a single hanging lamp, the green felt faded and torn in places. A couple of guys hovered around it, cue sticks in hand, exchanging easy laughs between shots.

    The whole place reeked of bad decisions and company.

    Agatha took it all in, slow and practiced. Every entry, every exit, every potential problem. A couple of men near the back were staring too long at a woman nursing a drink alone, their interest clear. A few others sat hunched over their beers, low murmurs exchanged, their shoulders tense in a way that read more business than pleasure.

    Lilia led the way, moving through the bar with the kind of confidence that made her blend in instead of stand out. Agatha and Rio followed in her wake, keeping close with eyes already sweeping the room.

    “Bar’s crowded,” Lilia muttered under her breath, her gaze flicking between groups as she walked. “Good cover but keep an eye on your blind spots. We don’t know who’s watching who in a place like this.”

    Agatha’s gaze cut through the dim haze. “Two points of entry in the front, one in the back by the bathrooms,” she noted, voice low. “Side door’s probably for staff, but I’d bet half the regulars use it when things get hot.” Rio’s posture was relaxed, but her eyes tracked movements across the room. “Back booths are mostly groups. No one sitting alone except the woman at the bar.” Her voice was measured, professional. “I can tell she’s been nursing that drink for a while. Not her first.”

    “Not the first to get clocked, either,” Lilia murmured. “We watch Johnson. See who he’s watching.”

    Agatha nodded, barely tilting her head as she tracked their target behind the bar. Johnson was moving with ease, pouring drinks, laughing at some joke a patron threw his way. But every now and then, his eyes wandered, pausing too long in certain places.

    He was watching.

    “So what’s the play?” Agatha asked, adjusting her jacket as they slowed near an open booth.

    Lilia barely spared her a glance. “You and Vidal take a seat, order drinks, look natural.”

    Agatha let out a slow breath. “Sure. No problem.”

    She didn’t miss the way Rio tensed beside her.

    Lilia, unfazed, gave them both a look that was half warning, half command. “We need to see if he makes contact, if he lingers too long on certain people. If he’s the one pulling the strings or just another cog in the wheel.” Her voice was low. “I’ll be at the bar. Johnson won’t think twice about me sitting alone, but you two?” She let the words settle, her sharp gaze flicking between them. “Sell it.”

    Agatha rolled her eyes. The idea of sitting across from Rio, pretending, playing into something that felt like it had already been breathing down her neck for weeks, was enough to make her want to turn right back around and walk out of this damn bar.

    Instead, she bit back the urge to scoff and gave Lilia a slow blink. "Right. Because that’s the hard part of the job.”

    Beside her, Rio exhaled quietly. “Understood,” she said, the word crisp, final.

    Lilia gave them one last pointed glance, eyes scanning them like she was sizing up a pair of faulty wires that were bound to spark at the worst possible moment. Then, satisfied enough, she peeled off toward the bar counter, disappearing into the dim haze of cigarette smoke and cheap neon lighting.

    The tension snapped into place like a wire pulled too tight. Agatha relaxed her shoulders, forcing her expression into something easy that didn’t scream discomfort or the unbearable weight of the past week. Blue eyes moved across the room, finding the open booth Lilia had motioned toward. “Let’s get this over with,” she muttered, already moving. Rio hesitated for half a second before following, silent but steady. The space between them felt noticeable. Not just physically, but in the way neither of them moved to close it. The music thumped overhead, drowning out the low hum of conversation as they weaved through the crowd. A few eyes followed them—half curious, half indifferent—but Agatha ignored it, her focus narrowing on the booth.

    She slid into the seat, leaning back like she belonged there, even if her entire body felt wound tight. Rio followed a moment later, carefully smoothing the fabric of her blazer as she settled across from her. The air between them sat thick.

    Agatha tapped a finger absently against the tabletop. “Guess we should order something.”

    Rio barely looked at her, scanning the room instead. “Guess so.” She didn’t move to flag anyone down.

    Agatha turned her gaze toward the bar. Lilia had already made herself comfortable, elbows propped against the counter, nursing a whiskey neat as she listened in on the conversations swirling around her. She blended in, a woman at the bar alone, unimposing but present.

    Meanwhile, Agatha and Rio sat across from each other, stiff as statues, as if the table between them were a canyon too wide to cross.

    Agatha huffed, finally lifting a hand to signal the nearest bartender. “It’s gonna be a long night,” she muttered under her breath.

    The waitress appeared beside their booth with the kind of effortless ease that only came from years of weaving through a crowded bar. She was pretty, blonde and blue-eyed with a smile that came easily. Agatha leaned back against the seat, tilting her head up slightly as the waitress’s attention zeroed in on her.

    “What can I get you?” The woman’s voice was smooth with a tone that lingered just on the edge of teasing.

    Agatha didn’t hesitate. “Whiskey. Neat.”

    The waitress hummed, barely sparing Rio a glance as she jotted it down. Then, with an easy smirk, she added, “Good choice. Strong and smooth— like you.”

    Agatha blinked, then let out a quiet snort. She hadn’t been expecting that level of directness, but before she could even think of a response, a scoff cut through the moment.

    Agatha flicked her eyes toward Rio, catching the way she rolled her eyes before adjusting in her seat. It was a small thing, barely noticeable to anyone else, but Agatha saw it. The tension in Rio’s shoulders and the way her fingers tapped against the table once before stilling completely. Interesting.

    Agatha wasn’t sure if that reaction was part of the act, if Rio was simply keeping up appearances, or if it was something else entirely. So, naturally, she decided to test it.

    Agatha turned back to the waitress, letting a slow smirk pull at the corner of her mouth. “Oh yeah?” she mused, her tone dipping just enough to be flirtatious. “That your professional opinion, or a personal one?” The waitress grinned, clearly enjoying herself. “Bit of both,” she admitted, tapping her pen against the notepad. “But I’d be happy to make it personal.” Agatha turned her head just enough to catch Rio’s expression out of the corner of her eye. The other woman wasn’t looking at her, instead scanning the room with pointed indifference. There was something in the way her fingers curled slightly against the table’s edge, a tension that wasn’t there before.

    Well, well.

    Agatha’s smirk deepened. She tilted her head slightly, watching Rio look at everything but her.

    Maybe this mission wasn’t going to be so unbearable after all. Agatha, ever the menace, let the grin curl wider, slow and sharp. If Rio was going to make this interesting, she wasn’t about to let the opportunity go to waste.

    “Oh, baby girl,” she drawled, dragging out the endearment just enough to make it drip with amusement. “I’d love to hear more of your opinions.” Her moved down briefly, all for show, before meeting the waitress’s eyes again. “Why don’t you tell me all about them when you bring that whiskey over?” The waitress didn’t miss a beat, laughing softly, twirling her pen between her fingers. “Careful, sugar,” she teased. “Flattery like that might get you into trouble.”

    Agatha arched a brow. “I like trouble.”

    That was apparently the final straw.

    A sharp thud against her shin made Agatha jolt slightly, and she barely bit back a curse. Across the table, Rio was finally looking at her with a neutral expression, yet eyes just a little too sharp.

    Agatha didn’t get the chance to react before Rio, in an utterly flat voice, said, “Gin and tonic.”

    The waitress glanced between them, clearly sensing the shift in energy. But Rio was already turning slightly in her seat, dismissing the whole interaction without so much as a second glance.

    Agatha blinked once, processing the blatant interruption. Then, a slow, smug grin crept across her lips as she rubbed at her shin under the table. “Subtle,” she murmured, low enough that only Rio could hear. Rio didn’t respond, just fixed her eyes on Agatha’s with a steady look before returning to scoping out the bar.

    Agatha, still reeling from the absolute delight of Rio’s reaction, wasn’t about to let the moment pass without twisting the knife just a little further. As the waitress turned to leave, Agatha let out a dramatic sigh, shaking her head with exaggerated disappointment. “Excuse my girlfriend,” she said, voice smooth and dripping with faux sincerity. “She’s a bit of a—what’s the word?” She tapped a finger against the table like she was thinking before flashing the waitress a knowing smirk. “Stick in the mud.” The waitress, catching onto the game, let out a soft chuckle but wisely chose not to comment. Instead, she simply shot Agatha a look that said good luck with that before heading off toward the bar.

    Agatha, smug as ever, turned back to Rio, giving her an expectant look. “Right, honey?” she prodded, voice light and teasing.

    Rio finally turned to look at her. Her eyes narrowed ever so slightly, unimpressed, before she gave Agatha the tightest, most painfully fake smile imaginable. “Of course, sweetheart,” she said, saccharine and lethal in the same breath.

    Agatha suppressed a laugh, biting down on her grin as she stretched an arm lazily over the back of the booth. “Knew you’d come around,” she mused, voice laced with nothing but smug amusement.

    Rio breathed slowly through her nose, before turning enough to pin Agatha with a pointed look. “Cut it out,” she muttered, her voice low but firm. “We’re on a mission.”

    Agatha merely arched a brow, her expression all mock innocence. “Am I doing something wrong?” she asked.

    Rio just stared at her, unblinking, until Agatha sighed like she was being so unfairly scolded.

    “Oh, come on,” Agatha drawled. “Did you not pay attention to Lilia’s very insightful lesson back there? We’re supposed to sell the part, sweetheart.” Shaking her head, Rio disagreed. “There’s a difference between selling the part and making a scene.”

    Agatha gave an exaggerated hum of consideration, as if truly mulling that over. “Nope. I don’t see a difference.”

    Rio debated if this was a fight worth having. She settled back into her seat, forcibly schooling her expression into something composed. “Just focus.” Agatha grinned. Oh, she was focused, alright. But not in the way Rio wanted her to be.
