    She leaned forward slightly, arms resting against the table, her voice dipping enough to be heard over the noise around them. “As if I’d ever want to go on a date with someone like you anyway.”

    Rio’s brows lifted in quiet challenge. “Someone like me?” Agatha shrugged, unbothered. “You know.” She made a vague gesture in Rio’s direction. “Uptight. Rule-following. A little boring.” Rio let out the softest, quietest scoff. “Boring?”

    Agatha nodded, feigning sympathy. “Tragic, really.”

    Rio studied her, the dim lighting making it harder to read what exactly was behind that stare. Finally, she gave the smallest shake of her head, more exasperation than actual irritation, before turning her attention firmly back to the crowd.

    Agatha, smug, leaned back again, stretching out comfortably. “See? Selling the part. You’re welcome.”

    Rio didn’t dignify her with a response.

    Which, if anything, only made Agatha grin wider.

    Rio tilted her head ever so slightly, analyzing Agatha with something calculating. Then, with a measured blink, “Right. Because you would make such a stellar date.”

    Agatha’s smirk deepened. “Obviously.”

    Rio hummed, unimpressed. “Yeah. Sure. Who wouldn’t want to go out with someone who picks fights for fun, drinks like she’s still twenty, and has the emotional availability of a brick wall?”

    Agatha’s lips parted slightly, just enough to register that as an actual hit. She recovered quickly, though, shaking her head with a dramatic sigh. “See, this is exactly what I’m talking about,” she mused. “No sense of fun.” “Oh, forgive me,” Rio deadpanned. “Let me properly appreciate your charming personality and absolute lack of self-preservation.” Agatha grinned. “That’s more like it.”

    Rio rolled her eyes, but Agatha could see the way her fingers subtly curled against the fabric of her blazer, resisting the urge to really dig into this. Agatha meanwhile, tapped a slow rhythm against the table’s edge. “You know,” she mused, voice low and teasing, “I don’t think this whole date thing is that hard. You’re already flirting with me.”

    Rio barely glanced at her, her expression flat. “That’s not what this is.” Agatha shrugged with a smirk. “If you say so.”

    Rio took a deep breath, clearly deciding it wasn’t worth the argument. Rather, she pointedly turned away, refocusing on the bar like it was the most interesting place in the world.

    Agatha kept her fingers against the table, watching Rio. “You’re getting awfully riled up for someone who definitely wouldn’t want to date me.” Rio didn’t bite. “I’m riled up because we have a job to do, and you can’t seem to focus.”

    “Oh, I’m focused,” Agatha countered smoothly. “Just multitasking.” She let her grin sharpen. “Keeping an eye on the mission and making sure you don’t die of boredom.”

    Rio finally turned to look at her. “Right. Because what would I do without your riveting personality to keep me entertained?”

    Agatha nodded. “Exactly.”

    Rio began to look like she was this close to losing whatever patience she had left. So, she angled toward Agatha slightly, “You know,” she began, “if I didn’t know any better, I’d think you’re the one getting into character too much.”

    Agatha raised a brow. “Oh?”

    Rio hummed. “All this teasing, the flirting. Really seems like you’re enjoying this.”

    Agatha snorted. “Please.”

    Rio leaned in, lowering her voice just enough that it forced Agatha to pay attention. “You’re doing a lot just to get a reaction out of me, Harkness.” Agatha blinked. Then narrowed her eyes. “Yeah? And what are you doing, Vidal?”

    Rio’s lips quirked. “Making sure we stay on task.”

    Agatha watched her. Was she? Or was she just as caught in this back and forth as Agatha was?

    Agatha twisted her thumb ring absently, feeling the cool metal press against her skin. She should drop it and back off before Rio actually called her bluff.

    Instead, she smirked. “You sure about that, babe?”

    Rio’s expression didn’t shift, but Agatha caught the way her fingers curled against the tabletop. She was getting to her.

    Rio didn’t say anything.

    Agatha grinned, satisfied. “Yeah. That’s what I thought.” Rio decided this wasn’t the moment to entertain whatever game Agatha was playing. “Try not to get too carried away,” she muttered, before pointedly looking away.

    “No promises.” The older woman shrugged.

    Rio molded her expression into something neutral as she turned her eyes away from Agatha and back toward their goal. The warm glow of the overhead lights cast long shadows, stretching across the wood-paneled walls and the faces of the patrons who had long since melted into the background noise of country music and murmured conversation. She wasn’t amused at all.

    Not by Agatha’s smugness, or the way she said babe with that infuriating little grin, like she knew exactly what she was doing. Not by the fact that Agatha was enjoying getting under her skin, testing her patience with every teasing remark.

    But—Rio’s tongue pressed against the inside of her cheek, biting down the twitch of a smile that almost threatened to form.

    A tiny, dangerous part of her was enjoying it.

    Because for the past week, all they had was silence. Cold, deliberate avoidance. The kind that sat in the air like static, thick and suffocating, like something waiting to break. A week of stiff conversations that barely lasted a sentence.

    This, at least, was something.

    The irritation was familiar. The push and pull, coupled with Agatha’s teasing with the constant challenge woven into every glance and smirk. It was how they worked. For a moment, it almost felt normal again. Rio shook off the lingering warmth of amusement curling at the edge of her thoughts.

    Focus. They weren’t here to argue or banter like they always did. They were here to do a job.

    Brown eyes lingered onto Lilia already in position, posture relaxed, a glass of something dark in front of her. She was listening and actually doing what they were here to do.

    Meanwhile, Rio was stuck playing this game with Agatha.

    “Keep your eyes on the job, Harkness,” she murmured, finally. Agatha, still lounging across from her like she had all the time in the world, lifted a brow. “I am.”

    Rio shot her a look, unimpressed.

    Agatha’s lips twitched. “Multitasking, remember?”

    The waitress returned with an easy, practiced sway, a tray balanced on one hand, her expression unreadable but mildly entertained.

    Rio kept her posture composed, already regretting the last few minutes of wasted time. She needed to be paying attentioninstead of indulging in whatever little game Agatha was playing.

    “Whiskey for you,” the waitress said, sliding the glass toward Agatha with an unnecessary extra glance, before turning to Rio. “And a gin and tonic for the broody one.”

    Rio scrunched her nose, keeping her expression carefully neutral. She barely acknowledged the jab, instead offering the woman a polite nod as she reached for her drink.

    Agatha, of course, leaned forward on her forearms, her grin still irritatingly present. “Broody, huh?” She shot Rio a sideways glance, all slow amusement, before taking a sip of her whiskey.

    Rio ignored her.

    The waitress smirked, clearly emboldened by the lack of reaction. “It’s a talent, really. Some people are good at poker faces, but you? You look deeply unimpressed at all times.”

    Agatha snorted, resting her chin against her fist. “You have no idea.” Rio rolled her eyes and took a sip of her drink, willing the cool bite of gin to settle the heat creeping up her spine. This was exactly why she didn’t let Agatha get under her skin.

    The waitress lingered a second longer before giving Agatha one last look. “Let me know if you need anything else.”

    She was looking at Agatha.

    Rio exhaled, the tightness in her jaw barely noticeable as she forced her shoulders to relax. Focus on the mission, not on this.

    The woman finally stepped away, disappearing back into the crowded bar. Agatha made a sound low in her throat, taking another sip of whiskey before setting her glass down, looking entirely too entertained. “Would you stop?” Rio muttered, keeping her voice low.

    Agatha lifted a brow, feigning innocence. “Stop what?”

    Rio shot her a sharp look, her fingers tightening slightly around the cool glass in her hand. “You know what.”

    Agatha smirked. “Didn’t Lilia tell us to sell it? Flirting’s part of the gig Vidal.”

    Rio felt the familiar irritation rise, slow and warm beneath her ribs. She let out a slow, quiet breath. “We’re here to do a job, Agatha.” Agatha eyes gleamed with something mischievous. “Right. I am helping.” Rio resisted the urge to close her eyes and count to ten. “You’re testing me.”

    Agatha smiled against the rim of her glass. “And you’re failing spectacularly.”

    Rio pressed her tongue to the inside of her cheek, willing herself to stay calm. She didn’t look at Agatha when she finally spoke. “Flirting with the waitress wasn’t part of the job.”

    Agatha swirled the whiskey in her glass before taking another sip. “Wasn’t it?”

    Rio dragged her fingers along the condensation on her drink. “No, it wasn’t.”

    Agatha leaned forward, resting an elbow on the table. “And yet,” she teased, “it seemed to get a reaction.”

    Rio did look at her then, glaring slightly. “You think this is funny?” Agatha nodded, unbothered. “A little.”

    “We’re supposed to be watching, not playing games.”

    Agatha’s fingers tapped idly against the glass before her. “I am watching. I told you I can multi-task.”

    Rio didn’t dignify that with a response, she simply set her drink down and went back to doing her job.

    Agatha chuckled lowly, still watching her. “You’re wound so tight, Vidal,” she murmured, a small tease under the words. “Maybe I should buy you another drink, loosen you up.”

    Rio kept her eyes locked on the crowd. “Or you could try taking this seriously.”

    Agatha gave a soft laugh. “I am,” she said, quieter this time. Rio turning her eyes back to Agatha with a look edged in exasperation. “You’re unbelievable.”

    “We’re on a mission, and you’re flirting with the damn waitress. That wasn’t part of the job.”

    Agatha smiled a bit before taking a slow sip. “Relax, Vidal. It’s called blending in.”

    Rio let out a humorless breath. “Blending in? That wasn’t blending in. That was you being wildly unprofessional.”

    Agatha’s brows flicked up. “Unprofessional?” She leaned forward, propping her elbow against the table. “Come on, babe. If anything, I was selling it.”

    Rio disagreed. “You were stroking your own ego.”

    Agatha pretended to consider it. “Mmm. Maybe. Or…” she dragged the words out, tone amused, “you’re just jealous.”

    Rio stilled.

    Agatha grinned. “That’s it, isn’t it? That little eye roll? The way you kicked me?” She tapped a finger against her chin. “Oh yeah. You hated that.” Rio set her glass down a little too forcefully, the sound muffled beneath the hum of the bar. She turned to Agatha, her stare pointed. “You think I care who you flirt with?”

    Agatha’s grin was damn near wicked. “I know you do.”

    Rio clenched her jaw, Agatha could always get under her damn skin, always knew how to push the right buttons.

    She squared her shoulders. “Get over yourself.”

    Agatha chuckled lowly. “Not likely.”

    Rio was determined to ignore her at this point, she had to. The job came first and she had been distracted long enough.

    But then Agatha moved.

    "Shit, it’s hot in here," she muttered, rolling her shoulders back before reaching for the sides of her leather jacket. The motion was unthinking, casual, but Rio felt it like a slow pull of gravity, dragging her eyes away from the crowd and straight to Agatha without permission. Her fingers moved beneath the lapels, gripping the worn leather before pushing it down the broad line of her shoulders. The movement flexed the muscles in her forearms, tendons tightening beneath tanned skin, the slow drag of fabric slipping down her biceps revealing the shape of them—lean, defined, strong.

    Rio clenched her jaw, but it was already too late.

    The jacket peeled away inch by inch, exposing the stark white of her t-shirt beneath the dim neon lights, the way it stretched across her frame, the way it fit—God.

    Rio forced herself to look away, to breathe, to focus on anything but the way Agatha shook out the jacket before tossing it onto the seat beside her. The damage was already done.

    Because now, the image was seared into her brain. She had seen this at Agatha’s house; the night of the kiss.

    The memory came unbidden, hitting like a slow, simmering burn beneath her ribs. She remembered how Agatha had done the same thing that night, the rolling of the shoulders, peeling off her jacket with that unconscious grace, shaking out her arms in the same way that sent a ripple of tension up her forearms.

    Rio had watched her then, too. Had taken in every detail and shift of movement, every flex of muscle beneath scarred, sun-warmed skin. Had felt something twist deep in her gut that she hadn’t had time to name before Agatha had turned to her, the air between them had grown heated and charged.

    Before Agatha had kissed her.

    Her throat worked as she swallowed, her grip tightening around her glass like an anchor, grounding, steadying.

    She needed to snap out of it.

    God help her.

    Rio forced the tension down where it could stay buried. She reached for her glass again, the smooth condensation slick beneath her fingers. The cold bite of the gin and tonic was sharp against her tongue as she took a slow sip, letting the crispness cut through the heat in her gut.

    The ice clinked softly as she set the glass back down, her fingers lingering a fraction too long against the cool surface. Anything to anchor herself and to pull her focus away from her—the woman sitting across from her. Agatha’s posture shifted, the teasing edge from earlier vanishing in an instant. Her eyes narrowed, focus locking onto something across the room. Rio noticed the change immediately. “What?” she asked, voice wary. Agatha didn’t answer at first. She was squinting, barely moving, lips pressing into a thin line as she tried to make out the words being exchanged. Johnson was still tending bar, but his attention wasn’t on the drinks in front of him, it was on the man sitting just to his right. Their conversation was quiet, brief, but whatever was being said had weight. Johnson’s mouth barely moved as he spoke, the words slipping out of the corner of his lips like he’d done this a hundred times before. A slow nod toward the far side of the bar punctuated whatever he said next.

    Agatha’s stomach turned.

    “He’s whispering to the guy at the bar,” she whispered under her breath, barely loud enough for Rio to catch.

    Rio followed immediately, turning to find the exchange. “You catch what he said?”

    Agatha kept watching, slowly blinking. “Not all of it. But I caught enough.” Rio’s shoulders tensed. “Go on.”

    Agatha let out a slow breath, eyes locked on Johnson. “Something about ‘the next girl.’”

    Rio didn’t respond right away. She didn’t need to. The meaning behind those words was enough to settle something cold into her chest. She followed Agatha’s line of view and spotted a woman sitting alone at the far end of the bar, swirling her half-empty drink absently. She wasn’t paying attention to anything, oblivious to the conversation happening just feet away from her.

    Johnson’s associate didn’t move right away. He simply nodded, barely a gesture, before adjusting in his seat like he was weighing his approach. Agatha spoke carefully. “He’s marking her.”

    “Or testing her.”

    Agatha’s eyes finally met with Rio for a second. “We let it play out?” The other woman nodded slightly. “For now. We need to see if he makes contact.”

    “If he lingers too long?”

    Rio’s voice was steel. “We intervene.”

    The music pounded around them, the bar carried on—laughter, voices, the clinking of glasses—but the world had narrowed to this. Johnson, his associate, and the woman sitting alone, unaware that she had just become the center of a transaction.

    From the bar, Lilia remained perfectly still, but Rio knew she had seen it too.

    Agatha shook her head, her tone hushed. “We can’t intervene.” Rio snapped toward her. “Excuse me?”

    Agatha didn’t look at her. Her focus remained locked on Johnson, assessing. Across the bar, he was still working, drying off a glass with slow, practiced movements, but his attention was split. His mouth moved in hushed conversation, words slipping discreetly to the man beside him. The guy nodded, casual, like they were talking about the weather. But Agatha knew better. She could see the way Johnson’s eyes flickered toward the woman at the bar.

    “If we step in now, we blow our cover,” she hushed, fingers tapping idly against the rim of her glass, though the rest of her body remained still. Rio’s grip on her own drink tightened. “So we just sit here? Let this play out while she—”

    “I didn’t say that.”

    Agatha’s voice had an edge, her words low under the music. This time, she did glance at Rio for a second before turning back to the scene in front of her.

    “We observe,” she clarified, “We establish a pattern. We figure out whether Johnson’s just a middleman or if he’s directing this.” She leaned in slightly, voice dropping lower. “If we jump too soon, we lose everything. If this is a coordinated effort—if there’s more than just him involved—we need proof.”

    Rio’s shoulders were rigid, her entire body coiled like she was barely holding herself in check. “And what if she leaves with him?” Agatha weighed the situation. “Then we move. But only if we have to.” Her fingers toyed against her whiskey glass before stilling completely. “Until then, we watch.”

    Across the room, the man finally made his move. He pushed off his stool, all ease and confidence, adjusting his jacket before sidling up beside the woman. His body language was relaxed, the kind that suggested familiarity without crossing the line into aggression. A casual ‘hey, let me buy you a drink’ act. The woman turned slightly, expression unreadable from this distance, but she didn’t immediately shut him down.

    “Eyes on him,” Agatha murmured, nodding her chin toward the bar. “He’s feeling her out. Seeing if she’s open to conversation.” Her voice dipped even lower. “They have a protocol for this. Testing interest first before taking any risks. If she pushes back, he’ll either pivot or press harder.” Rio watched the man closely. “If he presses?”

    “Then we see how far he takes it.” Agatha’s voice was steady. “If we have to step in, we do it smart. No guns, no badges, no slipping up.” Rio swallowed down the sharp retort lingering on her tongue. She knew Agatha was right. A bad move now could alert Johnson, could get the whole operation buried before they even had a chance to dig.

    “Move,” Agatha muttered lowly.

    Rio barely turned her head. “Excuse me?”

    Agatha didn’t take her eyes off the bar. “Slide over. You sit here; you can keep an eye on Johnson. I’ll watch the other guy.”

    Rio hesitated for half a second. Not because she didn’t trust the call— Agatha was right—but because it meant they’d be sitting shoulder to shoulder, no table between them.

    Rio took her drink and moved, sliding over into the seat beside Agatha, their legs nearly brushing as she settled in. The shift forced them into the same space, close enough that Rio could feel the residual warmth from Agatha’s body, smell the faint trace of whiskey and leather clinging to her skin.

    Agatha kept her vision forward. If she was aware of their proximity, she didn’t show it.

    “We wait,” she said again, softer this time.

    The low bass of the music mixed with the raucous laughter from the pool table in the corner faded into the background. Their attention was zoned in. The man leaned in, resting an elbow against the counter, cutting into the woman’s space. Not aggressively. He wanted to make his presence known. She turned toward him, her expression relaxed but cautious, lips moving as she responded.

    Agatha clenched her teeth.

    She wasn’t aware she was holding her breath until Rio moved slightly beside her, shifting just enough to let her shoulder press against the cushioned back of the booth. The movement was subtle, but Agatha caught the way Rio’s fingers curled tighter around her drink, the ice clinking softly against the sides of the glass.

    The tension that had once existed between them, now redirected—honed in on the interaction unraveling across the room.

    Johnson was still behind the counter, cleaning a glass, watching, pretending not to. His companion was doing all the talking now, testing the waters. The woman responded, tilting her head slightly, her body language open but not overly inviting.

    Rio breathed slowly. “She doesn’t seem fully engaged.”

    Agatha nodded, voice quiet. “She’s humoring him.”

    They watched as the man angled himself closer, fingers tapping against the bar as he said something that made the woman blink. She hesitated, then slowly shook her head.

    “There,” Rio whispered, gaze attentive. “She’s not interested.” The man didn’t immediately back off. Instead, he smiled, like a salesman sweetening the deal. His hand lifted, gesturing toward Johnson. “He’s not leaving,” The detective muttered.

    Rio leaned forward just slightly, a slow inhale through her nose as her jaw tightened.

    They were both waiting.

    Waiting to see if he’d walk away.

    Waiting to see if Johnson would get involved.

    Waiting to see just how far this would go.

    They weren’t being careful.

    Too focused, too tuned in, their eyes cutting across the room with singular purpose.

    Agatha and Rio had spent the last ten minutes tracking every movement, every flicker of expression between Johnson and his guy. But in their concentration, they’d made one crucial mistake—they weren’t being subtle. They might as well have painted a target on their backs.

    Rio saw it first.

    A change in the man’s stance, he stiffened, his easy charm faltering for a fraction of a second. A glance was thrown in their direction, quick and deliberate.

    Rio’s stomach dipped.

    He knew.

    Agatha, still fixated on Johnson, didn’t catch it immediately, but Rio did. Johnson’s conversation had shifted, He leaned in toward his guy, his lips barely moving as he murmured something under his breath. It wasn’t casual.

    The man at the bar turned to face them, full-on this time, suspicion creeping into his features.

    Rio muttered under her breath, voice just loud enough for Agatha to catch, “We have a problem.”

    Agatha’s attention snapped toward her. “What?”

    Rio didn’t answer right away. Instead, she dipped her head slightly, like she was adjusting in her seat.

    Agatha followed her line of sight and caught the way Johnson’s guy was staring now, his easy demeanor gone, his expression unreadable but wary. The kind of look that said he noticed them.

    The shift in the air was immediate.

    Agatha’s hand dropped to her lap, fingers brushing the edge of her thumb ring as if considering something, her pulse thrumming against her skin. “We’ve been made,” Rio murmured, barely moving her lips.

    Agatha’s eyes darkened as she breathed nervously. “Yeah,” she said in a whisper. “I see it.”

    Their cover was starting to slip.

    They needed to fix it—fast.

    The man didn’t look away, he whispered something to Johnson, fingers tapping an uneven rhythm against the counter. It could’ve been another low conversation in a loud bar; but it wasn’t.

    Agatha knew what a warning looked like.

    Johnson paused mid-motion, one hand still wiping down a glass, the other resting against the bar as he processed whatever had just been said to him. Then, like he was testing a theory, he glanced up.

    His eyes moved right to them.

    Shit.

    Agatha didn’t move. But she felt Rio tense beside her, that same instinctive pull toward control, toward reeling this in before it unraveled completely. They’d been too obvious, just a few more seconds and it wouldn’t matter. Then, right when things were about to tip, Lilia moved.

    Not urgently or like she was swooping in to save them. The kind of effortless that came with experience. She stepped away from her stool at the counter, her body sliding directly into Johnson’s line of sight as she made a slow, meandering path toward them.

    Agatha stopped just short of Rio’s desk, eyeing the folder like it had personally offended her. Her mouth twisted into something halfway between a scowl and a smirk. “Did I piss someone off this week and forget about it?”

    “Harkness this isn’t punishment. It’s necessity.”

    Agatha snorted under her breath. “Sure feels like punishment.” Lilia turned to Rio, who, of course, sat primly, not a strand of hair out of place. “Records. All of it. You’ll be digging through half the department’s clerical garbage until it shines.”

    “Seriously?” Agatha groaned as she reluctantly stepped closer, dragging her boots along the floor like she was being led to a firing squad. “I thought we earned a little mercy after last night.”

    Lilia arched a brow, clearly unmoved. “You thought wrong.” She nodded toward the folder now sitting on Rio’s desk, then flicked her gaze between them. “Besides you get to work in close quarters. How cozy right?”

    Agatha opened her mouth to complain and maybe toss out a dry one-liner and call it a day, but Lilia cut her off with a single raised finger. Her universal mom look that said I’m not done talking.

    “Save it,” Lilia said, tone firm yet fond. “This isn’t punishment. It’s logistics. I think you two make a good team. Even when you're kissing on the job.”

    Rio stiffened instantly, posture going ramrod straight like she’d been hit with a Taser.

    Agatha’s jaw moved like it wanted to form words, but no sound came out accompanied by a face flushed with mortifying pink. Dragging a hand down her face, she spoke, “You’re not gonna let that go, are you?” “Why would I?” Lilia grinned, leaning in with all the theatrical flair of someone who’d earned the right to gloat. “It was fun watching from ten feet away while you two made out like teenagers on prom night.” Agatha felt the heat creep further up her neck. “It wasn’t—” “Oh please,” Lilia cut in, clearly delighted now. “You were also so flustered when you walked her to her door. You even hesitated before saying goodnight, like a proper gentleman.” She clutched her chest dramatically. “My heart could hardly take it.”

    Rio cleared her throat, still studiously staring at the desk like it might open up and swallow her whole.

    “And hey,” Lilia added, “don’t hate me for honking. You two looked like you were about to go in for round two, and frankly, I didn’t have the patience to wait in the car while you figured it out.”

    Agatha gestured helplessly, trying and failing to look composed. “You said sell it.”

    “I said sell it, not swallow each other whole,” Lilia teased, tapping the folder again. “But I will say—I’m proud. I never told you two to kiss. That part was all you.”

    Rio finally looked up; voice smooth. “Did we at least get what we needed?” “We did.” Lilia’s tone softened, pride now settling beneath usual crispness. “Johnson let just enough slip when he got cocky. That guy he was with was definitely part of the chain. We’ve got new leads now thanks to your performance.”

    Agatha sighed and shifted her weight, one hand resting on her hip. “You done gloating yet?”

    Lilia motherly and infuriating all at once simply patted the folder like she was tucking them into bed. “Records room. Go earn your stripes.” Agatha made a face like she’d just been handed a death sentence. “Why can’t you make William do it?” she muttered, eyeing the folder like it might bite. “He lives for alphabetizing. I saw him color-code the snack shelf last week.”

    From across the room, William’s voice floated in, unapologetic. “Excuse you, Harkness. That shelf was a disgrace to pantries everywhere. And you’re welcome.”

    Lilia smirked. “William already filed two case logs this morning, reorganized the asset locker, and walked me through syncing my iCloud.” Agatha squinted. “Oh, what ever would we do without the serviceable twink?”

    “You also made out with your partner on a government mission,” Lilia added, sweet as sugar. “So consider this your penance.”

    Rio looked like she was actively trying to disappear behind her coffee cup. Agatha narrowed her eyes. “You're enjoying this.”

    Lilia turned to Rio and added with genuine warmth, “Try not to kill each other. Or flirt too loudly. The walls echo.”

    Agatha opened her mouth to retort, but Lilia was already striding away, bracelets jangling like wind chimes in her wake.

    The detective turned to Rio with a sigh. “Come on, partner. Let’s go alphabetize our sins.”

    ~

    The hallway outside the records room was cold in that sterile, fluorescent sort of way with all pale tile and outdated department posters curling at the edges, laminated corners yellowed and curling from too many years of being ignored. Agatha walked slightly ahead, the toe of her boot catching on a loose corner of the floor mat as she reached for the door. She caught it smoothly, barely breaking stride, but Rio still noticed.

    “Graceful,” Rio murmured behind her, a ghost of amusement in her voice. Agatha shot her a look over her shoulder, eyes narrowed in mock offense. “Careful, Vidal. I’m about to trap us in a windowless room for hours. Don’t give me ideas.”

    She shifted the box of case files under one arm and pushed the door open with her shoulder, letting it creak wide before stepping into the room. The air inside hit her immediately, cooler than the hallway and dry in a way that felt preserved, like the room had been sealed for years and forgotten. It smelled like old paper and toner ink, the faint bite of industrial cleaner clinging to the floor. Dust hung in the corners, disturbed only by the thin, whispering hum of a fan above.

    The room was narrow. Much narrower than she remembered. Rows of metal filing cabinets lined each wall, some drawers crooked from use, others labeled in fading Sharpie. The main table in the center barely left enough room to move around it, wedged between the cabinets and the back wall like it had been forced in years ago and never adjusted. A few boxes were already stacked unevenly on its surface, all were half-labeled, half- forgotten, and one creaked ominously as Agatha set their folder on top. The ceiling felt lower here too, like it was pressing down. The kind of space that amplified everything: the creak of a chair, the rustle of a sleeve, the sound of breathing when things went quiet.

    Agatha sighed through her nose.

    Tight quarters. Long hours. And Rio.

    She didn’t know if it was a blessing or a threat.

    Rio slipped in behind her, letting the door ease shut with a soft click. She glanced around, taking it in the way she always did—efficient, analytical, always two steps ahead. Her sleeves were still rolled halfway up her forearms like she meant business.

    This hole in the wall wasn’t just cramped, it was intimate. No room for distance, let alone space to breathe when someone like Rio Vidal was standing this close.

    Agatha muttered under her breath, “This is going to be hell.” Raising a brow, Rio moved near a table, setting her coffee down with the kind of calm purpose that always made Agatha want to rattle her. “You say that like you’ve already decided we’re going to be miserable.” Agatha leaned her hip against the edge of it, arms crossed loosely, eyes sweeping the too-small space with exaggerated dread. “I don’t have to decide. I can feel my soul leaving my body already.”

    Rio huffed a quiet laugh, dragging a box toward her with one hand and flipping the lid open. “I’ll try not to take that personally.” “Oh, it’s not personal,” Agatha said dryly. “I don’t do well with warehouse lighting and cop bullshit.”

    “Cop bullshit is our job,” Rio countered smoothly, eyes scanning a sheet of intake logs. “You’re good at it.”

    Agatha groaned dramatically and turned toward the nearest cabinet. “If I spontaneously combust in here, tell Lilia it was the filing system that killed me.”

    As her fingers already skimming over the first stack of folders, Rio smirked. “Noted. Death by paperwork. I’ll make it sound heroic.” The detective gave a short laugh despite herself, then caught the way Rio’s lips twitched around the corners as she pulled a drawer open. Something warm curled under her ribs.

    They didn’t speak again for a minute, the quiet rustle of manila folders and the low hum of the overhead lights settling over them like a blanket. Agatha moved toward the cabinet against the far wall while Rio took the one closest to the door, the space between them narrow but just wide enough to feel intentional.

    Agatha sighed again, softer this time, and opened the top drawer. “Alright,” she muttered. “Let’s get this over with.”

    The hours ticked by in that strange, slow way they always did when the task at hand was more muscle memory than thought. The kind of work that lulled you if you weren’t careful, paper in, paper out, name, date, file, next. Their movements began to sync without effort. When Agatha reached across to pass a file over, Rio’s fingers met hers without missing a beat. When one moved left, the other shifted right. When they needed to switch boxes, there was always a hand offered, a brush of knuckles, the warmth of skin ghosting across the back of a wrist or palm.

    At some point, Rio reached up to tuck a wayward strand of hair behind her ear, and Agatha caught herself watching how the motion was slow and casual, like Rio didn’t realize she was doing it.

    Outside, the pale light filtering through the frosted window had shifted to a more muted gray. Time was passing. Slowly, but surely.

    Still, neither of them had tried to leave the rhythm they’d fallen into. The occasional comment. The shared glances. The soft brush of fingers over paper. A beat too long here, a smile too soft there.

    Agatha reached for a half-labeled file, squinting. “Jesus. Who wrote this? A raccoon mid-seizure?”

    Rio didn’t look up from her stack, but her mouth twitched. “That’s Captain Delaney’s handwriting.”

    Agatha plucked another folder from the same open box, squinting down at the scrawl on the tab. Her lip curled. “Okay, I’m officially convinced Delaney writes like he’s being electrocuted mid-sentence.” Rio didn’t look up from her own pile, but her lips pressed together in that very specific way that meant she was holding back a laugh. “That’s his field shorthand,” she said, prim as ever.

    Agatha held the folder up. “Looks like he let a drunk pigeon take dictation.” Rio shook her head, but the smile threatened to break through. “And yet, somehow, he’s still your superior.”

    “Debatable,” Agatha muttered, flipping the file open and dropping into her chair with a dramatic sigh. “At least I don’t sign off on reports like a raccoon pawed the pen.”

    Rio finally looked up, “If you spent half as much time reading those reports as you do insulting them, we’d be done already.”

    Agatha arched a brow, smirking. “Aw, listen to you. So stern. It’s cute.” Rio looked away first, her cheeks subtly pink. “I’m being professional.” “You’re being uptight,” Agatha countered, dragging her next file toward her. “You’ve reorganized that pile three times.”

    “It was crooked.”

    Agatha leaned back in her chair and let out a soft laugh. “Jesus. You’re like a filing robot.”

    Rio rolled her eyes but quickly ducked her head, trying to hide the smile breaking across her face.

    Agatha was still grinning to herself when her eyes caught on a familiar name halfway down the page. Her smirk faded.

    She sat up straighter, brows furrowing, the paper crinkling slightly in her hands as she adjusted her grip.

    “Wait a second…”

    Next to her, Rio looked up immediately at the change in tone. “What?” Agatha didn’t answer right away. Her eyes scanned the page again, slower this time, tracking every line of Delaney’s jagged handwriting. The messy scrawl she’d mocked moments ago now felt suddenly deliberate. Like it was hiding something in plain sight.

    She flipped back to the first page of the file. A kidnapping case from five years ago regarding a teenage girl, never recovered. She remembered the buzz around it back then—how it fizzled out after a month, how Delaney had pushed it into cold storage faster than anyone expected. Buried near the bottom of the witness list, nearly illegible unless you were looking for it.

    John R. Johnson – dismissed. No follow-up required.

    Agatha’s stomach turned.

    “Fuck,” she muttered, more to herself than anything. Her voice had dropped low, tight with something sharp and rising.

    Rio was already moving toward her. “What is it?”

    Agatha turned the file slightly so Rio could see, tapping her finger under the name.

    “Johnson. He was a lead. Maybe not a good one, maybe a long shot—but Delaney waved him off. No follow-up, no interview, nothing.” Rio’s brows drew together as she leaned in, close enough that Agatha could feel the warmth of her sleeve brush against her arm. Her eyes flicked across the page, fast but focused.

    “Dismissed with no cause…” she murmured. “That’s not standard.” “No,” Agatha said. “It’s not.”

    They both stood there for a beat, the room suddenly quiet again. Agatha glanced at her. “Tell me that doesn’t smell like shit to you.” Rio straightened, already reaching for one of the adjacent boxes. “We need to find everything Delaney filed around that case. Any correspondence. Internal notes.”

    Agatha nodded, jaw set. “I’ll pull his supervisor logs.”

    They moved like a well-oiled machine, urgency threading through their movements—but underneath it, that same flicker of connection hummed. Unspoken, but there. Strengthened by trust, by proximity, by the way they now leaned closer without hesitating.

    Rio’s hand hovered over the next folder, but she didn’t move. Her brows pulled together, the quiet furrow between them deepening. The flicker of alarm in her eyes wasn’t panicked it was careful.

    “This goes deeper than I thought,” she said, voice just above a whisper. “If Delaney waved Johnson off from a kidnapping case five years ago, and now he’s stonewalling our questions—”

    “—Then he’s covering something,” Agatha finished, snapping the folder in her hands shut.

    Rio didn’t answer right away. Her fingers tapped lightly against the cardboard edge, an old nervous habit slipping through her composure. Agatha turned to face her fully. “You’re thinking about protocol.” Rio’s jaw flexed, like she wanted to deny it. “I’m thinking about how far this could go,” she said quietly. “Delaney isn’t some beat cop. He’s our captain. If he’s involved and we pursue this without backup, it could blow up in our faces.”

    Her voice stayed steady, but Agatha saw the tension at the corners of her mouth, the tightness in her shoulders. Not fear exactly, but caution rooted so deep it had been trained into her bones.

    Agatha stepped in, closing the space just a little.

    “You think I don’t get that?” Voice stern. “You think I haven’t done the math?”

    Rio met her eyes, and there was something raw in her gaze. “If we’re wrong —”

    “We’re not.” Agatha cut her off gently, but there was no room for doubt. “You saw the files. You saw how fast he shut down that interrogation. You felt how wrong it was.”

    Rio’s pursed her lips turning to look elsewhere.

    “I’m not asking you to go rogue,” Agatha said, softer now. “I’m asking you to stop pretending the rules are going to save you when the people writing them are hiding behind them.”

    That landed. Rio’s eyes darkened, just a shade. Her fingers curled slightly around the edge of the table.

    Agatha tilted her head. “You want to be cautious? Fine. Be cautious. But don’t be blind. We follow the trail. We document everything, if Delaney’s name shows up again, we don’t ignore it. We do the job.”

    For a beat, Rio didn’t speak.

    Then, finally, “You really think he’s involved?”

    Agatha’s lips pressed into a tight line. “I think the fucker knows more than he’s saying, but if we don’t dig deeper someone else is going to disappear.” Rio’s nod was quiet, her focus already shifting back to the stack of folders. Agatha stared at her for a second longer, like she was trying to memorize the expression Rio wore when she chose to cross a line with her. Not for Agatha. But with her.

    She exhaled a breath she hadn’t realized she’d been holding and turned back toward the pile of paperwork. “Let’s get to it, then,” she said. “Before I start fantasizing about flipping Delaney’s desk over in front of IA.” Agatha watched her for a second longer.

    She leaned back against a chair, arms crossed loosely. “You always like following the rules this much?”

    Rio didn’t glance up. “It’s protocol.”

    “That’s not an answer.”

    Rio slid a folder open with clinical precision, her voice level. “It’s how the chain of command works. It’s what keeps operations clean. No improvising, just order.”

    Agatha arched a brow. “That what got you into the Bureau?” Rio gave a noncommittal shrug, eyes still fixed on the file in front of her. “It’s what keeps people safe.”

    Agatha let that sit for a beat. The room was quiet except for the low hum of the overhead lights and the faint shuffle of pages.

    Then, softly, “You don’t trust yourself when it’s messy, do you?” Rio’s fingers stilled on the edge of the folder.

    “That’s what this is about, right? Not Delaney. Not orders. You don’t let yourself make calls like this, because if it gets complicated, if it goes sideways, there’s no one to blame but you.”

    Rio’s jaw flexed. Her expression didn’t change, but the silence said enough. Agatha turned toward the file cabinet again, flipping open another drawer. “You’re allowed to trust your gut, you know. Especially when it’s screaming at you.”

    Rio was quiet for a long moment.

    “That’s not how I was taught.”

    Agatha nodded once, the pieces clicking into place. “Well… whoever taught you forgot to mention that sometimes, the system breaks. When it does you either play along, or you get ahead of it.”

    Rio said nothing but her posture shifted, like a weight had redistributed within her.

    She turned back to the boxes, the motion stiff with her shoulders squared like she needed the structure of movement to hold her upright. She picked up another file, but her eyes didn’t track it. She simply stared through the paper like it might answer something for her.

    Agatha didn’t let the silence stretch too far as she walked towards the brown eyed woman, not invading her space but moved close enough that Rio could feel the older woman beside behind her.

    “You know,” she said, voice low, “you’ve been stonewalling me since the day I met you.”

    That made Rio blink. She looked over, a faint crease forming between her brows.

    Agatha tilted her head, studying her. “I don’t mean at work. I mean you, everything about you is locked down. Professional.” She paused. “Except it’s not, not really.”

    Rio’s lips parted slightly, a protest forming but Agatha cut in before she could deflect.

    “You do this thing, Vidal. Where you act like it’s about doing your job the right way. Like it’s about control.” Her voice softened, but the edge didn’t leave. “But it’s not control, is it? It’s fear.”

    That landed harder than she meant it to.

    Rio’s eyes narrowed, not in anger, but like the words had peeled something back that she wasn’t ready to look at. “I’m not afraid,” she said, quiet. Agatha didn’t back off. “Aren’t you?”

    She stepped closer again, just a breath. “You don’t have to tell me what it is. But I know what it looks like.”

    Rio turned her head then, eyes fixed on a box like it might let her disappear into it. But her voice didn’t shake. “I learned early that getting it wrong comes with consequences. Big ones. For other people, and not just me.” Agatha nodded slowly, her voice lower now. “So, you built a wall. Straight lines, clean rules, no gray areas.”

    Rio finally looked at her again. “And yet you bulldoze right through them.” Agatha smiled. “Sometimes walls need knocking down.”

    Rio stared at her, she then quietly asked, “You always push this hard?” With a small, crooked smirk, Agatha answered, “Only when I think it matters.”

    Rio didn’t move for a long moment, then she leaned back against the edge of a filing cabinet nearby, the folder still in her hands. “When I joined the Bureau,” she said quietly, eyes fixed somewhere ahead, “it was all about control and doing things right. That’s how you get ahead and how you stay ahead.”

    Agatha didn’t interrupt.

    Rio’s fingers fidgeted slightly around the folder. “The ones who kept their heads down, followed orders, didn’t ask too many questions, made it. The ones who hesitated, who improvised too much, who went off-book? They burned out. Or got blamed when things went wrong.”

    She glanced at Agatha, just for a second, like she was testing how much truth she could let out.

    “So yeah,” she added, a faint bitter edge creeping into her voice. “Control works. Polished and put-together gets results. No one questions the girl who’s always composed.”

    Agatha stepped closer, her gaze steady. “Except that’s not real. Not out here.”

    Rio gave a quiet laugh—one that didn’t reach her eyes. “It’s the only thing that’s ever worked.”

    Agatha shook her head once, slow and certain. “Maybe in the Bureau. Maybe in training manuals and performance reports. But not in real life, not when girls go missing and the people at the top tell you to look the other way.”

    Rio’s jaw tightened.

    “You keep playing by their rules, you’re going to end up protecting the wrong people.” Agatha muttered.

    That made Rio look at her again. “And what if the wrong people are the ones in charge?” she asked softly.

    “Then we burn their playbook.”

    Rio was quiet again. “I’m not used to this.”

    Agatha smiled. “Well,” she said, brushing past Rio to grab another folder. “Get used to it, Vidal. You partnered with the wrong woman if you wanted someone who plays it safe.”

    “You know,” she began, casually, thinking out loud, “you’re very... composed.”

    Rio blinked like she wasn’t sure if that was meant as a compliment. “I try to be,” she said carefully.

    Agatha tilted her head, the edge of a smirk forming. “Always this buttoned- up?”

    Rio’s brow furrowed just a little. “What’s that supposed to mean?” “I just mean,” Agatha said, her voice light, teasing, “are you this... composed with women, too?”

    That stopped Rio cold. A slightly too-long pause before she dropped her gaze back to the file in her hand and turned one page too slowly to be natural.

    “I don’t see how that’s relevant to the case,” she said, her voice quieter than before.

    Agatha’s smile widened, definitely amused. “God, you’re funny when you dodge.”

    Rio’s cheeks reddened. “I’m not dodging.”

    “You’re absolutely dodging.”

    “I’m being professional.”

    Agatha gave a low chuckle, eyes twinkling. “That’s not a no, Vidal.” Rio cleared her throat. “I’m normal.”

    Agatha scoffed. “You’re what?”

    “Normal,” Rio repeated, straighter now, as if doubling down. “I act normal when I like someone.”

    Agatha broke, the room filling with a loud laugh as she couldn’t help herself.

    “Jesus, who even says that? What does that mean?” she grinned. “Like— you show up on time and maintain eye contact? Is there a checklist? Is there a training video?”

    Rio narrowed her eyes, face burning, but the corner of her mouth was twitching like she was fighting a smile. “I don’t know—I just meant I don’t act… weird.”

    Agatha leaned a little closer, grinning like she couldn’t help herself. “So, what, you bring a clipboard to the first date? Take notes?” Rio let out a breath that was dangerously close to a laugh and looked down, tucking a loose strand of hair behind her ear. “I meant I don’t freak out. I don’t get all… flustered.”

    “Oh yeah?” Agatha said, voice low and teasing. “You sure about that?” Rio glanced up, and Agatha caught the exact moment she regretted it because the look Agatha was giving her was all amused challenge, warm and just a little smug, like she was already winning some game Rio hadn’t realized they were playing.

    Rio blinked, then looked away fast, busying herself with the nearest file. “I’m sure.”

    Agatha bit the inside of her cheek, watching her. “You keep saying things that make me want to test you.”

    “Harkness, we have a mountain of paperwork and elicit boundary crossing to do.” Rio murmured.

    “Fine, but I’ll keep finding more ways to torture you.” Agatha said, turning back to her own pile.

    So, they worked.

    For the next few hours, the banter faded into a rhythm of quiet rustling papers, low murmurs, and the occasional clink of metal drawer handles. The records room, stuffy and tight, seemed to shrink around them as the afternoon bled into evening, shadows stretching long against the floor beneath the hum of tired fluorescent lights.

    They made real progress. Box after box, file after file, sorting and scanning with the kind of focused determination that only came when something bigger was at stake. Every now and then, their shoulders would brush. Fingers would graze when passing over folders. But nothing more was said. The earlier warmth settled into something steady. Unspoken. Laced beneath every glance, every shared nod of agreement when they found something important.

    The file stacks grew smaller. The tension didn’t.

    By the time the clock on the wall clicked past six, the room had gone quiet again. The kind of quiet that only came at the end of a long day. The kind that settled in your bones and reminded you how tired you were once adrenaline wore off.

    Agatha leaned back in her chair, sighing as she rubbed a hand across her face. Her knuckles felt stiff and brain was fried.

    She glanced over at Rio, who was still flipping through a folder, hair slipping loose from the day’s chore.

    “Alright,” Agatha said decisively. “That’s enough for today.” Rio looked up, brow pinching slightly. “We’ve still got—” “Not tonight,” Agatha said, already standing. “Come on.”

    Rio blinked. “Where?”

    Agatha grabbed her coat off the back of the chair, shrugged it on, and tossed a glance over her shoulder with a small, crooked smile.

    “Rooftop.”

    Agatha tipped her head. “You need air. I need air. And neither of us is going to sleep tonight if we don’t walk away from this for five minutes.” Rio still looked unsure, but after a beat, she closed the folder and followed. Rio veered slightly to the left, passing by her desk to grab her coat. The fabric was cool to the touch, still holding the chill from the morning, and she tugged it on as she moved, glancing briefly over her shoulder before falling into step behind Agatha. Their footsteps echoed softly against the linoleum as they moved past the bullpen and down the main hallway, toward the back of the building.

    Agatha walked ahead with purpose, but unhurried, her jacket swaying with each step, the edge of her ponytail brushing the collar. Rio’s eyes stayed on her the entire way, observing how her fingers tapped absently against her thigh, and how she glanced once over her shoulder to make sure Rio was still there.

    They turned down a narrower hall, one lined with old corkboards covered in long-forgotten memos and curling precinct notices. The overhead lights flickered soft and slow before humming back into stillness. At the end of the hallway stood a red, unmarked door. No signage, but decorated with scuffed paint and a dent near the bottom, like someone had once kicked it out of frustration.

    Agatha reached for it without hesitation.

    The handle gave with a low creak, revealing a steep, narrow staircase that twisted upward. The kind that wasn’t meant for frequent use. The walls were tight, lined in chipped white paint, and the only light came from a single exposed bulb near the ceiling, casting long shadows as they climbed. Rio followed closely, her fingers brushing the metal railing as they moved. The air changed the higher they went to cooler, thinner, touched with the scent of brick and old stone.

    At the top, Agatha paused, hand resting against the next door, the one that led outside.

    She looked back at Rio once, something soft flickering behind her eyes. Then she pushed it open.

    The night air hit them immediately sharp and clean, a rush of cold that cut through the last traces of the stuffy records room. The rooftop stretched out in front of them, wide and flat, lined with low walls and an old metal vent that hummed faintly to the side. The city unfolded beyond it; lights glowing amber and white, traffic below moving slow and muted beneath the low hum of the skyline.

    The sky above was a deep navy, clouds scattered thin enough to let stars peek through, and the snow hadn’t started yet, but it was coming. The air smelled like it.

    Agatha stepped out fully, her boots thudding softly against the concrete. She exhaled hard into the cold, her breath curling like smoke. Behind her, Rio stepped into the chill, blinking against the wind as it lifted a strand of her hair loose from behind her ear.

    Agatha walked with her hands tucked deep into her coat pockets as she crossed the rooftop. The wind tugged gently at her brown strands as they fluttered near her cheekbones. She didn’t say anything; merely walked slow and steady, until her she met the low concrete ledge that bordered the roof. Rio followed a few steps behind, her own hands tightening in the sleeves of her coat as she approached. The chill bit at her nose, but she didn’t really feel it with the adrenaline of the case still humming low in her bones, and not with Agatha standing only inches away.

    They stood side by side at the edge, the city stretched in front of them with its buildings stacked like uneven teeth in the distance, streetlights smearing golden halos across wet pavement below.

    Agatha didn’t look at her. She kept her eyes on the skyline, blue eyes narrowed slightly against the wind.

    Rio mirrored her, the quiet settling between them not awkward, but easy. Familiar. Like they’d done this a dozen times before. Maybe in another life they had.

    The wind shifted and Agatha leaned forward slightly, hands now resting on the ledge. Her shoulder bumped into Rio’s and lingered.

    “City doesn’t look so bad from here,” she said quietly, like she didn’t want to break whatever spell had settled over them.

    Rio’s gaze followed the line of buildings. “No,” she murmured. “It almost looks peaceful.”

    They stood like that for a moment. Two figures framed by skyline and dusk, close enough to feel the warmth of each other’s presence, but not quite touching.

    It was quiet in the way only winter rooftops could be.

    Rio shivered as the cold brushed over her skin like a warning, but she didn’t move away from it.

    Agatha stepped closer.

    She closed the space between them with a soft shift, her shoulder now brushing Rio’s. The gesture was protective in a way she probably didn’t realize she was being.

    “Should’ve warned you,” Agatha murmured eventually, her voice low and dry. “It’s colder up here.”

    She didn’t look at Rio when she said it. Her blue eyes fixed on the skyline that was far away, breath curling into the air in soft clouds, fading fast. Rio turned her head, watching her.

    Agatha’s profile was stark against the navy sky, hair catching in the wind, her lips tight like she was holding something back. There was a stillness in her shoulders, almost as if she moved too much, something inside her might shift the wrong way.

    “I don’t mind,” Rio said quietly.

    They stood next to each other in silence. Breathing in the same cold air and staring out over the city, like they were waiting for it to give them permission to speak.

    “I came to Chicago because I couldn’t breathe in Georgia.” Agatha’s voice was gentle, lacking the sarcastic bite Rio was used to. Simple and bare, like she wasn’t trying to dress it up.

    Rio looked over at her.

    “Not just the heat, but God it was the kind that soaked into everything. Your clothes, your sheets, the back of your knees.” A pause. “Like the air itself was trying to drown you. No breeze. Just... heavy all the time.” “I remember standing in the middle of my mom’s backyard one summer, I couldn’t’ve been older than fourteen and thinking, ‘This place is gonna kill me if I don’t leave.’” Her laugh was short, humorless. “It was probably just a heat stroke, but I meant it.”

    She shook her head slowly, then looked down at the ground, scuffing the toe of her boot against the rooftop’s surface.

    “I know it sounds dramatic. But it wasn’t just the weather. Everything about that place felt like it wanted to hold you down. Make you small and polite.” Another breath. “Make you quiet.”

    She finally glanced at Rio, eyes unreadable but honest. “Chicago’s cold. But it breathes. It gives me space, and that’s all I wanted. Room to be something else.”

    Rio didn’t say anything right away, just listened by absorbing the quiet weight of it. The pieces of Agatha that weren’t all sharp edges and smirks. The wind picked up again, tugging at the edges of Agatha’s coat. Yet she stayed still, as if she was still back in that yard, trying to breathe through the thickness of a place she couldn’t stay in.

    “I used to think the heat was the worst of it,” she said, voice quieter now, almost to herself. “But it wasn’t.”

    Rio could see the tension in her profile, even in the low light. “I still remember the sounds my mother made after my shitty excuse of a dad hit her,” she said, almost flatly. “Every time. Like she’d already left her own body before it landed. As if she knew it was coming, and she’d decided not to be there for it.”

    “And after?” Agatha huffed out a humorless breath. “She’d cry. Not loud, but these little gasps—like she didn’t want anyone to hear it, even though I was right there.” Her fingers flexed inside her pockets. “And then she’d go still again, until he was done.”

    The wind stirred lightly through her ponytail, but she didn’t seem to feel it. “I used to think maybe she didn’t say anything because she was scared. But it was more than that.”

    She swallowed, hard. Her voice turned low, brittle.

    “When he turned on me right after he was finished with her and I happened to do something, say something, look the wrong way—she never stopped him. Not once. Didn’t lift a hand.” A beat. “Didn’t even look surprised.” Rio’s chest ached just listening.

    Agatha let out a quiet breath. “I think it was because they had a problem with me long before I ever did anything worth hitting. I was a walking offense to both. Didn’t act like the other girls. Didn’t like dresses. Wanted to play tackle football in boys’ shorts with my knees scraped and hair falling out of my braid.” A dry laugh. “I thought I was just being brave.” Her shoulders dropped slightly, the tension giving way to something heavier.

    “I was too rough. Too much like him, she’d say.” Her voice softened, so low Rio had to lean slightly to hear. “I was just... not what she wanted, and it showed.”

    She turned down at her boots, then looked up again, voice quiet. “I didn’t even know what I was yet. Not fully. I just knew I liked girls and hated everything about how I was supposed to move through the world. But I didn’t know how to hide it, I guess she hated that too.”

    The wind moved again, colder now.

    “I left because if I didn’t,” Agatha said, barely audible, “I wasn’t gonna make it. And I still don’t know if she was more relieved or disappointed the day I walked out.”

    She laughed again, quiet and hollow. “Maybe both.

    For a long stretch, she said nothing more, every word had taken something from her to give.

    Then, almost as an afterthought she added, “I’ve been cold ever since. But at least I can breathe now.”

    Agatha’s hands curled around the low concrete ledge in front of her, her knuckles going pale with the pressure. The wind tugged at the red tips of her ears, and she didn’t budge. Her voice came again, quiet at first. Like it had to crawl through everything lodged in her throat.

    “I used to think all my rough edges were a problem. Got told I was too blunt or aggressive. I never had enough manners. My teachers hated it. My mom hated it. Even some of the women I dated—especially the ones who wanted to fix me—hated it.”

    She looked over at Rio for just a second, then away again, like she couldn’t bear to keep eye contact too long.

    “But here?” Her voice sharpened. “Here, it counts for something. My stubbornness. My big mouth. I get to walk into rooms full of men who think they’ve got it all figured out and I get to wear them down. I get to make them squirm like bitches and put them where they belong.” Her throat worked visibly, eyes shining just faintly in the low light. Not from tears, but from the sheer weight of what she carried. “I couldn’t save the kid I was, or my mom. But maybe I can save Lisa, or girls like her.”

    Her voice cracked with the smallest fracture.

    “So I save who I can now. I drag the truth out of every sorry bastard who thinks women like Lisa don’t matter, because someone has to. Somebody has to look at that kind of pain and say, ‘Not this time.’” She shifted to face Rio fully.

    “Maybe it doesn’t fix anything and it’s never enough. But I need it to mean something, you know?”

    There was no armor in her expression now, simply bone-deep tiredness. So, Rio turned.

    It was a slow, deliberate movement at first. Like something in her had shifted too far to be pulled back; her gaze drifted away from the city ahead and sprawl of lights that offered just enough distraction to keep her breathing steady.

    Agatha felt the shift immediately, and her breath caught with the heat of eyes on her.

    Brown eyes locked onto blue, wide and unflinching, full of quiet intensity that it almost didn’t feel real. They shimmered faintly in the low rooftop light, catching the reflection of a dozen distant windows, but all Agatha could see was sincerity. The ache. The wordless confession neither of them had dared voice.

    Her chest tightened as she didn’t look away.

    The air between them seemed to thrum with something thick, soft around the edges. The kind of silence that didn’t beg to be filled but felt. Then, Rio moved.

    Slowly, like her body didn’t quite trust itself to reach for something so delicate, her hand came up, with fingers trembling just barely in the cold, and Agatha didn’t pull away. Rio’s hand found her cheek.

    It was a whisper of contact at first, Rio’s knuckles brushed along the edge of Agatha’s jaw like she wasn’t sure if she was allowed, like touching her might break something sacred. Her fingers were cold from the wind, but gentle, as if Agatha were something fragile she hadn’t meant to reach for. Agatha didn’t move and couldn’t. For a moment, it was like the whole rooftop narrowed to that single point of contact, the rest of the world falling out of focus.

    She herself leaned in.

    Agatha closed her eyes, gentle and unhurried.

    Rio’s palm settled more firmly against her cheek, the soft heel of it cradling her jaw. Her thumb rested just beneath Agatha’s cheekbone, anchoring her in place. Her fingers curved slightly into the side of Agatha’s neck, callused and cool, but grounding in a way that made Agatha’s chest flutter. The older woman’s breath hitched audibly this time. She cleared her throat with the effort of swallowing it down. The muscles in her jaw flexed under Rio’s hand, a tremor she couldn’t suppress.

    She felt Rio’s pulse through the pad of her thumb, the warmth of her hand bleeding into her skin. A soft contrast to the cold night that pressed in around them.

    The city ahead kept humming, indifferent.

    Up here, Agatha felt like time had slowed down and the rooftop had shrunk to the span of Rio’s hand and the distance between their bodies, close enough that she could see the slight hitch in Rio’s breath, the faint rise and fall of her chest, the way her dark eyes had gone glassy with tenderness. Agatha couldn’t remember the last time she’d let herself be touched like that and trusted it.

    So, she leaned in until her forehead came to rest gently against Rio’s. A feather-light press.

    The contact was subtle, almost reverent, but it cracked something open between them. Their noses brushed faintly, a whisper of skin, and their foreheads met in a quiet point of gravity neither seemed willing to break. Their breaths mingled in the cold, with little clouds forming and vanishing in the space where their lips almost touched.

    Rio’s voice came soft above the hum of the wind. “You make this harder than it should be.”

    Agatha didn’t flinch. Her voice was low, the crack beneath it was there if you knew where to listen. “That’s kind of my thing.”

    Rio’s eyes closed again like she didn’t want to but couldn’t help it. She felt that keeping them open made her too vulnerable, too seen. Quietly and gently, Agatha dipped her head just the smallest bit again. Her nose brushed against Rio’s, feather light. Barely a nudge. After a minute, Rio whispered, almost like it hurt to say, “You ever gonna tell me why you really brought me up here?”

    Without pulling away, the other woman smiled. “Figured you deserved a better view after being stuck in a windowless box with me all day.” Rio’s eyes opened slowly, brown meeting blue. “The view’s not so bad.” In that moment neither of them had anything else to say, not when everything they could ever want was seemingly already there.
