    Chapter 12: Unwanted Ghosts

    Chapter Notes chapter twelve: in which agatha once again pretends that love is illegal for her and confronts the fact that her competition is a ginger See the end of the chapter for more notes The precinct doors swung open with a soft creak, their hinges giving just enough protest to mark the return. Late afternoon light spilled across the tile floor, warm and amber through the glass, casting long shadows that stretched between desks and faded into the sterile wash of overhead fluorescents. The kind of light that made everything feel softer for a second before the reality of the building reasserted itself in the buzz of monotony and low murmur of tired voices.

    They’d spent the morning chasing down one of Lisa Moreno’s college friends in East Chicago—a girl who talked fast and smoked faster, whose version of helpful was three half-truths and a vague alibi. The afternoon interview had taken them across the city, to a librarian who claimed Lisa volunteered once a week but couldn’t remember her last shift. Nothing concrete. But Agatha could feel it in her gut that they were closer. Still, it had been a long frustrating day. Yet Agatha didn’t mind, especially not with Rio walking beside her, close enough that their coats brushed every now and then. For the first time in a while the older woman hadn’t let a bad work day ruin her mood, there was something about walking back together with her partner that made the quiet feel more like a shared breath than a lull. The kind of quiet where every glance lingered a second too long and meant a little too much.

    Agatha held the door for her without a word, and Rio gave her that soft smile that reached her gentle brown eyes and made something pull in Agatha’s chest.

    Rio glanced up at her. “Thanks,” she murmured, voice low and still a little wind-worn.

    Agatha nodded once. A shrug, casual, like it was nothing. Her hands stayed shoved deep in her jean jacket pockets, fingers curled tight around the inside seam like an anchor.

    They didn’t head straight to their desks. Instead, they lingered just inside the precinct doors, the hum of conversation filtering in from the bullpen. Agatha fidgeted with the buttons on her jacket out of habit, thinking back on earlier. “That girl from the dorm—Madison?—she was so full of it.” Rio gave a soft huff. “Three lies in five minutes, that’s impressive even for college students.”

    “I knew the second she started twirling her hair. Dead giveaway.” Rio glanced at her, amused. “You really think twirling means lying?” Agatha shrugged. “In this case? Yeah. That and the way she kept glancing at your badge like it was gonna bite her.”

    “She was just nervous.”

    “No way, she was bullshiting us.”

    Rio didn’t argue, her lips quirked like she was trying not to smile. “Well, the librarian wasn’t much better. Claiming Lisa showed up every Thursday but couldn’t even describe what she looked like?”

    “Dark hair and a kind smile,’” Agatha recited, making air quotes with her fingers. “Groundbreaking.”

    Rio laughed under her breath, nodding, “I’d say even I fit into that description.”

    Agatha’s voice softened. “Yeah. Your smile isn’t too bad I guess.” The quiet stretched between them again, but this time it was more thoughtful. The kind that came with shared frustration and focus. The case had worn at both of them, left them tense and running in circles. But even in this uncertainty, they had this; standing with each other, coats dusted with cold wind, something pulling tight between them.

    Agatha shifted her weight, boots scraping lightly against the tile. She was buzzing. Not the bad kind that made her want to pick a fight or snap at someone just to bleed the energy out. This was something quieter and restless in a different way.

    She glanced at Rio, watching the way the afternoon light caught in her eyes. Her coat was still buttoned, one strand of brown escaping the clip at the back of her head. There was something about seeing her tired but still sharp, soft at the edges, that made Agatha’s throat go a little dry. Rio noticed her nerves. “You okay?”

    The detective nodded, pretending it was about the long day. Before Agatha could psych herself out and overthink it like she always did, she cleared her throat softly and spoke. “So.”

    Rio curiously looked up. “So?”

    Agatha glanced at her. Then away, then back again.

    “I, uh… I’ve been working on this truck.” Agatha scratched the back of her neck, suddenly self-conscious. “Kind of a shitty old thing. I picked it up about six months ago. Been fixing it up in pieces when I get the time.” Rio blinked in surprise. “You’re fixing up a truck?”

    Agatha shrugged, her lips twitched like she couldn’t quite hide her smile. “Yeah, sort of. It’s nothing fancy. Stalls if you look at her wrong but she’s finally running.”

    Rio smiled fondly. “That’s very impressive.”

    Nodding her head in agreement, Agatha stepped just a little closer with her hands still tucked deep in her jacket. Her voice dropped, roughened with sincerity and softness.

    “Yeah she’s drivable now,” Agatha continued, nudging the toe of her boot gently against the tile, “and I was thinking maybe…if you’re not knee-deep in red string and conspiracy theories tonight—”

    A pause.

    “—I could take you for a drive. Maybe make you dinner after, at my place.” Rio blinked.

    Then color bloomed slowly up her neck, soft and rosy, settling high on her cheeks. She glanced down, then to the side, biting her bottom lip to contain the shy smile threatening to break free. Her fingers tightened slightly around her thumb for both comfort and grounding.

    When she finally looked back up, it was only for a second. Just a flicker of brown meeting blue before her gaze dropped again, she couldn’t quite handle the weight of Agatha’s attention without combusting. Rio’s voice came quiet, almost like she didn’t want to ruin the moment. “You’re serious?”

    Agatha’s smile twitched. “What? Don’t act so surprised, I’m a woman of many talents.”

    Rio laughed softly, the kind of laugh that curled around Agatha’s spine and stayed there. “I’d like that,” she said. Then, quieter, more certain, “I’d really like that.”

    Agatha smiled softly, genuine. “Yeah?”

    “Yeah.” Rio tucked a piece of hair behind her ear, her fingers lingering near her cheek like she didn’t quite know what to do with them. They stood there for a moment, suspended in something warm and fragile. Agatha tilted her head, eyes tracing the soft curve of Rio’s jaw. “Good,” she murmured. “It’s a date then.”

    Rio’s blush deepened. “It is.”

    Agatha gave a quiet, almost relieved breath. “Cool.”

    Rio bit her lip to hide the way her smile threatened to spread too wide. “Yeah, cool.”

    She had been holding onto this idea for days, and not because she thought it would win Rio over with the idea that she was a strong woman who was more than experienced with her hands. But she wanted to give her something quiet, simple, and good.

    The kind of night that didn’t ask for anything but presence. No reports, no precinct noise, no interrogations, or missing persons board hanging heavy in the background. They’d indulge in a calm drive with the windows down, the air sharp and clean, and the city slipping by in slow motion. Maybe some music humming low on the radio, with Rio laughing at how bad Agatha’s cooking turned out; accompanied by a silence between them that didn’t feel loaded for once, just easy.

    Perhaps after their drive and warmth of a shared drink or two, after Rio eased into the space the way she always did—Agatha could finally let her hands do what they’d been aching to.

    She’d thought about it. Quietly, in the dark, in the still moments between exhaustion and sleep. Wondered what Rio would be like when the uniform came off and the tight line of her mouth softened.

    Agatha didn’t want to rush it nor needed it to happen tonight, but she couldn’t lie to herself—not when the idea had been circling for days. The idea of Rio in her space and in her bed.

    She’d be gentle at first while letting her fingers skim the curve of Rio’s hip and waist. Learn the shape of her slowly, reverently, like a secret she’d waited too long to touch. Let her mouth trace the places Rio kept hidden beneath all that polish, along with soft skin under her jaw and the delicate line of her collarbone.

    She wanted to pull the tension from her, one kiss at a time. To feel Rio arch against her, legs open for her, hands clutching at the back of her neck like she didn’t want space between them. She wanted to hear the breathless way she’d say her name with that tremble in her voice when she stopped trying to be composed.

    To press Rio into the mattress with a rhythm that made her arch and be keen to forget her own name. Feeling the woman’s nails rake down her back would force her to break open a moan she couldn’t bite back. But even more than that the slick heat of it all and more than the bruises Agatha would gladly wear the next morning—she wanted Rio’s company. God, she wanted it; wanted to earn it.

    Tonight, if Rio let her, maybe she’d finally get to show her just how much. They now walked side by side through the building and Agatha kept her hands in her pockets, hoping it might keep her from doing something impulsive, like reaching for Rio’s. Her fingers drummed restlessly against the inside seams of her jacket, and a regular expression stayed carefully neutral except for a small, stubborn curve tugging at the corner of her mouth.

    Occasionally stealing glances at Rio, she watched the way her profile tilted downward; Rio was clearly flustered with cheeks still dusted pink from both the cold and the way Agatha had just asked her out like it was nothing. Pretending it didn’t mean the world to her.

    Rio tried to play it cool, of course. She was walking purposefully now, chin up and brows drawn in that soft pinch that usually meant she was cataloguing information. The woman was trying very hard to stay composed, and it made something warm bloom deep in Agatha’s chest. When they reached the nearest desk, Rio immediately sat down like her knees needed the support. She pulled a random file toward her and opened it, the motion just a little too sharp to be casual. Flipping through pages quickly, she clearly wasn’t reading.

    “So,” she noted, a little too fast, “we still need to verify the Moreno contact —the friend from her building. The timeline’s still murky between the last text she sent and—”

    Agatha leaned one hip against the edge of the desk, arms folded, watching her with blatant amusement. “Wow. Didn’t even let the moment breathe, huh?”

    Rio didn’t look up. “We have a lot of work to do.”

    “Mmhm.” Agatha leaned in just slightly. “You’re flustered.” “I’m not—”

    “Your ears are red, Vidal.”

    Rio’s hand froze on the edge of the file. She sighed quietly and sat back in her chair, still avoiding Agatha’s eyes.

    “I am not swooning,” she said stiffly.

    Agatha smirked. “Didn’t say swooning but thanks for the confession.” Rio finally looked at her—briefly. The eye contact only lasted a second, but it was long enough for Agatha to see the blush in full bloom, the tension around Rio’s mouth that softened the longer they held the silence. Agatha let herself grin, pushing with a subtle laugh. “You’re kind of a loser, but cute, you know that?”

    Rio groaned, pulling the file up to half-hide her face. “Please don’t.” “Too late.”

    Rio shook her head, but the smile she tried to smother didn’t quite go away. She peeked at Agatha from behind the file, and her voice came quieter now, steadier—but still soft around the edges.

    “You’re enjoying this way too much.”

    Agatha tilted her head. “I just asked out the most composed woman I’ve ever met, and she got so flustered she forgot how to hold a folder.” Rio let out a breath that might’ve been a laugh, then looked down again, her fingers tapping lightly against the manila paper in front of her. Even after a few minutes, Agatha didn’t move to her own desk. Instead, she remained half-sitting on the edge like she’d done it a hundred times before. One boot stayed planted on the floor, the other hooked on the crossbar of Rio’s chair. Her shoulder was brushing the corner of a stack of reports, but she didn’t care. She was too focused on Rio—who was doing an embarrassingly poor job of pretending to read.

    “So,” Agatha drawled. “Just to confirm, that was a yes for tonight right?” Rio’s fingers twitched on the edge of the folder. She didn’t look up. “Yes,” she said softly, then cleared her throat. “I mean—yeah. It was.” Agatha bit back a grin. “Didn’t sound super confident.”

    Rio finally glanced up at her, brief but flustered, then immediately back down to her folder like it might save her. “I just don’t usually get asked out in the middle of the precinct.

    Rio let out a small breath of a laugh. She didn’t answer, just tucked a loose strand of hair behind her ear and stared down at the file with such intent, Agatha was pretty sure she wasn’t reading a word.

    It was right then that Lilia’s bracelets jingled into earshot. “Fuck me,” Agatha murmured. “Brace for impact.”

    Lilia rounded the corner with a small stack of folders, already eyeing them like she knew exactly what they were up to. She stopped, one hand on her hip, and looked between Agatha on the desk and Rio looking like she wanted to disappear into her chair.

    “Well,” Lilia said, “look who’s finally not brooding at each other from across the room.”

    Rio’s spine stiffened. “We weren’t brooding.”

    “Mmm.” Lilia arched a brow. “Sure you weren’t. Funny that now Agatha just happens to be lounging on your desk like it’s a fainting couch.” Agatha grinned, completely unbothered. “It’s ergonomic.”

    Lilia dropped the files on a nearby desk. “I don’t know what happened, but I’m glad it did. You two were giving everyone tension migraines.” Agatha opened her mouth, probably to say something outrageous, but Rio reached for a pen like it was the only lifeline she had. “We’re just working,” she said quickly. “Like always.”

    “Mmhm.” Lilia raised a brow, her voice dipped in that maternal mischief she always managed to wield like a scalpel. She turned toward her own desk a few feet away, setting down her paperwork with a thump and tossing a wink over her shoulder as she moved. “Don’t work too hard, lovebirds.” The detective grinned, watching her with no small amount of amusement. Rio, meanwhile, let out a long, slow breath and visibly slouched in her chair.

    Agatha leaned in just slightly, elbows resting on her knees as she tilted her head toward her. “Lovebirds, huh?”

    Rio groaned, one hand coming up to cover her face. “I’m going to die.” Agatha chuckled, voice low and teasing but undeniably fond. “No, you’re not,” she whispered. “You’ve got a date tonight.”

    That made Rio shake her head in embarrassment before she peeked at Agatha through her fingers, trying (and failing) to smother the blush climbing back into her cheeks. She didn’t say anything, but her smile said plenty.

    Suddenly, heavy footsteps echoed unevenly across the bullpen floor accompanied by the faint creak of old leather and the rhythmic jingle of keys on a belt loop. Agatha didn’t need to turn around to know who it was; only one man in the building had a gait like that. Like the world owed him something, and his knees were still collecting interest.

    Captain Delaney stopped a few feet from the group, heavy footfalls and an air of quiet disdain settling over the space before he even opened his mouth. He looked like he hadn’t slept much with collar slightly askew and grey brows drawn tight; still, the sharpness in his gaze hadn’t dulled one bit. His eyes flicked between the three of them, finally landing on Agatha who was still perched casually on the edge of Rio’s desk. His lip curled faintly. “I see we’re all hard at work,” he said, voice low and cutting. “What is this now—storytime?”

    Agatha slid off the desk with a practiced calm, boots hitting the tile with a muted thud. Her spine straightened out of instinct.

    Lilia glanced over her glasses, her hands folding calmly over the paperwork she’d just set down. “Captain.”

    Delaney nodded in acknowledgment, but his focus stayed locked on Agatha.

    “I’ve been reviewing the last batch of reports from this unit,” he said, voice hardening. “And frankly, I’m not impressed.”

    Rio sat straighter, a breath catching in her throat. Agatha didn’t flinch, but now her hands were flexed at her sides.

    “This case is already bigger than we expected,” Delaney went on. “The Moreno girl’s disappearance has the public nervous. And what I’m seeing from this department”—he made a vague, dismissive gesture toward them —“is inconsistency, poor chain-of-command communication, and a hell of a lot of wasted hours chasing the wrong leads.”

    Agatha’s jaw tightened.

    “We’re following what we’ve got,” she said, carefully. “The trail isn’t clean, but we’re not sitting on our asses either.”

    Delaney’s eyes snapped to hers. “That supposed to make me feel better, Harkness? That you’re running around in circles instead of producing results?”

    The air in the bullpen shifted.

    “With respect, sir,” Lilia said stepping in, “we’ve been working the Moreno case around the clock. My team’s been thorough. The leads have been messy, not mishandled.”

    Delaney gave her a long, unreadable look. “Your team,” he repeated, dry as salt.

    Lilia didn’t back down. “That’s right.”

    Agatha could feel her temper scratching at her throat, rising beneath her ribs, but she bit it back. She’d heard this tone before and knew that people like Delaney operated blame first, understand later.

    The captain turned to Rio next.

    “And you,” he said again, tone cold. “You’ve got the résumé. The Bureau background. But I expected more discipline. More structure. I brought you onto this squad because I thought you could bring some order to the chaos, not become part of it.”

    Rio’s spine went rigid, her chin lifting by sheer force of will. “I’ve been following every directive, Captain.”

    “Then you’re not following them well enough,” he snapped. “Because right now, you’re not operating like a lead. You’re acting like a subordinate. Letting this case spiral and your partner run off-script. You’re supposed to set the tone, Vidal—not play catch-up.”

    Rio’s fingers tightened around the folder in front of her, knuckles pale. She didn’t blink.

    “I’ve maintained procedure on every front,” she asserted. “Our documentation is clean. Our timeline is organized. We’ve corroborated witness statements and cross-checked all incoming intel—” “That’s not what I’m talking about,” Delaney interrupted, his voice rising enough to carry around the room. “I’m talking about optics. You want to take initiative? Then get the house in order. You’re on the books as primary liaison with this department. That makes you accountable. You want that badge to mean something?” He leaned in a fraction, brow furrowed. “Then start acting like you know what the hell you’re doing.”

    Agatha’s hands curled into fists at her sides.

    He wasn’t done.

    “I’ve had to make choices on this case that weren’t easy,” Delaney continued, turning to pace a short step in front of them. “Resources are tight, and higher-ups are watching. My command staff downtown wants updates. Which means no more dead ends and no more wasted man-hours chasing ghost theories. I expect movement as well as chain-of-command compliance. Fieldwork logged within twenty-four hours of contact. And above all—containment. We don’t need this thing leaking into the press.” Delaney finally turned back to Lilia.

    “You’ve got seniority, Calderu,” he said. “I suggest you start acting like it.” Lilia’s chin tilted up, defiant and unshaken. “I’m acting exactly like it,” she replied, her tone level. “Which is why I trust my team.”

    Delaney let the silence stretch. His gaze swept over them once more, like he could burn his expectations into their skin by sheer will. He shook his head, “Your team is a mess, Calderu.” The words hit like a punch.

    “You’ve got one detective who doesn’t know when to keep her mouth shut, another who can’t seem to separate protocol from personal attachment, and a supervisor too sentimental to rein them in.” His eyes slid to Agatha. “Harkness has never met a rule she couldn’t break. She doesn’t follow orders; she dances around them. All while giving a smirk like she’s doing us all a favor.”

    Agatha didn’t move, yet her fury was simmering just beneath the surface— but she didn’t speak, not now.

    Delaney turned to Rio again, slower. “I expected discipline,” Delaney went on. “I expected precision. What I’ve seen lately? Is a field agent who’s seemed what the job demands.”

    Lilia started to speak—he cut her off with a raised hand. “I know what you’re going to say. That it’s all under control. That they’re making progress. But Calderu, progress doesn’t matter if the entire operation collapses from the inside out. You want to keep defending them? Fine. But you better hope your trust isn’t misplaced.”

    His voice dropped into something more pointed—almost mocking. “I hope, for your sake, you find it in yourself to be grateful for the help we’re about to receive on this case.”

    A beat of silence. He let that line settle like dust after a detonation, his meaning vague but heavy.

    “Because frankly,” he added, straightening his cuffs with a look that made Agatha’s skin crawl, “you’re going to need it.”

    “If this case slips any further off the rails,” he said, voice low, final, “it’s on you three. I won’t clean up the mess. I’ll replace the team.” The three of them stood in silence for a moment, the air left behind thick with pressure.

    Then a sound that followed didn’t belong to any of them. A sharp clicking of heels on linoleum that confidently sliced through the uneasy silence like a scalpel.

    A thin figure stepped into the bullpen from the main corridor, poised with the embodiment of control in a slate-gray tailored suit that looked like it cost more than Agatha’s monthly rent. Her coat was folded neatly over one arm, and a leather portfolio hung at her side like it was an extension of her will.

    And her voice was smooth and practiced—slipping in like a blade dressed in silk. “I don’t think that’ll be necessary from now on, Captain.” Agatha turned first, slow and guarded, and Lilia’s eyes narrowed; but it was Rio who froze.

    Like she’d been turned to glass.

    Her breath caught as her expression turned into the ripple of disbelief. The sliver of something complicated.

    “Wanda.” Rio’s voice was soft. Stunned.

    Agatha looked from Wanda to Rio and back again, something in her gut turning over.

    Wanda smiled, and it was all teeth and poise. “Agent Vidal,” she said smoothly, like she was greeting an old colleague over champagne. “It’s been a long time.”

    Her tone was warm and full of charm as if she had perfectly measured it. Seemingly already calculating the outcome of every word before she spoke it.

    Agatha had seen a lot of women in suits. Hell, she’d had some of them pressed against her mattress. But this one? This woman walked like the ground owed her something. And the room gave it.

    Captain Delaney stepped forward, clapping his hands once like her entrance was just another item on the agenda.

    “Well,” he said, almost pleased now. “Now that everyone’s here.” “This is Wanda Maximoff. She’s FBI and here on behalf of Interagency Liaison Operations. Effective immediately, the Bureau is treating the Moreno disappearance as a potential multi-jurisdictional trafficking case. Similar patterns are popping up across state lines. Their analysts flagged the overlap.”

    Wanda nodded once, her posture immaculate, every motion precise. “This case falls under federal concern now,” she said, tone crisp but calm. “I’ll be your point of contact moving forward for any coordination with federal task forces.”

    Agatha’s brow furrowed, but she didn’t speak. Instead, she was focused on Rio’s behavior.

    She noticed how her jaw clenched and how she avoided looking directly at Wanda.

    The softness had been building between them just minutes ago drained quietly from her posture. Whatever this was, it wasn’t just bureaucratic interference. This was personal.

    Wanda’s eyes flicked briefly to Agatha, showing no emotion there. The detective couldn’t tell whether it was interest or evaluation. Then they settled back on Rio with something that might have once passed for familiarity.

    Agatha’s fingers curled against the edge of the desk, slow and steady. Wanda’s gaze swept the room like she already owned it. Her attitude demonstrated that everything she needed to know had already been confirmed the moment she stepped through the door.

    She looked at Rio then, properly this time, head tilting the faintest degree. Her eyes softened just enough to suggest warmth, but her smile didn’t reach them.

    “My Agent Vidal.” she said smoothly, her voice warm in that performative kind of way—like it had been practiced in a mirror and perfected for charm. Rio froze.

    “Wanda,” she said, barely above a whisper. And even that seemed to cost her something.

    The woman’s smile bloomed slowly, too pleasant to be genuine. “Still so polite.”

    She took a step closer. The scent of her perfume was a clean, expensive blend of florals that cut through the stale precinct air like punctuation. She didn't stop at a respectable distance, simply stood close enough to blur the line between professional and personal.

    “We’ve missed you at the Bureau,” she said lightly, her tone like velvet. “Though I’d be lying if I said I didn’t miss you most.”

    Agatha watched the whole exchange from her spot at Rio’s desk, the air around her cooling by the second, feeling something shift. This woman was what Rio came from. Polished, controlled and untouchable. And now she was here, smiling with perfect lipstick and dropping words that folded neatly between implication and authority. Captain Delaney, oblivious to the weight in the air, stepped in with a finality that cut through the moment like a closing door.

    “For those of you who don’t know,” he said, addressing the room with his usual gruffness, “Agent Maximoff here was Vidal’s partner during her time at the Bureau as they worked together on multiple interagency operations. Top-tier results.”

    He looked between them, then gave a tight nod, as if the professional history alone spoke volumes.

    “Consider yourselves lucky,” he added. “You’ve got someone with Bureau oversight and field familiarity. Couldn’t ask for a better fit.” His gaze landed on Agatha for a beat longer than necessary. “I’ll leave you all to get acquainted properly. Calderu, a minute in my office please,” he muttered, already walking away with footsteps echoing off tile until they disappeared around the corner.

    Lilia diligently followed, but not before giving Agatha a knowing stern look.

    A silence painfully engulfed the bullpen again.

    Agatha shifted in her seat. “Didn’t know the FBI did house calls,” she muttered, leaning back, arms crossing over her chest.

    Rio didn’t miss the edge in her tone, or the way Agatha’s eyes had narrowed slightly, taking Wanda in like a threat rather than a visitor. Wanda turned her head ever so slightly, just enough to flash Agatha a smile that felt like a challenge wrapped in satin, delighted by the reaction. She gave the detective an appraising once-over, the kind of slow, calculated glance that wasn’t just assessing—it was baiting. “And you must be Agatha Harkness,” Wanda mused, voice carrying that same effortless charm. “I’ve heard quite a bit about you. You’ve got the reputation, huh? I’m surprised you stayed in one place long enough to get a badge.”

    Agatha didn’t blink.

    She flashed the kind of smile that didn’t touch her eyes. The one she used when every nerve in her body was humming, but she refused to give anyone the satisfaction.

    “Well,” she said coolly, “some of us make it work without needing a federal paycheck and a six-syllable job title.”

    The red-haired woman’s expression didn’t slip as she walked closer, pacing in front of both. Laughing, she muttered softly under her breath. “Oh, this is going to be fun.”

    Then, Wanda fixed her eyes on Rio, and with a smile that barely concealed something sharper underneath, she spat; “I didn’t expect to find you partnered with someone quite so... unorthodox.” There was something in her voice, almost pitying.

    Rio’s stomach twisted, but she didn’t bite. “You don’t know anything.” she said instead, keeping her tone neutral. Wanda knew exactly what she was doing.

    “Mm,” she hummed. “Still. Feels like a bit of a waste, doesn’t it?” Agatha scoffed before Rio could answer. “Right, because smaller scale detective work is so beneath the great and powerful FBI.” The sarcasm in her voice was thick enough to choke on.

    Wanda didn’t even look at her this time. Just briefly reached down to run a slow fingertip along Rio’s desk, stopping right before where Agatha was sitting.

    She tapped it once before straightening. “Thankfully, I’m here on official business,” she said, her tone shifting ever so slightly into something more serious. “Lisa Moreno’s case has piqued the Bureau’s interest.” Rio frowned slightly, forcing herself to focus on that—the case. “You think it’s federal?”

    Wanda lifted a shoulder in a noncommittal shrug. “Not yet. But we’ve been tracking some disappearances that bear a resemblance. Your girl fits a pattern we’ve seen before.”

    That got Rio’s full attention.

    Agatha, however, didn’t seem impressed. “So, the FBI’s stepping in now?” She clicked her tongue. “We’re already on it.”

    Wanda’s gaze flicked to her, sharp and unreadable. “You’re on it,” she echoed mockingly. “That’s cute. But see, here’s the thing. We have reason to believe Lisa Moreno’s case isn’t isolated. There have been similar incidents across three states in the past year—women vanishing with eerily similar circumstances. No forced entry, no struggle, no immediate ransom demands.”

    Agatha’s arms tightened across her chest as she stood from the desk, eyes narrowing. “And I suppose the Bureau thinks they’re all connected?” Wanda nodded, calm and certain. “It’s not just a supposition, Detective. It’s data. There’s a pattern here, and Moreno is just the latest entry. I figured I’d do you all the favor of giving you a heads-up before this gets bigger than you can handle.”

    Agatha let out a breathy chuckle, shaking her head. “Wow. Generous of you.”

    Wanda’s smirk remained, but there was something knowing in her eyes. “Always.”

    She turned back to Rio then, that teasing edge softening just a fraction. “I thought I’d drop by, check in on things. And, of course,” she said smoothly, “pay you a visit.” Before Rio could pull away, Wanda reached out and placed a light touch on the back of her hand—just a faint brush of fingers, barely there, but deliberate.

    Rio froze.

    Agatha’s entire expression darkened. It was subtle, but Rio felt the shift before she even turned to look. Agatha’s her fingers curled around a pen in her hand like she was resisting the urge to snap it in two. She bit the inside of her cheek, and her shoulders pulled just a fraction tenser. Wanda knew, and she was enjoying it.

    “You’re too good for this precinct, Rio,” Wanda said lightly, then glanced sideways at Agatha, a sharp, deliberate glint in her eyes. “Don’t you agree, Harkness?”

    Agatha let out a low exhale, sizing Wanda up properly now, the corners of her mouth twitching in something that wasn’t quite a smile. “You know, Vidal here sometimes has enough of a complex already. Not sure she needs you stroking her ego on top of it.”

    Meanwhile, the agent just laughed. “Oh, I love this,” she said, lips curving just slightly. “I can see why they put you two together.” Rio, silent between them, still hadn’t spoken. Her hands were in her lap holding the other tightly with knuckles pale. She wasn’t looking at Wanda anymore and wasn’t looking at Agatha either.

    That was the part Agatha noticed most. Rio wasn’t stepping in—not to defend her, not to deflect, not even to laugh it off. Something in her pulled back slightly. A quiet recoil she didn’t let show anywhere but in the stillness of her expression.

    The silence in the room had thickened like smoke while coating everything in a quiet, brittle tension. Walkies rang distantly across the precinct and a printer whirred somewhere near the back. The low hum of heater unit buzzed above them like static, but none of it touched the three women standing at the heart of it all.

    Wanda stood with that same unsettling poise, her heels planted like they belonged to the room. Rio hadn’t said a word in minutes. Her hands were still in her lap, and though her expression remained carefully neutral, Agatha saw the small giveaway: her thumb pressing rhythmically into the crook of her opposite arm, like she was trying to ground herself. Agatha’s gaze dropped for half a second to the spot on Rio’s desk where Wanda’s fingers had just lingered. The little brush of control. Possession masked as familiarity.

    She stepped forward enough to reclaim some of the air Wanda had been so casually filling.

    “So tell me,” Agatha said, her voice low and calm, though her jaw had tightened ever so slightly. “What’s the Bureau really want with this case?” Wanda scoffed. “Excuse me?”

    Agatha tilted her head, mock-thoughtful. Her eyes flicked deliberately between Wanda and Rio before settling on the agent again, gaze sharp. “I mean, it’s a local missing persons case. One we’ve had leads on, one we’ve been chasing for weeks. But now you—” she lifted her hands, curling her fingers into exaggerated air quotes, “—Agent Maximoff—just so happen to drop in? No heads-up, no request for files. Just... sweeping in like you own the place.”

    Wanda’s smile didn’t falter, but her fingers curled slightly at her sides. “You’re welcome to review my credentials if you’re confused, Detective,” she said, her voice honey smooth.

    “Oh, I’m not confused,” Agatha replied, taking another slow step forward. “I just don’t buy the sudden interest. Feels a little too polished. A little too... convenient.”

    She let her gaze drop to the shiny badge tucked at Wanda’s belt, then back up to meet her eyes again.

    “You feds ignore half the women who go missing in this city unless they’re blonde, rich, or wearing pearls in their last known photo. So don’t stand here and pretend you care now just because some red tape finally caught up to you.”

    The words landed loud in the quiet hum of the room. Rio’s eyes lifted at that, wide and uncertain, flicking from Agatha to Wanda like she was bracing for fallout.

    Wanda’s smile thinned. Her eyes darkened with the kind of amusement that curled slow, like smoke trailing from the tip of something burning. “You’re awfully passionate for someone with so little jurisdiction,” she said, tone cool. “You sure this isn’t personal, Detective?” Agatha didn’t blink. “Every case like this is personal.”

    The two women stood barely a foot apart now, the weight of their opposing selves sharp in the air between them.

    Agatha didn’t look away, especially not when Wanda tried to intimidate with poise and the space around them shrank under pressure of things unspoken. She didn’t even look at Rio.

    Because this wasn’t about jealousy. It was about control. About keeping the case, and the team, and Rio—her Rio—away from whatever Wanda Maximoff thought she could manipulate.

    But the seed had already been planted, and Wanda knew it. “Well,” the agent began smoothly, voice soft but slicing through the tension like a scalpel, “I suppose that’s why the captain brought me in, isn’t it?” Her smile returned, all teeth and calculation.

    “Because for all your... passion, Harkness, the department clearly doesn’t think you’re capable of closing this one alone.” Her eyes flicked down, a faint note of disdain coloring the next words: “Not competently, at least.” Agatha didn’t move until she felt her nails biting into the meat of her own palm.

    Wanda turned, leisurely now, addressing Rio like the conversation had shifted entirely, pretending Agatha was no longer in the room. Her voice took on that clinical, professional cadence used in briefings and pressers, though it carried an undercurrent of something far more pointed. “I’ll be conducting a series of Interagency Coordination Briefings,” she announced lightly. “Just to get up to speed with the case. And since we’re dealing with potential interstate victims and federal-level trafficking markers—”

    She looked at Rio now, smile cool and proprietary.

    “—I’ll need someone familiar with both the evidence and the department protocols. Preferably someone who can think like an agent.” Agatha already knew where this was going.

    Wanda made a show of glancing at the file on Rio’s desk. Then, with deliberate poise: “I’m assigning Vidal to assist me.”

    Rio blinked, her jaw tightening ever so slightly.

    “Effective immediately.”

    The words landed like a gavel and there was no room to argue—not aloud. Wanda let the silence settle by not bothering looking at Agatha when she said it, but the blow landed squarely in her chest.

    A wedge driven with surgical precision.

    “You’ll still have access to the shared files, of course,” Wanda added. “If there’s anything useful.”

    Agatha said nothing for a moment. Her eyes were locked on Rio—who hadn’t moved, hadn’t spoken, her face unreadable with her shoulders held stiff.

    Wanda stepped back slightly, arms folding like a queen surveying her kingdom.

    “I’m sure we can all agree,” she said coolly, “that what matters is getting results.”

    Agatha’s throat felt dry. Her voice, when it came, was low and dangerous. “I get results.”

    “Then prove it,” the other woman retorted softly, like it was a challenge. “But for now… let the professionals take point.”

    Now this wasn’t just about control anymore, it was ownership. Agatha felt the ground shift beneath her, because Wanda wasn’t just claiming jurisdiction; she was claiming Rio.

    Agatha’s arms uncrossed slowly as she stepped forward again, boots echoing softly against the floor. The distance between them shrank by a breath, maybe two, but it was enough to tilt the tension sideways. She tilted her head, blue eyes flaring with something goading. “You always this territorial, Maximoff,” Agatha said, bristling, “or am I just lucky enough to bring it out of you?”

    Wanda’s brow arched and Agatha continued. “Because if this is about proving who’s got the bigger clearance badge, congratulations! You’ve made it real clear who the government's favorite is. You want Vidal all to yourself? You just have to say it and it wouldn’t be the first time a fed threw their weight around because they didn’t know how to keep their shit personal.”

    Wanda adjusted the cuff of her blazer with infuriating calmness and said, “Let me remind you, Detective Harkness, that while you're still punching clocks at a precinct built in the ‘70s and getting scolded by your captain like a schoolgirl,”—her voice inked with venom—“I am a Special Supervisory Agent with federal jurisdiction and direct liaison to Interagency Coordination.”

    She stepped closer now, heels clicking once against tile. Her smile didn’t reach her eyes.

    “That means I don’t have to want Rio. I already have her. On official assignment.”

    Agatha’s eyes burned. “Right. ‘Assignment.’ Is that what you called it back then too?”

    Wanda scoffed and turned her attention back to Rio, her expression smoothing into something cool and composed, as if the verbal sparring with Agatha hadn’t just scorched the air between them. It was the kind of shift that came from long-practiced control and ease of someone used to command. When she spoke, it was with a calm edge, every syllable lined with precision.

    “Agent Vidal,” she said, deliberately reaching back for a title that hadn’t belonged to Rio in a long time, “I’ll need you to gather everything you’ve compiled on Lisa Moreno so far. All statements, logs, and intel. We’ll begin a proper debrief in the boardroom.”

    The words dropped cleanly into the space between them, but their weight settled hard.

    Rio didn’t answer right away.

    Her fingers hovered hesitantly over the file on her desk. The briefest flicker of discomfort crossed her face which Agatha caught it instantly. The stiffness in her shoulders and the second it took too long for her to react. The flash of regret, uncertainty, and reluctance behind her eyes. Agatha felt her stomach twist.

    Rio straightened slowly, spine taut as a drawn wire, eyes fixed on a neutral spot on her desk like it held the answer.

    “I—” Rio started, her voice low. “I should probably finish logging the witness interview from today with Detective Harkness first—” “It wasn’t a suggestion,” Wanda interrupted, steel under every syllable. “It’s an order.”

    There was no mistaking the emphasis. No room for sidestepping. Agatha barely breathed as Rio’s hesitation visibly shifted into resignation. She watched the way Rio hesitated before she finally nodded. Not because she wanted to, but because this was who she was and who she’d always been.

    The good soldier, even when it cost her something.

    “…Right,” Rio murmured after a beat. She stood slowly, quietly, like she was trying not to make noise, not to disturb the moment already in shards around them.

    The sound of it felt like gravel in Agatha’s chest.

    Rio moved with the precise, careful grace that came from years of being in control. She collected the file folders from her desk, fingers smoothing the corners like she needed the order, the neatness, to stay grounded. She didn’t look at Agatha as she turned and didn’t have to.

    Agatha felt the ache of her absence before Rio had even taken a full step away.

    She didn’t speak—couldn’t. Not with her throat tight and her hands curled into fists inside her jacket pockets. She told herself it was fine and that it was nothing. It was just a debrief.

    But as Rio followed Wanda toward the glass-walled boardroom at the end of it; something in Agatha arose. Not with anger or even jealousy, but resignation.

    It was full with the cold clarity of being reminded who got to give orders in the end—and who got pulled away.

    The last thing Agatha saw before the boardroom door swung closed behind them was the faint flick of hesitation in Rio’s step.

    Agatha walked back to her desk, the sound of her boots echoing dully against tile, each step heavier than the last. The warmth that had wrapped itself around her earlier, the quiet buzz of giddy nerves and soft smiles, was gone and snatched away in the time it took for Wanda Maximoff to cross a room.

    She dropped into her chair with more weight than grace, the old thing creaking beneath her as she leaned back hard and exhaled, trying to force the tension out of her body. It didn’t work. Her neck was stiff, tongue between her back teeth in stress. She shoved a hand through her hair and tugged once at the roots, a grounding motion that did nothing to ease the tight coil forming in her chest.

    From where she sat, she had a perfect view of the boardroom with Rio and Wanda.

    The glass between them did nothing to soften the image: Rio seated at the table, posture upright and rigid, hands folded neatly in front of her. Wanda was standing across from her with arms moving and slow precision as she laid files down like she owned the air between them. Her blazer hugged her shoulders just right. Her hair was too smooth, too intentional. She was the kind of woman who didn’t walked into a room, she stepped into it like it had been built for her.

    Agatha hated how obvious it was.

    It was like a bucket of cold water had been dumped over her head. One minute, she’d been standing with Rio in the warmth of something new, tentative, and full of possibility. Now, she felt like she was back where she always ended up. On the outside watching something she wasn’t allowed to want.

    She blinked hard and looked away from the boardroom. It was too much. Too loud, even in silence.

    What the hell had just happened?

    Wanda wasn’t just some suit from upstairs. She wasn’t just the Bureau stepping in because the case had started making federal ripples. No, Wanda was personal. She was part of Rio’s past and Agatha hadn’t even known there was a past like that. Now, it was sitting right across from her, in heels and red lipstick and a tailored jacket, laying claim to everything like it was already hers.

    Rio hadn’t said anything nor protested. Hadn’t questioned being pulled aside or even looked back.

    Agatha could still feel the ghost of her own offer lingering on her tongue. The date. The truck. The thought of her hands on Rio’s hips in the kitchen, cooking something terrible and pretending it wasn’t. All of that felt impossibly far away now, like it belonged to a different version of her—one foolish enough to believe she could have something good.

    She looked down at her hands, palms open on her thighs, and realized they were trembling.

    Fuck.

    It wasn’t just about the case. Or the captain’s threats. Or Wanda’s perfectly passive-aggressive jabs. It was the audacity of the entire thing. The way Wanda had slipped in like a needle under her skin—sharp, cold, and quiet. She’d been waiting for the perfect moment to cut the air between them. Agatha swallowed and glanced up again.

    Wanda was smiling now, perfectly at ease in the boardroom like she’d been born in it. Rio didn’t look as calm, not by a long shot. But she wasn’t pushing to leave, nor dismissing Wanda from standing too close to her. And that was enough to feel like a knife between Agatha’s ribs. This was her case. Her precinct. And yet the second a woman with letters in front of her name and a shiny Bureau resume came in, everything changed. Agatha felt like she was ten years old again, wearing secondhand jeans and sitting too loud at a table she didn’t belong at and she hadn’t earned her spot; like she wasn’t enough.

    She forced her gaze away again, back to her desk. Her files. Her work. Her chest still felt heavy because whatever that moment had been with Rio and whatever warmth they’d built between snide remarks and flustered smiles, had evaporated the second Wanda said her name.

    Agatha hadn’t stood a chance.

    Agatha had never liked mirrors. They showed too much with all the cracks and callouses. The hollows under her eyes that even sleep couldn’t fix and unevenness of her hair on the best of days with the shadows around her mouth on the worst. She’d never been polished and really never cared to be. But sitting at her desk now, with Wanda’s pristine silhouette still visible, she suddenly felt every inch of that roughness like it was a flaw carved into her skin.

    Wanda Maximoff wasn’t just composed, it was as if she carved from ice and crystal and a hundred-dollar manicure. Her lipstick didn’t smudge, and heels didn’t scuff. Voice was smooth in a way that made people listen without even realizing they were doing it. And when she’d walked in, she didn’t just take up space—she owned it like she’d always known she belonged there.

    Agatha could still hear the way her name had rolled off Wanda’s tongue. Soft. Dismissive. Patronizing in the way only women like her could be. “And you must be Agatha Harkness”

    God, the way she’d said that to make Agatha sound a punchline to a joke Wanda had already heard and didn’t find particularly funny. She stared down at her hands again, scuffed knuckles and all. The pads of her fingers were still raw from scraping rust off the undercarriage of her truck a few nights ago. Her boots were worn in and lived-in. The stitching at the heel was fraying a little bit.

    Wanda, on the other hand, probably had rows of coats that cost more than Agatha’s rent. Could probably pronounce champagne names without having to look at the label. She walked like she’d never tripped in her life and smiled like it never cost her anything. Everything she did had been trained into her. Elegant. Refined. One of those women who knew exactly which wine to order and which fork with a fucking salad.

    In contrast, Agatha had fought her way through public school and half- wrecked community college classes. She spent most of her twenties figuring out how to keep her head above water while dragging old trauma behind her like a busted suitcase. Her hair was never styled—just pulled back, or down, or shoved under a beanie when she didn’t feel like dealing with it. She wore jeans that were three years old because they were sturdy, not because they looked good. Her nails were short, bitten. She wasn’t a woman who looked good under fluorescent lighting. And it made her sick to admit but maybe…Wanda was right. Maybe Rio was too good for this place. For her.

    Because Rio—beautiful, composed Rio—was made of steadier things. She was neat lines and clean button-ups and thoughtful silences. Her handwriting was flawless with a mind that moved like clockwork; calculating and precise. She’d come from the Bureau, from a world of order and consequence and control.

    And Agatha was barely holding her shit together on the best day. So what was she thinking? That Rio would actually want someone with rough hands and a mouth that always ran faster than her brain? Someone who’d barely figured out how to want things without destroying them in the process?

    It was laughable.

    She shifted in her chair and glanced back toward the boardroom again. Wanda stood with arms folded loosely as she spoke. Confident, collected. Rio was still seated, nodding along, her expression unreadable from this distance—but she wasn’t objecting. She wasn’t doing anything to suggest that she didn’t belong there. That she didn’t want to be there. Agatha bit down on the inside of her cheek until she tasted blood. She’d known it from the start that there was something about Rio that wasn’t hers to want.

    “You’re too good for this precinct, Rio”. Wanda had said it with a smile, like it was a compliment.

    But Agatha had heard what she really meant: You’re too good for her. And it wasn’t jealousy that made Agatha sit back in her chair, crossing her arms over her chest. It wasn’t bitterness. It was acceptance. Cold, jagged, brutal. The kind that slid in slowly and settled into her ribs. She wasn’t angry which was the worst part.

    Agatha Harkness had been angry her entire life. It was easy and familiar. A weapon she could wield like muscle memory. Anger was fast and loud and burned hot enough to block everything else out.

    But this was a quiet dull ache at the base of her ribs. A tension behind her eyes that wouldn’t go away and she kept blinking hoping it might help, but it never did. The ache just sat there.

    She’d let herself believe something soft could grow between them. That after everything, there was a version of this where they got to want each other openly, stupidly. Where it wasn’t all sideways glances and near- misses. Where Rio smiled at her in a room full of people and didn’t feel the need to hide it.

    It was clear that the FBI badge and Wanda still meant something, and they held a history for Rio that she didn’t know how to compete with. Rio hadn’t said anything yet and hadn’t turned toward her and driven the nail in, but she didn’t have to because the silence said enough. If it had been Agatha sitting in that boardroom, and Rio outside looking in? Agatha would’ve stood up and opened the door and said something. Anything.

    She wouldn’t have let her sit out here and wonder.

    But Rio had always been careful and rightfully so she had been trained to be, which was stronger than whatever this thing between them had started to become.

    Agatha let her gaze drop to the desk, to the folder she hadn’t touched in twenty minutes. Her fingertips brushed the corner, but she didn’t flip it open.

    It was easier to stare blankly at the manila edge than to keep watching the glass.

    The thing was—Agatha wasn’t stupid. She knew herself and what she looked like next to someone like Wanda. She could already imagine the side-by-side comparison. The way Rio would feel caught in the middle of two extremes and realize, inevitably, which side she actually belonged to. Agatha wasn’t graceful. She wasn’t the type who made polite conversation at galas or filed her nails in clean little arcs. The blue eyed woman was loud when she cared and mean when she got scared.

    That’s what it was, beneath all the bravado.

    Hope had slipped in somewhere along the way between that rooftop moment with the almost-kiss that ghosted her lips, it now felt like a trap she had set for herself.

    It had been nice to let herself want something without expecting to be punished for it.

    But Agatha Harkness didn’t get the girl. She got moments with flashes of warmth and glimpses of what it might be like to be chosen. Not the whole thing; never the whole thing.

    She knew herself well enough to recognize the signs now. The way her mouth stayed shut when it should bite back. The way her hands wouldn’t move toward the work because they were still trying to hold something that was already slipping away.

    The worst part was Rio hadn’t done anything wrong, that’s what made this so fucking impossible. Rio was still kind, still too good. Agatha didn’t know how to be angry at kindness. Only at herself. She’d been foolish enough to hope. To believe just for a second that maybe Rio wanted her back. But women like Rio didn’t end up with women like her.

    Agatha leaned back in her chair, letting her gaze blur slightly, her arms folding loosely over her chest—not with the cocky air she usually wore, but with something much quieter. Like she was trying to hold herself together from the inside out. Her jaw tightened. She didn’t even realize she’d started clenching it.

    She wanted to light a cigarette or to go out into the alley and put her fists through something solid.

    And to wonder how stupid she must’ve looked earlier, talking about her truck and homemade dinner like it meant something and she had something to offer besides cynicism and sarcasm with the kind of affection that came out all wrong.

    Maybe Wanda was the one Rio needed. Maybe that was the kind of love Rio could accept.

    The kind that came with a security clearance and a proper last name. She sat there, in the hum of overhead lights and the soft rattle of the room and let herself ache quietly, and decided that finally, she was done trying to want things that would never be hers.

    Inside the boardroom, the atmosphere was quieter, but no less tense. The overhead lights buzzed faintly, casting a sterile, too-white glow over the long conference table. Folders were spread in tidy stacks, pens aligned like weapons. On the far wall, a projected map remained frozen with faint blue gridlines tracing the sprawl of the city, red dots marking the missing. Each pin glowed like a wound, Lisa Moreno just the latest in a string that was beginning to look like a pattern.

    The air was still and cold, humming with too much restraint. Rio sat straight-backed at the table, her hands folded tightly in her lap, the tension visible in the sharp lines of her posture.

    Next her, Wanda Maximoff moved with the same unnerving calm that she always had. No wasted motion. No strain. Her tailored suit was pristine, deep charcoal and her red hair swept back at the nape of her neck that looked effortless. She opened the top file with two fingers, flipping pages like she was reading for pleasure. Her nails were immaculate, a ring caught the light with every movement.

    The silence was intentional, measured. She turned one page. Then another, slowly. Letting the rustle of paper settle into Rio’s ears. When she finally looked up, it was with the kind of smile that never reached the eyes, a practiced smile.

    “So,” she said, tone light, “this is what you’ve been doing with your talents.”

    Her voice cut through the quiet.

    Rio didn’t look at her. She kept her eyes on the documents in front of her, though she hadn’t registered a single word on the page. Her fingers remained laced tightly together, knuckles whitening just a little, not biting. Wanda sat back slightly, one leg crossing neatly over the other, her heel clicking softly against the tile. “You know,” she continued, tapping her fingers gently against the folder’s edge, “I always wondered what it would take for them to pull you away from the Bureau. From everything we built there.”

    She tilted her head, lips curling faintly. “Didn’t expect it to be a precinct like this.”

    Rio still didn’t answer.

    Wanda chuckled, soft and knowing, as if the silence was an affirmation. Then she leaned forward just slightly, like she was about to tell a secret. “Didn’t know you were into strays now.”

    That made Rio still.

    “But I suppose everyone has a type,” Wanda went on, the edges of her words sharpening under the calm. “Rough around the edges. Dangerous in a sort of… emotionally compromised way. What a rebellious dyke.” She said it like she was complimenting a bottle of wine. Dry and slightly unpredictable.

    Rio’s fingers tightened. “Don’t talk about her like that.” The words were quiet, but they landed hard. Steel beneath the softness.

    Wanda raised her brows, all innocence. “Relax. I’m just surprised, is all. Last I checked, you were the definition of clean-cut. Never missed a field report. Always kept your personal life… separate.”

    “I still do,” Rio replied coolly.

    Wanda smirked. “Do you?”

    She stood then, taking her time as she walked toward the whiteboard at the far end of the room, heels clicking a rhythm that felt designed to grate. Her fingers brushed the edge of the table as she passed until she came to stand before the glowing city map.

    She wasn’t looking at it.

    She was letting the silence wrap around her again, coiling it like a scarf. “I just didn’t realize you’d trade a career for…” Her hand waved vaguely at the precinct outside. “This.”

    Rio looked up at her sharply now, the heat finally cracking through. “I didn’t trade anything,” she snapped. “And the people out there? They’re more of a team than anything I had in Quantico.”

    That flicker in Wanda’s expression was almost imperceptible. “Touché,” she murmured.

    She turned back slowly, her gaze sweeping over Rio again—curious. Trying to understand where the break had started.

    “You used to be sharper,” she said softly. “You didn’t let anyone get close.” Rio held her gaze. “Maybe I used to be more afraid.”

    “Well,” she said, stepping closer now, “let’s see how far bravery gets you.” She walked back to the table, heels muted now on the carpeted edge, and picked up a pen, clicking it once before pointing it toward the case file in front of them.

    “Bring me everything you’ve got on Moreno,” she said, voice shifting into something more formal. “Every interview. Every timestamp. Every inconsistency you haven’t accounted for.”

    Rio didn’t move right away.

    Wanda noticed.

    She smiled again, just faintly. “Unless you’ve forgotten how orders work.” Rio stood. Not quickly, but with purpose, her eyes darkening with something unreadable. She crossed the room in a few strides, gathering the files from the far counter. Her fingers brushed one of the printed interview sheets with Agatha’s handwriting scrawled along the margin, messy and direct, she hesitated just slightly before stacking it.

    Behind her, the glass wall of the boardroom stretched like a mirror, throwing back the outlines of the precinct. Outside, somewhere past the desks and clutter, Agatha sat still. And Rio knew she’d seen everything. Wanda’s voice came again, softer now, as Rio turned back. “You always did like a challenge,” she mused, eyes flicking briefly to the door. “But I didn’t take you for someone who’d settle.”

    Rio’s brow furrowed. “What is that supposed to mean?”

    Wanda shrugged, like it wasn’t important. “Just that you were never one to play house, Vidal. And from what I’ve seen… she plays dirty.” There was a pause. A long one.

    Then Rio said, voice low and even: “We’re not having this conversation.” Wanda stepped forward, just close enough for it to feel like a test. “Then stop acting like she matters.”

    Rio stared at her, and in barely a whisper. “She does.”

    For the first time since she walked in, Wanda had no immediate answer. Her smile faltered momentarily.

    Turning away, she cleared her throat as she flicked through the stack of files Rio handed her.

    “Mm. Well. Let’s keep things professional then, shall we?” she said briskly. “Wouldn’t want anyone thinking you’ve gone soft.”

    The glass door clicked shut behind Rio with a dull finality, the kind that felt louder than it was. The boardroom lights dimmed behind her, Wanda’s sharp silhouette finally swallowed by the walls. Outside, light filtering through the blinds had gone dusky, tinged with indigo, with the last streaks of sunset melting into the steel of the skyline.

    For the past two hours, she’d been sitting across from Wanda, trying not to feel like a rookie again. Trying not to flinch every time the woman said something laced in silk but barbed underneath.

    Rio’s feet slowed against the tile as her eyes found Agatha who was still at her desk.

    She stood stiffly, her back half-turned, slipping her worn denim jacket over her shoulders like armor. The collar flipped awkwardly, and she didn’t bother fixing it. One hand swept over the desk, collecting files with the kind of absent precision that said her mind was a thousand miles away. The folders stacked neatly, the paper edges aligned, but it felt too careful like she was keeping her hands busy on purpose.

    The usual sharpness in her posture was missing along with that subtle tension she always carried in her shoulders, ready for a joke, or a drink. It had been replaced with something softer. Heavier. She looked tired in the way that said I worked all day.

    Rio approached slowly. Not wanting to startle her or wanting to say the wrong thing.

    “Hey,” she said gently, trying for normal. “Are we still okay for tonight?” Agatha didn’t look up at first.

    She paused only a second, then closed the folder with a neat snap and placed it down with care. Her eyes finally lifted to meet Rio’s, “Don’t worry about it,” she said, her tone not cold, just neutral. Rio’s chest ached without warning.

    Rio blinked. “I just meant—” She shifted her weight, suddenly unsure what she’d meant. “Dinner. The ride. If you still wanted—”

    Agatha shook her head as a hand tightened around keys that were on her desk. She offered the faintest smile, one of those polite kinds people gave in hallways. It didn’t reach anywhere near her eyes.

    “Something came up,” she said.

    The words landed like a dull thud in Rio’s chest. She hesitated, “What came up?”

    Agatha let out a quiet laugh that barely made it past her throat. She finally glanced sideways, not enough to meet Rio’s eyes, but enough for the expression to show. Her mouth twisted into something that wanted to be a smile but had too much grief behind it.

    “Remember Delaney said he wanted preliminary drafts of the Moreno briefs compiled before tomorrow,” she said. “Figured I’d get a head start.” Rio nodded slowly, though her hands remained still at her sides, unsure what to reach for. “I could help,” she offered, tentative. Agatha shook her head, already stepping back. “Don’t worry about it.” Rio opened her mouth to say something, but Agatha had already slipped past her, shouldering her accidentally then muttering a quiet “Excuse me,” before she headed toward the door with that slow, familiar saunter except tonight, there was no bite in it.

    The brown eyed woman stood with words caught somewhere in her throat. She didn’t know what she should say or do.

    It hadn’t been like this earlier. They’d left that interview sharing quiet smiles and warm glances, talking about wind-down drives and bad dinners and Agatha’s stupid old truck. There’d been momentum, a lightness that Rio hadn’t realized she needed until it was already there. A step toward something real.

    Now it was like she’d walked back into a room where the rules had changed without her.

    For the first time Rio felt like she’d lost her footing—adrift in silence she didn’t know how to fix, watching the door swing closed with the weight of longing settling over her shoulders.

    Rio hadn’t even made it a full step after her before she felt the unmistakable weight of a hand settle on her shoulder.

    “Hmm,” Wanda murmured behind her, syrupy and saccharine, like she was tasting a joke only she understood. “What’s wrong with her?” The question slithered between them too smug to be harmless. Rio said nothing, she breathed quietly through her nose, steady and quiet. Wanda’s hand lingered a second longer than it should have—just enough to remind her who had always known where the cracks were. Then it slipped off, slow and smooth, her fingers trailing down the length of Rio’s sleeve like water tracing a familiar path.

    “Aww,” Wanda continued, a faux sweetness in her tone that curdled the air between them. “Don’t tell me that has a crush on you?”

    Her voice dipped with mock concern, pitched like she was talking about a high school drama. It was the same voice she used during interrogations when she wanted suspects to flinch. Rio knew it was never kind. Rio’s voice, when it came, was measured. “Her name is Agatha.” Wanda hummed like she hadn’t heard it at all.

    “Right,” she said, drawing the word out. “Harkness.”

    She moved past Rio then, slow and fluid, fingers trailing from shoulder to sleeve as she stepped in front of her and leaned against the edge of a desk. She folded her arms like she had all the time in the world. “She seems… passionate. Is that the word they’re using now?” Rio’s eyes went cold.

    Wanda caught it and smiled like it thrilled her.

    “I’m just saying,” she continued smoothly, “you’ve worked hard to build your reputation. You clawed your way through Quantico, through D.C., through the Bureau’s little girls club. You’re a professional, Rio. Always have been.” Her voice dropped an octave, laced with mock sincerity. “Don’t let some brooding wildcard in leather boots derail that.” Rio didn’t breathe but she stared straight ahead, focusing on the case board through the glass wall on the glowing pin where Lisa Moreno’s name still burned red. Something steady to hold onto.

    “You’re better than that,” Wanda said softly, the cruelty buried beneath. “We both know a good agent doesn’t mix business with pleasure.” “I’m not an agent anymore.”

    Wanda’s smile didn’t waver. “Right,” she said lightly. “Uniform change. I remember.”

    She let the moment stretch, her expression cooling into something more clinical, something darker.

    “But let’s not pretend that changes who you are. You don’t stop being the one who keeps everything in line. Who follows protocol. Who doesn’t lose sight of the bigger picture because someone gave her bedroom eyes across a precinct floor.”

    The heat behind Rio’s sternum bloomed fast and furious, but she didn’t want to give Wanda the satisfaction of a lash out, or reaction. Wanda, meanwhile, gave her a look that was too smug.

    “You’ve always been good at staying focused, Rio. Don’t let whatever that is”—she tipped her head in the direction Agatha had gone—“distract you.” Then she tapped the desk once, her perfectly manicured nail clicking faintly against the surface.

    “See you tomorrow, Mi Amor,” she said over her shoulder, walking away. “We’ve got more coordinating to do.”

    The phrase hit like a slap, soft on the surface, but bruising underneath. Rio’s breath stalled.

    Mi amor.

    It was a nickname Wanda had used back in D.C.—never in public, always in that same faux-affectionate tone that turned intimate moments into power plays. Back then, Rio had never liked it, never asked for it, but Wanda wielded it like a signature. A stamp of possession. It was less about endearment and more about reminding her who called the shots. Now, hearing it again with the fresh loss of Agatha somewhere down the hall made Rio’s skin crawl.

    She stared hard at Wanda’s retreating back, at the way her heels clicked with the same deliberate rhythm they always had. The sound of her leaving echoed like a metronome. A countdown Rio didn’t know how to stop. When the last of it faded and the corner swallowed Wanda whole, Rio remained rooted to the spot. For the first time in a long time, she didn’t feel in control of anything at all.

    Chapter 13: Fault Lines

    Chapter Notes have you ever been threatened by your girlfriend’s ex in a dark parking lot while holding stolen evidence and fighting the urge to cry? agatha has.

    The house was the kind of quiet that made the walls feel closer than they were, like the whole place was bracing for something that wouldn’t come. Agatha hadn’t turned on any lights, there was only the dim underglow of the lamp above the stove, casting long shadows through the kitchen and into the living room where she sat, hunched over the coffee table, surrounded by file folders and cold air.

    A stack of the case files sat spread in front of her, some marked with red tape and some with none at all. The oldest one, the one on the edge, still had Rio’s handwriting in the margin.

    She didn’t touch that one.

    Too many pages were spread out as Agatha sat slouched at the edge of her couch, in an old police academy issued t-shirt; the black cotton faded to a stormy gray with time. The sleeves had long since stretched out, and the collar was misshapen from being tugged too many times. She didn’t care. Her cargo pants were unbuttoned at the top, undone like she’d started to get ready for the day but forgot somewhere along the way.

    Her hair, still pulled into a messy ponytail from earlier that morning, had started to loosen with long strands falling out to frame her face, sticking to the skin along her temples. She didn’t fix it, just pushed them back once and let them fall again.

    Agatha's eyes scanned the same paragraph she’d already read five times. Nothing new jumped out. Nothing had changed. The photo clipped to the top corner of the file stared up at her, Lisa Moreno. Brown eyes, hesitant smile, still frozen in that second just before something went wrong. Agatha exhaled slowly through her nose, the kind of breath that burned on the way out. Her jaw had been tight for hours and it ached now, the tension crawling up into her temples. She leaned back into the couch and stared at the ceiling, dragging her tongue along the back of her teeth like she was chewing on a curse she wasn’t ready to say out loud.

    She didn’t know how long she’d ended up staring at the ceiling, but it was long enough for the light above the stove to hum louder, for the shadows on the wall to shift just slightly. Long enough for her thoughts to slide back where they always went these days.

    Always to Rio.

    Agatha shut her eyes briefly, but that was worse. She could see how Rio had looked that afternoon when she’d said yes to the date, how her voice had dropped shy and her smile had tucked itself just behind her blush. That soft red blooming along her cheeks, blooming just for her. And then later, how quiet she’d gone when Wanda walked in. How fast that silence had settled like cement in her chest.

    She wasn’t angry. Anger would’ve been easier, it would’ve burned it all down and left room for something else. But this was a hollow weight that didn’t have a name, yet it sat heavy right behind her ribs. Rio hadn’t done anything wrong. She hadn’t chosen Wanda, hadn’t walked out hand-in-hand and made it cruel. But she hadn’t stopped her either. She’d followed her into that boardroom like it was muscle memory. Like that version of her from the bureau still lived under the skin and Agatha was a glitch in the system.

    The part that didn’t belong.

    She let her head fall back against the couch. The cushion dipped under the weight, but it didn’t offer comfort. Her blue eyes glanced toward the window, its closed blinds and the city beyond it just a smear of orange streetlamps and static. The world kept moving, and here she was, stuck in this holding pattern of almost.

    Because that’s what she was, wasn’t she?

    Almost the one Rio smiled for. Almost the one who got to bring her home. Almost the reason she stayed.

    Agatha exhaled again, slower this time, and rolled her head to the side. One of the files was slipping from the coffee table, so she reached out to catch it, fingers brushing over the paper. It was cold. Everything in the house had chilled with the quiet, with the way she hadn’t bothered to turn on the heat since that came home a few days ago and hadn’t left.

    So, Agatha had done what she always did when the edges of herself started to splinter—she worked. She rewrote case notes and re-read transcripts until the words blurred into a mess of ink and fury. She memorized the angles of Lisa Moreno’s last known photograph and mapped out her bus route while pouring over call logs like they might give her something to hold onto. Something that made sense.

    Unlike the rest of her life.

    Unlike the way Wanda had looked at Rio like she still had a claim by knowing every button to push and exactly how to make Agatha feel small. She gripped the bridge of her nose with two fingers and felt her skin warm with exhaustion, her temples tight. The beer bottle on the table caught the light as she reached for it and took a slow sip.

    She realized that of course Wanda was polished, poised, and fluent in six kinds of intimidation. Of course she wore perfume that smelled like money and history, and walked into the precinct like the floor had been paved just for her and Agatha knew women like that. The way they took up space with ease and expected the world to meet them halfway.

    She wasn’t that, would never be.

    She knew how to build things, fix things and how to punch, duck, and bleed. But she didn’t know how to be soft in the way Rio deserved or how to wear confidence like silk and walk into a boardroom with her mouth already rehearsed.

    Agatha could make Rio laugh and make her come. But she couldn’t offer the stability Wanda could, at least not with clean edges and federal titles that had a whole past lined in gold.

    Still, Rio had made her believe for a second that she could have it anyway. Perhaps there was a version of this where they could stand shoulder to shoulder in the precinct, laugh over bad leads, and go home together after. That maybe Agatha’s bruised edges didn’t make her unlovable. Just… human.

    But then Wanda walked in, Rio didn’t say anything, and Agatha had heard everything that silence meant. That was what had sunk her chest deep into this mess of papers and empty bottles with a case that didn’t give a shit about her heartbreak.

    So now, she was here. Sitting in a cold living room full of colder paper. The only heat left in the place radiated from the burn in her chest that never fully went out. She’d buried it under the case files, under red tape and timelines and names she couldn’t forget. But it was still there; quiet and waiting.

    God help her, she missed Rio.

    The woman blinked hard, dragged her hands down her face, and reached for the next file. Forced herself to focus. To lean into the cold clinical facts that didn’t ask her to feel anything. There was work to do and women like Lisa didn’t have the luxury of waiting for Agatha to get over herself. Enough.

    The thought came pointed and final, slicing clean through the ache. Agatha cleared her throat deliberately and sat up straighter.

    This was a waste of time; sitting here thinking about things she couldn’t have, couldn’t hold. Letting herself slip into that soft, vulnerable place that did nothing but make her stupid.

    That wasn’t who she was.

    She reached forward, dragging a case file toward her, knocking the others slightly askew in the process. Her hands were steady now, movements brute. She clicked the pen beside her with one hand, the tip biting into the margin as she began scribbling notes, circling phrases, underlining dates with the kind of pressure that left faint dents in the page beneath. Agatha sat forward, elbows braced on her knees, scanning the interview notes from Lisa’s old classmate. Every third word was bullshit, but there were details she hadn’t caught before. Minor things. The kind of inconsistencies you had to be looking for to find. She combed through it again, annotating margins with her usual shorthand. It was mechanical, and sharp enough to keep her from spiraling.

    This was what she knew.

    The ache in her ribs didn’t go away, but she shoved it to the back of her mind and stacked more files over it. One after another. Pages rustled, tabs clicked. A red Sharpie was uncapped, bleeding urgency onto the timeline she had scrawled across the inside of a manila folder.

    She worked like someone trying to outrun something—because she was. Every note she jotted down, every page she highlighted, was a distraction. A substitute for the warmth that had once sat beside her in the car, for the softness of brown eyes that used to linger too long.

    She told herself she had to switch back to the version of herself who walked into Delaney’s office and took up too much space on purpose. Who called bullshit when she saw it and didn’t care whose feathers got ruffled. The Agatha who got things done and didn’t flinch, who didn’t hope. That version of her had survived things this soft, stupid version never could. The woman who kept her tongue sharp and ran leads into the ground and kept going even when the rest of the squad folded.

    That woman didn’t ache.

    She didn’t stay up at night thinking about the way Rio had looked when she smiled just for her or spend hours imagining how it would’ve felt to trace her fingers along Rio’s spine and whisper things no one else had ever heard. She sure as hell didn’t sit on her couch in the dark with a pile of case files like they might somehow put her back together.

    No, she chased down truth like it owed her something while pulling confessions out of suspects with nothing but a grit and a stare. She didn’t need backup.

    Agatha caught herself, teeth grinding once. She reached for the next file, flipping it open fast enough to nearly rip the corner.

    Lisa’s workplace log-in records. Clean on the surface, but something felt off. She grabbed a highlighter and started marking entries, cross-checking times with witness statements. Her brain ticked back into place. The rhythm was familiar, brutal; and it worked.

    Work didn’t lie to her. Files didn’t pull away.
