    This case was hers, not Wanda’s, not the Bureau’s. Not anyone’s but hers. If she had to tear it apart piece by piece until it made sense—then so be it. Rio could stay in that boardroom and be poised and polished and press her back against that glass while Wanda smiled too close.

    Agatha didn’t need softness.

    She needed answers.

    A low hum from the fridge cut in and out with a struggling motor, and the quiet groan of the old pipes made the house feel older than it was. Outside, the wind scraped bare branches against the siding in slow, arrhythmic drags. But inside—there was nothing but breath and pages and silence. Agatha didn’t notice the knock at first.

    It came once. A soft, measured tap. Enough to pull her eyes up from the case file in her lap and make her blink like she was surfacing from underwater.

    She straightened slowly, her knees stiff, her muscles aching in that dull, tired way that came from staying hunched over too long. She peeled herself off the couch with a hand on the armrest and one lingering on the file spread open in front of her.

    Agatha crossed the living room without turning on a light. Her socked feet made no sound on the wood floor. She paused at the door, one hand on the knob, the other brushing back a loose strand of hair that had fallen from her messy ponytail hours ago.

    Lilia stood on the porch, bundled in a long navy coat with her scarf looped once at her throat, curls frizzing slightly from the cold. She didn’t look surprised to see Agatha’s face, only tilted her head slightly like she had been expecting it. Her bracelets clinked softly as she lowered her hand from knocking again.

    “Well,” Lilia said gently, “look who’s still alive.”

    Agatha sighed. “I’m sure you’d hear from Delaney if I died via RSVP for a department wide celebration.”

    The older woman gave a faint smile, warm but not prying. “Can I come in?”

    Agatha stepped aside without a word.

    Lilia entered with slow, familiar steps, taking in the half-darkened space, the cold lamp-glow, the scattered files. She unwrapped her scarf as she walked, the quiet music of silver and brass layered around her wrist like a habit she never broke. Her flats tapped gently across the hardwood. Lilia never rushed. Not unless she had a damn good reason.

    “Thought I’d find you at the precinct,” she said, scanning the living room. “But everyone said you were off the radar.”

    “I’m working,” Agatha rubbed the back of her neck as she walked back to her spot on the couch.

    “You left the precinct four days ago without a word,” Lilia said, setting her bag down on the edge of the armchair. “Didn’t check in today either.” Lilia gave the coffee table a long look with its papers and photographs splayed out. Her eyes flicked briefly to the untouched cup of coffee on the side table, the subtle tremble in Agatha’s hand as she adjusted a file out of alignment.

    Agatha let out a dry sound that might’ve been a laugh. “Didn’t realize I was being monitored.”

    “You’re not.” Lilia paused, setting her scarf on the arm of the couch. “But I notice when my stubborn and loud-mouthed detective disappears.” Her eyes landed gently on Agatha again.

    “You’ve got the look,” she said. “The ‘if I just stare at the crime file long enough, I won’t have to think about the rest of it’ look.” Agatha didn’t reply.

    “You’re chasing ghosts,” she said gently. “There’s nothing in these pages you haven’t already seen.”

    Agatha scoffed. “That what you came here to say?”

    “No,” Lilia said, and sat down with a soft grunt, knees creaking a little as she folded one leg under the other. “I came because William said you’ve been quiet. Too quiet.”

    Agatha huffed, resting her elbows on her knees. “Didn’t know the twink had me on surveillance.”

    “He worries,” Lilia replied easily, crossing her arms. “We all do. Even if you make it hard.”

    A long silence settled between them. Lilia didn’t press. She never did. That was her trick—she let silence stretch just long enough to make people fill it. Agatha didn’t bite.

    Lilia leaned forward slightly, voice lowering. “I know you, Agatha. Know what you do when you feel like something’s been taken from you. You dig in and close off. You make everything about the job, because that’s safer than letting yourself feel anything.”

    Agatha’s jaw tightened. Her hands stilled.

    “This isn’t about the case,” Lilia added, softer now. “Not really.” Agatha looked away.

    “I’m fine,” she said at last, voice tight. “I just needed space. Time.” “From the case, or Vidal?” Lilia asked.

    Agatha didn’t answer. But she didn’t need to, not really. Lilia’s eyes flicked to the far end of the coffee table. The untouched file with Rio’s handwriting still visible in the margin. The one Agatha hadn’t touched.

    “You’re allowed to want things, Agatha,” she said gently. “Even if they scare the hell out of you.”

    Lilia stayed quiet for a beat, letting the words settle. Letting Agatha sit in the silence she was so used to filling with noise or sarcasm or movement. But this time, the detective didn’t reach for another file. She didn’t offer a deflection or a change of subject. She just sat there, hunched in her old academy shirt and shadows under her eyes, like the air had gotten too heavy to fight against.

    The older woman’s gaze slid again to the untouched file on the corner of the table—the one with Rio’s handwriting curling neat and precise in the margins, contrasting all the rougher notes scrawled in Agatha’s own hand. It wasn’t a coincidence. She knew it. Agatha had pulled everything apart except for that one. Like it might burn if she looked at it too long. Lilia’s voice broke the quiet, soft but unmistakably pointed. “You miss Vidal, don’t you?”

    Agatha let out a sharp breath, like she’d been holding it too long. She rubbed a hand over her mouth and finally leaned back against the couch cushions, eyes half-lidded, not looking up.

    “Don’t do that,” she said flatly, fingers tightening in her lap. “Don’t make it a thing.”

    Lilia sat down slowly across from her, ignoring the scattered paperwork, the mess of post-it notes, and coffee stains. She folded her hands in her lap, bracelets resting against her wrists like punctuation marks. “I’m not making it a thing,” she said. “I’m naming what’s already here.”

    Agatha’s jaw tensed. She didn’t speak. Just stared past Lilia’s shoulder at the wall like it held answers she didn’t want to say out loud. “She’s not why I’m here,” Agatha muttered after a second. “This isn’t about her.”

    Lilia arched a brow. “No?”

    Agatha let out a quiet scoff. “It’s not,” she repeated. “It’s about the case. About Delaney, and Wanda, and the fucking jurisdiction circus that’s pulling the rug out from under everyone.” Her hands moved now, finally, gesturing toward the table. “It’s about keeping my head on straight and trying to figure out who’s taking women like Lisa while the feds play chess in our precinct.”

    Lilia nodded slowly, still watching her.

    “You always were good at carrying the weight,” she said. “But you’ve also always been terrible at admitting when you’re hurt.”

    Agatha’s mouth twisted, but she didn’t argue.

    “She matters to you,” Lilia added, softer now. “That’s not a weakness.” Agatha leaned forward, bracing her elbows on her knees. Her head dropped slightly, fingers lacing together. She didn’t say anything, but the silence this time wasn’t stubborn. It was crumbling.

    “She looked at me like she meant it,” she said finally, voice low. “Like I was something she wanted. And then Wanda walked in and—” She swallowed hard. “She didn’t look back.”

    Lilia’s expression didn’t change, but her eyes softened.

    “She didn’t look back,” Agatha repeated, quieter now, more to herself than to anyone else.

    Lilia reached across the space between them, her hand resting gently on Agatha’s wrist. “Maybe she didn’t know how,” she said. “Or maybe she was scared too. That doesn’t mean it didn’t matter.”

    Agatha didn’t flinch from the touch, but she didn’t move either. Her breathing was shallow, her eyes fixed somewhere far away. “I don’t want to be something she settles for,” she murmured. “She doesn’t see you that way.”

    “You don’t know that.”

    “I know her better than you think,” Lilia said gently. “And I know you; what I see is a woman who’s terrified to be wanted in a way she doesn’t understand. And another woman who wants to reach for her but doesn’t know if she’ll be caught and get in trouble.”

    Agatha’s eyes burned. She pressed her fingers to her temples and leaned back into the couch again, exhaling long and slow through her nose. “I just needed to clear my head,” she said after a while, her voice hoarse. “That’s all.”

    Lilia let it lie, just for now. But her hand remained on Agatha’s wrist. “You don’t have to clear her out,” she said. “Just let yourself want her. That’s enough for now.”

    Lilia’s hand withdrew after a moment, her bracelets giving a soft clink as they settled back against her wrist. She sighed, leaning back against the worn cushion of the chair with the familiarity of someone who’d done this quiet consoling of stubborn younger detectives more times than she could count.

    “We know how she is,” she said finally, voice quiet but certain. “You do too. Collected. Precise. The rulebook might as well be stitched into her damn blazer lining.”

    Agatha let out a breath that was almost a laugh, bitter and too short. “Yeah, sure.”

    “She can’t not follow protocol,” Lilia continued. “Not when it’s coming from the Bureau or when it’s Wanda in charge. It’s who she was trained to be; who she used to be.”

    Agatha didn’t say anything at first. She just stared at a half-creased folder on the table like she might be able to sink through it if she looked hard enough.

    Lilia’s voice softened. “And they go back. That matters, whether she wants it to or not.”

    Agatha’s jaw flexed. Her voice, when it came, was low and tight. “That’s exactly why,” she muttered.

    Lilia raised an eyebrow, waiting.

    Agatha ran a hand through her hair, catching on a knot and pulling it loose with too much force. “That’s exactly fucking why. She comes from that world. Structure, protocol, polish. And Wanda—Wanda fits into it like a tailored suit. Literally.” Her voice twisted on the last word, half-humor, half-heartache. “She walks in here and knows exactly where to stand, what to say, how to look at her like she already owns the fucking air she breathes.”

    Agatha’s gaze dropped to her lap, to her cargo pants still half-undone and her calloused fingers twitching against her thighs like they were searching for something solid to grip.

    “She fits,” Agatha said. “In the Bureau, in Delaney’s office, in whatever version of Rio existed before she got stuck with me.”

    Lilia didn’t argue. She just let the silence stretch until Agatha was ready to fill it herself.

    And after a moment, Agatha did. Her voice quieter now. Like it hurt to say. “I can’t compete with that. And I don’t want to. But I’d be lying if I said it didn’t make me feel like a fucking idiot for thinking even for a second that I could be something she wanted.”

    Lilia’s eyes were kind but firm.

    “She does want you,” she said, steady. “Anyone with eyes can see that.” Agatha didn’t lift her head. Just exhaled again, long and tired. Her hands folded over her stomach, like she was trying to hold everything inside. “She didn’t stop Wanda,” she said. “Didn’t even hesitate.” “That’s not the same as choosing her.”

    “No,” Agatha murmured, more to herself than to Lilia. “But it’s not choosing me either.”

    Lilia let out a long, heavy sigh that came from too many years of watching emotionally constipated people dance circles around their own feelings. Then, without warning, she reached over and gave Agatha’s thigh a firm pat. The kind of gesture only Lilia Calderu could make feel both comforting and completely unbothered.

    “I can’t make you stop being stubborn,” she said, her voice dry but not unkind. “God knows I’ve tried.”

    Agatha huffed out the ghost of a laugh but didn’t look up. Lilia gave her a look. “But you know what I think? I think you two will figure it out. You’ve got that ridiculous tension and longing and stupid half- smiles in hallways.”

    Another pat. This time gentler.

    “My little lesbians,” she said with a sigh of faux affection, “always doing everything the hardest way possible. But you’ll get there.” Agatha let her head fall back against the couch cushion, eyes closing, lips twitching like she wanted to argue but couldn’t quite find the energy. “Jesus,” she muttered. “You’re worse than a therapist.”

    Lilia stood, her bangles jangling softly as she smoothed the wrinkles from her slacks. “Cheaper, too.”

    She glanced at the untouched folder one last time. Then back to Agatha. “Just don’t sit in the dark forever, kid. It doesn’t suit you.” Lilia didn’t move to stand. Instead, she let her hand rest there for a beat longer on Agatha’s thigh before withdrawing, “Well I didn’t just come here to play therapist,” she added, her tone shifting into something steadier. “I came because we’ve got a lead. I’ve been doing some digging of my own, especially after our charming little tongue-lashing from Delaney the other day.”

    Agatha’s brows furrowed, eyes lifting halfway open.

    Lilia pulled a folded file from her bag, worn and creased; the corner marked with a faint coffee ring like it had been passed between too many hands. She set it on the coffee table, carefully avoiding the folder with Rio’s handwriting in the margins.

    “It’s not in the system anymore,” Lilia said. “Delaney pulled it. Labeled it a dead-end and dumped it into the ‘irrelevant’ pile.”

    Agatha’s jaw clenched at that word, of course he did. She straightened a little. “And?”

    “There’s a family out in South Chicago,” she said. “Girl named Megan Hartley went missing last year. Nineteen. Low-income housing. Report was filed, but Delaney wrote it off as a runaway. The file got buried.” Agatha leaned forward slowly, eyes narrowing. “She matches Lisa?” she asked.

    Lilia nodded once. “Too many similarities to ignore. Same profile. Same window of disappearance. No struggle. No follow-up.”

    Agatha sat forward, arms braced against her knees. Something sharper had settled in her spine now. “And you’re just telling me this now?” “I only found it last night,” Lilia said. “Been digging through some of the precinct’s neglected drawers since Delaney chewed our asses out in front of God and the Bureau Barbie.”

    Agatha’s jaw twitched, but her lips curled faintly at the dig. Lilia continued. “This girl’s mom reached back to me directly this morning following a voice mail I left. She said she wasn’t going to bother filing again because no one gave a shit the first time.”

    Agatha picked up the file slowly, flipping through the brief report. Photos. Timelines. A list of calls that had gone unanswered.

    “I want you to take a ride out there,” Lilia said. “Scope it out. Talk to the mother. Quietly. Off the books for now.”

    She stood with a small sigh, stretching her back, bracelets clinking. “William’s going with you.”

    “Oh, fuck me,” Agatha groaned.

    “He’s already looped in,” Lilia said. “Been digging through CTA camera logs and pulling whatever’s left from the records that hasn’t been scratched over. You’ll meet him in the morning—8AM.”

    Lilia continued. “We’ve got a thread, and I want it pulled before it gets buried again.”

    Agatha traced a finger down the edge of the report. “You think this one’s connected.”

    “I think we’re on to something,” Lilia said. “And I think if Delaney had spent half as much time listening to us as he does shining Wanda’s badge, we’d already be on it.”

    Agatha stared at the folder. “You trust me with this?”

    “I trust you more than anyone else in that building,” Lilia said, steady. “Not because you’re the easiest to work with—God knows you’re not—but because you don’t stop when something matters.”

    She nudged the file gently toward her. “And this matters.” Agatha didn’t say anything, just stared at the folder like it was something she already knew she wouldn’t be able to let go of.

    Lilia headed towards the door, one hand resting lightly on the frame. She paused before leaving, glancing back once.

    “Harkness?”

    Agatha looked up.

    “Try not to light anything on fire before morning.”

    “No promises.”

    Lilia smiled faintly, shook her head, and moved toward the door. It clicked shut behind her, leaving Agatha alone again, but not quite as aimless as before.

    The rain had been falling in a steady, frozen sheet since before dawn. Not dramatic but relentless. The kind of rain that soaked you from the soles up, that crept into the stitching of your boots and refused to leave. It had started sometime around four, and now, just past eight, it hadn’t let up. Agatha stood by the side of her unmarked Crown Vic, arms crossed tight over her chest, jaw set against the wind. The lot behind the precinct was half-empty as shifts hadn’t changed over yet, and most people with any sense were still inside nursing shitty coffee and dodging Wanda’s coordinating efforts.

    But not her.

    She was wrapped in her black hoodie and jean jacket combo, both damp now, the sleeves of the hoodie peeking out like she hadn’t had the energy to roll them properly. Her jeans were cuffed at the ankle, boots already soaked through. Her hair was pulled into a tighter ponytail than usual, like she’d needed to do at least one thing with purpose this morning. Still messy, still hers.

    She leaned back against the car, arms folded, keys hanging from one finger. The manila folder Lilia had handed her last night sat on the dashboard inside the car, barely visible through the fogged windshield. Megan Hartley. Nineteen. Same profile. Same window of disappearance. Buried by Delaney, unearthed by Lilia.

    Agatha hadn’t slept. Not well, at least. Just laid on her couch, hoodie still on, staring at the ceiling with that weird low thrum in her chest that didn’t feel quite like anger. It hadn’t faded. If anything, it’d calcified. She wasn’t going to let another girl disappear into the cracks of Delaney’s arrogance and Wanda’s jurisdictional pissing match. Not if she could help it. Agatha stared at the sidewalk, at the way the rain collected in the cracks, turning the pavement slick and silver under the early morning gloom. Footsteps.

    Then a thump. Then the flurry of curses.

    She looked up just as William rounded the far end of the lot with the umbrella snapped backward; sleeves hiked up like he was mid-argument with the wind. “Jesus,” he called as he approached, rain bouncing off his jacket. “I should’ve called off. I’m too gay for fieldwork in a Chicago monsoon.”

    Agatha didn’t blink. “You’re late.”

    “You’re lucky I’m here at all,” William muttered, shaking out his arms as he approached. “Do you even own an umbrella?”

    “No.”

    “Of course not.”

    He eyed her soaked hoodie and scowled like she personally offended the concept of basic comfort. “You look like you slept on your porch.” Agatha pushed off the side of the car and opened the door, the creak of old hinges cutting through the rain.

    “Get in,” she said.

    He slid into the passenger seat with a dramatic sigh, shaking the water from his sleeves and immediately wincing as he caught sight of the file. “Oof. We’re opening with dramatics. Love that.”

    Agatha started the engine with a grimace as the wipers shrieked across the windshield, barely clearing the blur.

    William buckled in, flipping open the folder. “Megan Hartley. Delaney’s throwaway file. South Chicago.”

    “Low-income housing. No press coverage. Delaney called it a runaway and buried the report.” Agatha’s voice was flat. Focused. “Mom reached out to Lilia in the A.M yesterday.”

    “And we’re just now going?”

    “We go quiet. We don’t spook her.”

    William glanced at her from the corner of his eye, gauging the sharp line of her jaw and the way her fingers flexed on the wheel. “And Rio’s… where? Still doing that sexy auburn goddess’s bidding?”

    Agatha didn’t answer.

    He tried again. “You gonna tell me what happened, or are we doing the whole ‘stoic pain behind the wheel’ routine all morning?” She exhaled through her nose. “I’m not in the mood.”

    William leaned his head back against the seat, letting the silence hang a beat. “Which mood are we in then? Brooding but sexy? Or emotionally unavailable with a side of barely repressed heartbreak?”

    Agatha’s hand jerked on the turn signal. “Don’t push it, William.” He held up both hands, palms up in mock surrender. “Okay, okay. My bad. Just—y’know, Lilia and I are rooting for you. There’s a lot of ‘Will they, won’t they’ energy.”

    She didn’t even look at him.

    He lowered his hands slowly. “Cool. So we’re doing the glacial silence thing. Got it. Copy that.”

    The car pulled away from the curb, tires hissing on wet asphalt, and the city blurred past in smudges of gray and rust. The heater kicked on with a reluctant whir, the vents groaning as Agatha twisted the dial—halfhearted at best, but it gave the cabin the faint illusion of warmth. She kept her eyes forward, one hand on the wheel, the other drumming restlessly against the gearshift.

    William flipped through the file again, quieter this time. The only sound between them was the slap of rain on the roof and the low hum of the city trying to wake up around them.

    “Just so you know,” he said eventually, voice softer, “I’m not trying to be a dick. I get it.”

    Agatha didn’t answer, but her jaw unclenched slightly.

    He took the hint. Went back to the file.

    And they drove on, the Crown Vic slicing through the rain like it had something to prove.

    The house was small, one of those aging brick duplexes tucked along a narrow side street in South Chicago where the gutters always seemed one storm away from collapse. The rain had turned the walkway into a shallow stream, and the Crown Vic’s tires splashed curb water before Agatha killed the engine and stepped out into the gray.

    Inside, it smelled like furniture polish and cinnamon. The kind of smell that clung to the baseboards and the coffee table, a smell that suggested someone was always trying to keep the place just nice enough for company, even if no one came around anymore.

    Megan Hartley’s mother had led them inside with the quiet stiffness of a woman who didn’t trust the world not to bite. Her eyes were dark and wary, crow’s feet carved by years of squinting too hard at things she didn’t want to see. She had offered them coffee with the politeness of someone who didn’t trust kindness anymore but still did it anyway, because that’s what you did when someone in uniform walked through your door. They sat now in the tight living room, coffee mugs mismatched and chipped at the rims, the heat rising soft and curling into the stale warmth of the air. One window let in a dull slant of overcast light. Lace curtains hung limp at the edges, too tired to sway.

    Agatha had her elbows on her knees, hands wrapped around the ceramic, watching the woman carefully. She didn’t take notes. Just listened. “I filed the report the next morning,” the woman said. Her voice was low, the kind that had weathered too many phone calls and too many officers who hadn’t remembered her name. “Megan always came home. Always called. Even if she was late, even if she was upset. That night… nothing’. Just didn’t come back from her shift at the diner.”

    She paused, eyes flicking between the two detectives. “No signs of a fight. No messages. Not even a missed call. It was like someone just… picked her up off the street and disappeared her.”

    Agatha’s voice came low, even. “And the response?”

    The woman gave a humorless laugh, dry and emotionless. “A patrol car rolled up six hours later and a regular cop took a statement. Then someone higher up, not the kind you forget.”

    William leaned forward; brow furrowed. “What did he look like?” She blinked slowly, like pulling the image forward took effort. “He was heavyset with greying hair, slicked back like he thought it still looked good, and his suit didn’t fit right. Kept pulling at the collar like it bothered him. He said my daughter was a runaway and that they didn’t open full investigations for those unless there was proof of danger.” Agatha’s stomach dropped, Delaney had personally showed up to cover his tracks.

    The detective sat up straighter. “Did he give a name?”

    She shook her head. “No. Just said he was the one who signed off on the file. Kept saying it wouldn’t go anywhere. That girls like Megan had a ‘pattern.’” Her jaw clenched around the word. “He didn’t even ask me for Megan’s friends or go through her room. He simply said, ‘These things happen, ma’am,’ and walked out.”

    A long pause.

    “And then…” The woman trailed off, her fingers tightening slightly around the mug, as if she wasn’t sure if she should admit further. “About two days later, another one came but it was a woman this time. She was so well dressed and had hair like one of those weather girls on Channel 5. Red. Expensive red.”

    Agatha’s blood went cold.

    “She said she was with the Bureau,” the woman continued, unaware of the silence pressing into the room like a weight. “and she was following up on similar disappearances. She did ask me questions about Megan’s friends, work schedule, routes home. But when I told her about the man from before — whoever he was — she didn’t write it down.”

    William frowned. “She didn’t log his visit?”

    “Nope. Just nodded like she already knew.” The woman’s mouth pulled tight. “Then she offered me money.”

    That made both of them sit straighter.

    “Money?” Agatha asked carefully.

    “Not a bribe, not exactly,” the woman clarified. “She called it a ‘polite compensation.’ That the Bureau had a fund for families. That sometimes, it was better to accept when there were no real leads. That closure came in many forms.”

    Agatha stared at her, the world narrowing.

    “She said if I signed a document, the case would be formally closed. No further contact. Just… silence.” She looked up now, meeting Agatha’s eyes for the first time in minutes. “She smiled the whole time like she was doing me a favor.”

    Agatha’s grip on her mug tightened so hard her knuckles went white. Her face didn’t move. Her posture stayed still.

    Wanda.

    She was pre-closing cases. Handing off hush money and red-tape phrasing like it was a customer service transaction. Pulling strings wasn’t enough anymore, she was tying off the loose ends.

    Delaney had let her.

    Her voice, when it came, was steady. “You didn’t sign anything, did you?” The woman shook her head. “No. I didn’t trust her. Something about it felt… off. Too easy. I wanted someone to look instead of paying me to stop asking.”

    William exhaled slowly, breaking the silence. “Do you still have her card?” “No.” The woman’s brow furrowed. “She never left one but said she’d be in touch. She’s still yet to come back.”

    Agatha didn’t answer. She just sat there for a beat longer, eyes locked on the wall just past the woman’s shoulder, where a photo of Megan hung in a cheap silver frame. Her smile was bright and awkward, the kind that didn’t know what was coming.

    Then, quiet and careful, she reached into her coat, pulled out her own card, and set it down.

    “You said you wanted someone to look,” Agatha said, voice low. “We’re looking.”

    Megan’s mother held the card delicately, like it might vanish if she looked at it too long. Her hands trembled faintly, knuckles pale from how tightly she gripped her coffee mug. Grief aged a person faster than years ever could. It was all in the posture, the hush of her voice, the way she hadn’t touched the photo on the shelf since the day it was framed. Agatha leaned forward, elbows planted on her knees now, voice low but full of that quiet, cutting force she reserved for things that mattered. “I don’t care what the last man said,” she told her. “I don’t care what they offered you or how neatly they tried to shut this case down.” Her fingers tapped once against the edge of her badge where it rested on her belt, deliberately.

    “I’m going to find out what happened to your daughter.”

    The woman blinked, startled. Justice for her had become a kind of myth people danced around, no one dared name it out loud anymore. “I promise,” Agatha added, and there was no room for doubt in the way she said it. Not even a crack. “You have my word.”

    From beside her, William shifted slightly. His expression twitched just enough to register concern. Then, under his breath he leaned toward her and whispered, “Harkness, you can’t say that for liability. If this turns—” Agatha held up one finger without looking at him. Not to silence him harshly, but with finality. The kind that said drop it.

    William leaned back with a sigh, hands lifting in surrender. “Okay,” he mumbled, resigned. “Cool. Love it when we get sued.”

    But Agatha didn’t waver.

    She met the mother’s eyes again, gaze steady as stone. “We’re not done. If anyone else shows up whether it be feds, suits, anyone asking you to sign anything; you call me. Immediately.”

    The mother nodded slowly, her lip trembling. “Okay.”

    “I mean it.” Agatha reached out, resting her hand gently on the woman’s wrist. “You don’t owe them silence.”

    For a long moment, the only sound in the room was the distant groan of pipes in the wall and the slow, uneven breathing of a woman who hadn’t been able to sleep in months.

    Then the mother whispered, “Thank you.”

    Agatha nodded once, and for the first time that morning, the steel in her spine didn’t feel like armor. It felt like purpose.

    Agatha shrugged her denim jacket back over her hoodie, tugging it by the collar and rolling her shoulders until the fabric settled across her frame. The old seams cracked faintly with the motion, stiff from the chill that clung to them like a second skin. She gave the woman a nod, one hand gripping the edge of the door.

    “Thanks for the coffee,” she said quietly. “And for trusting us.” Megan’s mother hovered in the doorway, still clutching her mug with both hands. She looked like she wanted to say more having spent so long being ignored that the sudden attention sat uneasy on her shoulders, but in the end, she just gave a faint, solemn nod.

    “Thank you,” she said. “For listening. I don’t know what you’ll find… but I appreciate you even looking.”

    Agatha welcomed it with a small grin. “We’ll find something,” she said, not quite promising.

    They stepped out into the cold, the door closing behind them with a tired click. The rain hadn’t lifted, it actually thickened while hugging the sidewalk and seeping into the cuffs of their sleeves. Agatha closed her jacket halfway and shoved her hands into her pockets, boots thudding against wet concrete as she stalked toward the car.

    William followed a step behind, his brow furrowed, his breath forming pale ghosts in the air.

    “She said red hair and a suit,” he muttered under his breath. “And a ‘polite compensation.’ God.”

    Agatha’s lips twisted into something bitter. “That’s one word for it,” she muttered. “Wanda probably called it a kindness, too. Probably even smiled while doing it.”

    William winced. “I mean she is terrifying. In a Disney villain sort of way.” Agatha yanked the car door open, the metal creaking slightly. “Wanda’s not a villain,” she muttered, deadpan. “She’s a fucking disease.” William blinked, hesitating. “Jesus. You’ve been workshopping that one?” “Didn’t have to.” Agatha slid into the driver’s seat. “It wrote itself.” He opened the passenger door and climbed in, shaking the water from his sleeves. “Okay, well, between that and Delaney greasing grieving mothers? I’m gonna be honest, this whole thing is starting to feel above my pay grade.”

    Agatha smirked darkly, starting the engine. “What, you scared?” “A little,” he admitted, cinching his seatbelt. “I mean feds, bribes, cover- ups? This is some HBO Sunday night level corruption.”

    She pulled the car into gear, eyes fixed on the slick, gray street ahead. “Yeah,” she said. “And we just kicked the hornet’s nest.” They didn’t say anything after that. This case was getting bigger; and they were in it now—deep.

    The boardroom lights buzzed above her like they had something to prove. Cold fluorescents hummed overhead, flattening everything into sterile white and shadow. The long conference table was littered with folders and printed reports, maps bleeding with red string logic, and government letterheads stamped like threats. It was a bureaucratic graveyard with every paper polished, and word weaponized.

    Rio sat at the head of the table, posture rigid, blazer unbuttoned due to the heat that was too high in the room. A half-empty mug of coffee sat beside her, gone cold hours ago with the rim smeared faintly where her lipstick had once been. She hadn’t noticed and hadn’t tasted it anyway. She’d been in this seat for hours now pinned in place by duty, decorum, and Wanda’s endlessly rotating carousel of directives.

    A thick binder was open in front of her, its spine cracked, notes scrawled in her own even, meticulous handwriting along the margins. She could still hear Wanda’s voice echoing from earlier in the day directing the flow of the so-called “interagency coordination” meetings like she was orchestrating a symphony. Except Rio knew the music by now and the shape of this performance.

    Every task, every form, every status report handed to her felt like a leash— tightening, inch by inch. And she kept smiling through it. Kept her voice even because that’s what professionals did. That’s what the old Rio had always done. Play the part and follow the structure.

    She flipped a page with too much force, the sound snapping through the empty room.

    God, she was tired.

    Not the kind of tired that sleep fixed, but the kind that rooted itself in your bones. A tiredness that came from swallowing too many words, too many instincts, too many pieces of herself she kept cutting out in the name of professionalism. In the name of obedience. She'd thought being dropped from the Bureau would mean leaving this behind. She’d thought that maybe she could breathe again in this precinct. With her team. With— Her heart gave a traitorous tug.

    Agatha.

    Her chest tightened instinctively; she hadn’t seen her in days. Hadn’t said more than a handful of words to her since she’d walked out of that boardroom with Wanda’s shadow still clinging to her like perfume. She’d felt Agatha’s absence immediately, like a chair left suddenly empty at the dinner table. Something had shifted and Rio knew exactly when it had happened.

    When Wanda had said “Agent Vidal will assist me.”

    Rio, like the coward she was, had nodded and followed orders even when it meant leaving Agatha behind with that look in her eyes.

    God, she could still see it.

    That moment of shock, then hurt. Agatha had shut the door on her with that maddening, beautiful stoicism. That tightness in her jaw that meant don’t let it show and silence that said, I get it. You made your choice. Except Rio hadn’t chosen Wanda. She’d just… reverted with muscle memory. Her obedience hard-wired into her from the days of tight collars and tighter expectations.

    She rubbed at her temple, eyes falling to a line of text she couldn’t read. This wasn’t how it was supposed to go.

    She was supposed to leave that part of her behind. The Rio that let her world be dictated by clearance levels and hierarchy and cold-blooded women with Harvard degrees and diamond-cutter smiles. She was supposed to be building something new like a different life, one where she could be her own person making her own calls.

    She was supposed to go on a date with Agatha. In a stupid truck that stalled at red lights over food that was probably too salty; and music playing on the radio with Agatha’s laugh spilling through the cab like something sacred. Instead, she was here.

    Working side by side with the woman that called her mi amor in the same breath she signed disciplinary notices. Who saw affection as leverage and wielded proximity like a weapon.

    Rio shut the folder and pressed it flat against the table to still it. She needed to focus. Wanda was relentless when it came to precision, and Rio wasn’t about to let her see the cracks. Not again.

    But the truth was, Agatha was a crack.

    A glorious, maddening, beautifully jagged fault line running straight through the center of her ribcage, yet Rio had let her walk away. She hadn’t realized she was crying until the tear hit the back of her hand. Sitting here now, trapped under the weight of Wanda’s precision and her own silence, she realized she’d been checking the hallway every time someone walked past. Expecting to see worn boots and a sharp jaw. Hoping for a sarcastic comment tossed her way with that crooked half-smile. It hadn’t come in the days prior, nor this morning and she was starting to think it wouldn’t.

    Her heart twisted in that quiet, aching way when you realized you’d already messed something up and there was no rewind button.

    Agatha had looked at her like she’d expected more.

    But Rio had sat there, like always, and played it safe. And Agatha had gone still as if the light inside her dimmed just slightly. Maybe she’d known exactly how this story ended the second Wanda walked through that door. You’re too good for this precinct, Rio, Wanda had said.

    Rio believed her.

    Not because she thought the precinct was beneath her—God, no. The people here fought harder and cared more than anyone she’d known in D.C. But because somewhere, buried in the part of her that still flinched at her own reflection, was the sick, familiar fear that she didn’t belong anywhere. Not at the Bureau, or here, and definitely not next to someone like Agatha. Agatha, with her little rough edges and tired sarcasm and hands that always looked like they’d just come from doing something real. Agatha, who called her out with a single look and didn’t flinch when Rio pushed back. Agatha, who had opened the door for her. Offered a place beside her. Yet Rio had walked away letting the door close behind her. She pressed her hands to her face now, fingertips digging into the hollows of her eyes, the pressure sharp enough to blur everything behind her lids. It didn’t help. She’d told herself she didn’t have a choice and that professionalism came first. That this was temporary.

    Except it didn’t feel temporary anymore and she didn’t know if there was enough left in her to fix it.

    Maybe Agatha really was better off with someone who didn’t freeze every time old ghosts showed up in sharp suits and called her by titles she hadn’t used in years. Someone who didn’t carry the scars of being trained to keep people out.

    Rio had spent her whole life making herself into someone efficient, but, she wasn’t those things around Agatha.

    Around her, Rio softened. Laughed. Wanted.

    It terrified her because if she let herself really want Agatha and cracked open that door all the way—she didn’t know if she could survive it when it slammed shut.

    She had seen what happened when people like her leaned too hard on people like Agatha. She’d seen it in D.C., in Quantico, in every training facility that whispered keep your heart out of it. She’d learned the hard way that love wasn’t something women like her got to keep. Not without consequences.

    So maybe this was safer.

    Agatha should keep pushing and bury herself in the case. She should smirk while rolling her eyes and pretend nothing had ever happened between them.

    Rio would stay here doing what she was told; letting Wanda tighten the leash again and again.

    Maybe that way Agatha would forget what it felt like to want her. Rio closed her eyes. It was easier to believe that distance was a favor. That silence was safety and that she hadn’t already broken something precious. That Agatha Harkness was better off without her.

    The brown eyed woman reached for the next file again, so into her thoughts that she hadn’t heard the soft click of the door behind her. She didn’t register the faint perfume that followed it in which was floral, expensive. The kind that came in a glass bottle with a foreign name and cost more than a week’s groceries.

    “Agent Vidal.”

    The voice slithered in like a breeze under a locked door—smooth and poised, deceptively warm.

    Rio blinked, spine stiffening.

    Wanda’s heels clicked softly on the tile as she crossed the room, long coat swaying behind her like a whisper of silk. She didn’t sit—just leaned lightly against the edge of the conference table, crossing one ankle over the other with an air of lazy elegance. In her hands, she held a thin manila file that she made a show of reviewing, flipping it open with a practiced ease that reeked of performance.

    She smiled faintly.

    “Oh, don’t tell me,” she said, not even bothering to keep the amusement from her voice. “You’re thinking about that scruffy butch beat detective again.”

    “Harkness,” she said slowly, letting the name stretch out like gum on her tongue. “Honestly, it’s adorable, the way you’ve latched onto her. Like a baby duck to the first pair of combat boots you saw.”

    Wanda chuckled under her breath. A soft, condescending sound. “You really do wear it all over your face, you know,” she said, her tone wrapped in satin. “It’s honestly kind of sweet. The whole tortured heartache thing. Very ‘forbidden precinct romance.’ You should pitch it to cable.” She shifted slightly, just enough to lean into Rio’s field of view. “Is it the attitude?” she asked. “The flannel? The masculine energy? I mean, really, Rio—you used to have standards.”

    Rio’s pen scratched once on the paper, a quick line just to do something with her hands. She still didn’t look at her.

    Wanda continued, almost idly now, like she was making polite conversation.

    “I mean, I get it,” she said. “Harkness has… presence. That whole disheveled charisma thing going for her. There’s a certain appeal in that, I suppose.”

    She tilted her head, the diamonds in her earrings catching the light. “But let’s be honest,” she added, smile sharpening, “she’s not exactly built for this kind of work.”

    Rio’s brows drew together, faint but perceptible.

    “She doesn’t take orders,” Wanda continued, voice still gentle, still sweet. “She doesn’t understand structure. Hell, she can barely keep her case files organized, let alone follow federal protocol. She’d burn this place to the ground if you let her. You know that.”

    Her gaze drifted down, studying her perfectly manicured nails before flicking back to Rio.

    “I worry about you, that’s all,” she said. “You’ve worked so hard to build something here. A real career. Not just a reputation, but respect. You don’t want to throw that away for someone who doesn’t even understand what it costs.”

    Rio’s throat tightened.

    Wanda’s smile didn’t reach her eyes.

    “Besides,” she added softly, “women like Harkness? They don’t stay. Not when it gets complicated. Not when they start to feel caged. She doesn’t belong in places like this, Rio. You know that better than anyone.” Rio finally looked up, eyes sharp but unsure. “You don’t know her.” Wanda’s lashes fluttered once, elegantly unimpressed.

    “Oh, sweet girl,” she murmured, as if that were enough of an answer. Then she pushed off the table and began a slow circle behind Rio’s chair, her heels deliberately measured.

    “She left you standing out there, didn’t she?” Wanda asked. “After I reassigned you?” She tutted softly. “Didn’t even fight for you.” “She wanted to,” Rio said quietly.

    Wanda hummed. “But she didn’t.”

    The silence that followed was cold and heavy. It slotted neatly into place like a final puzzle piece.

    “She doesn’t care about chains of command,” Wanda continued, circling to the other side of the table now. “She doesn’t care about the job in the way you do. About accountability. That’s romantic in theory, but dangerous in practice, and selfish.”

    Rio’s mouth parted slightly, but no words came.

    Wanda smiled again, collected like she’d just offered sage advice and expected gratitude.

    “You’ll outgrow her,” Wanda said finally, adjusting the cuffs of her blazer with the smooth detachment of someone who had never rushed a day in her life. The motion was precise slow fabric gliding cleanly over her wrists like the closing of a statement. “You always do.”

    A beat of silence passed. Then, her voice was strangely tender. “Be realistic, Rio.”

    The way she said her name made it sound like a sigh. Familiar in a way that pressed up against the edges of things Rio had tried very hard not to remember.

    “You really think this is sustainable?” she went on, tone gentle in a way that felt far too rehearsed. “You, with your high clearance, your spotless record, your impeccable command of protocol mixed her with duct-taped morals and that ridiculous jacket she wears like armor?”

    There was something cruel in the tilt of her smile.

    “She doesn’t speak the language of this world. Of our world. She fights it every chance she gets.”

    She took a step closer again, voice lowering.

    “But me?” Wanda said, her heels clicking softly. “I know you. I know what you are. You liked waking up at five a.m. to run drills before coffee. You liked sitting across from me in strategy briefings, finishing each other’s analysis before the interns even had their pens out. We made sense.” She reached out to trail a finger across the arm rest of Rio’s chair, slow and deliberate.

    “Quantico was good, wasn’t it?” she said softly. “You and me—working late, pushing each other. Driving each other crazy.” Her voice dipped like a secret. “I still think about that winter in Richmond. Remember? The snowstorm? Power outage in the dorms. Just the two of us and a bottle of contraband whiskey.”

    Wanda leaned in, lips just shy of Rio’s ear, her breath warm and honeyed. “You can lie to me all you want, Agent Vidal,” she whispered. “But does Agatha know about that? About us?”

    The question landed like a crack in glass.

    Rio’s spine straightened, her hands curling around the file in front of her. “She doesn’t, does she?” Wanda murmured, backing off with a smile just shy of cruel. “Interesting.”

    “You belong with someone who knows what it means to want the work more than the recognition. Who won’t get intimidated every time you step into a suit and remind the room you’re in charge.”

    She stopped just beside Rio, her voice low and nearly intimate. “Someone like me.”

    Rio’s eyes flicked upward, just briefly. Her mouth didn’t move. Wanda smiled again, stepping back with maddening ease.

    “You don’t belong in this shit hole,” she said. “You don’t belong in this precinct with their cheap cops, and you sure as hell don’t belong sitting around waiting for some emotionally repressed beat dyke to figure out how to say she gives a damn.”

    “Come back to where you belong, Rio. To where you’re understood.” Her voice dropped one more time into something close to amusement, wicked and final. “Next time she makes you doubt yourself like this, just remember who taught you how to lead.”

    “So,” she said, voice sliding back into her clipped Bureau cadence, smooth and unreadable. “Lisa Moreno.”

    The name cut through the haze, grounding them back into the case, though it felt disjointed, the conversation had been flipped too abruptly. Rio didn’t move and let Wanda speak with her hands folded over the folder in her lap like it might anchor her to the present.

    “Our preliminary cross-state data points show three matching patterns, all within the past eighteen months. Victims fitting Lisa’s profile: women between eighteen and twenty-two, last seen alone, most within three blocks of transit lines or low-income housing clusters. No forced entry. No witnesses. No credible follow-up.”

    She spoke like she was delivering a postmortem.

    Rio didn’t respond, eyes fixed on the edge of the case board across the room.

    Wanda turned toward her, one slow pivot on her heels.

    “We’re starting with a reclassification,” she continued. “Human trafficking, multi-jurisdictional. That’ll allow us to pull federal strings, subpoena cross- agency records, tap Homeland for resource redundancy. We can initiate joint intel threads by the end of the week—assuming your precinct catches up.”

    Wanda pulled a chair beside Rio’s and was now close enough that Rio could feel the warmth of her thigh through their dress pants. Near enough that if either of them turned their heads too quickly, they would meet without meaning to.

    Rio stayed still and didn’t comment on the proximity.

    She simply nodded silently. Because what was there to say? Wanda knew her rhythm and her protocol. Ultimately, this was what she was here to do, work; and it didn’t matter who with nor how.

    Wanda picked up a pen from in front of Rio, clicking it once in thought before reaching across to jot a note in the margin of one of the supplemental reports. Her hand brushed Rio’s wrist as she did.

    Rio didn’t move. That this was just Wanda being… Wanda.

    She glanced sideways, just once.

    The auburn-haired woman was still angled toward her with lips parted in some half-thought, the line of her jaw in the office light. Her fingers drummed lightly on the folder between them. Her smile, when it came, was faint.

    Rio didn’t say a word, because even now her mind was full of blue eyes and the way Agatha used to smirk like she was about to pick a fight just to see how far she could push. That crooked grin that only came after midnight. The way she used to say Rio’s name like it meant something. But that was gone.

    So, Rio sat still allowing the silence between them settle in like cement with Wanda’s leg pressed against hers. For a moment, she let herself forget how soft it felt when Agatha kissed the edge of her jaw and whispered promises into the air like she wasn’t afraid of them.

    Rio turned the page of the file, and this time she didn’t flinch when Wanda leaned closer.

    The rain had stopped by the time she reached the end of the block, but the cold had only sharpened in its place. The kind that settled deep and silent, that clung to concrete and turned breath to smoke in the air. The streets glistened under the weight of it, damp and glassy, like the whole city was waiting to freeze.

    Agatha kept her head down as she walked, hands shoved deep into her jacket pockets, shoulders hunched beneath the weight of the night. Her hoodie was still damp along the sleeves where the rain had soaked through earlier, but she hadn’t changed. She hadn’t even thought about it. Just grabbed her keys and the kit she hadn’t used in months and walked out the door without looking back.

    The city felt different like this, all slick and brittle and mean. No music from the bars. No cars passing slow. Just the soft crunch of gravel under her boots and the steady sound of her own breath, fogging in front of her with every step.

    She rounded the corner by the utility shed and kept going, the precinct rising ahead of her like it always did—square, ugly, institutional. A building that looked like it was made to keep secrets. All the lights were off except for the glow of a single exit sign near the second floor, and one hallway bulb blinking behind a frosted window, off-on-off again in uneven intervals. The east side entrance didn’t look like much, just a slab of metal and rust behind the building with no camera, no foot traffic, but a keypad and a dent in the doorframe where someone had kicked it in two winters ago. This was the door you used when you didn’t want to be seen. Agatha stepped into the narrow patch of gravel leading to it, her boots catching against the loose stone. Her fingers flexed inside her pocket, brushing the edge of the lockpick kit nestled beneath her lighter and an old key she hadn’t used in months. Once she reached the door, she was greeted by old metal with green paint chipped around the handle. Rust climbed the hinges like it was trying to swallow the frame whole.

    The air felt sharper and threatened to cut through you if you stood still too long.

    Agatha didn’t flinch while crouching down as her jacket creaked when she bent down. The keypad blinked at her, dull yellow; she didn’t need the picks, at least not yet.

    She typed in 2-7-2-3 with numb fingers. Delaney’s precinct ID. The man was nothing if not predictable.

    It beeped, then green. The bolt clicked open, soft and unassuming. She smiled without humor. “Figures.”

    The door gave under her hand, heavy and slow as she slipped inside without looking back.

    The precinct was nearly pitch-black. No overheads except the dull red glow of the emergency exit signs and the occasional flicker of motion sensors that had long since given up. One buzzed to life at the far end of the hallway, humming faintly as it lit up a single square of tile in sickly white before dying again, like it hadn’t meant to.

    Agatha stood still for a second and let her eyes adjust.

    The silence pressed in hard like the building itself didn’t want her there. She moved anyway.

    Her boots were nearly soundless on the tile, each step measured, steady. She passed the bullpen without looking as desks were all shadows now with their empty chairs, half-finished paperwork still scattered from the day shift, a coffee cup gone cold beside the printer.

    She kept a head down with her jacket brushing against her hips with every step. Every creak in the floor made her shoulders twitch just slightly, muscles tight and ready to bolt. It was still breaking and entering, even if the badge on her belt said otherwise.

    She turned the corner toward Delaney’s wing.

    That hallway was worse, narrow and lined with framed commendations that gleamed faintly in the dark. A dozen smiling faces caught in grayscale with names engraved in brass that meant nothing. It smelled like dust and old heat, the vents definitely hadn’t been cleaned in decades and no one cared enough to notice.

    Agatha’s footsteps slowed.

    She knew exactly where she was going. Past the lieutenant’s office, past the door marked STORAGE, to the one that sat at the very end of the hall that Delaney kept locked even during the day. Records. Closed files. Internal review materials he said were too sensitive for the squad to handle. And girls who were never found.

    Her hand brushed the edge of her jacket, fingers grazing the cool metal of the lockpick set in her pocket.

    This was a bad idea. She knew it. Every part of her training—the little voice that used to sound like a captain back in the academy—whispered; don’t do this.

    But then again, so was hiding evidence and letting girls go missing, calling it “unfounded” while the Bureau signed off on the quiet burial of another cold case.

    The hallway flickered again, light stuttering above her head like it was trying to blink a warning.

    She didn’t stop.

    If Delaney was right and none of this was worth chasing, why did it feel like her chest was on fire when she thought of those girls? Why couldn’t she breathe until she knew what was in those damn files?

    Boots slowed in front of the door.

    It was nothing special but a solid slab of wood with a reinforced lock and a sign that read; RESTRICTED: AUTHORIZED PERSONNEL ONLY. Her breath ghosted in front of her again, barely visible now in the stale warmth of the hallway.

    She let her fingers slide into her pocket and found the tools by feel. The weight of metal on her palm was familiar, comforting in its own way. Agatha stared at the lock as her heart raced.

    This was a line and once she crossed it, there wasn’t going to be any walking it back.

    Except she’d already made her choice when she stepped out of her house in the middle of the night, soaked and tired and still aching with the echo of Rio’s silence.

    This was her case, and not Delaney nor Wanda would get to hide it from her anymore.

    After about twenty seconds of picking, the door gave with a slow, reluctant groan. Not loud, but enough to make Agatha wince as it echoed down the empty hallway behind her. She stepped in quick, pulled it shut, and locked it again from the inside.

    Dark.

    Blue eyes adjusted once again as the black corners pressed in and shadows leaned too close. She reached into her jacket, pulled out her flashlight, and clicked it on.

    A thin, sharp beam cut through the air as the dust caught in it like smoke. The records room was colder than the hallway. Smaller, and barely wide enough to pace across in four long steps. Steel filing cabinets lined the walls, labeled in cheap embossed tape, most of it peeling at the edges. A desk sat crooked in the back corner, papers still stacked from whatever clerk had last bothered to care.

    Agatha swallowed and moved to the classified cabinet.

    The top drawer rolled out first with a low scrape of metal on metal. Her flashlight swept over the folders and alphabetical tabs with its color- coded stickers faded from years of indifferent use. Nothing. She flipped to the next drawer, zero. Then the next; still nothing. Finally, the third drawer was her jackpot with a section tagged "Unsolved – Female, 18–25" in sloppy, blue marker.

    Her breath caught as she pulled it open.

    The light skimmed over a dozen folders lined tight together, each one marked in bold red lettering across the top: RUNAWAY.

    NO LEADS.

    CLOSED.

    RECLASSIFIED: DEAD-END.

    Agatha reached in and started pulling one by one.

    Emily Hartman, 2021.

    Vanessa Thorne, 2022.

    Alyssa Minh, 2020.

    Jasmine Martin, 2021.

    Each name came with the same pattern: Last seen alone and late at night. Usually near a bus stop or walking home from work. Same age range and same fucking margins of error.

    No follow-up reports. No recorded interviews past the mother or roommate. No confirmed sightings. All of them closed within seventy-two hours. Red stamps everywhere. Words that read like excuses.

    "Known history of running away."

    "Low income family—unavailable for follow-up."

    "No credible witnesses."

    She turned to the desk behind her, dropped the folders, and laid them out in a rough arc, splaying the pages beneath the narrow beam of her flashlight. Her breath was coming faster now, fogging up the glass of a framed certificate still hanging above the desk with Delaney’s signature on some outdated award for "community service excellence."

    Agatha scoffed under her breath. It came out bitter.

    She snapped open her phone with shaking hands and started taking pictures. Page by page. File by file. The shutter sound was muted, but it still sounded too loud in the stillness.

    She flinched at the creak of a pipe overhead.

    Agatha kept going, flashlight pinned between her shoulder and cheek, thumb working fast over the camera roll. The light flickered once, and her stomach turned.

    She could feel herself running out of time thanks to that tightness in her chest. The electric thrum of adrenaline under her skin. The weight of risk pressing against the base of her skull like a warning.

    The detective skimmed one of the folders.

    Same lack of substance, but this time, at the very bottom of the file—barely visible, hidden behind a clipped status report—was a signed clearance form. Two signatures. Both scribbled fast.

    J. Delaney W. Maximoff Her blood ran colder than the air outside as she stared at the names, then at the timeline printed above them.

    Closed. Forwarded to Federal—No Further Action Required.

    No investigation or warning but conveniently closure stamped with two names that had no right to be on the same goddamn page.

    Agatha’s chest ached with the force of helplessness and a furious grief sitting behind her sternum like a second heartbeat. The pile of missing women was growing.

    She lifted up her hoodie halfway and tucked four folders inside. The sharp corners dug into her ribs, and she didn’t care.

    Agatha grabbed her phone again, snapped one last photo of an overhead of the desk, the files, the layout and killed the flashlight. The dark swallowed the room again, colder now.

    Her heartbeat was in her ears. Every sound felt magnified as her boots against the tile, the soft rustle of paper inside her jacket, the way the door handle cooled under her palm like it remembered what she’d just done. She slipped back into the hall and pulled the door shut behind her. Agatha moved fast, but with precision. Every step was measured and soundless except for the subtle scrape of her boots against the tile. Not running. That would be sloppy.

    Her breath came steady through her nose, the burn of adrenaline still pressed high in her chest, just beneath her collarbones. The files zipped inside her hoodie felt heavier now, like they’d grown weight the moment she stepped into the hallway as if paper could know it carried consequences. Down the final stretch of hallway, the air turned colder as she neared the outside. Agatha could see the faint red glow of the exit sign above the east side door. Her steps lengthened without speeding up, her heels whispering over linoleum. The air felt tighter here, like it knew she wasn’t supposed to be walking this direction, not at this hour. The building didn’t try to stop her; it just watched.

    She reached the door with a practiced hand, her fingers curling around the handle, cold biting at her knuckles even through the fabric of her sleeve. Pushing the door open, she stepped outside and the cold hit like a wall— sharp and immediate.

    Her boots landed on gravel that was damp from the earlier rain, and her shoulders rose slightly against the wind. The night had gone even more brittle then when she walked in.

    Then a voice cut clean through the silence.

    “Out a little late, aren’t we, Detective Harkness?”

    Agatha froze. Every muscle in her body drew tight with surprise, but with restraint. She didn’t turn right away, just let the door shut behind her with a soft click and exhaled once through her nose, slow and sharp. When she finally looked up, Wanda stood across the lot, half-silhouetted against the dull glow of a streetlamp, her figure outlined by the gleam of chrome and black paint. She was leaning, almost lazily, against the passenger side of a sleek black Mercedes, one heel crossed elegantly over the other, arms folded, expression unreadable.

    Agatha shook her head and kept walking.

    Wanda pushed off the car with a practiced ease, the motion fluid and graceful, like she’d rehearsed it. She let her eyes drift slowly over Agatha’s form as she approached, looking over the woman’s hood that was pulled up halfway, collar damp, boots scuffed and trailing grit.

    The agent tilted her head slightly, and the corner of her mouth curved. Not a smile but an expression that sat somewhere between amusement and dismissal.

    “You really have that scrappy thing going for you,” she said, voice like silk wrapped around a knife. “And breaking into buildings, I see. I suppose some habits never make it through academy training.”

    The implication wasn’t subtle, but Wanda didn’t care for subtlety when she thought she was winning.

    Agatha didn’t stop, the sound of her boots echoed low and even across the lot.

    “I’d say it’s charming,” Wanda added, her voice trailing after her, light and cutting, “but I’m not sure how long ‘charming’ holds up under a federal audit.”

    Agatha’s lips twitched, not quite a smile, not quite a snarl. She let the words roll off her back like she’d never heard them.

    She didn’t get more than five steps past the car before Wanda moved again into her path like she’d always meant to be. There was no sudden lunge or posturing, but a measured pivot and a single step forward, enough to slide herself directly into Agatha’s line without ever touching her. Agatha stopped.

    Her boots ground to a halt against the damp pavement, shoulders tensing as her weight shifted forward. Fight or flight compressed into one clenched breath behind her teeth. The cold stung at her fingers, but the heat rising from beneath her hoodie felt sharp enough to scald.

    Wanda tilted her chin slightly, her auburn hair catching faint light from the lamppost above the lot, and for a second, it looked like a halo made of glass shards. Her voice, when it came, was low enough that it forced you to listen.

    “Let me guess,” she said, and the disdain bled through every syllable. “Broke in. Dug through the captain’s locked drawers. Smuggled out your little rebellion like some back-alley vigilante.”

    She let it hang there, looking Agatha up and down once more—the hoodie damp along the collar, her boots stained with city sludge, hair pulled back in a way that had clearly been done with her hands instead of a mirror. The exhaustion still clung to her face, barely hidden beneath grit. “It’s almost charming,” Wanda added, her tone so sweet it curdled, “how beneath you this all is.”

    Agatha’s fists twitched in her pockets but didn’t speak.

    Wanda stepped in again, like a closing gate.

    One heel forward, subtle but firm, her coat brushing lightly against Agatha’s arm. She didn’t raise her voice.

    “And what happens when Rio finds out?” she said, voice honeyed and delicate. “You think she’ll applaud your loyalty? Or hate you for dragging her into another mess?”

    The words sliced cleaner than they should have.

    Agatha’s jaw set, throat tightening.

    “She’s already breaking under the pressure,” Wanda murmured, slow and deliberate. “You really want to be the final nail?”

    The detective didn’t respond but she could hear her pulse behind her ears. Wanda’s gaze stayed steady and impossibly calm. A stillness that came from always knowing exactly where she stood in the food chain. “You think she’d risk her spotless record for someone like you?” she continued, voice a breath now, low and cruel. “A woman who doesn’t even wear a clean shirt to work?”

    The jab landed. Wanda’s eyes flicked to the stretched collar of Agatha’s hoodie, the faint stain of old coffee along the hem, the edge of a police- issue t-shirt barely visible beneath.

    Agatha scoffed, low and bitter, the sound pushing past her throat before she could bite it down. “Right,” she said, finally breaking the silence. “Because tailored blazers and perfume make you morally superior. Forgot that was part of the job description.”

    Wanda’s brow arched, lips parting in a mock pout. “No need to get defensive.”

    The cold wrapped around them, thick and unmoving, but she stood like it didn’t touch her. Perfect posture, lips just barely curved. The wind tugged faintly at the hem of her coat, but she smoothed it once with the back of her hand, glancing down as if brushing off an invisible fleck of dust. Her fingers moved with the kind of control that was practiced, ritualistic. Everything about her was intentional, and that was the most dangerous part. When she looked back up, she tilted her head slightly, the edge of her smile sharpening in the shadow.

    “Rio’s been talking in her sleep again,” she said softly. “Always did when she was overwhelmed.”

    Agatha didn’t move.

    Wanda leaned in closer, like she was opening the door to a memory you had no right to enter and made you watch anyway.

    “I used to help her with that,” she went on. “Back in Quantico. She never slept much, not when the pressure got bad. Rio used to carry it in her hands by always rubbing her thumb against her pen during debriefs like she was trying to keep herself from splintering.”

    Agatha’s chest rose once, then stilled.

    Wanda’s voice lowered further, personal. “We had… routines.” The words hung like a trap in the air, and Wanda let them linger just long enough to bloom.

    “You ever notice how she craves control?” she asked, voice treading into something sweet and unkind. “How safe she feels when someone else sets the rules for her?”

    She stepped back half a pace, just enough to let her weight shift, heels silent against the wet ground.

    “You think she chose you because it’s real?” Wanda asked, brushing a speck of lint from her coat sleeve with deliberate elegance. “Or because it’s rebellion?”

    Agatha’s fists clenched inside her pockets as blue eyes stayed locked on Wanda’s, unblinking.

    “You know,” she said, voice so calm it burned, “for someone with such prestige and class you sure like standing in alleys and talking shit.” Her boots shifted on the slick pavement.

    “Move.”

    Wanda didn’t, she just shook her head, as if considering the request like it was optional. The smile never left her mouth, but her eyes went sharp to a flash of something meaner buried beneath all that polish. Then came the final blow.

    “You’re in over your head, Detective.” Wanda said, voice soft but soaked in superiority. “But go ahead, keep pretending this is about justice. Or love. Or whatever it is you’re calling this teenage compulsion now.” She walked back and leaned one shoulder back against the car, perfectly poised again.

    “Keep pushing like this, and you won’t just lose your badge,” she said, almost tender. “You’ll lose her.”

    Wanda didn’t linger. She stepped back into the car with the same impossible grace she arrived in—heels silent on wet pavement, coat folding cleanly as she slid behind the wheel. The engine purred to life like it recognized her, and the sleek black Mercedes pulled away from the lot without so much as a stutter.

    Agatha stood still.

    The cold seeped deeper now, threading through her hoodie, into her sleeves, down the back of her neck like ice water. The lot was empty again, wind curling at the edges, stirring bits of trash near the gutter. She didn’t move even as her breath kept fogging the air in front of her in steady clouds. Wanda was gone but she lingered like a bad taste.

    Agatha’s fingers curled tighter around the files beneath her hoodie, the paper soft and worn now against her ribs, like it had been pressed there long enough to memorize her shape. She swallowed hard, eyes dropping to her boots—mud-crusted, rain-slicked, the kind of soaked that wouldn’t dry for hours. Her laces were uneven, she didn’t remember tying them. Her throat felt dry.

    Beneath the chill and weight of everything she didn’t say, something stung. A drop of warmth at the corner of her eye froze with the air biting her face. It wasn’t the cold that did it.

    It was the memory of Rio’s voice, her laugh tucked behind soft smiles, the way her eyes always searched for something gentle in Agatha even when she didn’t believe it was there.

    Wanda had said it like a promise; You’ll lose her.

    Agatha blinked hard, the sting didn’t go away.

    The woman stayed there for a second longer, just standing in the empty lot, letting the dark settle back around her, the wind brushing past like it didn’t know what to say either.

    Then, she turned and walked into the night with that single tear, sharp from the cold and tracing a line down her cheek like it refused to fall quietly. She didn’t wipe it away.

    She just kept walking.

    Chapter 14: Cold Water

    Chapter Notes to everyone who doubted her, rio would like her apologies written in triplicate, notarized, and hand-delivered with a little black coffee. See the end of the chapter for more notes The trees stretched tall and brittle around them, their bare branches threading into a gray sky that hadn’t seen sun in days. Mud squelched underfoot, the kind that sucked at your boots like it wanted to keep you there. Cold air clung to everything like your coat, your lungs, your silence; and the forest floor was soaked with the remnants of three days' worth of rain. Puddles pooled in the hollows of roots, reflecting dull smudges of the overcast sky.

    Rio stood still at the edge of a narrow trail, the weight of her navy FBI- Wanda-issued jacket heavy across her shoulders, already damp around the collar. The fabric was flecked with stray droplets and leaf matter, the hem stained where it brushed against the undergrowth. Her boots were caked in mud, and each step left behind the shape of her heel like a temporary imprint that the rain would wash away by morning.

    Behind her, the trees creaked as somewhere in the distance a crow called once, sharp and high. But otherwise, the woods were all too quiet. She adjusted the strap of her sidearm, breath curling faint in the cold as she exhaled. Every part of her body was tense, locked into its own corners of restraint. Her hands stayed in her jacket pockets, and her teeth had been clenched for so long they had begun to ache.

    It was brutally cold.

    Not the kind of cold that passed you by, but the kind that settled into bone, wrapped itself around your spine, and didn’t let go. An icy, windless chill that sank into the seams of your clothes and made your muscles ache. Chicago’s classic cold in late November, where the air didn’t move much but still bit at every inch of exposed skin.

    The women walked in silence with the trail wound through the edge of a state-managed forest preserve, but there was nothing managed about it now. Just wild brush and tangle, gray fog threading between tree trunks like smoke. A low mist hovered across the ground in some places, clinging to dips in the earth like it had nowhere else to go.

    Up ahead, the woods broke slightly, the trees thinning just enough to give way to a wider clearing. The ground here was worse, all uneven and soggy, churned from old tires or maybe deer. Rusted wire fencing sagged at the edge, half-buried beneath the muck, and a collapsed hunting stand leaned sideways against a trunk like it had given up the fight months ago. They paused at the clearing’s edge.

    No prints in the mud nor birds or cigarette butts or broken branches; just the wind beginning to pick up and curling around them with small icy teeth, sharp enough to cut through denim and fleece.

    The air here was still, but instead of peaceful, it felt rather suffocating. Above it all, the trees stood silent and thin, their bark slick with old rain, their shadows long in the gray afternoon.

    Wanda broke the silence first.

    Her voice came low and steady, the kind of cadence trained into you after too many years in briefing rooms and incident response calls. “Local report came in yesterday. Civilian—mid-40s, works maintenance at the water treatment plant two miles east.”

    She didn’t look at Rio as she spoke, just scanned the tree line with eyes that moved like searchlights, methodical and exact. “The man claimed he saw someone matching Lisa Moreno’s description walking along the access road. He also mentioned that she looked disoriented with no coat.” She paused briefly then continued, businesslike. “That was almost a month ago. Weather would’ve made survival out here near impossible without gear or shelter.”

    The silence crept back in between her sentences. Rio said nothing, her eyes trained forward on the woods as if she could will the truth out of the fog. Her shoulders were squared and she didn’t move.

    Wanda moved beside her. The navy of her jacket barely rustled, as she stood with her collected efficiency, that grace that always felt rehearsed. “There’s a split in the trail about fifty yards ahead,” she continued. “East breaks off toward the quarry. West loops around through the deeper part of the preserve. My guess is if she was moving on instinct, she followed the sound of the water.”

    She paused, letting the moment stretch as her gloved fingers adjusted the strap of her field bag. “Assuming, of course, she was ever actually here and not just some ghost civilians like to see when the missing posters get too familiar.”

    Still, Rio didn’t speak, her eyes remained pinned ahead.

    Wanda looked at her sideways with a glance and her tone softened slightly. “I know the timing’s not ideal,” she said. “But we can’t afford to overlook fringe leads, especially not this late in the pattern.”

    Her eyes scanned the tree line again, assessing.

    Then, as if bored by the silence—or perhaps emboldened by it—Wanda tilted her head, her voice dipping thinner. She took a step closer, her boots squelching faintly in the mud. “Why so quiet, Rio?”

    She said it like a tease, it was anything but light.

    Her eyes drifted lazily to Rio’s profile, studying the set of brown eyes along with the faint line between her brows. “You used to talk more than this,” she added, a small smirk curving the corner of her mouth. “Even when we were elbow-deep in worse terrain. Remember the Everglades? You wouldn’t shut up about the mosquitoes.”

    She gave a soft, low laugh, false and fond all at once. “God, you hated the humidity. Always adjusting your sleeves like you thought they gave a damn about comfort.”

    Rio didn’t reply as her focus remained locked on the tree line, like the wind might part the branches and hand her an answer if she stared hard enough. Wanda stepped closer so that their shoulders nearly brushed. “You don’t have to pretend with me,” she said, quieter now. “Not out here. I know how your silences work and that little storm cloud look you get when you’re running too many calculations at once.” She smiled faintly. “You still do that thing with your fingers, you know. Curling them into your gloves like you need to ground yourself. Always was your tell.”

    Her hand drifted up in a slow, almost absent motion like she was thinking about reaching for Rio’s arm and decided against it at the last second. “I worry about you sometimes,” Wanda murmured, with the kind of condescension that wore a bit of care. “The way you’ve… shifted since we got here. You used to be so attentive but now I catch you staring out windows like some brooding novelist. It’s not very you.”

    The smirk came back, sharper this time. “Unless it is and someone’s been teaching you bad habits.” She didn’t say the name, but it hung thick in the space between them anyway. Wanda didn’t need to say Agatha to make her point.

    “Has she gotten under your skin that much?” she asked, tone teasing and with her usual edge.

    “God, it’s so obvious. The way you flinch when she’s in the room.” She took one step forward, mud dragging slightly under her heel. “Tell me something, Rio,” Wanda said softly. “Is this how it starts? Mishandling a case because someone looks at you like you hung the moon and the stars?” Her brows lifted just a touch. “Does she make you forget who you are?” The auburn haired woman was smiling now as if she’d already decided how this played out and was just waiting for Rio to catch up. There was something triumphant in the curve of her lips as if silence meant she’d won. Rio didn’t answer at first, she wanted to let the silence stretch between them like frostbitten wire, enough to sting if you weren’t careful. The trees around them didn’t move and even the wind had grown cautious, threading between branches like it didn’t want to interrupt.
