    Finally, she turned enough to glance over her shoulder, eyes meeting Wanda’s with a calm that didn’t quite reach her eyes.

    “If there was a sighting, we should be looking for footprints. Broken branches, or any sign she came through this way.” Her voice was steady with pure protocol. “Not standing still waiting for her to walk out of the trees.”

    The look in her eyes was flat and said she wasn’t interested in conversation; at least not the kind Wanda wanted, anyway.

    The younger woman moved and her shoulder brushed past Wanda’s, not aggressive but undeniably intentional. A small shift of power in the quiet while the contact left a whisper of heat in its wake, quickly eaten by the cold.

    Rio’s boots hit the ground, purposefully stepping away from the worst of the mud and into firmer brush where pine needles crunched beneath her soles. Her movements were clean with legs carrying her deeper into the woods using the kind of precise, clipped rhythm drilled into her years ago, before everything got messy.

    Branches curved above like ribs, filtering the pale afternoon light into fractured patterns across the forest floor. Patches of silver frost clung to roots and fallen logs, untouched by sun, and the smell of wet moss attached to decaying leaves thickened with every step.

    Rio didn’t wait to see if Wanda followed.

    Her eyes swept from left to right, cataloging broken twigs, shallow impressions in the dirt, and even a scrap of fabric caught on a thorn that turned out to be nothing.

    Rio crouched low near a cluster of flattened brush, gloved fingers ghosting over the damp ground. A line of prints, half-formed and mostly ruined by the last night’s freeze, led nowhere in particular, but, maybe they were something.

    “You mentioned she was spotted heading northeast,” Rio murmured, more to herself than to Wanda. “Just past the service road? That would put her here, maybe twenty minutes before dusk, depending on when the sighting came in.”

    Her voice was the pure cadence of someone fitting puzzle pieces into place. Her eyes didn’t leave the trail as she straightened, brushing her hands together.

    “She would’ve been cold with the visibility already low,” she added. “If she was being followed, she wouldn’t have come this far without leaving more of a trail.”

    Wanda, a few paces behind, nodded. “We’re close to an old maintenance shed that’s in the Northwest quadrant. Records show it hasn’t been used since ’07, so if she knew the terrain or had been here before, she might’ve aimed for shelter.”

    Rio gave a clipped hum in agreement, pushing a low-hanging branch aside as she pressed forward.

    “She wouldn’t wander this far without purpose,” she said. “If it’s not Lisa, it’s someone who knew how to move through this without panicking. No sign of struggle, no blood, no drag marks.”

    Wanda moved up beside her now, her breath clouding faintly in the freezing air. “So, she came here willingly.”

    Rio nodded once, scanning the treeline. “Or someone made it seem like she did.”

    A silence settled between them with just the crunch of boots on frozen brush accompanying the faint groan of wind threading through brittle pine needles. Both women stood poised, observing opposite edges of the trail the way they’d been trained to move before everything complicated who they were to each other.

    “This ground’s been disturbed recently,” Wanda said, finally stepping forward, her voice even again, all Bureau. “Tire marks by the ridge are deep enough to suggest heavy weight, maybe a van. Could’ve been dropped off?”

    “Or taken,” Rio replied, eyes narrowing.

    The brunette stepped forward, her boots cutting clean through the frosted underbrush. She paused near the ridge, brown eyes tracing the shallow impressions in the dirt—the indentation of a tire track that curved too tightly to be accidental.

    “We’ll need to bring in a physical anthropologist,” she said, voice decisive. “Someone from the local field office with terrain experience. If she passed through here, we’ll need gait analysis to tell us of any variation in stride or pressure shifts. Especially in this terrain.”

    She glanced back over her shoulder at Wanda, not quite meeting her eyes. “And if there’s submersion anywhere in nearby ponds, culverts or runoff sites—we’ll need a forensic unit with water recovery training and add soil testing too. If a vehicle stopped here, they might’ve left behind transfer materials we can lift.”

    Wanda’s expression didn’t change, and she gave an approving nod. “I’ll call it in. I do want a K9 unit sweeping the north side before the temperature drops again. We’ve got maybe two, three hours of viable light left.” “Assuming snow doesn’t hit later,” Rio added, checking her watch. “If it does, the compression artifacts in the ground will be compromised by morning.”

    They moved in sync again for a moment, two silhouettes in matching navy jackets, shoulders squared to the cold, backlit by a sky so overcast it felt like walking beneath steel.

    They stood side by side beneath the pale expanse of cloud-thick sky, their breath fogging in short bursts. The woods held a faint groan of wind through wet branches and the slow drip of meltwater falling from limbs that hadn’t quite yet frozen over.

    Wanda moved next to her, a breath slower than Rio. Then she turned toward her, a gloved hand reached out and rested lightly against Rio’s shoulder, fingers brushing the shoulder seam of her jacket. It wasn’t a correction but light contact.

    “You know,” Wanda said, her voice low, nearly warm, “it really is like old times.”

    Wanda leaned in enough as her fingers moved, brushing the wind-swept strands of Rio’s hair behind her ear with slow precision. “You and me out here,” she added, smiling faintly. “Reading terrain like scripture. Calling shots without saying them out loud.”

    Her hand lingered a second longer before she let it fall. Rio turned, slowly, the mud sucking faintly at her heel as she pivoted to face her. Wanda stood relaxed as if the cold didn’t touch her, and she belonged there with the trees bowing around them like silent sentries. Rio’s eyes lifted calmly and locked onto Wanda’s.

    She met the kind of green that didn’t soften even in shadow. She remembered thinking once years ago that Wanda’s eyes held quiet storms. Now, she just saw calculation.

    Rio observed her, taking in the neat collar and clean sweep of auburn hair tucked behind one ear, the perfect posture even in the damp chill of late November. Wanda always looked curated, even in the woods. Even in mud as if no matter how far they walked into the brush, she’d never let the world muss her edges.

    Wanda smiled, the corners of her lips lifted with ease, like she was used to being watched, and always passed inspection.

    Rio didn’t look away as Wanda’s hand found its way again to rest at her shoulder, her fingers light and deliberate, but Rio didn’t flinch. Her eyes stayed locked on hers, green meeting brown, hard angles against measured calm.

    There was no warmth in her eyes.

    She studied Wanda’s face quietly, the faint tension at her brow and the practiced tilt of her smile. Lashed lowering soft enough to look unbothered, sharp enough to veil intent.

    Wanda’s fingers flexed slightly at Rio’s shoulder, with a bit of pressure to feel intentional. She stepped in the smallest fraction closer, as if proximity could coax meaning from silence. The wind tugged at the hem of her coat, strands of her hair brushing her cheek in a red-gold flicker against the gray, but her smile didn’t waver. It deepened.

    “You used to look at me like that,” she said softly, voice just loud enough to cut through the hush of trees around them. “Back in Quantico when you thought I didn’t notice.”

    Her thumb moved in a barely-there motion across Rio’s jacket seam— intimate, practiced. Her words dripped with remembered closeness, each syllable warm and round like she was wrapping Rio’s name in velvet, even if she didn’t speak it.

    “You had that such a fire in you”, she went on, green eyes holding Rio’s. “Always standing just close enough to burn but never letting anyone close enough to catch the flame.” She tilted her head, hair falling perfectly into place. “Except me.”

    Wanda’s voice lowered again, becoming gentler, she was like honey left too long in the jar.

    “We worked well together. You remember that, don’t you?” she asked. “You and me, always the same pace. Same instincts. You used to tell me I could read your thoughts before you had them.”

    Her fingers trailed slightly down Rio’s arm, slow enough to be suggestive, soft enough to be deniable.

    “And I always did,” Wanda said with a breath of a smile, tilting her chin upward just slightly. “Still do.”

    She leaned in, but not enough to breach the space fully, just enough that Rio could smell the faint floral perfume she still wore, expensive and exact. The kind that was designed to stay on skin for a while.

    “You don’t have to pretend with me,” she murmured, voice silken now. “Not here. Not after everything.”

    The woods held their breath. Rain dripped from the trees in soft intervals, and the distant hum of wind moved through the branches like a whisper trying not to be heard.

    Rio let out a soft, incredulous laugh, bitter at its edges like something finally cracked under the weight of being played with. She turned her head, just slightly, breaking the line of Wanda’s gaze. Her lips twisted into a sneer smile before she looked back with her eyes stern now; alive in a way Wanda hadn’t seen in a long time.

    “I’m not here because I want to be, Wanda,” Rio said, tone cold. “Let’s not pretend this was ever my call.”

    Her words hit, carrying weight under every syllable.

    “You think I asked for this reassignment?” she continued, stepping back enough that Wanda’s fingers slipped from her shoulder, the touch falling away like it never belonged. “You think I wanted to be dragged into your little jurisdictional match just so you could play memory lane in the middle of a crime scene?”

    Wanda’s smile faltered for a second, but it was enough to reveal the hairline fracture beneath the polish. Rio saw it and for once didn’t look away. “I didn’t come here to reminisce,” she went on. “I didn’t come here for you.”

    She paused then, letting the silence settle between them like a blade balanced on edge.

    “I’m here for the case. For Lisa, and the girls the Bureau keeps forgetting, or reclassifying, or sweeping under someone’s rug because they don’t fit the narrative.”

    She took a step forward, aggressive and rooted in finally having enough. “And Agatha,” she added, eyes locked now, tone firmer. “She’s my partner. My real partner. You don’t get to come in here and rewrite that just because you miss being in control of me.”

    The name rested heavy between them.

    Wanda blinked once with that calm, always-unbothered veneer flickering— briefly—but it signified that the hit had landed.

    Rio didn’t flinch.

    “She’s the one who’s been doing the work and asking the right questions. Agatha doesn’t need to manipulate anyone to stand beside me.” Wanda’s hands flexed at her sides as Rio shook her head giving a long breath into the cold, white curling in front of her face. “I don’t owe you anything,” she said. “Not anymore.”

    The smile Wanda had been holding shattered like porcelain. Although still pretty, the cracks were visible now as her fingers curled at her side like she was deciding whether to reach again or ball them into fists. “You’re lucky I even chose you for this,” she scoffed. “Do you know how many names were floated for this assignment? How many eyes were on this case? But I made sure it was you.”

    Her chin lifted, defiant. Her green eyes shimmered sharply with something darker, desperation now masked as superiority.

    “I pulled you into this, Rio,” she said. “I made sure they remembered who you were. That they saw your record, not your… fallout.” The last word was clipped, bitten off like it tasted bad in her mouth. “Without me, you’d still be pushing paper in a windowless box waiting for scraps from a department that doesn’t even know what to do with someone like you.” She took a step forward, into Rio’s space again, the mud slick under her heels but never breaking her balance. Wanda never stumbled. “And instead of gratitude,” she continued, “you’re wasting your time playing house with some… greasy, half-functioning burnout with authority issues and a persecution complex.”

    The insult landed with a venomous snap, that precise cut of tone meant to strip Agatha down to nothing.

    Rio’s eyes darkened, but Wanda didn’t stop once her voice climbed, not in volume, but intensity. She was trying to make Rio see something and trying to drag her out of whatever imagined fog she thought she was in. “You don’t see it yet, but you will,” she said, words falling harder now, faster. “You belong with me, Rio. We make sense. Quantico. D.C. Richmond. The boardroom, the field—fuck, even your breakdowns used to be manageable when I was there to catch you.”

    Wanda’s mouth curved into something almost pitying. “This little phase you’re in, it’s a fantasy. A distraction. You’ve always needed someone to sharpen your edges, to keep you from unraveling.”

    Her voice dropped low again, syrupy and cold.

    “That’s not her,” she said. “That will never be her. You’re not built for chaos. She’s going to drag you under, so snap out of it.” The words fell heavy in the air, like some final verdict. Wanda stood still, eyes narrowed now, watching Rio for a reaction, any reaction. Something that said her grip wasn’t lost yet. That she could still bend the steel back into shape if she just pressed in hard enough and reminded her who she used to be.

    In one step, she closed the space between them. The cold pressed in on all sides, but she didn’t seem to feel it. Her hands came up and cupped Rio’s face like she had every right to. Her thumbs brushed along Rio’s cheekbones, the touch deceptively tender.

    “You belong with me,” Wanda murmured, her voice desperate and cutting just the same. “You always have.”

    Her green eyes searched Rio’s face looking for the girl she used to mold, the one who answered to her name and wore structure like a second skin. “We were unstoppable,” she breathed, her fingers splayed just behind Rio’s ears, her grip deceptively soft. “You and I… we could be again. If you’d just stop pretending this sad little detour is your real life.” Her voice dropped, coaxing.

    “Come back with me,” she whispered. “To the Bureau, where you belong. Where people like you actually matter.”

    But something in Rio’s chest cracked, irreparable.

    The words hit harder than Wanda could’ve known. Not because of their content, but because of their precision.

    Rio had heard them all before, she’d spent the last two weeks suffocating under a mountain of Wanda’s reports, red tape, and assigned briefings that stripped her of every inch of identity she’d fought to rebuild. Now Wanda stood there, belittling the only good thing Rio had dared to let herself want again.

    Agatha.

    Her precinct.

    Her goddamn self.

    Wanda’s perfect fingers were on her face, like Rio belonged to her. Pretending she was a project unfinished, a career salvaged, a command waiting to be obeyed.

    Rio’s breathing grew shallow. Rapid.

    She didn’t look away, but the pulse at her throat kicked up, fast and hot. The noise of the woods dimmed. The cold felt further away now, and her mind began to spiral—fast.

    Agatha’s laugh in the passenger seat. The case files. Wanda’s perfume. The red folders. The boardroom. The insults.

    The silence.

    Agatha’s eyes the last time she looked at her.

    The tension broke like a snapped wire.

    “Stop,” Rio rasped.

    But Wanda didn’t. Her thumbs brushed gently beneath her jaw. “You’re not yourself,” she said desperate, coaxing. “You’re exhausted. Let me help—” “Don’t.”

    Rio’s voice came louder this time. Her hands came up pushing hard against Wanda’s wrists, breaking the contact.

    “I’m not here because of you.”

    The wind surged around them like it felt the rupture.

    “You think you get to waltz back into my life, throw out protocols like leashes, insult the people I—” She caught herself.

    A tear, sharp from the cold, pooled at the corner of her eye and she blinked it away fast. Furious.

    “You don’t get to call what I have now a detour,” Rio spat. “Agatha is my partner, and unlike you she doesn't need me to play a part. I don't want to hear you say anything about her again.”

    Wanda stepped back visibly thrown, not in surrender but in recoil. Sharp and offended.

    Disbelief flickered behind her eyes as an old, wounded pride, the kind that didn’t understand how dare could exist in someone else’s mouth. That polished calm she wore so well was gone.

    “Wow,” she said, voice tight with venom. “There it is.”

    She took a step back to observe, green eyes cutting over Rio like a scalpel. “You know what?” she said, a dry laugh breaking on her tongue. “I spent weeks thinking you were just disoriented. Stuck in your little precinct purgatory, chasing ghosts and playing dress-up in that beat cop costume.” Her words started slow, then they sharpened.

    “But this pathetic, self-righteous, bleeding heart act?” Wanda sneered. “You’re not disoriented, Rio. You’re just broken and too fucking cowardly to admit it.”

    Rio’s breath hitched as her hands were still at her sides, anger cursing through them like they didn’t know whether to form fists or fold inward. Wanda saw it and leaned in.

    “I used to think you had potential,” she hissed. “God, I fought for you back in Quantico. Everyone said you didn’t have the edge. But I told them no— she just needs someone to show her how to lead.”

    She shook her head, eyes glittered like frostbite.

    “You’re nothing but a washed-up rule-follower with a badge that doesn’t mean shit outside a city you didn’t even want to transfer to. You cling to mediocrity like it’s a lifeline, but we both know what it really is.” She took another step forward, brutal. “It’s the only thing left you haven’t fucked up.”

    Rio’s lips parted to meet her speeding breath. A spike of cold lanced through her chest as her legs felt locked in place.

    “Agatha?” Wanda spat the name like it burned her mouth. “She’s not your partner. She’s your distraction. A moody, underqualified burnout in a thrift store flannel who makes you feel brave for once in your goddamn life.” Rio’s vision swam.

    “You think she’s gonna hold your hand when this all goes south? You think she gives a fuck about what happens when your little rebellion falls apart? News flash, sweetheart—she’ll run. People like her always do.” Wanda’s voice dropped to a hiss.

    “You’ll be left with nothing. Just like before.”

    Rio’s breath broke—ragged and too loud in the freezing air. Her lungs weren’t cooperating anymore, expanding too fast, too shallow. The wind felt distant. Her heart was pounding against her ribs like it wanted out. She tasted salt on her lip and didn’t know if it was from the cold or her own body breaking down.

    Wanda didn’t stop.

    “You’re only here because of me. You were made by me. And now you want to act like that life didn’t exist?”

    She stepped close again this time with no tenderness.

    “Let’s not pretend this little sad-sack precinct gave you purpose. All it gave you was distance from what you did. From who you are.”

    The trees blurred at the edges. The cold was still there, but she couldn’t feel it, only her heartbeat. Her breath. The rush of adrenaline biting at the backs of her knees. Her fingers were numb, even curled into fists now. She was spiraling, and Wanda could see it.

    She liked it.

    “You’ll never be anything more than what I carved out of you,” Wanda whispered, stepping even closer, voice full of cruel knowing. “No matter how many rules you hide behind.”

    Rio’s eyes finally blinked. Yet it didn’t break the spiral because the one voice screaming loudest in her head wasn’t Wanda’s.

    It was her own.

    She’s right. If something didn’t snap soon, it was going to swallow her. Wanda let out a dry, humorless laugh, shaking her head like she couldn’t believe what she was hearing. “You really think any of this is about your illusion of partnership?” she spat. “You’re pathetic.”

    She took a slow step forward, slicing through the wet ground like it didn’t dare stop her.

    “Wasting your time with that emotionally stunted beat cop, pretending she sees you, like she gets you? It’s embarrassing, Rio.”

    Another step.

    “She doesn’t respect you, doesn’t even understand you, and you know what?”

    Wanda’s voice dropped to a whisper; teeth bared in something that wasn’t a smile.

    “You’ve always been an easy fuck up, I mean, ever since...Santiago died.” That name was a sharp blade that slashed directly through Rio’s chest, immediately making her stumble back, breath catching in her throat. Wanda didn’t let up.

    “You needed someone to take your leash the second he stopped waving goodbye and bled out because of you.”

    Rio froze.

    Wanda’s eyes narrowed, cruel and gleaming.

    “So, you let me, because you needed someone to follow. Someone else to blame.”

    Her voice turned venomous. “You weren’t demoted because of me, Rio. You were demoted because you’re a fucking mess without someone telling you what to do.”

    She leaned in, just slightly.

    “Just ask your brother. Oh wait—”

    She smiled.

    “He’s dead.”

    Rio didn’t move at first.

    The cold wrapped tight around her ribs, but it wasn’t the weather that made her tremble. It was everything else. The way Wanda said his name like it was leverage, like Santiago had been a tool in her game.

    Her eyes were burning before she realized they were wet. The first tear hit her cheekbone, hot and unwanted, streaking down through the wind-chilled flush of her face.

    Then another.

    Her chest heaved once, then again. Her own body was betraying her. “You don’t get to say his name,” she said, her voice hoarse, almost cracked. “You don’t get to use him against me.”

    When she turned, the sound of her boots grinding against the wet mud, her body twisting fast like she was finally done with all of it. But she didn’t get far.

    Wanda’s hand shot out with inhuman speed, fingers latching around her wrist like a snare trap. Not graceful at all as she yanked her back with enough force to stagger.

    Rio gasped, stumbling half a step, her breath catching. Wanda’s grip was vice-like—cruel—driving straight into the bone beneath the skin. It was the kind of hold meant to bruise.

    “You ungrateful little bitch,” Wanda hissed, her voice no longer smooth silk, but jagged glass wrapped in fury. “After everything I’ve done for you, this is how you pay me back?”

    Rio winced, her wrist twisting as she tried to pull back, but Wanda held firm.

    “I pulled you out of that wreck. I shielded you when no one else would and tried to cover for you in front of the Bureau. Yet you want to throw it away for what? For some broke-down dyke and shitty police department?” Her eyes were wild now, the green sharp and feral. The mask had slipped completely, exposed rage.

    “You think she’s going to protect you? Love you?” she spat, jerking Rio’s arm again. “She’s going to leave you, just like everyone else. Of course, after you manage to fuck everything up again.”

    Rio’s breath was coming in sharp, fast pulls now. Her face wet with tears, her pulse pounding loud in her ears.

    Wanda leaned in, her face inches from Rio’s, voice like poison in her ear. “You belong to me, Rio. You always have, always will.”

    Rio yanked her wrist back with a force that made Wanda stumble a half-step forward, fingers slipping loose with a hiss. Pain surged up her forearm, but she didn’t care. She was already stepping in, getting in Wanda’s face, eyes blazing with tears.

    “Don’t you ever touch me again.”

    Her voice was low, guttural.

    “Don’t you dare say his name like it means something coming from you. You didn’t protect me, Wanda, you fucking used me like you always do,” she spat, “You fed me that bullshit about how we had to take the risk, and then when it all went to hell, you let me take the fall. You stood there in front of that board, called me reckless, and let them strip me down while you got your goddamn promotion.”

    Wanda flinched.

    “You used his death to climb the ladder. You think I don’t know that? You think I haven’t relived it every single day?”

    Rio’s voice cracked, but she pushed through it. “I’m done with your control, your leash, your fucking guilt trips. I’m not your partner, and I’m not your puppet.”

    Her jaw clenched.

    “That’s why you’re mad, isn’t it? For once in your life, you can’t bend someone’s existence to fit you anymore.”

    She stepped back, eyes glassy but blazing. “You lost, Wanda.” As quickly as Rio had snapped, her back had turned, jacket catching the wind as she stormed through the underbrush. The branches whipped at her thighs, her boots carving deep into the softened ground as she pushed forward.

    Her breaths came short and shuddering every second, but she didn’t try to slow them down.

    The tears welled before she could stop them, hot against the rawness of her cheeks, catching cold air like ice the second they slipped loose. She blinked hard, wiped at her face once with the sleeve of her jacket, but they kept coming anyway. Not soft or graceful, but furious and uncontrollable. They came after too many months of pretending she didn’t feel anything. The kind that burned.

    “Rio!”

    Wanda’s voice cut through the trees behind her like a whip crack, desperate and laced with fury now. That polished tone was gone as her rage and disbelief watched something slip beyond her reach.

    “I’m talking to you!”

    Rio didn’t pause.

    “Don’t you fucking walk away from me!”

    She kept moving. The branches didn’t part cleanly anymore; they tore and scraped at her sleeves. Her boots slipped once on a patch of wet moss, and she caught herself on instinct, breath heaving, chest hollowed out like it had been carved from the inside.

    Wanda’s voice followed, louder.

    “You owe me Rio, you think she knows you like I do? You think she’s gonna stick around when it gets hard?”

    Rio clenched her jaw, wiping at her face again with the back of her hand, not slowing down.

    “Is she gonna be there when they throw your badge in the trash? When Delaney turns on you? When the whole department implodes because of your pet project?”

    The trees were denser now, light breaking through in fractured beams from the sky, weak and dull behind the clouds. Rio’s breaths came faster when the trail curved around a split in the hill. She could see the edge of the road now, the shape of her car faint in the distance through the trees. “Don’t forget who built you,” Wanda spat, voice cracking. “You were nothing before me, and you’ll be nothing after me.”

    Rio didn’t turn around, nor did she scream back like she wanted to. The woman simply gave one last breath that turned to steam in the frozen air, rising past the curve of her jaw like smoke off a dying flame; and she kept walking.

    The sound of her boots fading into the trees.

    Wanda didn’t follow.

    She just stood there, the forest closing in quiet around her, the cold settling deeper into the silence she’d been left with. Rio kept going with her tears still hot, breath still shaking, but for the first time in years, every step she took felt like her own.

    Every step she took up the hill was muffled by wet earth and the dead hush of late November, her boots dragging through mud that clung like guilt. Her legs felt heavy, muscles tight with cold and anger, but she didn’t stop. Not when her wrist throbbed where Wanda had grabbed her, her ears still rang with the sound of Santiago’s name spat like it was a curse. The incline steepened just ahead, the forest thinning out into frostbitten brush and gravel that crunched beneath her steps. Her breath came short now, fogging with each exhale, quickening with the burn rising in her chest. The wind clawed at the edges of her jacket, slipping down her collar, stinging the tracks her tears had carved along her cheeks. The road emerged like a wound between the trees, cracked with old lines of salt from storms past. Her unmarked car sat at the crest of the slope, parked sideways in the gravel turnout, its hood glistening faintly beneath the pale gray sky. The doors were speckled with dried dirt from earlier trails, the tires already half-buried in leaves.

    She didn’t slow down.

    Her boots hit pavement with a dull slap, and for one moment she stood there beside the car, chest rising and falling in ragged rhythm, shoulders hunched beneath the weight of everything Wanda had dredged up. The air was brutally cold, cracking her knuckles and turned breath sharp as glass. Rio’s fingers trembled as she reached for the door handle. Her palm closed around it too tight, her wrist still aching where bruises were already forming under her jacket sleeve.

    She yanked the door open hard enough that it rattled, dropped into the seat, and slammed it shut.

    Inside, it was suffocating. This silence pressed in on your temples and blurred the edges of your thoughts.

    She swallowed, hard, but it caught somewhere in her throat. Her breath came through her nose, then shakier on the exhale. And then she moved fast, almost angry by jamming the key into the ignition.

    The engine turned over with a metallic growl.

    Her foot slammed the gas as tires skidded against wet gravel as she peeled out of the turnout, mud flinging behind her in thick arcs, her car jolting forward with a vengeance that wasn’t about speed, it was about escape. Not being able to breathe in the same air Wanda Maximoff touched. The car surged down the road, and Rio gripped the wheel like it was the only thing keeping her tethered to the world.

    She blinked as tears blurred the headlights. Somewhere, under the roar of the tires, she heard Santiago’s voice again.

    And she broke, the burn in her chest was back now stronger than ever. Her grief had come to visit her again.

    That long-dormant, marrow-deep ache she kept under lock and key. The one she’d buried under Bureau protocol and clean blazers and early morning runs until it stopped trying to crawl out of her throat every time she closed her eyes.

    It was back.

    The image of him flared behind her eyes without warning, smiling, always smiling, eyes just like hers but warmer. Dark hair uneven from a bad haircut days prior, uniform wrinkled at the collar, smile crooked like he was always on the verge of teasing her about something.

    They’d been on the same unit for three years before that mission. Different assignments, usually, but he always waved the same way when they split for the field—two fingers from his temple, a quick flick outward, that quiet little grin like it was all routine.

    He’d waved to her that morning, two fingers off the brow, casual and sure. “Te veo.”

    And she’d said it back that day. “I won’t let you down.”

    He’d winked, then he was gone.

    Operation Bluewater.

    The name alone made her jaw clench. The last mission she took before everything cracked open and bled.

    They were investigating a laundering ring with deep cartel ties, fronted by a logistics firm moving product through Florida into South America. Dangerous, but high reward.

    Santiago, Wanda, and Rio had gone in together. He’d been calm, collected as always; her big brother by two years, steadier than she ever managed to be, and twice as brave. They were trained as a precision unit within the Bureau, two sides of the same coin. He was the quiet anchor; she was the hammer.

    She remembered the warehouse. Concrete walls, corrugated metal doors, the stench of bleach and rubber from the shipment crates. Wanda had been the tactical lead. She was perched in the command vehicle with a radio and a screen full of heat signatures, barking orders like a general. Rio had seen movement. A shape slipping out the back of the building. Not on the blueprints or in the goddamn plan.

    She’d called it in, “Target moving east, unmarked corridor. Advise.” Wanda's voice in her ear: crisp. “Pursue. Take the lead Rio, I’ll reroute backup.”

    He had hesitated. “But protocol says we clear the primary.” Rio remembered looking at him with his brow furrowed, but his stance firm. She’d nodded like she believed it would be fine. Wanda said it would be fine, so Rio decided to take the risk, it had worked out many times before.

    “I got you,” she’d promised.

    She took the corridor, but it was a dead end.

    By the time she doubled back, shots had been fired. Comm lines were static. When she finally reached the main floor again, the team was scattered, the target had vanished, and Santiago was down. His face was pale, and his shirt was blooming red through his vest, eyes already empty. She’d screamed his name. Not into the comms; into the air. Wanda had emerged minutes later, unscathed, flanked by agents who’d never been in the room when she gave the order.

    When the time came to account for the loss, Wanda had signed off on Rio’s insubordination; Said the risk was unsanctioned, and she’d tried to stop her. Rio took the fall and she was demoted. Stripped of her badge with a stained file and a ghost on her shoulders.

    Wanda got promoted and earned a commendation for salvaging the rest of the team. The woman told Rio behind closed doors that it had been a necessary sacrifice. Collateral damage.

    The Bureau called it a tragedy when they sent him home in a flag-draped box and told her that bravery wasn’t always rewarded.

    Rio gritted her teeth so hard her jaw popped. The tears came faster now, falling without grace down her cheeks as the road blurred beneath her. Driving over the speed limit with no care wasn’t something she did often. Her hands were ice on the wheel, and the ache in her chest was no longer grief. It was rage. Self-hate. Guilt.

    She’d believed Wanda and let herself be led by her again and again, even when the leash was made of paper trails and paperwork.

    Wanda had her cornered again between guilt and obligation, with a past that never let her breathe and a future she was too afraid to ask for. Agatha was the only thing that made her feel like she wasn’t drowning. Yet, Rio had let Wanda push her into that boardroom and wrap protocol around her neck like a noose. Ultimately, letting Agatha walk away. The world outside was a smear of wet asphalt and rust-colored trees as she pressed harder on the gas. Rio had no care of where she was going as long as it was forward.

    No more silence. No more leashes.

    She would never let anyone use her brother’s name like that again or let anyone use her grief as leverage. The road opened up ahead, and she didn’t stop.

    The road unfurled before her like a wet ribbon of grief, silvered by the mist and streaked with the ghost of rain that had finally stopped after a week. Rio drove fast but it still didn’t feel like enough. The Crown Vic shuddered on the sharper turns, tires screeching faintly as she pushed it harder, the engine growling like it was begging her to slow down. She didn’t. Her hands clenched tighter around the wheel, knuckles bloodless and trembling, the cold metal biting against her fingers like penance.

    The late afternoon outside was a smear of wet pine and glinting asphalt. Puddles scattered across the road caught her headlights and flared like signal lights, but none of them told her where to go. She wasn’t sure she’d stop even if they did. Her eyes were wide and glassy, the tears that had started in the woods still trailing faintly down her cheeks, drying against the heat of her skin only to be replaced by more.

    The cold inside the car was sharp and Rio hadn’t turned on the heat because she didn’t want it. Rio deserved the sting in her fingers, the ache in her shoulder where Wanda had gripped her like she was still property. Deserved all of it.

    Because the truth was… that day in the woods wasn’t the first time Rio had let herself be pulled too far in the name of something she thought was worth chasing.

    Operation Bluewater had been the last time she allowed herself to be brave. Back then, God, back then she’d been a different woman. Before his name became something she couldn’t say aloud and the weight of loss carved order into her bones. She remembered the way her brother used to laugh when they walked into briefings late, still eating toast and brushing crumbs from their badges. How they’d grin at each other like they were invincible. How he’d never said goodbye, just "I’ll see you."

    They had been on the same task force for years in their unorthodox yet efficient style. Rio had been the one to push boundaries, to question strategy, to suggest alternatives that didn’t sit well with the suits upstairs. But they got results and Wanda had noticed. That had been the beginning of everything—of Rio being groomed, pulled closer, fed just enough attention to keep her obedient and thinking it was respect.

    Bluewater changed that.

    Wanda had stood in front of the Bureau panel like a goddess in a storm, calm and beautiful, bleeding courage and control. She’d watched as the badge was pulled from her chest. As the title was stripped from her name and the only person she had ever truly trusted was lowered into the ground beneath a flag she could no longer look at.

    From that moment on, she vowed she would never break protocol again. Never let herself be led by gut or feeling or foolishness. Never let herself be brave.

    Because bravery had killed her brother.

    She choked on a breath now, her windshield streaked with rain, fingers so hard around the wheel that the leather groaned under her grip. The life she’d chosen since filled with order and structure, was pushing her farther away from the only thing that had started to feel like home again. Agatha.

    She didn’t know when it had started. Maybe the first time she saw her roll her eyes and call bullshit in front of a captain. Or the first time she sat beside her in silence after a bad lead, shoulder to shoulder, breath shared in quiet grief. Possibly just the way Agatha didn’t ask for permission to be herself; she gave herself space to be messy and soft and angry all at once. Rio envied her, she wanted her, and she was losing her.

    She wasn’t sure what hurt more, the memory of her brother’s smile, or the thought of Agatha walking away from her one day with that same quiet finality.

    Maybe she had built her life around control because it was the only thing she had left. Because without it, everything else came loose. But today proved that order didn’t protect her, playing by the rules didn’t keep her from breaking.

    She wanted to pull over and scream into the woods until her voice broke like everything else.

    Rio’s eyes felt raw as her heart ached while trying to outrun a past that would never stop following her. It was all collapsing with a present that was slipping through her fingers no matter how tightly she held on. Rio Vidal, agent, detective, sister, survivor—kept driving like she could make it to the end of her grief if she just didn’t stop.

    Because the moment she did, she wasn’t sure she’d be able to breathe again. The road narrowed into gravel without warning, crunching loud beneath her tires as she pulled off to the shoulder. The lake had been creeping into her periphery for the last few minutes, a long sprawl of slate-grey water framed by gnarled tree lines and skeletal brush. It wasn’t beautiful. Not in the way people always talked about lakes being peaceful or serene. This one looked as cold and tired as she felt; restless and relentless, surface rippling even without wind, like it couldn’t stay still.

    Rio cut the engine. The sudden silence hit like a punch.

    She sat there for a moment with her hands still on the wheel, watching the reflection of the water dance in the windshield. It was late enough that the sky had gone full overcast, and the light filtering through the trees was weak and bruised, casting long blue shadows across the dash. Her breath fogged faintly in the cabin, and her fingers had gone stiff from how tightly she’d been gripping the wheel.

    When she finally moved it was mechanical, door opening, boots crunching down on frost-laced gravel, the cold immediate and biting against her skin. The wind was stronger out here, coming off the water in sharp, cutting bursts that yanked at her open jacket and nipped beneath the collar of her shirt like it had teeth.

    She didn’t button it. She didn’t pull her hood up. She just walked down the short incline until she reached the rocky edge where the gravel met the shoreline. Her boots sank slightly into the wet earth before catching on firmer rock. The water lapped at the stones in small, persistent waves. Not violent, or loud but consistent. The lake had decided a long time ago that it didn’t matter who was watching, it would keep hitting the shore anyway. She stared at it for a long time, her arms crossed over her chest, shoulders hunched against the wind. The tip of her nose burned from the cold; her ears already numb. Her boots were caked in dried mud, the last remnants of the forest still clinging to her, as if the confrontation hadn’t been enough to shake it off.

    The wind pulled at her hair, and for once, she didn’t smooth it down. Her chest ached, not from the cold but from everything else. This place, this cold, empty patch of road beside a lake that didn’t give a damn—this felt like a pause. A breath held too long. And maybe that’s what she needed. Just a place to finally feel it.

    She closed her eyes.

    Operation Bluewater had been three years ago. But it still lived in her like it had happened yesterday. It lived in her spine, in her clenched jaw, in the way her fingers curled into fists when she wasn’t looking. It lived in her nightmares, and in the mornings where she still expected to hear Santiago’s laugh coming from the locker room before briefing. It lived in the silence after Wanda’s praise, when her voice was just a little too sweet and a little too rehearsed.

    Rio lost everything.

    So since, it had been rules. Order, structure, case files, and policy memos and boxes checked in black ink. The illusion of safety hugged by the armor of discipline. If she followed the rules, nothing would hurt her like that again. That was the bargain she made with herself.

    She opened her eyes again, and the lake was still moving. But now, even that was starting to crack as the life she’d built on control was the same one that was tearing her away from what she wanted most. From the person she wanted most.

    Agatha, with her sharp mouth and sharper heart. Who made her laugh when she didn’t want to and made her feel like she could still be something whole, worthy. The woman saw right through her bullshit and sat with her through silence like she was already enough, even when Rio couldn’t believe it herself.

    Rio had shut her out and had gone with Wanda’s leash over soft eyes. Although she knew it wasn’t without reason, she didn’t know how to stop being the woman who followed orders; who took the blame while never taking up too much space in case it made someone look twice. Standing here now, with the cold gnawing through her sleeves and the wind painting color into her cheeks, she realized something had to give. She couldn’t live inside this grief anymore. She couldn’t keep letting her brother’s ghost make every choice for her. She loved him. God, she missed him. But he wouldn’t want this, he wouldn’t want her to calcify into someone unrecognizable or want her to bury every soft thing inside her just to survive.

    She couldn’t be Wanda’s soldier anymore, not the Bureau’s apology. Couldn’t be the hollow outline of someone who used to know how to hope. Maybe, Rio could learn to be brave again. Not in the reckless way, but a way that meant showing up, reaching out and saying what needed to be said before it was too late.

    Maybe she could say it to Agatha; that she was scared but she was trying. That she wanted her, and didn’t want to live in the shadow of her own failures anymore.

    Rio let out a cathartic sigh, her chest rising and falling as the wind tried to hollow her out.

    She was still here, still standing.

    That could be enough for now. Hopefully it could be enough for Agatha too. Slowly, deliberately, she reached into the inside pocket of her jacket. The fabric resisted, stiff with cold and damp at the hem. But she found it nestled deep against the lining, it was no longer an honor, but a reminder. A scar in her throat, a weight pressed flat and heavy like punishment. Her old badge.

    It was cold in her palm. The metal dull with age but still catching enough light to gleam, just faintly. Her thumb dragged across the engraved letters out of habit, over the faded seal that once meant everything to her. That had defined and controlled her.

    She stared down at it. At everything it meant, everything it had cost. This badge had seen her through Quantico, through the early mornings and every sleepless night writing reports Wanda took credit for. It had been her shield, her excuse, her entire goddamn identity. But when it all fell apart, it became her penance. A token of the life she couldn’t get back. A life she didn’t even want anymore.

    Her fingers curled around it, tight.

    Then, without ceremony, Rio pulled her arm back and hurled the badge into the lake.

    It sailed in an arc, small and silver, catching one final glint of the cloudy sky before it disappeared beneath the water with a soft, distant splash: swallowed by the grey.

    She stood there for a moment, her chest heaving, throat thick. Her hand remained outstretched, suspended in the cold air, as if waiting for the weight to return. But it didn’t, it was gone.

    And with it some small, heavy, part of her.

    Rio blinked hard when a tear slipped down her cheek, carving a clean line through the wind-chapped skin.

    It didn’t bring her brother back or erase the guilt. It didn’t rewrite the past, but it was something.

    A start.

    She lowered her hand slowly, exhaling like she’d been holding her breath since the day she lost him. Her pulse was still frantic, her body still numb with cold, but inside—something had felt lighter.

    Rio looked out over the lake one last time, eyes tracing the endless line where grey sky met grey water, and whispered, voice barely audible over the wind.

    “Te veo.”

    For a beat, the world went still. The breeze slowed and even the lake seemed to hold its breath, as if it too understood the gravity of what had just been said.

    This was the first time she’d chosen herself.

    Chapter End Notes and so finally, everything comes to a head next chapter.
