    Chapter 15: Spaces in Between

    Chapter Notes this chapter was too massive to make it into one. therefore you will get a double update this week with internationally adored suit butchagatha coming up next. you'll see why she deserves her own chapter. The first snow came slow, like it wasn’t sure it was allowed to fall just yet. A lazy drift of pale flakes spun outside the tall squad room windows, barely visible against the steel-gray of late afternoon sky. The flurries hadn’t started to stick yet but every so often a snowflake caught on the glass before dissolving into nothing. This was the weather that made you feel like time was moving sideways.

    Inside, the squad room was half empty, the hum of activity dulled by the climate and the hour. Phones buzzed dully, and an old printer clicked out a report in the corner. The overhead lights were a little too bright against the cold light outside, making the whole room feel like it was caught between a slow workday and something quieter; the wind might sweep it all away if it got any louder.

    William was sprawled on the side of the big conference table, one boot propped against the edge of a chair he wasn’t sitting in. A paperclip chain dangled from his fingers, swaying slightly each time he flicked it. He hadn’t moved in fifteen minutes and every so often he made a dramatic sigh loud enough to remind the world he was bored.

    “I swear to God,” he muttered to no one in particular, “if one more memo comes through about proper break room cleanliness, I’m setting Delaney’s coffee pot on fire.”

    Lilia didn’t look up. She was standing by the whiteboard sorting through a manila folder half as thick as her wrist, lips pressed in a way that suggested she was doing real work while still fully listening. Her earrings caught the light each time she turned a page.

    “Maybe that’s because you keep leaving wet socks on top of the microwave,” she said flatly.

    William looked deeply betrayed. “That was one time.”

    “Two,” Agatha said quietly, not looking up from the report she was annotating. She circled a timestamp, then underlined a name. The ink smudged slightly, the pen dragging over where her hand had pressed too hard. She frowned and flipped to the next page.

    “Are we counting the sock incident separately from the time he reheated leftover nachos with the lid on?” Lilia asked, dry.

    William gasped. “That was a domestic accident, and I resent the implication.”

    It was all background noise, the kind that filled the room like insulation. The easy banter and familiar rhythms were the peace that still felt new if you’d gone long enough without it. Agatha didn’t say much while her mouth twitched in amusement, but her focus was trained on the stack of case notes in front of her. More leads and dead ends accompanied by a few flagged inconsistencies she still hadn’t made sense of.

    Her fingers tapped lightly against the folder’s edge, more out of habit than intention. She was halfway through drafting a new internal timeline accounting for the traffic cam footage they got in last night, but it all felt a little futile. The hours blurred into each other, the pages filled with numbers and names and half-confirmed sightings, but Agatha kept having to reread every line twice, even three times, because her mind refused to stay still. It wasn’t the case she kept circling back to; it was someone’s absence. Rio hadn’t been in all week.

    That shouldn’t have mattered, at least not the way it did because they weren’t partners, not anymore. They weren’t anything, if Agatha was being honest. Not after what happened and the way Rio had looked at her the last time they spoke, it seemed like they snapped beneath the surface, and whatever held them together had unraveled before either of them could catch it.

    Nonetheless, it mattered to her.

    She hadn’t asked where she’d gone and didn’t know if she even had the right to. But she'd overheard enough to piece together that Rio hadn’t taken medical leave or gotten pulled into fieldwork. No reassignment, no mandatory debrief.

    That was strange.

    Even Lilia had mentioned offhandedly that it wasn’t like Rio to miss this much time. A day or two? Maybe. But a full week? No phone calls, nor clipped messages about scheduling or reports. Perhaps more telling than anything else was that there was no Wanda. Not once had Agatha seen her slipping around corners or barking into radios or pulling Rio into closed doors like she had been every day before that.

    The absence of them both should have been a relief. Instead, it made her stomach knot in quiet, helpless ways.

    She wasn’t stupid, she knew it wasn’t her place to wonder. Not after everything that had been said—or worse, left unsaid since Rio had stood in the boardroom, distant and unreachable, wrapped back up in protocol like armor.

    Yet every time the door to the room creaked open or footsteps echoed from the hallway, Agatha’s chest would tighten automatically. Her eyes would flick up before she could stop them. So, evidently some part of her, uninvited and traitorous, was still hoping.

    It wasn’t just hope though, there was some worry tied into everything. Rio was steady and predictable, meaning that even when she was stormy, she was present. She showed up and did the work while filling the room with her quiet, coiled energy and neat piles of annotated files. Not knowing where Rio was, nor knowing what she was carrying and if she was okay, made Agatha’s thoughts feel loose around the edges. She hated it the helplessness and hated how soft she still was for someone who had so clearly chosen to walk away. But that didn’t stop the ache, and it didn’t stop the way she missed her.

    It was pathetic, maybe, selfish; or both. She told herself a hundred times that Rio didn’t need someone like her thinking about her. That if she ever found out and Rio ever looked her in the eye and saw how much Agatha still cared, it would only make things worse.

    So, she held it in and folded it small, pressing it under her ribs. She didn’t let it show, except maybe in the way her handwriting had been shaky all morning. In the way her sentences trailed off when no one was looking and three pages she had to rewrite because they didn’t make any sense when she read them back.

    She’d let herself believe that after that boardroom fallout, things between them had reached some quiet, unspoken end. That she had imagined all of the softness, closeness and look in Rio’s eyes that night on her porch had felt dangerously like a promise.

    She thought she'd accepted it, but her heart felt traitorous in its persistence. Every time her pen stilled; it asked the same question: Where are you? Will you come back?

    Snapping her out of a trance, Lilia moved past her desk, brushing a gentle hand on Agatha’s shoulder as she went. The touch was casual, but it grounded her.

    “Almost done?” Lilia asked.

    Agatha nodded, chewing the inside of her cheek. “Yeah. Just need to pin the timeline.”

    Lilia tilted her head toward the board. “You want help?”

    Agatha’s gaze flicked back to her paperwork. “I’ve got it.” William, somewhere near the window now, sighed again and flopped back into a seat like the weight of winter was a personal insult. “God, do you think we’ll get early release if the snow picks up?”

    “You’re not in elementary school,” Lilia said without looking up from her stack of sorted folders.

    “We should be,” William shot back, squinting dramatically at the slush gathering in crooked little clumps against the windows. “Feels unconstitutional to be stuck in here during a blizzard without hot chocolate and cartoons.”

    “It’s barely flurries,” Lilia said dryly.

    “First flurries of the season,” William corrected. “A sacred event. Should be honored appropriately with pizza, maybe, or a government-mandated nap.” Agatha couldn’t help the faint tug at the corner of her mouth. The banter was stupid, but it filled the squad room with a kind of warmth, the way it sometimes did late in the afternoon when the cases had dulled enough to make room for normalcy.

    It was almost comfortable, in a way Agatha never would have admitted aloud. Not soft, exactly but familiar.

    Or it had been before the air in this room started feeling thinner and her body learned to brace itself everytime she heard boots in the hallway, every time her eyes darted up and found not Rio, but empty space. She pushed the thought aside again and tapped the cap of her pen against the desk, once, twice, steadying herself. Focus. Finish the report. It didn’t have to mean anything, Rio being gone.

    The sound of William flipping a paper football across the table snapped her attention back. Lilia caught it one-handed without even looking and dropped it neatly into the trash can at her side.

    “Show-off,” William grumbled.

    Lilia smiled faintly without lifting her gaze from the open folder in her lap. Agatha stretched her legs under the table and tipped her chair back slightly, pen still tapping absently against her folder. For a second, she watched William sulk like a wet cat in his seat, his whole posture a protest against being trapped indoors. The snow outside had thickened, clinging in lazy swirls against the glass.

    On a whim Agatha leaned forward and said, “Hey, make another one.” William blinked at her like he wasn’t sure he’d heard right. “Another what?”

    She nodded toward the crumpled ball of scrap still rolling lazily in the trash can. “Paper football. Come on. I need a win today.”

    He gave her a mock-wounded look. “You’re trying to hustle me.” “You’re scared,” she shot back with a grin, flipping her pen into the air and catching it with a lazy sort of flourish.

    William narrowed his eyes but started folding a sheet of scrap paper anyway, his fingers working quickly to crease the edges. “I’m just saying, it’s cruel to ask a man to get humiliated in public.”

    “Think of it as a warm-up,” Agatha said sweetly, leaning her elbows on the table. “Besides, I always wanted to be on the football team in high school.” That made William pause mid-fold, eyebrows shooting up. “You? Pads and helmet and all?”

    Agatha laughed, low and a little rough around the edges. “Fuck yeah. I would’ve been great too. Quarterback, obviously.”

    “Obviously,” William deadpanned.

    She shrugged, the grin tugging wider at her mouth. “Didn’t happen, though. School wasn’t exactly falling over themselves to let a five-foot-nothing tomboy with a mouth full of profanity onto the roster.”

    William barked a laugh, shaking his head. “Tragedy.”

    Agatha smirked. “Eh. I survived, but I spent my time flirting with the cheerleaders instead.”

    Across the room, Lilia made a soft noise that might’ve been a laugh, hidden behind the shuffle of papers.

    William gaped at her like she’d grown a second head. “Wait, seriously?” Agatha leaned back in her chair, letting it rock dangerously onto two legs. “What, you think I had all this charm and no one to test it on?” She wiggled her brows in an exaggerated, ridiculous way, and William let out a full, unguarded laugh, the kind that bounced off the walls and made the squad room feel a little less bleak for a second.

    “Remind me never to underestimate you again,” he said, finishing the new paper football with a flourish and setting it upright between his fingers. “Alright, hotshot. You want kickoff?”

    Agatha’s smile turned sly. “You’re gonna regret that.”

    She squared up, elbows braced on the edge of the table, tongue poking slightly between her teeth in concentration as she aimed. The world narrowed to the stupid little triangle of folded paper and the ridiculous sense of competition bubbling up in her chest.

    It was dumb, and harmless, and absolutely necessary.

    Because if she let herself sit still long enough to think—really think about why her chest ached in the silence, why the door creaking open made her heart lurch into her throat—she’d break wide open.

    So instead, she flicked the football straight across the table with a sharp snap of her fingers, sending it sailing clean over William’s haphazard goalposts and smacking into his forehead with a satisfying little thwack. William flailed back in theatrical agony, clutching his head. “Illegal move! I demand a review!”

    Lilia didn’t even look up. “Play stands.”

    Agatha grinned wide and leaned back, tossing her pen onto her folder with a little victorious shrug. “Told you.”

    The snow kept falling outside, thick and soft against the gray afternoon. The world for a moment was just bad jokes and paper footballs and the warmth of people who, somehow, felt like home.

    Agatha let herself enjoy it.

    William scooped the paper football off the table with a flourish, balancing it between two fingers like he was about to launch a missile instead of a crumpled scrap of printer paper.

    "Alright," he said, cracking his knuckles theatrically. "Prepare to witness greatness."

    Agatha leaned back in her chair, arms folded across her chest, a smirk tugging at her mouth. “You’re shaking,” she teased. “You want me to call a timeout so you can gather yourself?”

    William stuck his tongue out in concentration, moving his fingers over the ball like a magician about to perform a trick. "You wish you had this level of raw athleticism," he said, narrowing his eyes in mock seriousness. Agatha whistled low under her breath. “If that ball so much as touches the floor, you’re buying me coffee for a week.”

    “That’s extortion,” he muttered, squinting at his target like the stakes were life or death. "And illegal coaching interference."

    “Cry harder, quarterback,” she drawled, tapping the side of her chair with the toe of her boot in a lazy, taunting rhythm. "Game’s on." Lilia glanced up from her folder long enough to arch a dry eyebrow at both of them, but said nothing, just flipped a page, the soft crinkle of paper somehow making the entire scene feel even more absurdly serious. William lined up his shot and took a deep breath. His whole body poised in tense anticipation as if the fate of the precinct depended on this flick of his fingers.

    Agatha leaned in slightly, lowering her voice in a conspiratorial whisper just loud enough for him to hear. "Don’t choke."

    William’s hand twitched.

    But just as he let go, the squad room door creaked open. The stream of air hit first, curling into the warmth of the room like a ghost dragging winter in behind it. Everyone turned instinctively toward the door. There, stood Rio Vidal.

    Standing just inside the threshold, she looked different. Her hair was pulled back into a ponytail, a few loose strands escaping at her temples, catching the soft overhead light. She wore a maroon turtleneck that clung clean against the lean lines of her frame, the color pulling warmth to her skin and making her eyes look even brighter as they scanned the room. Her hands relaxed at her sides, a steadiness in her posture that hadn’t been there the last time Agatha had seen her. The presence hit the room like a dropped match in a dry field—silent, and immediate William’s paper football veered wildly off course midair, spinning end over end before bouncing harmlessly off the side of the table. He stared at Rio with wide, incredulous eyes, hand still frozen mid-flick. “Oh my god,” he blurted out, voice cracking in the heavy hush that fell over the squad room.

    “The ghost of Precinct Past has returned.”

    Lilia, ever composed, simply closed her file and rose slowly to her feet, her eyes steady, a small almost-smile tugging at the corner of her mouth like she’d known this was coming somehow.

    But Agatha couldn’t move, she was frozen in place and couldn’t bring herself to speak.

    Her body reacted before her brain could catch up with the sudden, hollow thud of her heart against her ribs. Her throat tightened as she adjusted herself in the chair, putting down her fingers as they no longer expected William’s paper football that now rested idly on the floor next to her boot. Rio’s eyes swept the room once, brief and practiced. She nodded once toward William and Lilia politely. But when her gaze found Agatha, it stuck; like something magnetic and inevitable.

    Agatha’s breath caught, a small, traitorous hitch in her chest. There was something lighter around her shoulders now and it settled behind her eyes that hadn’t been there before.

    Agatha didn’t realize she was still staring until William muttered, loud enough to break the spell, “Well, don’t everyone welcome her at once.” Lilia was the first to move. She set her folder aside with careful precision, her flats clicking softly against the tile as she crossed the room toward Rio. She stepped in and offered a hand that Rio clasped firmly before Lilia pulled her into a brief, tight hug that held no fanfare, just something honest and sturdy in its simplicity.

    “Good to see you back kid,” Lilia said, voice warm, meaning every word of it.

    Rio smiled, a little crooked and shy at the edges, that it looked like it surprised even her to be giving away so freely.

    Before Rio could say anything back, William was already closing the distance in a few long, eager strides, arms thrown open wide like he was about to tackle her. Agatha caught a glimpse of Rio’s eyes widening in alarm a second before William barreled into a hug; loud, clumsy, and full of heart.

    Rio let out a soft, startled laugh, the sound rough and bright and so achingly familiar that Agatha’s throat tightened around it.

    “Jesus, William,” Rio huffed, half-laughing, half-wheezing against his shoulder. “I would like my ribs intact please.”

    William squeezed tighter before releasing her with a flourish, stepping back with his hands raised in mock surrender. “Just making sure you’re real.” It pulled a ripple of quiet amusement through the room, but Agatha barely heard it. Her hands were clammy against the worn denim of her jeans, her body moving on autopilot as she scraped her chair back and rose to her feet. Agatha couldn’t believe that Rio was right there and closer now that the others had stepped back, suddenly she didn’t know what to do with her arms, her mouth, or her heartbeat stuttering in her ears. She stood up nervously, shifting her weight from one boot to the other, fingers tapping uselessly against the sides of her thighs. She cleared her throat and tried to swallow the anxious energy clawing up her spine. Rio was now smiling at her like she was seeing something she wasn’t sure she deserved to look at. She waited, patient and steady, not closing the gap between them, not forcing it.

    Agatha, feeling like she might combust under the weight of that gentle attention, stiffened her spine, and with squared shoulders like she was about to recite a deposition thrust her hand out between them; formal and desperate for something to hold onto.

    “Welcome back, Vidal,” she said, voice pitched higher than she meant, too breathless to sound anything but ridiculous.

    Immediately after, Agatha thought, God, that was stupid, why did I do that. For a half-second, Rio looked at her. Then her expression melted, not in mockery, or even amusement, but in softness like Agatha had just offered her a life raft she hadn’t known she needed.

    The brown eyed woman reached out, wrapping her fingers around Agatha’s offered hand with a kind of careful reverence that made Agatha want to crumble on the spot. Her grip was warm, steady, and for a second, neither of them let go.

    Rio’s thumb brushed once over the back of Agatha’s hand before she pulled away, slow like she was reluctant to break the contact. Agatha’s stomach flipped helplessly. Her fingers twitched in Rio’s grasp like she couldn’t decide if she wanted to pull away or lean in.

    “Thanks, Harkness,” Rio murmured back quietly in almost a whisper, and it snagged against Agatha’s ribs and refused to let go.

    Agatha felt her ears burning as she suddenly dragged her hand back a little too quickly, like it had betrayed her by wanting to linger. Lilia, standing a few feet away, arched a brow in quiet amusement. She exchanged a glance with William, then shot Agatha a small, knowing nudge with her elbow as she passed, her expression all but screaming: Smooth, Harkness. Real smooth.

    Agatha barely resisted the urge to flip her off. Instead, she mumbled something incoherent about “getting back to the files” and practically fled toward the nearest stack of case folders, trying very hard not to look like she was fleeing at all.

    Lilia, for her part, let it slide with a flicker of amusement in her eyes, then turned smoothly back toward Rio. She tucked a loose strand of hair behind her ear, shifting the weight of the folder in her arms with a dry, almost fond sigh.

    "Anyways," Lilia said, casual but measured, "we're just catching up today. Cleaning up case notes, tightening the internal timelines before the board review next week. Nothing groundbreaking."

    She handed Rio a folder from the pile on the table without ceremony, like it was the most natural thing in the world. "You’re lucky," she added with a wry tilt of her mouth. "You missed the morning argument about the proper format for labeling traffic cam footage."

    Lilia shifted an arm against her hip, her voice smooth, practiced. "Today is mostly clerical. Internal memos, cross-referencing evidence reports. Keeping the Moreno case threadbare enough that it doesn’t fall through the cracks if higher-ups start asking questions."

    Rio accepted the folder with a nod, flipping it open with the easy, absent competence Agatha remembered too well, her fingers moving steady and sure over the pages, like even after weeks away, she hadn’t lost the rhythm of this place. She scanned the top page quickly, the muscles along her jaw shifting with focus, and for a moment she was all clean lines and contained energy.

    Professional, yes. But not the same kind of professional Agatha had come to dread. Rio’s version felt different and self-owned. It almost seemed like she wasn’t using the work to disappear anymore, but rather to anchor herself. It took Agatha a second to realize she was still staring. She jerked her eyes back to her own folder, lifting it higher like a shield she could duck behind and pretended to read. Yet she didn’t process a single goddamn word on the page. Because Rio was here, and Agatha could feel it like a magnetic pull low in her chest.

    Agatha pressed the folder a little higher toward her face, pretending to be utterly enthralled by a list of traffic cam logs. Her pulse thudded unhelpfully in her ears, drowning out half the conversation she was pretending not to be hyperaware of.

    But it shouldn’t matter, it wasn’t like Rio owed her anything. Out of the corner of her eye, she stole another glance.

    Rio had settled against her side of the table, balancing the folder in one hand, the other braced casually at her hip. Her ponytail was coming a little loose already, dark strands brushing the curve of her neck where the maroon turtleneck clung close. She looked real, solid, breathing the same stale precinct air as the rest of them.

    Agatha swallowed against the tightness rising in her throat. It gnawed at the edges of her composure the way she’d spent days trying not to care when Rio hadn’t shown up. How right now she kept waiting for Wanda to sweep in with some smug smile, announcing that Rio was back under her thumb where she belonged.

    Then she would spend the rest of her days resenting herself for waiting at all and for giving a damn when she shouldn’t; for checking the door like some lovesick idiot, telling herself over and over that Rio Vidal wasn’t hers to miss. That if Rio had chosen to vanish into the Bureau’s leash again, then Agatha had no right to chase after someone who never promised to stay. But now she was here looking more like herself than Agatha had dared to hope.

    All the detective could do was clutch the folder a little tighter and pretend she wasn’t bracing for the door to creak open again and shatter it all. She ducked her head and willed her heart to stop tripping over itself. Tracking the muffled sounds of movement, she heard the scrape of a chair leg, the soft thud of Lilia handing Rio another case file. “I’m sure you’re sad to learn that you just missed the little Superbowl game Harkness and Kaplan gave me.”

    “Yeah?” Rio gave an amused hum; a sound that was so familiar it ached somewhere behind Agatha’s ribs.

    She squeezed her eyes shut briefly behind the folder, willing the feeling down, down, down where it couldn’t crack open her chest any wider. This wasn’t fair, the way Rio could just exist in a room and make it feel different. Fuller. Heavier in all the ways Agatha had taught herself to survive without.

    Agatha forced herself to move, folder still clutched tightly in her hand like it was the only thing anchoring her to the floor. The chair scraped loudly as she stood, the sound swallowed quickly by her roughly clearing her throat. She kept her chin lifted, her spine ramrod straight, like she had somewhere important to be, something crucial to inspect, and not because her skin was prickling from how close Rio still felt, even without touching her. She strode toward William, who had wandered back to the case board, poking half-heartedly at one of the pinned maps like it might start telling him secrets if he stared long enough. He glanced over when he heard her footsteps, offering a loose, lazy salute with two fingers, but Agatha ignored it, planting herself beside him with a kind of stiff, unnecessary seriousness. She cleared her throat once, unnecessarily loud, then jabbed a finger at the chaotic mess of pinned leads and color-coded strings.

    “We need to reevaluate the trajectory of Moreno’s last confirmed sightings,” she said in a low, clipped voice that barely sounded like her own. “Based on the anomalous inconsistencies between timestamp cross- referencing and field interviews, there’s a statistically significant probability that the suspect path deviated from the established quadrant routes.” William blinked at her, completely thrown off. His mouth parted slightly like he was trying to process the sudden shift in language, the heavy words landing like bricks in the otherwise easygoing air of the squad room. Agatha, oblivious or pretending to be, kept going.

    “We need to optimize board organization," she continued crisply, tapping the side of a pinned map with the folder edge. "Reassess the chronological markers and reestablish temporal continuity. Immediate recalibration would enhance strategic deployment of manpower assets.”

    William squinted at her, baffled. “Okay... who are you, and what have you done with Agatha Harkness?”

    Agatha shot him eyes that were broody and full of faux intensity, all as she huffed through her nose like she was too deep in tactical planning to dignify the comment. She turned back to the board, arms crossed, face set into the kind of scowl she usually reserved for broken printers and moronic civilians who wasted her time.

    William leaned sideways, whispering like they were sharing government secrets.

    “You good?” he asked. “Did someone swap your coffee with black-market pre-workout?”

    Agatha didn’t answer, just narrowed her eyes at the board like she was calculating military formations.

    Across the room, Rio watched the entire display unfold with thinly veiled amusement. She hadn’t moved from the table, one hand resting casually on the table, but the slight quirk at the corner of her mouth betrayed her. She wasn’t laughing out loud, but a smile was tucked into the edges of her posture, softening something in her chest that had been locked up for far too long.

    Next to her, Lilia crossed her arms loosely and tilted her head, murmuring just loud enough for Rio to hear, “Welcome back to the circus.” Rio shook her head fondly; God, she’d missed this.

    Meanwhile, Agatha kept her gaze stubbornly pinned to the board, forcing herself to talk about strategic routes and evidentiary thresholds like her life depended on it — all while acutely, painfully aware of the dark brown eyes she could feel on her from across the room.

    William kept staring at the board like it had personally betrayed him. “So...what I’m hearing is we need to do some intense cop things...” he made little quotation marks in the air, his voice dripping with bewildered sarcasm, “because the timestamps have anomalous inconsistencies?”

    Agatha turned her head slowly, fixing him with the driest look she could summon. Her brow lifted with theatrical patience, as if explaining complex math to a child.

    “Correct,” she said gravely.

    Then, realizing how ridiculous she sounded, Agatha let out a breathy huff and lifted both hands in mock defeat.

    “You know what? Forget it. Just...do more homework, Kaplan.” William barked a laugh, still looking dazed as Agatha pivoted sharply on her heel like she could physically march herself away from this disaster of her own making.

    She crossed back toward the big table, folder clutched tight to her chest only to find Rio already watching her when she looked up. Rio wasn’t smiling but there was a softness in her expression that made Agatha’s heart kick against her ribs. A quiet, steady sort of focus seeing straight through every single layer Agatha had tried to throw up between them. Agatha froze mid-step for half a second then forced herself to clear her throat and nod once.

    Professional and totally casual.

    “Vidal,” she muttered, her voice low and weirdly rough in her throat. Rio nodded back, the barest tilt of her head, her hand still resting loosely atop a report. The sleeves of her maroon turtleneck pushed up just enough to expose her wrists, strong and elegant, her fingers tapping absently like she hadn’t decided whether to reach for her yet.

    Agatha dropped into her seat too fast, scraping the chair legs against the floor with a screech that made William wince audibly across the room. She shoved her folder open again like it was the most fascinating thing in the world, pen poised over blank forms she couldn’t even focus on. Notes.

    She needed to take notes. Very important, case-related, crucial notes. Her hand hovered uselessly over the paper all the while, she could feel Rio’s quiet, steady presence beside her. Agatha was trying to keep her pen moving across the page, but the lines were starting to blur together, the letters slipping out of focus no matter how tightly she clenched the muscles in her jaw. She hated that it was happening and hated even more that she knew exactly why; because Rio was sitting just a few feet away, breathing the same air, and it was too much and not enough all at once. There was a shuffle of movement to her left, and Agatha glanced up with her pulse stumbling when she caught the deliberate way Rio had risen from her seat, file in hand, crossing the narrow space between them. Rio didn’t say anything at first as she stood there quietly, the edge of her shirt brushing faintly against Agatha’s elbow as she leaned closer to glance over the pages scattered in front of her.

    For one awful, electric second, Agatha thought she might actually flinch from the sheer, staggering relief of being near her again. Then Rio’s voice came low, a roughness threaded through it like maybe she was just as uncertain as Agatha felt.

    "Timeline’s out of sync," she said, her words careful.

    Her gaze flicked over the papers, then back to Agatha. "Want me to walk through it with you?"

    It was simple, almost gentle like an open hand.

    Agatha hesitated as her pride fought reflex for a heartbeat, she wanted to say no to prove she could handle herself, to show Rio that whatever had broken between them hadn’t broken her, but when she glanced up, the warmth in Rio’s eyes undid her better judgment completely. She nodded once, tight and clipped.

    "Yeah. Sure," she muttered, like it didn’t cost her anything. Rio smiled then and shifted a step closer setting her own file down beside Agatha’s, shoulder brushing hers just barely as they leaned over the same sheet.

    The nearness hit Agatha like a slow tidal wave as the heat of Rio’s arm ghosted along hers. Their breath mingled faintly over the spread pages. She could smell the cold still clinging to Rio’s clothes, the faint trace of shampoo she used—clean, unpretentious.

    Rio’s fingers brushed the edge of the file, tapping once near the error without touching Agatha’s hand.

    "Your surveillance timestamps," she began, "are solid here, here, and here. But after the bar sighting, there’s a fifteen-minute gap before the next camera picks up the suspect."

    Agatha nodded, willing her hand not to shake as she traced the same line Rio had. "I thought the battery might’ve cut out on that street cam." Rio hummed quietly, a soft thread of sound that made Agatha’s skin prickle. "Maybe. Or they knew where the blind spots were."

    Now their voices softened naturally, like the world outside the file had dulled to a faint white hum. They bent closer without thinking, shoulders nearly aligned, arms brushing whenever one of them shifted to point something out.

    Rio corrected a detail, murmuring something about alternate routes, and Agatha didn’t bristle or retreat. Instead, she just listened and nodded while adjusting her notes with a care that almost startled her. When Agatha caught a mistake Rio missed, she cleared her throat and pointed it out, quietly. "You skipped the westbound lane marker here. It wouldn’t line up."

    Rio’s mouth lifted at the corner—a half-smile, gentle and warm. "Good catch."

    Agatha nodded her head like it was nothing, biting back the instinct to grin like an idiot. The ridiculous, dangerous truth was that her heart was pounding hard enough to shake her whole ribcage.

    They kept working quietly as the minutes bled out without either of them noticing. Rio’s hand occasionally ghosted close to hers, and every time it happened, Agatha felt herself hold her breath like she could catch the whole moment between her teeth and not let it go.

    It wasn’t the proximity or rhythm in which they were working together but it was the way Rio’s voice softened when she asked a question. It was their shoulders brushing and neither of them pulling away. This served as proof that despite the chaos of everything outside this room, Wanda, the guilt, the waiting, it still felt easy.

    Agatha tilted her head slightly, pretending to study a timestamp, but really she was stealing a glance at Rio’s profile and the set of her mouth, the small furrow between her brows when she was thinking.

    God, she’d missed her so much it ached, but it scared the hell out of her how badly she wanted to lean into it and trust that it would stay. Nonetheless, Agatha sideswept those feelings and decided to focus on the task at hand, what really mattered. So, they worked in tandem without saying much, the air between them heavy with all the words they weren’t brave enough to offer.

    Agatha’s pen scratched lightly over the paper, her head bent, hair slipping loose from the low ponytail she’d wrangled it into earlier. Rio kept her gaze fixed on the files, but it was getting harder by the second not to look at her. Every brush of sleeves, muted breath and movement Agatha made, drew Rio in without permission. It wasn’t just familiarity; it was gravity. A pull low and aching in her chest, heavier with each second they spent pretending this was normal.

    God, she wanted to reach out. To touch her wrist. To tuck that stray piece of hair back behind her ear. To just say I missed you without the world collapsing around them.

    But the words stuck like glass in her throat, because when Wanda had twisted the knife, Rio hadn’t fought hard enough to keep standing at her side. Agatha knew that some bridges didn’t burn with fanfare, they just crumbled quietly under the weight of silence.

    Also, William and Lilia were still in the room, pretending not to listen. Rio swallowed against the knot forming in her chest, forcing herself back into the safety of the casework. Her hand moved in slow mirrored motions beside Agatha’s, fingertips brushing the same pages, smoothing down the same edges. The points where their arms almost touched or where their hands almost met, felt electrified. The barest collision might undo them both.

    Agatha didn’t look at her, she kept her head down, professional, clinical, her shoulders tense like she was bracing for something, and Rio caught the way her fingers sometimes stuttered over the page, how she tapped her pen too fast when the silence stretched a little too long.

    She felt it too; Rio was sure of it.

    The urge to say something rose so fast it nearly choked her. She needed to offer some small proof that this wasn’t unsalvageable. That they could still find their way back, even if it meant fumbling through the dark with their hands out, hoping the other would reach back.

    She shifted her weight, exhaled slow, and without looking at Agatha, let herself speak.

    "I saw the follow-up you wrote," she said, keeping her gaze pinned on the paper instead of the way Agatha’s shoulders stiffened slightly beside her. "It was clean."

    For a second, Agatha didn’t react as her pen kept moving like maybe she hadn’t heard, like maybe if she pretended hard enough, the words would slide right off her skin.

    Then, quietly, she replied with no sharpness, or bravado, simply a muted self-defense worn smooth from practice.

    "You would've torn it up three weeks ago."

    The words landed with more weight than Agatha probably meant, it was a sharp confession folded into a throwaway comment.

    Rio smiled small and fleeting, invisible to anyone not looking hard. She would have. Three weeks ago, she would’ve redlined every typo, every timestamp that didn’t line up perfectly and found every flaw because it was easier than seeing what was underneath. Easier than being soft. So, she let the silence settle for a moment as they sat side by side at the table, their hands moving in mirrored rhythm while they passed files and slid case notes into neat rows piecing together a case that wasn’t the only thing broken between them.

    The whole time, Rio fought the urge to say more. To tell her she was sorry and that she didn’t stay away because she wanted to, but because she had to figure out how to come back the right way. Coming back half-alive wasn’t enough for someone like Agatha.

    But she held her tongue, it wasn’t the right time yet.

    Instead, she let the simple fact of being here, working beside her, shoulder to shoulder in the fading light of the squad room, be enough for now.

    They had been hovering over the case file for what felt like hours, the low murmur of paperwork and muted conversation filling the squad room like static. Outside, the snow drifted against the windows in lazy, quiet spirals, blurring the edges of the world into soft white.

    Inside, everything between them felt painfully sharp.

    Every small movement seemed exaggerated in the tight space as Agatha’s hand moved across the pages, the way her shoulder shifted ever so slightly as she leaned in to better trace a line on the map taped to the table. Rio found herself cataloging everything in quiet desperation via the curve of Agatha’s knuckles, the faint crease in her brow as she concentrated and how her pen tapped once against the margin before sliding to another point. The entire time, Agatha kept her head down while tracing invisible paths across the margins of the tens of documents. She could feel Rio's presence like a magnetic pull at the side of her body that was close enough that their sleeves brushed every time they shifted. Every few minutes Agatha could hear the subtle cadence of her breathing, steady but remaining cautious. Rio murmured something under her breath about the timestamp inconsistencies, the technical jargon slotting into the space between them, but Agatha barely registered it. Her hand moved automatically, her pen circling an entry she wasn’t actually seeing.

    A breath between them, then again.

    One after another.

    Then almost inaudible; like she wasn’t even speaking to Rio at all, Agatha's voice finally broke the fragile rhythm. Barely there, like it wasn’t meant for anyone to hear.

    "I kept checking the door."

    It had slipped out so soft, so human, that for a moment Rio wondered if she’d imagined it.

    Agatha's hand stilled against the page, her fingers tightening around the pen just slightly, her head bowing lower like she could hide from her own admission if she just folded herself smaller.

    She didn’t look at her as she kept her blue eyes locked on the paperwork as if the ink on the page might save her from what she'd just confessed. The air between them tightened, brittle with the weight of what neither of them had dared say until now. Agatha didn’t dare look up when her chest burned with embarrassment, and her mouth felt dry, like she’d betrayed herself somehow.

    For a minute, there was nothing but the faint scratch of Rio’s sleeve against the table as she shifted slightly to look at her.

    Silence pressed in between them—thick, waiting.

    Agatha kept studying the faint smudge of a coffee ring in the corner like it was the most important thing in the world. She braced for the awkward deflection, the polite nod, or the carefully professional sidestep that would remind her they weren’t, and couldn’t, be what she wanted them to be. But it didn’t come.

    Instead, Rio answered with a gentleness she hadn’t realized she still had. "I didn’t mean to leave you that long."

    Her voice was raw in her own ears, scraping and soft at the same time, like something exposed to the cold too long and finally cracking. The admission sat there between them, delicate and bruised. The words were so simple. So unbearably honest.

    Agatha blinked slowly, her vision blurring slightly before she forced herself to inhale as her throat tightened. She could hear the sincerity in Rio’s voice, like maybe she was scared too.

    The woman’s fingers resumed their methodical tracing along paperwork, but Rio could see the way her shoulders stiffened, the way her breath caught for just half a second before she masked it.

    "You don’t owe me an explanation," Agatha said firmly with habitual armor, but there was something underneath it that trembled at the edges despite how hard she tried to pretend otherwise. Her voice was steadier than she felt. But as the words left her, she faltered, the shield slipping for just a heartbeat longer than she meant it to.

    Lower now, almost a whisper; "But I think...I would’ve listened." The words cracked the space between them wide open.

    Rio’s heart lurched, painfully tender in her chest, and for the first time in what felt like forever, she let herself feel it—the ache of missing her, the regret of silence stretched too long, the fragile, ridiculous hope that maybe it wasn’t too late to bridge the gap between them.

    Rio didn’t answer right away.

    Agatha could feel her turn slightly, could sense the way she was looking at her now, not at the file or at the caseboard, but at her. The younger woman watched Agatha out of the corner of her eye and saw the way her lips pressed into a tight line like she was working hard not to show anything more. But it was there anyway along the soft tremble at the corner of her mouth, the too-slow blink that betrayed the emotion rising in her chest.

    They both stared at the same report now, but neither of them was really seeing it. Their bodies remained poised in the old rhythm of professionalism, but it was a lie. The real conversation was happening in the silence accompanied by the trembling heartbeats stretched thin between them, the longing neither could name without breaking whatever fragile truce they had stitched together in these last few minutes. It was unbearable to meet her gaze, so Agatha kept her eyes stubbornly locked downward, the tension in her spine so tight she thought it might snap.

    Out of the corner of her vision, she saw Rio shift her weight, the leather of her belt creaking softly with the motion. She must have been clenching her hands at her sides, the way she always did when she was trying to hold herself together.

    The air felt heavier, like the room was folding in on itself, cocooning them away from everything else. The noise of William and Lilia somewhere off to the side blurred into background static. The snowfall outside pressed gray light through the windows, making everything feel muted, suspended in some strange in-between.

    Finally, Rio’s voice came quietly again. "I didn’t want to come back until I could do it right."

    The confession was so quiet it almost got lost under the soft hum of the vents.

    Agatha’s fingers twitched against the table. Slowly, she lifted her gaze. Rio was looking at her with an expression so open it made Agatha’s heart ache; she wasn’t hiding anymore. She could tell Rio wasn’t sure what she'd find in Agatha’s face but had decided to risk it anyway.

    Across the room, the steady rhythm of paper shuffling slowed. Lilia, halfway through organizing a stack of case binders, paused with her fingers hovering just above the next sheet.

    She didn’t say anything at first as she lifted her head slightly, watching the space between Agatha and Rio from beneath her lashes.

    It wasn’t obvious and it wouldn’t be to anyone who wasn’t looking. But Lilia had always been perceptive in ways that mattered by catching the small things, the shifts in posture, the breaths held too long. She saw the way Agatha’s pen was frozen half an inch above the page, the way Rio’s shoulders, usually drawn tight had softened, leaning just slightly into the invisible pull between them.

    A tiny tilt of her head accompanied by a soft exhale.

    The older woman turned her head sideways to where William was perched backwards on his chair, balancing a pencil precariously across his upper lip, entirely oblivious.

    Lilia let the moment stretch just a heartbeat longer. Then, with practiced ease, she straightened up, setting the folders down neatly on the corner of a nearby filing cabinet.

    "William," she called casually, her voice pitched just loud enough to carry, but still low and unobtrusive.

    William startled slightly, the pencil tumbling from his lip with a clatter onto the desk. He blinked at her, eyes wide and boyish, the perfect picture of someone who had no idea they were needed for a discreet exit strategy. Lilia didn’t smile. She just raised a brow and said, smoothly, "We’ve got that call with the coroner, remember?"

    William blinked again.

    He tilted his head, confusion flickering across his face as he then darted a glance at Rio and Agatha who were still sitting far too close, and it clicked in less than a second.

    "Oh," he blurted, scrambling to his feet with an awkward jangle of chair legs. "Right. The, uh… urgent body stuff."

    He cleared his throat, shoving his coat on backwards at first before fixing it properly, grabbing his badge with unnecessary speed.

    Lilia gave no reaction except to gather her own things calmly, tucking them against her side. As she moved toward the door, William trailing half a step behind her and still fumbling with his sleeve, she allowed herself a single glance back.

    Her eyes found Rio first.

    The detective stood very still, as if caught mid-breath. The look on her face wasn’t one Lilia had seen often unmasked like she was standing in a place where she didn’t know what would happen next but was willing to stand there anyway.

    For just a second, Lilia's gaze softened. A quiet, almost imperceptible nod. Be brave.

    Then she turned, slipping through the door with William in tow, the quiet thunk of it swinging closed behind them like a signal dropped into still water.

    And just like that, Agatha and Rio were alone.

    The room stretched wide and quiet around them, the snowfall outside painting everything in muted silver. The low hum of the vents filled the silence, but neither of them moved right away.

    Agatha swallowed thickly, the knot in her throat threatening to undo her as she realized she could now let loose in sole company of the woman who had been slowly unraveling her.

    "You didn’t have to be perfect," she said, her voice soft and searching Rio’s eyes like she could somehow anchor herself there. "You just had to come back."

    For a moment, neither of them breathed.

    The world shrank even smaller, just weight of unfinished apologies, and the fragile, blooming thing neither dared name yet.

    The air between them felt too thin, like the smallest shift might tear it open completely. Agatha’s fingers curled slightly where they rested on the edge of the file, like she wasn’t sure whether to hold onto something or let go. Rio’s gaze stayed steady on her, brow furrowed just slightly, like she was fighting herself while measuring the risk against the need pressing up sharp inside her ribs. She was tired of waiting and hiding behind protocol and fear and the thousand tiny ways she’d taught herself not to want things. Now, standing here, with Agatha looking at her like she could still be wanted was too much.

    Slowly, Rio shifted her hand across the surface of the table. Barely an inch, but enough for her fingers to brush against Agatha’s.

    It wasn’t an accident.

    It was a question.

    Agatha startled slightly, like a bird sensing a shadow overhead. Her gaze flicked down to where their fingers touched—barely there—and then back up again, something raw flickering behind her eyes. She didn’t pull away. Rio let out a breath she hadn’t realized she was holding, the sound catching in her throat. Her voice, when it came, was rough around the edges, the way it always got when she was trying too hard to be careful. "You don’t have to pretend either," she murmured, voice low. "Not with me."

    Agatha blinked, once, slow and stunned, as if the words physically knocked something loose inside her chest. The kind of thing she’d spent too long locking up in a place even she didn’t look at anymore.

    She tried for a smirk, but it faltered halfway up, never reaching her mouth. "I’m not pretending," she said, but it came out too quiet and soft to be a lie. Rio’s thumb brushed against her knuckle, almost apologetic in its gentleness. "You looked like you wanted to disappear when you saw me," she said simply, a whisper of a smile at the corner of her mouth. Agatha flushed, the heat crawling high on her cheekbones. "I didn’t—" She stopped and sighed, deciding then to drop the pretense entirely.

    "Yeah," she admitted, voice dropping lower. "I did."

    For a moment, Rio just looked at her, cataloging every tiny crack and softness she hadn’t let herself reach for before. The slight downturn of Agatha’s mouth, the little crease between her brow, how her throat moved when she swallowed hard.

    "You don’t have to be afraid of me," Rio said quietly, her voice threading the space between them meant to hold, not pull apart.

    Agatha’s fingers twitched again, grazing against Rio’s knuckles, a contact so feather-light it could’ve been a mistake.

    "I’m not afraid of you," Agatha said, and for once her voice didn’t shake. It was steady, even as her heart slammed against her ribs hard enough she thought maybe Rio could hear it too.

    "I’m afraid of wanting something I can’t have," she added, softer. Barely there.

    The confession hung between them like a secret passed in the dark. Rio’s hand shifted again, a steady reminder to the beautiful woman in front of her that she was present.

    "Agatha," Rio said, voice almost a breath. "Whatever this is...I feel it too." Agatha’s eyes burned, and she blinked fast, her whole-body humming. She wanted to lean in, to touch, to believe it could be that simple. But her boots stayed planted, and hands stayed tight on the table. She wasn’t ready to fall unless Rio was going to catch her.

    Rio’s fingers hovered just barely off Agatha’s own and began to trail up her wrist. The distance between them an ache in her chest now, a silent, gnawing thing that refused to quiet down.

    And then, slowly, her hand lifted from where it rested, cutting through the narrow space between them, fingers trembling faintly from nerves she couldn't quite wrestle down.

    Agatha stiffened at first, like instinct, but she didn’t pull away and didn’t move at all.

    Rio’s knuckles brushed the side of her jaw first, feather-light, tentative like she thought Agatha might shatter if she touched her wrong. Then, with a breathless sort of reverence, she let her hand curve fully against her cheek. Agatha’s eyes fluttered closed almost immediately.

    The kind of closing that wasn’t flinching, but surrender. Her breathing hitched quietly, but Rio felt it like a hook straight through her ribs. The heat of her skin burned beneath Rio’s palm, soft and startling, grounding her in a way no rulebook, no training exercise, no carefully orchestrated life ever had.

    Rio’s thumb brushed along the high curve of Agatha’s cheekbone, once, slow and aching, memorizing the shape like she’d forget it if she didn’t etch it into her hands right now.

    Without thinking, without letting herself think, she scooted her chair closer. The legs dragged faintly against the tile floor, but neither of them noticed. Now their knees almost touched. Their breath mingled in the sliver of air left between them.

    Agatha’s head tilted slightly into the touch, unthinking. Trusting, like a flower turning toward light.

    Rio swallowed, breath uneven.

    "Let me show you it’s real," she whispered, her forehead nearly brushing Agatha’s now, the words trembling loose and low from someplace she usually kept bolted shut. "Like you’ve been trying to show me all along." Her hand slid, tentative but certain, curling along the side of Agatha’s neck. Her thumb resting just at the hinge of her jaw, feeling the slight, rapid pulse hammering there.

    Rio leaned in; slow, reverent, patient.

    Closing the distance with the kind of aching inevitability that felt less like a choice and more like gravity, finally giving up pretending it didn’t exist. Agatha’s breathing stuttered against her mouth—close enough now Rio could feel it, warm.

    Their noses brushed, just barely with faintest contact, sending shivers ricocheting through Rio’s chest like a struck chord.

    Agatha’s hand twitched against the table, like she didn’t know whether to grab hold of Rio or the world or herself.

    Rio hovered a heartbeat away from kissing her, her hand still cupping her jaw, her own heart slamming desperate and stupid against her ribs. Agatha’s lips parted slightly in a sweet invitation.

    Rio tilted her head—and Agatha pulled back.

    Not harshly, but with a tiny, heartbreaking shake of her head, her hand curling lightly around Rio’s wrist, not to push her away, but to pause her there.

    Agatha cleared her throat, strangled. Her eyes still closed for a beat longer, like she couldn’t quite look yet. When she did open them, they were glossy and wide and full of something Rio couldn’t name without suffocating on it. Agatha’s voice cracked low between them, barely a whisper: "I don’t want you to get in trouble."

    Rio blinked, still breathing her in.

    Agatha’s thumb rubbed along the inside of Rio’s wrist absently, a slow, apologetic little stroke that undid her more thoroughly than any kiss would’ve.

    "Not with her," Agatha added, even softer. "Not because of me." Rio’s hand stayed where it was, cupping her jaw like she didn’t know how to let go. Maybe she didn’t.

    The world around them felt ten thousand miles away. The faint scratch of snow against the windows, the slow hum of the radiator—they all dissolved into static at the edges of Rio’s awareness.

    There was only Agatha and the space she refused to take.

    Only the longing Rio wasn’t sure how to keep swallowing down anymore. She exhaled slowly letting her forehead drop until it just barely touched Agatha’s, their bodies bowed together like some prayer.

    "I don’t care about her," Rio whispered against the sliver of air between them. "I care about you."

    Agatha’s fingers tightened at her wrist, small and involuntary, like she couldn’t help it.

    Rio could feel the shape of Agatha’s breathing and could feel how it trembled at the edge of her own mouth, a fragile shudder in the few inches that still dared to separate them.

    Neither of them moved away, nor closer.

    Rio shifted slightly, her fingers brushing an impossible, delicate line down the side of Agatha’s jaw where she still cradled her. The touch wasn’t confident; it was tentative, nervous in the way that only something real could be.

    She felt Agatha’s eyelashes flutter against her thumb, felt the way her body went rigid and soft at the same time, maybe part of her wanted to step back and part of her would break if Rio let go.

    Rio’s voice, when it came, was almost a whisper, rough-edged from all the weeks she hadn’t used it like this. "I kept thinking about what I’d say when I saw you again."

    Agatha’s eyes opened slowly, lashes still damp, her gaze meeting Rio’s from under the heavy, aching weight of everything unsaid. She asked, quietly, voice like paper against flame, "And?" Rio inhaled shakily, feeling the tremor of it all the way down her spine. She didn’t try to pretty it up or dress it in protocol, or all the other bullshit armor she used to survive.

    She let it fall raw and honest from her mouth, "I missed you." Agatha’s mouth parted slightly, a breath catching at the back of her throat like she hadn’t prepared herself to actually hear it.

    Rio’s thumb brushed the side of her face again, slow, reverent. Agatha leaned closer without meaning to, the pull between them tightening like a live wire coiled between heartbeats.

    They sat like that, forehead to forehead, breath to breath, the world outside the tiny gravity of this moment nothing but blurred edges. The snow outside the windows thickened, soft white filtering the gray, hiding them away from everything that could break this fragile, tender thing they were building between them.

    Agatha’s hand, still curled around Rio’s wrist, loosened just slightly not to let her go, but to let her stay. The heat of her skin burned against Rio’s palm, the pulse there rapid, betraying everything Agatha might have tried to tuck away.

    Rio felt the fear and the absolute unbearable tenderness of wanting someone you weren’t sure you deserved. For the first time in too long, she didn’t try to outthink it; just let herself have.

    The fragile silence between them thickened with the weight of too many hopes kept on too short a leash.

    Agatha’s fingers brushed against Rio’s wrist, and she shifted slightly, her forehead pulling back just enough that her eyes could find Rio’s fully. The way she looked at her now was pained.

    For a moment, Agatha just breathed, trying to steady herself. Then she spoke while cracking at the edges. “It’s hard," she admitted. "It’s hard being near you," she said again, barely a whisper. "Because I don’t know when she's gonna come rip you away from me again." Rio’s chest tightened.

    Agatha’s mouth tilted, a sad almost-smile that didn’t reach her eyes. She cleared her throat softly, as if trying to muscle through it, but it didn’t work. Her voice stayed broken in a way Rio had never heard from her before. "I don't know if you'll just go with her again," Agatha said, her words falling heavier now. "If you'll leave. Be the compliant good soldier and I’ll be left here looking like a goddamn idiot for thinking..." She stopped herself with a sharp breath, closing her eyes briefly like it physically hurt to say the next part.

    "For thinking you might actually choose me this time."

    The admission gutted Rio. For her everything was moving, breaking, and reforming all at once.

    She felt it right behind her ribs, in her blood.

    The shame, the guilt, the overwhelming need to reach out and tell Agatha she was wrong, and she wasn’t going anywhere. Not anymore. Something stung at the corner of Rio’s eye before she could stop it. A small, traitorous tear gathered in the seam between her lower lashes. The brown eyed woman felt that didn’t deserve to cry, especially not when Agatha was the one still standing there, bruised from all the ways Rio had failed to choose her before.

    She opened her mouth to speak—and that’s when the soft clearing of a throat cut through the tension. They both jolted slightly, blinking out of the moment.

    Lilia stood just a few paces away now, an apologetic tilt to her brow, her hands folded neatly in front of her hating herself for interrupting. Her voice was graver than usual. "Sorry," Lilia began, glancing between them, her gaze a soft, knowing thing. "But the Delaney’s asking for everyone. Said he’s got something to announce before end of shift." The weight of reality pressed back in around them, heavier now that the moment had been cracked open.

    “Got it.” Agatha replied, straightening slightly, her expression shuttering as she pulled herself back into professionalism, but Rio caught the way her voice shook before she looked away.

    Rio let her hand drop back to her side, curling her fingers into her palm so tightly it hurt.

    Agatha cleared her throat first, dragging her hand down her face and smoothing it over her thigh like she could erase the tremble in her fingers. Rio mirrored the motion with more restraint, adjusting the hem of her turtleneck with two tight tugs. She didn't trust herself to look at Agatha for more than a second, not when the edges of what had almost happened still buzzed so close beneath her skin.

    Without speaking, they straightened themselves up and followed Lilia out of the squad room toward the bullpen.

    The bullpen was already filling up with the usual late afternoon chaos; chairs screeching against the floor, phones buzzing unanswered on desks, the low hum of tired officers thumbing through paperwork as the flurries of snow continued to dust the high, narrow windows.

    When the three of them stepped into view, it didn’t take long for the attention to shift.

    Captain Delaney, standing near the center of the bullpen with his arms crossed and a mug of coffee steaming in one hand, caught sight of them first. His mouth twisted into a smirk that was half amusement, half exasperation.

    “Well, look what the wind blew back in,” Delaney drawled loudly enough that a few heads turned. His voice carried with that dry rasp he always used when he was about to be a smartass.

    He lifted his mug like he was offering a mock-toast.

    “Glad you could join us, Vidal. Harkness. Calderu. Thought maybe you three were starting a book club back there without the rest of us.” There were a few chuckles across the bullpen. William—who was leaning lazily against a desk—rolled his eyes.

    Rio ducked her head slightly, a flush rising high on her cheeks despite herself. She gave Delaney a respectful nod, but her heart was thudding hard against her ribs. Not from the Captain’s comment but from the way she could still feel Agatha standing just beside her, close enough that their shoulders almost touched.

    She risked a glance sideways.

    Agatha was staring straight ahead, posture tight, arms crossed defensively over her chest, but Rio caught the way her fingers flexed once against her sleeve, she was fighting the same urge.

    But their responsibility to the room buzzed louder around them and Rio knew better. She couldn't ruin this for Agatha, not again after what Wanda had already taken from both of them.

    Still, the longing was there rolling beneath her skin, making it hard to stand still without shaking. Her hands ached for contact she wasn’t brave enough to claim.

    Agatha, for her part, was trying very hard not to crack as she stood there stiffly, her arms crossed, pretending to listen as Delaney raised his voice and called for everyone’s attention.

    Her mind wasn’t on the words at all but on the faint, persistent warmth of Rio standing beside her. The way Rio’s shoulder almost brushed hers whenever either of them moved.

    The way Rio had looked at her back in the squad room, like Agatha was something precious she didn’t believe she deserved but wanted anyway. Agatha’s heart thudded traitorously against her ribs.

    She shouldn’t hope for this when Wanda’s shadow still loomed too large and choked out too much oxygen whenever Rio was in the same room. Agatha didn’t trust it, or herself to step forward again and not get left bleeding for it.

    Yet even knowing that she couldn’t stop herself from sneaking a glance sideways. There little Rio was, head tilted slightly down, lashes dark against her cheekbones, the faintest twitch of a performative smile fighting its way onto her mouth whenever she thought no one could see it.

    Agatha’s fingers twitched against the crook of her arm.

    She wanted to reach out to pull Rio down by the belt loops and kiss her until they both forgot who had ever made them afraid to be wanted by each other this much. Instead, she stayed where she was, her throat tight and her body filled with longing.

    At the front of the room, Delaney cleared his throat, bringing the room to order.

    “All right, you degenerates,” he said, raising his coffee mug like a gavel. “Now that we’re all accounted for, I’d like to remind you all about a very important upcoming event.”

    There was a collective groan from a few corners of the room. “Annual Commendation Awards,” Delaney went on, ignoring them. “Outstanding officers, exemplary cases, the whole nine yards. Ceremony’s Friday night at the District Club downtown. We’ll be eating steak and pretending to be fancier than we are for one evening.”

    A ripple of mild amusement moved through the room.

    Delaney continued, waving a hand, “Food’ll be halfway decent this year. Luckily the budget let us spring for a live band, and—” he grinned, sly and knowing “—everyone’s encouraged to bring a plus one.”

    There were a few hoots and whistles from the younger officers. Someone wolf-whistled. William clapped Lilia dramatically on the back, and she responded by shoving his face lightly away from her.

    Agatha’s heart skidded so fast she nearly forgot to breathe. A plus one? A date.

    The words rattled through her chest like they belonged to someone else’s daydream, not hers.

    She glanced sideways at Rio instinctively in the barest movement and caught her doing the exact same thing.

    For half a second, their eyes locked.

    Agatha’s heart hammered painfully against her ribs, the weight of their half- second glance lodging itself somewhere deep and bruising. This wouldn’t be a casual plus-one situation as Delaney had said it deliberately, with that smirk and implication that carried straight through the room on a current of teasing laughter.

    Agatha felt her throat close up.

    She should ask her and lean over, tilt her head toward Rio’s, maybe murmur it low enough that no one else would hear—Come with me. Come as mine. The thought made her chest ache, made her palms damp where they pressed stiffly against her sides. She didn’t dare, because it wasn’t supposed to be like this anymore.

    Agatha could still feel the echo of Rio’s fingers against her cheek from earlier, the ghost of almost-kissing in the air between. Yet still some buried survival instinct snapped its teeth at her ribs, warning her against it. You can’t, her conscience hissed.

    You don’t get to do that anymore. You’re supposed to be professional. Distant. You’re supposed to be protecting her, not making her life harder. It didn’t matter how much Agatha wanted to reach out or that Rio had looked back at her with the kind of longing that should’ve been enough to raze every ounce of common sense to the ground.

    What mattered was that Wanda still existed and had power over both of them and Agatha would not be the reason Rio got punished again after what it had already cost them once.

    So, she stayed tethered to the ground by obligation and the faint tremor in her hands she tried desperately to hide.

    She would be polite and follow the rules for once, even if it made her want to crawl out of her own skin. Even if it hurt and the safety of it all meant standing here, heart cracking open in her chest, while the captain droned on about logistics like nothing in the world had shifted at all. Across the room, Delaney cleared his throat, snapping a few people’s attention back to him.

    "And before you all start bitching like the pussies you are," he said, a faintly amused rasp threading through his voice, "Yes, formal dress is required. Full suits, dresses, real shoes. The whole nine yards."

    A few groans rose from the officers clustered near the back as someone muttered something about needing to actually find a tie.

    Delaney ignored them with a smirk.

    "I want to see all of you looking respectable for once in your lives," he said dryly, lifting his mug like a toast. "Shine your damn shoes. Pretend you like each other for three hours. Dance if you’re brave enough. It'll make for a hell of a better photo op than last year’s shitshow."

    The room broke into a low ripple of laughter.

    Agatha barely heard any of it while her whole world narrowed down to the distance between her and Rio. The invitation was hanging there between them now, silent and real. The detective didn’t know how she could possibly pretend she didn’t want to reach for it.

    She wanted more than anything to walk up to Rio right now and ask her to be hers, even if just for one night, and claim her openly even if it meant risking everything she had left to lose.

    But her throat stayed locked up tight along with her hands remaining stubbornly at her sides.

    Do the right thing, she told herself again, each repetition sinking its claws deeper into her chest.

    Protect her.

    When she stole one more glance sideways at Rio who stood there so carefully pretending so hard not to look back—Agatha thought, for one heartbeat, that maybe Rio was telling herself the same thing. Maybe they were both just too afraid to reach first.

    Captain Delaney clapped his hands once, jolting the room’s attention back to him. “All right, before you all skitter off like mice—” He pointed toward the bulletin board in front of his office. “Reminders posted by me for the dinner. You’ve got a plus-one slot. Don’t show up solo and mopey unless you’re angling for pity votes.”

    Another ripple of laughter and Agatha swallowed against the sudden tightness in her throat, forcing herself to smile faintly like the others. The Captain waved them off with a snort. “Dismissed. Go file something so I can pretend we’re a functioning precinct.”

    Chairs scraped and shuffled along with William’s exaggerated groan as Lilia smacked him on the back of the head for saying something undoubtedly stupid.

    She took a deep breath, the scene in front of her blurring a little at the edges. In her mind at least for a second, she imagined it all. The soft melody of violins and clinking glasses. Rio, standing at the edge of a dance floor in a dress just a little too tight, hair pulled back, or down, looking at her like she was the only person in the room.

    She imagined Rio reaching for her hand, in a silent answer to her question. Will you trust me?

    Agatha curled her fingers tighter inside her old jean pockets. She wanted to take Rio’s hand and forget the rules to the walls they’d both built out of necessity and pride, and let herself believe in something soft for once. Even if this couldn’t be her reality, maybe for a moment it was enough to imagine being brave.

    Chapter 17: Held in Light

    Chapter Notes warning, this chapter contains: a butch in a suit and at least one autistic detective making questionable emotional decisions under low lighting. p.s: with smut, this was on track to be at least 32k words. for my sanity (bc I still have to edit it and because I’m a dyke who believes in the slow, exquisite suffering of others) it will be split. therefore, next chapter will be only smut.

    p.s.s: thank you for the warm welcome back! it makes me happy to see these losers are still so loved and remembered The foyer was quiet, lit only by the low glow of two nearby lamps, one perched on the console table and the other casting a soft halo from the living room beyond. The light was warm and it pooled gently across the hardwood floor, turning sharp corners soft and catching faint on the edge of the mirror she stood in front of. The space, usually passed through without a second thought, now felt like a threshold that was expectant. Tonight, it signified a breath held between who she was every other night and whoever she needed to be for this one.

    She stood in front of the tall mirror leaning against the far wall, golden trimmed with a slightly curved edge and a warped corner that bowed the reflection enough to make her look like a stranger. Her posture was rigid; she couldn’t slouch in this. The shirt was crisp to the point of discomfort. The white button-up clung in all the wrong ways, too stiff at the shoulders, a little too clean-looking for someone who spent her days wading through back alleys and case files in her usual muddy boots. She’d ironed it herself twice and still found wrinkles. She adjusted the collar again, then again, fingers rough from years of working too hard, fumbling a little at the edge where it folded just slightly uneven.

    Her sleeves were rolled up to the forearm. Agatha wasn’t sure if that made her look effortlessly composed or like she’d given up halfway through dressing. The thin black tie hung around her neck, undone and languid like it belonged to someone far more confident. She hadn't knotted it yet and couldn’t quite bring herself to, it felt like sealing something shut. Also, she hadn’t tied one since the last funeral she’d been forced to speak at. Beside the mirror, her new boots sat neatly in place, they were jet black and leather, no scuffs yet. Shiny in a way that made her nervous as she’d bought them on a whim the day before, talked herself into it with the justification that “everyone else will be dressed up” and “it wouldn’t kill you to wear something that isn’t falling apart for once.” She’d even let the too eager salesman talk her into the weatherproofing. That’s how unsure she’d been; she hadn’t even argued. She stared at them like they might bite. Then, Agatha caught her reflection again. Her posture stiffened, eyes scanning up and down like she didn’t quite believe what she was seeing. Black trousers, tailored to fit too well with no wrinkles or scuffs. Clean in a way that made her feel exposed. Her boots again that were expensive sat obediently in her eyesight, shining under the soft lamp light like they didn’t belong to her at all. They weren’t scuffed from chasing suspects or soaked from standing too long at a crime scene; not hers yet and it felt strange. Then there was her hair; dark brown and long, curling in soft waves down past her shoulders. Today it was washed, dried, and carefully let down for the first time in longer than she could remember. It framed her face like someone else’s and didn’t look like the low, messy ponytail she yanked it into every morning, the one that stuck out under her collar or curled uneven from the rain. This was intentional, it was pretty.

    Agatha hated that word.

    She didn’t want to be pretty. Pretty was delicate and easy to break, it was pink dresses and ballet recitals and all the things her mother wanted her to be.

    Still…she looked at herself. The slacks, the tie, the hair; the whole thing. She looked like someone a woman could fall in love with; like someone Rio could fall in love with.

    She pressed her hands to the sides of her pants, maybe grounding herself physically would stop the spiral. The fabric was smooth, and she felt like a mannequin version of herself. Like one of those department store displays she used to laugh at under her breath when she walked past with muddy boots and a busted phone in her pocket. A display that said: Look, this is what respectable looks like. This is what you should’ve been. Agatha tugged at the bottom hem of her shirt, trying to straighten the tuck, then let go with a sharp exhale when it didn’t help. Her fingers hovered over the tie again, hesitated, then dropped to her sides. She rubbed a hand down the front of her pants, she already knew the shirt would wrinkle by the time she sat down. The boots would probably pinch. Her shoulders looked too squared off and masculine and—

    This wasn’t a date.

    God, she needed to get that through her thick skull. It wasn’t a date, and she wasn’t dressing for anyone. Least of all Rio Vidal.

    She wasn’t wearing this for her.

    Agatha relaxed her shoulders and tried to loosen the tension that had been nesting there all afternoon. The tie still dangled around her neck, taunting her. She thought briefly about leaving it off. Maybe that would make the whole thing feel less formal. But then she imagined the District Club’s chandeliers, the polished wood floors, the goddamn violins, and changed her mind. She’d rather choke on the thing than show up looking like she hadn’t tried.

That moment when Rio’s thumb brushing the line of her jaw, the whisper of breath between them, the nearness. The way Agatha had pulled back but not because she didn’t want it, but because she’d wanted it too much and she was afraid wanting it out loudly might burn the whole thing down. She paused; the knot still loose. Her thoughts softened without meaning to; I hope she notices.

A little too tight, so she adjusted it again, fingers smoothing down the length with more care than she’d give any other accessory in her entire goddamn life.

    Agatha glanced at herself again with the shirt tucked, tie straight, sleeves neat, boots still waiting.

    She’d never worn this much intention on her body before. Everything she normally wore had been curated to deflect or disappear in order to survive a world that never gave her space to want more than what was necessary. Yet tonight she wanted something unspeakably small and stupid. A glance across the room, or even a soft smile that lasted a second longer than it should. A compliment, maybe. The brush of Rio’s eyes tracking down the line of her sleeves, catching on the clean knot at her throat, and flicking back up.

    “You too”? Too simple. Obvious.
