    She sat down slowly on the small bench by the door, reached for the boots, and tugged them on with the kind of reverence she usually reserved for her service weapon. They fit tight across the top of her foot, snug around the ankle. New leather, not yet broken in, creaked faintly as she stood. Finally, she slipped the suit jacket over her shoulders and took a step back. The full picture stared back from the mirror now, Agatha in the crisp white shirt, black tie knotted with sharp lines, pressed black trousers, clean leather boots laced tight. The black jacket hugged just right through her shoulders, the sleeves grazing her wrists. Sleek and sharp enough to impress even the captain.

    It should’ve felt like some power move, but Agatha stared at her reflection, arms limp at her sides and felt like she was wearing someone else’s skin. She looked fine; good, even. If anyone else were watching, they might say she cleaned up nice. Hell, William would probably whistle and crack a joke about how she looked like she could headline a detective noir flick. And Rio— well, Agatha didn’t even want to go there; at least not yet. She shifted her weight and stared harder.

    This version of her—suit and shine and all—isn’t who she usually was. Not in the everyday, not even on good days. She was rougher than this with a little scuff around the edges, and she liked it that way. She liked her old boots with the worn heel and the loose leather jackets she never bothered to condition. With her jeans faded by time, cuffed twice at the bottom, dusted in motor oil or ash from working late in the garage fixing the truck. She liked her hair shoved back in a low ponytail with frizzy flyaways curling around her ears that was practical and barely brushed. That version of her was easier to carry.

    This? The shine and polish of it made her feel like she’d stepped into a costume.

    Agatha frowned. She didn’t like this feeling.

    It wasn’t vanity because she wasn’t trying to impress anyone. At least that’s what she kept telling herself, but something about tonight made her skin itch with self-awareness. Every wrinkle she smoothed, every adjustment she made, it all felt like trying too hard and still falling short. She hated that she didn’t know why.

    The detective hated the idea that it might have something to do with Rio walking into the same room later tonight, also dressed up. Agatha sighed, frustrated. Her fingers twitched at her sides while she tried standing straighter, shifting her stance the way she did in front of the captain with her weight even, chin high and shoulders back. It still didn’t look right. Her eyes drifted to the mirror again, gaze catching on her waistline, then lower.

    There was a pause. A thought.

    She turned from the mirror and padded softly toward the bedroom. The light there was low, a warm bedside lamp throwing shadows across the quilt. She crossed to the dresser, pulled open the third drawer down, and pushed past a stack of worn undershirts to the back corner. She didn’t reach for it right away but stood there for a second, fingers hovering. Then she pulled it out: the soft, familiar weight of her packer—a simple one. Flesh-toned, pliable, not built to perform, but to exist. She ran her thumb over the edge of the silicone slowly, as if reacquainting herself with the feeling.

    She didn’t wear it every day.

    Hell, she barely wore it most weeks. Only when she felt off, like her center of gravity was a little out of reach. When something bigger loomed like an interrogation she had to command or a precinct full of brass she needed to stand tall in front of. On nights like this one, when the mirror didn’t reflect the full truth of who she was.

    Agatha knew her strength came in calloused palms, in the easy lean against a doorway or low rumble of her laugh when it caught people off guard. But there was something about the weight of the packer resting low on her hips that straightened her spine. It didn’t make her someone else, but it helped her feel more like herself.

    So, she unbuckled her belt and slipped her trousers down. Agatha stepped into the harness with practiced ease, drawing the strap up snug over her hips. She adjusted it carefully, the silicone resting against her, firm and familiar. Then she pulled her boxers back into place, smoothing the fabric over the ridge, settling it low.

    The weight was minor and barely noticeable if she wasn’t thinking about it. God, it helped; the hollow feeling in her chest began to still. She ran her palms down her thighs, then over her hips, pausing briefly over the spot where it rested. Re-orienting.

    There it was.

    The shift in her posture wasn’t obvious but she felt it. Agatha’s chin lifted more naturally now with a stance that widened slightly. Her legs planted more firmly on the floor while she looked down and gave a small, half-scoff of a laugh.

    Not because it was funny, exactly, but because she knew how stupid this would sound to anyone else. It was a piece of silicone and not magic but for her, it was something.

    She slipped her trousers back up, buttoned them clean, adjusted her belt until it sat right. Agatha tugged the hem of her shirt down and squared her shoulders while stepping back in front of the mirror.

    Now she saw herself again. A butch woman who didn’t need permission to take up space. Who didn’t need to soften her edges to be beautiful or polish her image to be worthy. A woman who’d been called intimidating and cold but still cared enough to iron her shirt just right, to tuck her tie flat and make sure she showed up with dignity.

    Maybe she even wanted to look like someone Rio could stand beside. Not for show, and certainly not to blend in with the straight couples at this fancy-ass country club; but just to meet her there as equals and as the women they both were.

    Agatha stared at her reflection for a long time then she gave a quiet nod to the woman staring back. Now, finally, she was ready.

    The District Club glowed like a monument.

    Marble stairs curved up from the valet loop, slick with a wet sheen from the snow that hadn’t quite stuck yet left everything shining under the streetlamps. Agatha climbed them slowly, her new boots landing too cleanly against the white stone, the soft click of her heels echoing under the arched entry. A gold-trimmed doorman opened the glass-paneled door without a word, nodding as she passed like this was just another evening and the place didn’t smell like wealth and the authority that never had to raise its voice.

    Inside, the warmth hit her all at once. Soft violin music floated from somewhere unseen; live, not recorded. The chandelier overhead looked like it belonged in a museum: ten feet wide and dripping in crystal, refracting light into a thousand tiny glints across the vaulted ceiling. The marble floors caught it too, polished to a blinding mirror-shine that made Agatha immediately wish she’d scuffed her boots on the sidewalk outside. The lobby opened into the main ballroom if you could even call it that. It looked more like a palace than a precinct function. A wide domed ceiling stretched overhead with gilded arches, warm uplighting casting golden halos on every surface. Officers and their dates moved through the space in dark suits and gleaming badges, silk dresses and gold earrings. Laughter drifted like champagne bubbles across the room.

    People milled near a circular fountain centerpiece, the kind with a Grecian statue in the middle and tiny floating lights bobbing in the shallow water. To the right, a long bar spanned nearly an entire wall, all mirror-backed and glowing bottles, where someone in formalwear poured neat scotches and champagne flutes in quick succession. The scent of garlic butter and charred steak carried faintly on the air from behind a half-closed banquet door. Agatha didn’t move at first.

    She hovered just inside the entrance, her tie tugged ever so slightly looser at the collar to keep from choking. Her breath caught on something invisible as she took in the crowd.

    Too many bodies covered in expensive fabric. All accompanied by smiles that looked practiced and pinned in place. Everyone looked comfortable here, like they’d grown up attending fundraisers or knew instinctively how to hold a wine glass the right way.

    This wasn’t her world; she’d never had been to events like this since they always made her feel like a loose thread in a tailored suit. As if someone was going to pull her aside at any second and say, “Sorry, this space wasn’t meant for people like you.”

    Her fingers flexed at her sides, a half-second away from reaching for a cigarette she didn’t carry anymore. Her old habits didn’t fit the uniform tonight.

    The music swelled gently as a new song started—orchestral and aching—it made her want to bolt.

    Agatha hated the way people stared too long at her in a suit like they were trying to decide if it was flattering or defiant. The whole thing felt like a performance with fake compliments and everyone pretending they didn’t work twelve-hour shifts filling paperwork and chasing drunken suspects. Still, she was here because she was supposed to be. It apparently mattered to someone that she show up clean and pressed and proper. Even with the tie and the confidence and hours spent convincing herself that it didn’t matter if she belonged, some part of her still itched under the scrutiny. Her hair, down and curled now in loose, careful waves, felt like it belonged to someone else. Not the version of her that lived in squad cars or always kept her boots scuffed.

    Someone brushed past her, murmuring a polite “excuse me” as they passed into the main hall. Agatha followed, slowly, weaving through the growing crowd.

    As her boots clicked across the marble and the chandeliers glittered overhead, Agatha couldn’t help the tension curling under her ribs. Every nerve was stretched tight in her spine from anticipation. Even though this wasn’t about Rio and she told herself over and over that this was just another precinct function—nothing more—some deep, stupid part of her kept waiting.

    Not for some speech or drink, but for her; and that more than anything was what terrified her the most.

    Agatha spotted them through the low hum of movement and violin. William’s familiar grin catching the light while he was already half- standing from his chair at a front table near the edge of the dance floor, waving her over with a crooked arm and that signature blend of charm. Lilia sat beside him, poised as ever in an elegant maroon dress with a sharp neckline and silver cuffs. Her chin lifted slightly when she caught sight of Agatha, offering a small nod, one that managed to carry warmth, and a quiet thank God, you're here.

    Their table was stationed right at the front, tucked beneath a soft canopy of chandelier light and positioned just beside the sprawling parquet of the ballroom floor. All dark wood and glowing candlelight centerpieces, their seats weren’t just good but were almost important.

    Agatha hesitated for the barest breath, the simple act of being called forward like that to a front table at the center of the room made her throat dry up again. She tugged gently at the hem of her suit jacket, let out a slow breath through her nose, and stepped forward.

    As she moved through the crowd, she felt the weight of the room around her. The sound of forks tapping glass, the rich murmur of conversation, the quiet shh of silk brushing past wool blurred into something ambient as her boots clicked softly across the marble. Her stride evened out, the rhythm steady now and the earlier nerves melting.

    Halfway to the table, a server appeared beside her with his black vest and a tray of amber glasses nestled into crystal grooves. He offered one with a quiet gesture.

    Agatha didn’t hesitate.

    She plucked the glass from the tray with a grateful nod, fingers wrapping around the cool curve of cut crystal. The whiskey inside caught the light like liquid fire and she brought it to her lips in one clean movement. The burn hit instantly—it stung at the edges of her throat, curling hot in her chest and settling beneath her ribs, loosening something tight in her shoulders.

    This was good, she needed this little heat.

    She reached the table just as William leaned in, clapping a hand to his chest in exaggerated awe.

    “Look at you,” he said, eyes wide, “our very own goddamn James Bond.” Agatha snorted softly, raising her glass halfway in a mock toast. “Don’t make me regret it already.”

    Lilia gave her a once-over, eyes scanning from boots to collar, and offered a small, knowing smile. “You clean up nice, Harkness.”

    Agatha dropped into the chair beside them, letting the candlelight flicker across her glass, and said dryly, “Don’t get used to it.” Now in the warmth of the whiskey and the glint of crystal featuring the muted hum of live strings behind her, she almost didn’t mind being seen. The moment Agatha sat herself into the chair, William leaned dramatically across the table like he was about to deliver a prophecy. “You know,” he said, swirling the ice in his untouched cocktail with a flourish, “you’re dangerously close to causing a heart attack tonight, Harkness.” Agatha raised an unimpressed brow over her glass. “You’re annoying me already.”

    William ignored her. “I’m just saying, you walk in here looking like some hot secret agent and you didn’t expect me to react?” He let out a low whistle. “That suit’s doing God’s work. Handsome as hell.” Agatha smirked, but there was a flush creeping high under her collar that she tried to smother with a long sip.

    Lilia didn’t even look up from adjusting the bracelets on her wrist. “It’s earned.”

    Agatha chuckled, but William wasn’t finished.

    He leaned in with a wolfish grin, eyes narrowing like he was about to light the match. “Oh, so ladies like...Rio?”

    Agatha’s smirk twitched. “Don’t.”

    William’s grin only widened. “Ohhh my god.”

    “Kaplan—”

    “I am not—”

    Agatha rolled her eyes so hard she nearly gave herself a headache. “God, you’re like a twelve-year-old.”

    “Yeah, well, you’re the one over here kicking imaginary dirt with your boots and getting all flustered over your little crush.”

    “It’s not—” Agatha started, then stopped herself. “I’m not flustered.” William leaned back smugly, folding his arms across his chest. “That’s what someone that’s so flustered would say.”

Agatha tried not to react, so she took another slow sip of her drink instead, pretending it didn’t matter. Lilia glanced at her, then down at the flickering candlelight between them. “I talked to her this morning,” she said. “She was... hesitant.” “Hesitant like ‘nervous about seeing a room full of people’ or hesitant like ‘trying to ghost the entire department again’?” William asked. Lilia’s eyes flicked toward Agatha. “More the former, she said she didn’t know if it was a good idea to come but didn’t say why.”

    Agatha’s thumb ran absently along the rim of her glass. She didn’t trust her voice, so she didn’t speak. The warmth she’d felt from the drink earlier was curling differently, caught somewhere in her chest.

    William glanced between the two women, then nudged Lilia. “Ten bucks says she walks in mid-toast, looks like a movie star, and pretends like she wasn’t late on purpose.”

    “She’s not dramatic,” Agatha muttered without looking up. William snorted. “Aggs, she would wear turtlenecks in July. I guarantee she’s dramatic.”

    Lilia gave a small smile as she folded her hands neatly on the table. “If she shows, she’ll make it count.”

    Agatha said nothing, just reached for her glass again with fingers a little too tight around the stem, heart already thudding. Even if she didn’t want to admit it out loud, she was hoping Rio would show up more than she wanted to.

    The minute Agatha set her glass back on the table again, William propped his elbow dramatically against the linen-draped edge and stared at her like a puzzle he couldn’t quite figure out but was very much enjoying the process of trying.

    “You know,” he said, swirling his drink with far too much flourish, “I always assumed you were allergic to ironing.”

    Agatha cut him a dry look. “I am. I broke out in hives halfway through steaming this shirt.”

    William ignored the retort, eyes raking over her ensemble again with performative awe. “But here you are in a crisp collar, polished boots and tie like you walked out of some lesbian Bond reboot...I mean, Harkness—who even are you right now?”

    William leaned in like he was sharing a secret. “And look this good doing it? Tragic you didn’t discover suits earlier. You could’ve had the whole city at your feet by now. Maybe even me if you played your cards right.” “God help us all,” Agatha muttered.

    Lilia who had been silently nursing her drink and watching them with quiet amusement finally let out a soft laugh, her eyes warm as they flicked over to Agatha.

    “He’s not wrong,” she said, her voice smoother than her cocktail, and just as potent. “You do look nice.”

    Agatha was caught a little off guard, Lilia didn’t compliment lightly but when she did it was never plain flattery, but real admiration. “Thanks,” Agatha said quietly, brushing a wrinkle off her thigh that didn’t exist. “I clean up when I need to.”

    Lilia gave her a slow, knowing smile, then leaned in just slightly close enough that William, who was now distracted trying to balance a cherry stem on the rim of his glass, wouldn’t catch it.

    “Did you maybe,” she said quietly, “clean-up for someone?” Agatha didn’t respond right away, she stared down into the amber swirl of her drink, the light from the chandelier catching the glass and turning it gold. Her throat tightened, the muscles ticked in her jaw. When she looked back up, Lilia saw a flicker of vulnerability. This woman would’ve died before admitting the truth out loud but couldn’t quite hide it this time from someone who knew her too well.

    Lilia, perceptive as ever, didn’t press.

    She just reached over with the ease and patted Agatha’s thigh once, like a hand on your shoulder.

    Then she leaned in closer and murmured, “I hope you get your girl.” Agatha didn’t say anything, yet her mouth twitched at the corner as her eyes flicked toward the door without even meaning to.

    Lilia saw that too and smiled.

    Eventually they’d settled into the kind of lull only good food and low lighting could coax out of a room full of cops pretending to be civilized. Plates half-finished, glasses sweating faintly onto crisp white linen. The band had switched to a low waltz that nobody was dancing to, the soft shimmer of violins threading through the hum of conversation. Agatha had just leaned back in her chair, one arm slung over the back lazily as she reached for her drink. The whiskey was smoother now, the warmth settled in her chest. Across from her, William was saying something animated about someone’s poorly-tied bowtie. Lilia chuckled quietly at his expense.

    Then William’s whole face lit up, his posture straightening. “Oh,” he said, voice pitched just loud enough to cut through the noise between them. “Oh my God.”

    Agatha frowned, turning just slightly. “What?”

    William leaned in, grin splitting wide. “Look who just walked in.” Agatha didn’t even need to look, she felt the pressure change in the room like a fuse was being lit.

    Rio.

    There, standing just inside the entrance, framed by the massive gold and glass doors and the glittering chandelier overhead.

    She wore black. A very short dress with long sleeves and an open back that revealed the smooth, unbroken line of her spine, every inch of her posture elegant. Her heels—tall, sharp—clicked against the marble. Her hair, always somewhere between professional and soft, was pinned up tonight, leaving her neck bare. The bare line of her throat, the clean sweep of her collarbone catching in the low light.

    Agatha’s breath caught before she could stop it.

    Her fingers tightened involuntarily around the glass, and she swallowed hard, the whiskey suddenly bitter and dry in her mouth. Her pulse jumped somewhere beneath her jaw with a stuttering thud that felt too loud in the quiet swell of her chest.

    She hadn’t prepared for the way her eyes would lock with Rio’s across the room, nor the way Rio smiled when she spotted her. It was too much. Agatha felt herself stand before she even realized she was doing it. It wasn’t planned but it happened like muscle memory or a reflex. As if some part of her knew it wasn’t possible to stay seated when Rio Vidal walked into a room looking like that. The sudden bone-deep impulse to meet her halfway as Agatha’s chair scraped faintly behind her.

    She only saw Rio.

    Rio, who held her gaze the entire walk across the floor, even as she passed officers and servers and glittering decor. Even as others turned to look at her, because how could they not? She didn’t glance at any of them as her eyes never left Agatha.

    The older woman hadn’t realized she’d shifted her weight forward, like her body had started walking again without her permission, but now she was frozen with one foot angled out and shoulders squared halfway between panic. She inhaled through her nose, smoothing a hand down the front of her jacket before she could second guess it.

    Don’t be stupid, she told herself. Say something.

    Rio opened her mouth at the same time Agatha did—but before either of them could speak, William shot out of his chair like a spring-loaded bottle rocket.

    “Holy shit, Vidal,” he blurted, wide-eyed and grinning like someone had handed him front-row tickets to the opera. “You can’t just show up here looking like that. It’s so rude.”

Her cheeks flushed pink immediately, delicate and visible even under the soft golden lights of the ballroom. “Looking like what?” she asked gently, almost bashful in a way Agatha had rarely seen. William gestured broadly, like he was trying to encompass all of her at once. “Like you walked out of some movie as everyone’s fantasy? She dropped her hands, brushing the side of her trousers like she was smoothing out a wrinkle. In reality, her palms were starting to sweat.

    If Agatha’s heart ached quietly behind her ribs, well, no one had to know. His suit looked decent, if a little lived-in, the tie knotted in a way that screamed that he tried.     Agatha felt her spine tighten a little at that with recognition or repulsion, she wasn’t sure.

    Agatha noticed it instantly. The way her shoulders tightened with a shift in her jaw as if bracing for impact. Her expression didn’t change—too well practiced—but her hands curled in her lap, fingers threading together like she needed grounding.

    Something about the way she sat made Agatha’s chest pull tight. Without thinking, Agatha leaned forward slightly and caught her gaze for a second. Rio glanced up, and the moment their eyes met, Agatha gave her a small, subtle nod. The kind that didn’t say anything specific, I’m here. Rio inhaled a little deeper and Agatha didn’t look away until her shoulders relaxed the tiniest bit.

    Delaney, mercifully unaware, was still going.

    “—recognitions tonight for long-standing service, for integrity in the field, and for leadership on critical cases that helped move the needle in a year where every damn inch counts. So let’s not drag this out any longer, huh?” He flipped a page on the podium and leaned in, squinting at the first name. Agatha sat back, the tension easing out of her shoulders in slow increments, but her attention remained half on Rio. She watched the way Rio’s fingers relaxed, how her eyes flicked toward the stage and then back to her untouched drink.

    Whatever Wanda’s presence, or even the mention of her had stirred up, it was still lurking beneath the surface.

    Agatha’s knee bounced beneath the table, a familiar rhythm of nerves and restless energy, but she tamped it down. She reached again for her glass and took a slower sip this time. Not because she needed it but because Rio might. If this night was going to unravel, Agatha wanted to be eased enough to hold it together for them both.

    The ballroom had shifted into applause now as Delaney read the first commendation, but Agatha barely heard it.

    Her eyes lingered on Rio. Under the music and the sound of polite clapping echoing off marble floors, Agatha thought: You’re not alone. Not tonight.

    Rio held her gaze a second longer than she meant to. Then she blinked and looked down at her glass, letting Delaney’s voice wash over the room again. “…and it wouldn’t be a proper awards dinner without recognizing the people who do the real work,” he said, flipping a card for dramatic effect. “Which brings me to our first commendation of the night.” He glanced up, letting the pause stretch just a little too long. “For continued excellence in managing a team of lunatics, especially one known pain in the ass in particular—Detective Lilia Calderu.” A ripple of laughter broke out across the room. William let out a cheer and clapped excitedly. Agatha, without hesitation, raised both hands and flipped a solid, unapologetic middle finger in everyone’s direction, lips twisted in a wolfish grin. Lilia groaned under her breath but pushed back her chair with a grace that made the movement look effortless.

    “Finally,” William said, still clapping as she walked past him. “Justice for the long-suffering.”

    “She’s not that long-suffering,” Agatha muttered, but the spark in her eyes said otherwise.

    Rio couldn’t help it, she laughed too. A real laugh that was unguarded in the way it slipped past her lips.

    As Lilia stepped up toward the stage and Delaney offered her the small plaque with a wink, Rio then became quiet and still.

    She was too aware of the woman sitting across from her.

    Agatha hadn’t said much since she stood up to greet her, hadn’t spoken more than a few polite nothings over the din of clinking glasses and passing trays. But she didn’t need to when Rio could feel every ounce of attention still quietly tilted in her direction. Her suit fit her like armor, and her sleeves were rolled up just enough to show her forearms, the slim black tie, neatly knotted now, hanging sharp against her chest.

    Rio felt her pulse thud beneath the delicate chain of her necklace. She didn’t mean to stare, not really. But her eyes kept finding their way back and drawn again to Agatha’s collarbone, the clean line of her shoulder under the dark suit jacket. The woman was magnetic in a way that wasn’t flashy. Rio could feel it thrumming low in her chest anyway, that quiet gravity.

    Every once in a while, Agatha would glance at her in a sidelong look too brief to be anything but accidental; each time it happened, Rio felt her heart thud like she’d been caught doing something illicit.

    Maybe she had. Right now, seated here in a room full of colleagues and noise, all she could think about was the exact moment she’d first spotted Agatha from across the room and how she’d nearly forgotten how to breathe.

    Her suit had clung in all the right ways along with her presence that even from a distance, made the din of chatter feel like static. And how for a split second, she’d almost turned around to leave, not because she didn’t want to be here but because it scared the hell out of her how badly she did. Now, watching Agatha smile and tease and give the finger to half the room without blinking made Rio’s throat go dry. She blinked slowly and forced her gaze to the stage, clapping as Lilia accepted her plaque and gave Delaney a dry, unimpressed nod in return.

    The applause rose up around them like a soft tide, and still, Rio’s eyes drifted back to Agatha.

    The room had dulled into a soft blur with crystal glasses catching warm chandelier light, violinists threading their music through the clink of silverware and the captain’s voice, still droning on somewhere up at the mic. Names were called, commendations given and polite laughter rose and fell around her like waves, but Rio barely registered any of it. Her gaze was fixed.

    Agatha’s hands were folded loosely on the white linen tablecloth, the lean shape of her body tucked cleanly into that tailored suit like she’d been poured into it. The jacket fit snug around her shoulders, tapering down the sharp line of her torso. Her tie sat perfectly knotted, the ends swaying gently every time she shifted. The fabric caught on her chest when she leaned forward to murmur something to William, and Rio felt it like a hook low in her stomach.

    God, she looked so good it almost hurt.

    Agatha looked masculine in a way that was entirely hers. It was her unique identity pressed into each detail. The rolled cuffs, the collar sitting proud against her neck, the ring on her finger that Rio now couldn’t stop tracking whenever Agatha lifted her glass to sip. It all made her look so sure of herself, grounded in this version of womanhood that Rio had always quietly worshiped from a distance.

    The kind of masculinity that had a purpose, it didn’t demand space but occupied it effortlessly.

    Agatha’s hair had caught Rio off guard from the moment she’d walked in. It seemed like she had run her hands through it one too many times before leaving the house. The warm lights caught in those waves, painting them amber and bronze at the tips, making Rio’s fingers twitch with the ache to touch them. She wanted to reach across the table, tangle her hand in that soft brown and tug gently, just to see what Agatha would do, to see if she’d lean in.

    But she couldn’t.

    They hadn’t come here together, it was by design and Rio knew that. Agatha had drawn that line and Rio had honored it without complain since she understood. Wanda was still hovering in the background like a threat neither of them could quite shake, her presence a weight around Rio’s neck. She knew Agatha had pulled back because it wasn’t safe and she didn’t want to be the reason things got worse.

    She wished they were here under different circumstances.

    Rio wished they’d walked in together. That she’d had the right to trail a hand along Agatha’s arm as they entered, to lean close and whisper something reckless about how good she looked. To feel her lean back with that sly smirk Rio had only ever seen in private moments, the one that said, yeah, I know.

    She wanted to be sitting next to her, not across from her. Close enough to feel the heat off Agatha’s body, to hear her breathing change when Rio leaned in. She wanted to slip her hand beneath the table and lace their fingers together. Trace lazy lines over the back of her knuckles. She wanted to slide her fingers along Agatha’s tie, curl them around the silk and tug just slightly.

    A quiet, unspoken command.

    Come here.

    Agatha would come, Rio knew she would; that was the problem, she imagined it far too vividly.

    Leaning into her ear, her voice soft and low. “You always wear the hell out of a uniform Agatha, but this is unfair.”

    Agatha, flushed and biting the inside of her cheek to keep from smiling, would murmur something dry in return. “How unfair?”

    Rio, without missing a beat would reply in a low voice, barely above a whisper. “Get in the car with me, and I’ll show you how unfair it is.” The thought made heat crawl down the back of her neck.

    She could picture the end of the night, too. No bright lights, no polished marble, no voices. Just Agatha, standing in the doorway of her apartment in that suit. The tie undone and jacket tossed over a chair, with those blue eyes now dark watching her with that measured stillness that always meant she was deciding whether to let go or stay quiet.

    Rio would be the one to take the decision away.

    She’d step forward and thread both hands into Agatha’s hair, messy and already half-tangled. The brown eyed woman would whisper her name before leaning in and pulling her close by the knot of her loosened tie. Close enough to feel her breath, her mouth. Rio knew Agatha would let her; she’d sigh into her in surrender.

    Rio blinked and reached for her water glass instead of the wine. Her mouth was suddenly too dry.

    Across the table, Agatha rolled her shoulders beneath the jacket like it didn’t quite sit right, maybe she wasn’t used to wearing it this long. Her fingers, those strong, blunt hands that Rio thought about way too often, reached up to adjust the cuff again. Her thumb brushed the edge of the button, and Rio stared by the easy way her hands moved. Capable of wrecking her in every way.

    She wanted them on her; against her ribs, her throat, pressing into her hips with purpose. She wanted to feel that weight again, wanted to be held like Agatha had something to prove and no reason to hold back. Rio bit the inside of her lip and forced her gaze away, back to the stage where someone else was getting recognized for something she couldn’t even pretend to care about.

    She tried to focus on the speech, the names; but every second her attention drifted back to Agatha.

    Back to the curve of her neck, the rise and fall of her chest under that buttoned-up shirt, the gentle shake of her foot beneath the table that betrayed how nervous she still was, no matter how calm she looked. Rio’s heart ached with it. The wanting, the not-having. The knowledge that she could reach across the table right now and Agatha might even let her, but not here. Not like this.

    So instead, she watched and let the want simmer behind her eyes, burn soft behind her ribs. Rio held onto the quiet ache of sitting just close enough to taste the distance between them.

    When Agatha looked up suddenly and caught her staring, Rio didn’t look away. She held her gaze and let the longing show, albeit this was all she was allowed to have tonight and perhaps forever.

    Agatha shifted in her seat deliberately, the way someone might when they felt eyes on them. And Rio knew hers were obvious, she hadn’t tried to look away. She couldn’t. Not with the air between them stretched so thin it felt like the room might tear in half if either of them moved too suddenly. Agatha stared back in that cool and unreadable in a way that made Rio’s lungs tighten. There was something simmering underneath all that practiced stillness that was hot and too steady to be accidental.

    Then, with that same calculated ease, Agatha reached for her drink. The thick glass of whiskey sat waiting near her right hand, catching the low light of the chandelier above and painting her fingers gold as she wrapped them around it. She lifted it slowly, her gaze still locked on Rio’s the entire time. A silent challenge, or a signal, maybe both.

    Her fingers were sure around the rim, confident in a way that made Rio's breath catch. The gesture shouldn’t mean anything but did. Everything Agatha did was unintentional seduction wrapped in her muscle memory. And now, under the glow of marble and music and polished decor, it was almost cruel.

    Agatha didn’t raise the glass all the way to her lips at first. She held it in the space between them with one elbow propped lazily on the edge of the table, the other resting low in her lap as if the glass itself were an extension of her stare.

    She tilted it just slightly toward Rio.

    A toast.

    Merely the ghost of a smirk on her lips and the barest lift of her chin. A small, precise gesture meant only for her. Agatha brought the glass to her lips, finally, and drank slow.

    It was natural and quietly devastating. The way her throat worked when she swallowed, the flex of muscle down the column of her neck. Blue eyes never left Rio’s even as the liquid slid past her lips, over her tongue, and down—slow, steady. As if she knew what she was doing and she wanted Rio to think about it.

    Rio watched her like a woman starved. Imagined what it would be like to be the thing in that glass; the burn that weighted like heat. To be swallowed slow and the reason Agatha’s chest warmed from the inside out. To live inside her for even a minute, carried somewhere deep and secret. She imagined tracing the path of that sip with her mouth. Down Agatha’s throat, across her collarbone, lower still. She imagined kissing her there, pressing her tongue to the hollow just beneath her jaw, where her pulse fluttered. Where her skin would still taste like whiskey. She imagined pulling her in by that perfect tie, sliding onto her lap and saying all the things she hadn’t dared whisper yet. Let me stay.

    It was unbearable, this restraint. The hunger of it. How they were both dressed for the kind of night that should end in a kiss against the side of a car, or a shirt pulled loose in the dark, but instead they sat on opposite sides of a table, surrounded by noise and playing pretend.

    A soft tap landed on her shoulder—quick.

    Rio blinked and the ballroom came rushing back in pieces; the clink of cutlery on porcelain, Delaney’s grating voice still grinding through some tired paragraph about “partnerships across state lines,” and the soft hum of string music looping quietly beneath it all.

    She turned her head slightly and found William grinning at her like a child. “Hey,” he whispered with faux innocence, leaning far too close and shielding his mouth like he was about to reveal a state secret. “Serious question.”

    Rio arched a brow. “That’s never once been true.”

    He ignored her and jutted his chin toward the far corner of the dance floor, where two cops from another precinct were huddled near the bar. One was tall and broad-shouldered with a shiny gold chain peeking from beneath his unbuttoned collar, the other leaner, darker, laughing into his drink like he owned the room.

    “Okay,” William whispered dramatically. “Chain Guy or Smirky in Black— who do I let ruin me tonight?”

    Rio blinked slowly. “You’re joking, William.”

    “I mean look at them,” he hissed, elbowing her gently. “I feel like one’s gonna call me ‘bro’ and the other’s gonna whisper poetry into my collarbone. Either way, I’m dying a little happy.”

    Rio fought a laugh, still warm from the way Agatha’s eyes had just burned into hers. “Go with the poet. At least he’ll text you in complete sentences after ghosting you.”

    William sighed dreamily. “You always know just what to say.” He leaned back in his chair, eyes still trained on the pair across the room like he was mentally measuring them for a tuxedo or handcuffs, or maybe both. Then, after a long moment of silence where Rio dared to glance at Agatha again, William made a suspiciously innocent sound. She turned. “What.”

    He smiled sweetly. “Soooo... speaking of people who make us feel things —”

    Rio narrowed her eyes.

    He only grinned wider. “Detective Harkness. Your date…or not-date. Or emotionally tortured handsome dream in a suit? Thoughts?” Rio fought hard not to let the flush crawl up her neck.

    “She’s not my—”

    William raised both hands. “Okay, okay, fine. Your professional source of heart palpitations. That better?”

    Rio stared at him flatly. “She’s very... formal tonight.” William choked on a laugh. “Formal? That the best you’ve got? She looks like she walked out of a fucking dreamscape noir film to steal my girl and solve a double homicide with only a cigarette.”

    Rio bit the inside of her cheek to keep from smiling. “She does look... really good.”

    “Really good?” William gaped. “Rio, you were practically eye-fucking. Don’t play modest now.”

    “I was not—”

    “You were,” he insisted, smirking. “And she was drinking like she knew it too. Honestly? Kind of iconic. Power move.”

    Rio groaned softly and gave a deep sigh as she tilted her head back to look at the chandelier above them. “God, I didn’t even know she owned a suit.” William gave a low whistle, still watching her sideways. “If she walked in like that and told me to commit a felony, I wouldn’t even ask.” “She could just...command a room,” Rio murmured before she could stop herself.

    William tilted his head. “And you, apparently.”

    She shot him a look, but it didn’t have much bite.

    He smiled, softer this time, nudging her elbow. “Hey. Jokes aside... it’s nice to see you like this.”

    “Like what?”

    He shrugged. “Wanting something for yourself and not hiding it as well as you think.”

    Rio looked down at her glass, tracing a finger around the rim. Before she could reply, Delaney’s voice thundered louder from the stage clearly reaching the final stretch of his speech and the room began to shift again, attention snapping forward, murmurs quieting.

    William leaned closer and whispered, “You should ask her to dance.” “Don’t be ridiculous,” Rio muttered, eyes still forward, spine just a little too straight in her chair. “I can’t ask her to dance.”

    William leaned back like she’d just told him the sky was fake. “You can’t?” Rio scowled lightly. “No, we’re just partners for the case. That’s it.” He squinted. “Partners who yearn like Victorian lovers and stare at each other like they’ve been reincarnated to be together for three lifetimes? Right.”

    She shot him a flat look. “You’re being dramatic.”

    “I’m being observant,” he replied with a flourish, tossing a bread roll into his mouth. “There’s a difference. One of us studied theater in college and the other is lying to herself.”

    Rio laughed despite herself, but her fingers were still white-knuckling the stem of her glass.

    William tilted his head, softer now. “Come on. You really think you’re just her coworker?”

    Rio didn’t answer right away.

    She looked back toward the stage where Delaney was still droning on about something of mutual aid task forces or the logistics of interagency resource pooling. Her gaze slid from the podium to the small cluster of officers seated nearby, then drifted, inevitably, back to Agatha.

    She was laughing quietly at something Lilia had leaned over to say. Her hand rested lightly on her thigh, rings catching the light, tie just barely askew. Her posture was relaxed now, and Rio couldn’t look too long without feeling like she might fold in half with the weight of it. “She’s...” Rio paused, then looked down at the hem of her dressed and smoothed it out like it might hide the sound of her vulnerability. “She’s important to me.”

    William turned fully toward her now, one leg crossed over the other, chin propped in his hand like he was settling in.

    “I know,” he said quietly. “And she knows too.”

    Rio let the silence sit for a moment, heavy with the kind of honesty that made her want to crawl out of her skin.

    “It’s not that simple,” she muttered.

    He nodded, like he’d expected that answer.

    “Because of Miss Maximoff.”

    Rio didn’t reply.

    William sighed, tapping his fingers against the side of his glass. “Listen, I know I’m usually the idiot who asks you for romantic advice while I try to win the affection of married men or emotionally unavailable baristas, but I’m gonna say something kind of smart now, so brace yourself.” Rio looked at him sideways, one brow raised.

    William shrugged. “I think you’re punishing yourself.”

    “I think,” he continued, quietly but steadily, “you think that if you let yourself be happy—really happy—then you’ve failed whatever loyalty test Wanda’s guilt installed in your brain. If you enjoy anything, it means you’re irresponsible, or selfish, or not trying hard enough to fix what she broke.” Rio stared down at the tablecloth, the fine embroidery of it blurring under her gaze.

    “I know you,” he said softly. “You carry shit that’s not yours. I know Wanda made you feel like the only version of yourself is the one who follows rules and stays silent.”

    The knot in Rio’s throat felt sharp and sudden.

    “But Agatha doesn’t want that version of you. I don’t think you want that version of you.”

    Rio blinked, her eyes stinging.

    “She makes you different,” William added, gentler now. “Not in a fix-you kind of way. Just... I’ve seen it. You laugh more when she’s around. You stand taller. Even when it’s hard.”

    Rio let out a shaky breath, her tone coming unsteady. “It scares me.” William smiled, not teasing this time. “Of course it does. Wanting things is terrifying. But if you don’t reach for what matters, you end up stuck watching the person you love raise a glass to you from across the room, and all you can do is imagine what it would’ve been like to kiss her instead.” Rio went quiet, the words sinking in deep past the bravado and the caution, past the fear of Wanda and the fear of herself.

    She looked at Agatha again. At the way she adjusted her tie every so often like she hadn’t realized it was already perfect. How she laughed with her whole body when Lilia elbowed her in the ribs.

    God, she wanted to sit beside her and lean in close, to whisper some stupid compliment about how good she looked in a tie and mean every goddamn word. She wanted to rest her hand on Agatha’s thigh under the table. To hear her voice low in her ear instead of across a room full of people she couldn’t trust.

    Rio sighed. “I can’t ask her to dance.”

    William tilted his head again. “Why not?”

    “Because I don’t want to make her choose,” Rio said, quieter now. “Between keeping herself safe in front of Delaney and Wanda... or starting something with me.”

    She didn’t say the other part: and what if she wouldn’t choose me at all? William reached across the table, laying his hand over hers. “You don’t have to make her choose. You just have to show her you’ll stand with when she does.”

    Rio swallowed hard.

    William grinned, the warmth back in his voice. “Also, I think she’d say yes. Because, Rio, you look like a damn dream tonight and if she doesn’t drag you into the shadows and make out with you in the coat closet by midnight, I will personally file a complaint.”

    Rio gave an earnest laugh, a smile reaching her eyes.

    “You’re impossible,” she murmured.

    “I’m right,” he said, smug.

    She looked over at Agatha again. How she leaned in slightly toward Lilia, listening with that quiet intensity she always carried when someone spoke. Her blue eyes followed Lilia’s words with sharp focus, the crease between her brows softening just slightly as she nodded along. There was something about how Agatha gave people her full attention, that made Rio’s chest ache.

    She watched the small gestures Agatha made as she listened, the slow drag of her thumb along the rim of her glass, the way her jaw flexed when she was thinking, or the barely-there lift at the corner of her mouth when Lilia said something dry. Rio knew that look—half patience, half amusement. She’d seen it during their late-night case briefings and their conversations, but here, under soft golden lights, surrounded by wine glasses and low music, it struck her differently.

    There was a softness in her tucked beneath the crisp lines of her suit and the bite of her usual sharpness. Rio stared, helpless and reverent, at the woman who had learned how to be still and yet be so alive. Rio thought about what she wouldn’t give to be the one Agatha turned that kind of attention to tonight.

    Then, softly, Rio whispered to herself, “Yeah. You might be right.” Suddenly, Captain Delaney’s voice rolled out louder this time, gravelly and dry, with just enough grit to snap everyone’s attention back to the stage. “Alright,” he drawled, his tone amused, a little indulgent. “Before any of you get too cozy—or too drunk—to stand, I’ve got one more bit of ceremony to ramble through.”

    The murmur of conversation tapered off. Glasses were lowered. Heads turned toward the small riser at the front of the room where Delaney stood alone beneath the soft halo of a spotlight. Behind him, the string quartet began shifting quietly in their seats, bowing instruments and adjusting music stands. The low hum of tuning notes thrummed under his words. “All right, assholes.” Delaney stood at the front of the room as a man who hated formalwear but liked having the floor. His collar was crooked, and his glass was already three-quarters empty.

    “You’re not off the hook yet.”

    A few groans rose from the crowd.

    “I know you’ve all been trying to sneak out early, like I can’t see you fuckers sliding toward the exit every time the dessert trays come out.” He squinted dramatically. “But before you sprint back to whatever hole you crawled out of, I’ve got one more thing to torture you with.” Agatha looked up from her conversation with Lilia, brows knitting slightly. Across the table, Rio straightened in her chair, her smile fading into the kind of posture one adopts when they know they’re about to be dragged by name.

    Delaney took another sip, then pointed out into the crowd with mock accusation.

    “Now, I know most of you barely qualify as functional human beings once you're off duty. God knows I wouldn’t trust half of you to parallel park sober. But somehow, despite that, a few of you managed to clean yourselves up tonight.”

    There was scattered applause, ironic and self-congratulatory. “Don’t get cocky,” he snapped. “You’re not that impressive. I’ve seen mall cops with better grooming habits than some of you.”

    Laughter broke louder this time, a ripple of shoulders shaking around the room. Someone at a nearby table let out an audible snort. “So now that we’ve survived the speeches and the plaques, I figure we’ve earned a little suffering of a different kind.”

    He stepped aside with a dramatic sweep of one arm.

    “Ladies, gents, and everyone else who’s too emotionally damaged to commit, this one’s for you. Grab your sweetheart, your side piece, or that coworker you’re definitely not making eye contact with Monday, because it’s time for the slow dance.”

    The ballroom groaned.

    Even the quartet looked like they wanted hazard pay as they picked up their bows and started into the first few aching, romantic notes that were slow and indulgent, entirely too intimate for people used to keeping their emotions locked in filing cabinets.

    The first stroke of violin strings spilled into the air; rich, slow, and aching with intention. A soft, lingering melody seemed to pull the breath straight from the room.

    Overhead, the chandeliers dimmed to a very low amber glow, casting everything in a honeyed hush. Shadows stretched long across the floor as pockets of couples began to move toward each other, the space narrowing into intimacy. The music curled around them, tender and shamelessly romantic meant for hands to find waists and for cheeks to brush. Lilia’s mouth twitched into something smug as she stood up with a whisper of silk. “Well,” she murmured to the table of colleagues, “time to see if my knees still work the way they used to.”

    Agatha blinked. “Lilia—”

    “My husband knows I indulge in harmless extracurriculars,” she said smoothly, already walking toward a silver-haired man in a navy suit. “He married me for my charm. Not my fidelity.”

    She paused just long enough to glance over her shoulder, eyes glinting with wicked amusement.

    “I’ve been eyeing the guy from accounting for weeks,” she added. “Tonight, I’m cashing in.”

    With that, she turned back toward her target, heels clicking against the marble as she strode with unapologetic confidence. The man was tall, broad-shouldered, and smiling like someone who absolutely knew he was being sought out and opened his arms as she neared. They exchanged a few words neither Rio nor Agatha could hear, but the body language said everything.

    Agatha blinked as she watched her hand already sliding into the crook of his arm like she’d done it a hundred times before. He welcomed her with a warm, familiar smile and leaned in to say something that made her laugh— openly, unabashedly, her earrings catching the low light as she tilted her head back. They were already halfway into a slow sway by the time Agatha turned back toward William and Rio, disbelief etched into every line of her face.

    She leaned across the table, eyes still wide. “What the fuck was that?” she questioned. “Did you know Lilia wanted Bill from HR?”

    William didn’t even look surprised. He gave a casual shrug and reached for what remained of his cocktail. “Of course I knew,” he said breezily. “He brings her her favorite soup from Panera every Tuesday. Guy’s got rizz.” Agatha blinked, uncomprehending. “He’s got what?”

    “Rizz,” William repeated, as if she were the one being slow. “Charisma. Old man charm. C’mon, Harkness, keep up.”

    Agatha stared at him like he’d grown a second head. “You need to stop talking to the interns.”

    William smirked. “Tell that to Bill. The man’s pulling a high-level seasoned detective with nothing but team building policies.”

    Rio let out a quiet laugh from beside him, her glass half-covering her mouth. Agatha, watching the edges of her smile peek through, felt something warm curl low in her chest. The music floated through the air now, syrupy and slow, and more couples had begun rising from their tables to move onto the dance floor. The center of the room bloomed with swaying bodies and soft, murmured conversation. Even some of the more stone- faced precinct veterans had been pulled into the orbit of it all, letting themselves be guided into dances with awkward grace and shy laughter. Suddenly, one of the men William had been watching earlier peeled away from the bar, the tall guy in his suit, crisp collar opened enough to show he knew how to carry a little danger.

    He came straight for their table and walked right up to William and stopped beside him, steady and assured, a faint smile tugging at the corners of his mouth. Then he put his hand out, palm up, like it was the most obvious thing in the world.

    “Dance with me?” he asked, smooth enough to be criminal.

    Agatha’s eyebrows crept up. Rio blinked once, then twice. William, for a full beat, didn’t move. He stared at the guy’s hand like it was ticking. He shot a glance at Rio, eyes wide, whispering behind clenched teeth, “What do I do?”

    Rio nudged him gently with a smile. “You say yes, you idiot.” “I didn’t think—he’s actually—oh my God,” William muttered. Agatha, deadpan, swirled her drink and said, “If you don’t go with him, I will.”

    That snapped William into motion. He shot her a betrayed look. “You don’t get to steal my mysterious dream man. Back off.”

    Then he turned back to the guy who was still waiting, patient and unreadable. “Okay,” William said, lifting his chin. “But just know…I bruise easily.”

    The man smiled, faint but real. “I’ll be careful.”

    William gave Rio one last helpless glance as he stood, muttering, “If I’m not back in ten minutes, assume I’ve eloped.”

    He smoothed his shirt, brushed invisible lint off his sleeve, then placed his hand gently in the stranger’s.

    “I’m trusting you with my life and, more importantly, my reputation,” he said.

    Then, over his shoulder as they walked toward the dance floor, “Tell Lilia I died a hero.”

    Agatha smirked. Rio just shook her head, still smiling as William disappeared into the slow-moving crowd, the man guiding him confidently toward the center of the room like he’d been planning it all along. The feeling crept up to her slowly.

    Agatha didn’t register it at first, not consciously. The music had slid from something orchestral and triumphant to a slower melody, low winding and indulgent. It felt like music meant for confessions whispered too close to someone’s ear, or kisses stolen beneath dim lighting, even fingers curling just a little tighter around someone’s waist than professionalism allowed. The tables around them were thinning. Chairs scraped softly back from linen-draped tables, heels clicking faintly against tile as bodies drew together. The couples had changed their stances softer, more intimate now. Arms looped around waists, foreheads brushing together in the low golden light.

Her whiskey had long gone warm, the ice having given up minutes ago, but she took a sip anyway. The burn was dull this time, even it had settled tiredly. She didn’t need to look to know Rio was sitting there quietly, probably fidgeting with her napkin or the edge of the tablecloth, maybe watching the dancers.

    Agatha didn’t meet it.

    She kept her eyes somewhere over Rio’s shoulder at first, focused on the chandelier above them, on the glitter of gold catching crystal, or the vague motion of other couples spinning slowly around them. They found their rhythm like that; a silent, wordless agreement to stay here, just like this for however long the music allowed.

    Agatha glanced down in front of her.

    Her breath caught; Rio was already looking at her.

    The woman’s expression wasn’t coy or teasing. No, she looked at Agatha without fear, memorizing her.

    The air thickened between them.

    Agatha’s hand shifted ever so slightly on Rio’s waist, drawing her in just a fraction more. She couldn’t help it; her body made the decision before her brain caught up. Rio followed, their torsos brushing now in the smallest, most excruciatingly perfect way. It was nothing and everything. The press of her hip, the way Rio’s hand flexed once at her shoulder, the sheer, brutal intimacy of their joined hands.

Her body answered for her in the way she held Rio just a little tighter, in the way her thumb rubbed lightly against the back of Rio’s hand. In the way she allowed herself not to think three steps ahead. She wanted to say something. Anything.

    You look beautiful.

    Please don’t look at me like that unless you mean it.

    Rio adjusted slightly, her hand on Agatha’s shoulder tightening, her fingers pressing just a little more firmly into her palm. God help her, because Agatha leaned into it.

    Finally, Rio broke the silence. Her voice was soft, like she was speaking into Agatha’s collarbone.

    “You’ve been avoiding me.”

    Agatha didn’t answer right away. Her eyes flicked away, focusing on the buzzing of other bodies around them, as though she could dodge the weight of the question entirely. But the truth was, she’d known it was coming. Had felt it inch closer with every look and every moment where their eyes met for too long and said too much.

    “I wonder why.”

    There was a pause. Rio’s hand squeezed just a little tighter where their fingers interlaced, like grounding herself, or maybe Agatha. Rio swallowed as she looked at the other woman, for her, the chandelier’s glow and the violins muted by clinks of glasses were all just white noise. “I don’t care about her.”

    Agatha’s jaw twitched, blue eyes flickered up to Rio’s face before darting away again. “Sure seemed like you did.”

    Rio’s breath caught, and she took a beat too long to respond. “Nothing happened between us,” she said firmly, she needed Agatha to hear it from somewhere deeper than just her mouth. “It never will.” Agatha really looked at her now, but with caution in her expression. Her heart had been half-closed for weeks and didn’t know how to open without cracking wide open. Her breath came out slow and careful, like the truth itself might knock her sideways.

    “You didn’t say anything,” she murmured. “She talked down to me like I wasn’t—like I didn’t matter.”

    Rio’s brow furrowed, but she didn’t interrupt and let Agatha keep talking. “She came into the precinct,” Agatha said, tone fragile. “Pretending she owned the place, like she still had a hold on you.”

    “She doesn’t.”

    “She made sure I knew what I wasn’t.”

    “She talked to me like I was just some…detour for you and you’d go back to her when you were done slumming it with someone who didn’t understand your world or protocols and expectations.”

    “Agatha—”

    “I believed her, and you knew that.”

    That admission landed heavy. Agatha’s voice had lowered further, rough and almost ashamed. Her eyes were locked on a point somewhere over Rio’s shoulder.

    “I believed her because she looked like someone you belonged with. She knew you in a way I never would. She spoke your same language.” Rio stepped in just a fraction and Agatha faltered from the proximity; her hand trembled slightly against Rio’s, but she didn’t pull away. “I never wanted you to feel like that,” Rio said, voice cracking slightly. “Not for a second.”

    Agatha scoffed. “Well, I did.”

    “I froze,” Rio said immediately. “I—God, Agatha. I didn’t know how to handle it. She knew how to push every button I had and made me doubt everything, and I let her. I didn’t know how to stand up for myself, let alone for you too.”

    Silence.

    Agatha looked down again. Her mouth pressed into a thin line. “She said you’d never want someone like me. That I wasn’t smart enough, clean enough, put-together enough. That I was just... something you were tolerating.”

    Rio’s breath left her in a rush. “That’s not true.”

    Agatha shook her head. Not disagreeing, but she didn’t know how to let herself believe it.

    “Agatha, she made sense for the version of me I thought I had to be,” Rio said, with quiet certainty. “She never saw me except for who I used to be. Who I pretended to be.”

    There was a pause. The music dipped into something gentler, and the lights above them caught the edge of Agatha’s cheekbone, the collar of her shirt, the thin tie that rested neatly between them like a fragile boundary. “You think I belong with someone like her?” Rio asked.

    Agatha shrugged one shoulder, the movement making her hand shift slightly on Rio’s waist.

    “You belonged somewhere before I even met you,” she said. “You had a life. I’m just…”

    “Just what?”

    Agatha’s jaw clenched again.

    “Just me.”

    Rio’s hand left Agatha’s shoulder, just long enough to brush a strand of hair behind her ear, fingers warm and lingering at her temple. “I like ‘just you,’” she said. “I want ‘just you.’”

    Agatha didn’t breathe for a second. Her eyes dropped to the floor, then to their joined hands, then finally back to Rio’s face.

    “You say that now,” she whispered. “But Wanda still exists, and she’s still trying to tear all of us apart.”

    “She doesn’t get to win,” Rio replied, the sternness in her voice unmistakable. “Not with this. Not with you.”

    Agatha let the silence hang between them again. Her thumb rubbed gently along Rio’s fingers, trying to memorize the shape of her. The music pulled them along in slow, swaying steps. Neither noticed the other dancers anymore.

    “I thought if I stayed away,” Agatha murmured, “maybe you’d be safer. Maybe you’d get your job back, or get reassigned. Maybe she’d back off.” “I didn’t want distance,” Rio said. “I wanted you.”

    Agatha winced, the confession sweeping the breath out of her. They were silent again, but this time, the air was different. Full of tension, yes, but also clarity; a kind of quiet surrender.

    “I missed you,” Rio added, voice barely above a breath.

    Agatha’s eyes lifted. She let the words sit between them for a moment, soaking through her layers of doubt. She wanted to say it back, she ached to, but instead she leaned her forehead lightly against Rio’s temple. If she had learned one thing about working amongst sleezy cops, was that someone was always listening, so she whispered to the woman in front of her.

    “I don’t know if we’re allowed to do this,” she said. “Not with Wanda still watching, or with the job.”

    “But even if everything goes to shit after tonight,” Agatha continued slowly, “I needed to know if you felt it too. If I wasn’t making it up in my head.” Rio inched herself closer to the woman holding her. “You’re not imagining it. If all I get is one dance, I want it to be with you.” Agatha closed her eyes and let herself lean into it just a little more. Her body softened.

    “Okay,” she whispered.

    They kept swaying as one song bled into the next in a soft hum, the pulse tentative and blooming.

    The music wound down slowly, the final notes of the string quartet lingering in the air like the softest sigh. It was an ending that didn’t feel like an ending at all, the moment felt suspended in the hush of everything unsaid. Around them, the ballroom stirred, murmurs picking up as couples stepped apart, clapping lightly, smiling, returning to their tables or drifting toward the bar. But in the center of it all, Agatha and Rio remained. Agatha’s palm was still resting against Rio’s waist, fingers barely curled, and Rio’s hand lingered on her shoulder like it didn’t know how to let go. Their joined hands remained clasped, unmoving, suspended between them. Agatha’s breath ached in her chest in that familiar, quiet way she’d grown used to, a private longing she’d shoved deep down too many times. Her eyes flicked to Rio’s face, and found it just inches away, flushed in the soft golden light. The expression there was open, unguarded and waiting. Her heartbeat thudded in her ears.

    Then she shook her head slowly, maybe she could physically jar herself out of whatever spell had taken root in her chest as she exhaled hard through her nose. The sound broke whatever fragile thread had been holding them still. Her hand tightened just slightly around Rio’s.

    “Come on,” she muttered, voice low, rough, cracking on the edges. Rio blinked at her, surprised. “What?”

    Agatha didn’t wait. She took a step back, hand still wrapped around Rio’s fingers, tugging gently enough to let Rio choose to follow. Her pulse was hammering in her ears.

    Rio followed.

    Their hands remained joined as they weaved past the other officers, who were too absorbed in conversation or laughter to notice. Agatha didn’t look at anyone, she kept her eyes fixed ahead until she reached the heavy double doors that led to the terrace. She pushed through them, the cool night air immediately wrapping around her, sharp against the heat she hadn’t realized had built under her collar.

    Outside, the noise dulled into a distant hum. The terrace was quiet, lit by soft amber sconces and the glow from the ballroom windows. The stone balustrade gleamed faintly in the moonlight, overlooking manicured hedges and the glitter of a distant city skyline. It was quiet and private. Agatha’s grip loosened only once they were far enough away from the door. She stopped by the edge of the railing, looking out. She didn’t speak right away, her mind running a million miles an hour and overthinking what she was about to do. Rough hands held tight onto the surface that overlooked a lush garden.

    Rio’s voice finally broke it, quiet. Tentative.

    "Agatha."

    Agatha didn’t answer right away.

    Rio whispered, barely. "I meant what I said in there."

    Agatha turned slowly. Her eyes were darker out here, rimmed with something tired, or scared.

    “Stop looking at me like that,” Agatha muttered, but there was no real bite. A thread of vulnerability.

    Rio’s brow furrowed. “Like what?”

    Agatha’s jaw tightened as she looked away again, briefly enough to try to pull herself together. But it didn’t work, not with Rio standing there like that, somewhere between uncertainty and something braver. “Like I’m everything you’ve ever wanted,” Agatha said hoarsely, voice rough with restraint.

    Rio’s breath caught, and then she stepped closer.

    “I do mean it,” she whispered. “I’ve meant it every time.” Agatha stared at her, a crack in the armor she’d kept bolted tight for months.

    “I can’t do this anymore,” Agatha muttered.

    She tugged Rio forward and turned them with a single step until Rio’s back hit the cool stone railing of the terrace, the soft amber light catching in her hair. The breath caught in Rio’s throat, her body going still as Agatha stepped in close, the heat of her chest nearly brushing against Rio’s dress. Her face hovered inches away, close enough to kiss.

    Her hand remained at Rio’s wrist, thumb tracing a slow, agonizing circle against her skin.

    “Tell me you want me to stop.” Agatha said lowly, her breath warm against Rio’s lips, her voice barely above a whisper but thick with need. Rio’s mouth parted, and her lashes fluttered with a quiet kind of surrender. “No,” she breathed.

    Agatha didn’t hesitate.

    She surged forward, crashing her mouth to Rio’s in a kiss that stripped every layer of restraint she had left. Her hands found Rio’s waist, trembling with want, then slid up, palms spanning the bare expanse of her back. Skin to skin. The contact made her groan against Rio’s mouth, deep and guttural, like the pressure of finally touching her like this physically hurt. Rio gasped, lips parting instantly, and Agatha took the invitation without pause. Her tongue slid in, tasting and claiming. Their mouths instantly moved in hungry tandem, teeth clashing with lips already swollen from how eagerly they chased one another. It wasn’t gentle nor polite. It was heat and hunger and months of biting their tongues, pouring out all at once into the spaces between them.

    Rio melted under the touch, her hands curling into Agatha’s suit jacket, dragging her in, her back arching just slightly to press them closer. Her head tilted, mouth open wider, tongue meeting Agatha’s with a soft, helpless moan that vibrated right through both of them. Her hips shifted instinctively, grinding just once against the thigh slotted between her legs, and Agatha’s hand on her back clenched.

    She deepened the kiss, shifting her stance, tilting her head to the other side so she could kiss her again, harder. Wet sounds filled the quiet terrace with their tongues stroking, lips dragging, and a faint click of teeth when they moved too fast. Every time they pulled apart to breathe, they dove back in just as quickly, breathless and desperate for more.

    Agatha’s hand began to slide lower over Rio’s hip, gripping it tight. She pressed forward, her body flush against hers now, feeling the soft pressure of Rio’s chest against her own, the way she shivered when their bodies finally aligned with nothing but fabric and heat between them. Rio whimpered, broken and breathy, her fingers trembling where they clutched the edge of Agatha’s blazer. She kissed her again, greedily, her lips red and chasing the taste of her even when Agatha slowed, just barely, to suck gently at her bottom lip.

    Agatha sighed at the feel of her and dragged her lips one more time across Rio’s before finally breaking away, foreheads pressed together with both of them panting.

    Rio’s lips stayed parted, kiss-drunk and flushed with eyes heavy-lidded as she stared up at her. Her breath hitched as Agatha’s thumb brushed the corner of her mouth, wiping the slick away, then trailing lightly along her jaw.

    Agatha’s voice was low. Intimate in a way that sounded almost reverent. “Come home with me,” she said, barely breathing the words. Rio just nodded, eyes flicking down to Agatha’s mouth like she might pull her in again.

    Agatha didn’t kiss her again, she simply stared for a beat longer, committing the look on Rio’s face to memory; flushed cheeks, swollen lips, and a yes hanging from her mouth before it was spoken. Then she stepped back, enough to take Rio’s hand again, interlacing their fingers. Neither of them said anything else as they left the terrace. The ballroom lights glowed warm behind them, but Agatha didn’t look back. Her world had already narrowed to the woman walking beside her, heels clicking softly against the marble, her fingers curled tight in Rio’s own.
